# German Rural Settlement Between the Prosna, Pilica, and Vistula Rivers

## Table of Contents

- Introduction
- Chapter I: Olęder Settlement. The First Attempt at Modernization in Agriculture
- Chapter II: Settlement in South Prussia. The Lights and Shadows of Cameralism on the Fringes of the State
- Chapter III: A Difficult Legacy. German Colonists in the Duchy of Warsaw
- Chapter IV: Continuity and Change in the Settlement Process in the Kingdom of Poland (1815–1866)
- Chapter V: Religious Life and Education in the Lives of German Colonists
- Chapter VI: German Colonists and Their Neighbours. Influences and Interactions
- Chapter VII: The German-Polish Historiographical Dialogue on Settlement in the Kingdom of Poland
- Conclusion
- Bibliography
- Summary
- Zusammenfassung

## Introduction

Nearly two centuries of continuous presence of a large German population in central Poland, and several waves of settlement passing through this territory from north and west to east, have long attracted the interest not only of historians, but also of geographers, regional explorers, and specialists of various fields. It is therefore no surprise that questions regarding the causes, course, and consequences of the presence of German newcomers have generated an enormous body of literature, both Polish and German. Even the very terminology used to describe migration and settlement from the 18th and 19th centuries aroused no less emotion than the process itself to which it referred. Given its scale and significance, and with reference to the 13th-century colonisation under German law, scholars wrote for decades of the second wave of German colonisation on Polish lands. German historiographers created the myth of the German colonist as a Kulturträger, fulfilling a civilising mission in the East. This very understanding of colonisation — as a mass emigration from a country with a higher level of economy and culture to one that was backward — long determined how the influx of foreign, German newcomers to Poland was perceived. Today, colonisation is understood as the settlement of inhabitants and the creation of settlements on uninhabited or sparsely populated territory. According to the origin of the settlers, a distinction is made between internal colonisation, carried out within a single ethnic area (one state), and external colonisation, in which settlers from other ethnic areas (states) participate. There is also a growing tendency to treat colonisation as a form of investment, consisting in the creation of new productive holdings — rural or urban — by settlers coming from a different economic or ethnic region. Understood in this way, colonisation processes occurring in the modern era can be divided into spontaneous, directed, and planned.

The process of colonisation is always linked to migration, that is, the movement of population aimed at a permanent change of residence. One of the main stimuli driving this movement is the desire to improve one's livelihood. Recent historical studies distinguish, depending on the causes, two types of migration: forced and voluntary.[^1] Forced migrations are usually caused by human agency or by natural disaster. Such population movements encompass all social groups. Voluntary migration, by contrast, is a social process; it proceeds spontaneously, elementally, and most often involves groups situated on the lowest rungs of the social ladder, seeking better living and working conditions.[^2] Migration can be shaped by creating conditions that either encourage or discourage emigration. Historical research still lacks a broad perspective that would combine into a single picture both the effects that migration produced in the country of origin, the characteristics of the emigrants, their migration routes, their impact at the place of settlement, and the almost entirely unexplored phenomenon of returns to the homeland.[^3] At the turn of the 20th and 21st centuries, the explanation of migration processes using the Push and Pull model — popularised through American sociology — became widespread. Applied to the problem of the movement of German peasants eastward across Europe, it goes far beyond the claim, popularised by Eduard Heinrich Busch in the mid-19th century, concerning the typical German fondness for wandering, rooted in the tradition of Gesellenwanderung, which was fostered by the political and economic fragmentation of the Reich.[^4] On the Push side, economic factors appear dominant — above all, economic crises of various origins. The broader social context of these crises is also important, however. The Enlightened absolutism prevailing at the close of the 18th century subjected society more strongly to state authority, which manifested itself in increasing taxation of the population, greater engagement in military service, and a drive to increase production. To a greater or lesser extent, the state also began to interfere in religious matters. At the same time, traditional patriarchal relations were weakening, which inevitably affected relations between authority and its subjects. Traditional forms of life were also beginning to change. Emigration thus became attractive — emigration to countries where the social structure was less rigid and where, in a foreseeable period of time, it was possible to maintain more freedom and distance from existing conditions. The feeling of not belonging to a place, of not being rooted, and ultimately of the marginalisation of one's social position, may also explain the psychological factors inclining people towards emigration.[^5] Religious motivations could also serve as a spur to emigration — or more precisely, a certain attitude shaped by religion. Numerous examples (Mennonites, Swabian secessionists, Pietists, Herrnhuters) indicate that this factor was very significant for the migration movements at the turn of the 18th and 19th centuries.[^6] It sometimes happened that all the inhabitants of a given village emigrated, as was the case with Lwówek (Leonberg). The entire community of this Württemberg village, steeped in Pietist ideas, emigrated to the Kingdom of Poland and settled near Sanniki in Gostynin county in 1802.[^7] A year earlier, the village of Nowosolna (Neu-Sulzfeld) had been founded to the east of Łódź, whose inhabitants were likewise united by a sense of religious brotherhood.[^8] Over the years it became a centre of the Brethren movement (Moravian Brethren, Herrnhuters) radiating across the entire Kingdom of Poland.[^9] Among the Pull factors, one usually lists the entire set of conditions created in the receiving country with a view to the permanent settlement and integration of immigrants with the local population.[^10] Receiving countries offered newcomers relatively wide autonomy. They guaranteed periods of exemption and freedom from taxes, granted land — often (especially in Russia) free of charge — exempted settlers from military service, and assured respect for their religion and language. Newcomers also perceived the relative stability of social relations in the country of settlement as attractive. The ultimate result of both migration and colonisation is the binding of their participants to territories that are new to them — that is, settlement. In definitions of settlement formulated by both historians and geographers, it is emphasised that the essence of this process is the mutual interaction between human groups and territory. The result is the creation of settlement points that are not merely settlements in the strict sense, but also centres of economic activity, which simultaneously constitute the framework within which the social relations appropriate to the level of civilisational development of a given era are formed. Settlement can thus be examined in two aspects: as a process of occupying a certain territory, or as a complex of processes constituting the sum of physiographical conditions and the activity of a human group.[^11] This second, broader approach has been adopted as the appropriate one for the problems discussed below.

The use of the term *settlement* [*osadnictwo*] in reference to the problems described in this study seems more apt than the use of the concept of *colonisation* for two reasons. First, it breaks with the tradition — still resonating in the word *colonisation* — of evaluating cultures; second, it emphasises that the main object of the author's interest is the answer to the question: what happened between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula rivers as a result of the settlement of newcomers from the German cultural sphere? While the economic consequences have already been the subject of research, knowledge of the social consequences does not extend beyond a few rather general observations. Therefore, the aim of this study — beyond characterising the distinctive features of the process of the settlement of German peasants in central Poland — has been an attempt to reconstruct the source-based details of life within their villages, and of relations between the settlers and their surroundings. Beyond the scope of the issues addressed in this work remain the consequences of emigration of the peasants migrating to Polish lands for their home countries, as well as questions relating to the technical aspects of their journey. For a presentation of the title problem as colonisation, an analysis of both of these elements would be necessary, which would require extensive use of dispersed German sources.[^12]

The expression used above — *newcomers (settlers) from the German cultural sphere* — is more precise and historically more accurate than *German colonists*, which was born in the same intellectual climate as *German colonisation*. The immigrants settling on Polish lands came from the territory of the German Reich and its borderlands, and the strongest bond uniting them was language and religion (Protestant confessions). Within this vast area, different emigration centres can be found at different times. Until at least the mid-19th century, a strong sense of belonging to various small homelands (*Heimat*) persisted among the German diaspora on Polish lands. Upon settling on Polish soil, they identified themselves as Prussian, Württemberg, or Saxon subjects; and their descendants, born in the Kingdom of Poland, were loyal subjects of the Russian tsars. Contemporary testimonies speak clearly of differences in origin felt and expressed by the settlers. They manifested their attachment to their home regions — private homelands — left behind mainly in the south-western part of Germany (Württemberg, Hesse, Baden, the Palatinate) — in various ways. The majority were therefore ethnic Swabians. This circumstance contributed, among other things, to the development in colloquial Polish of a synonym for *Niemiec* [German] — *Szwab* [Swabian].[^13] At the same time, until the 1870s, the use in Polish of the term *Niemiec* [German] as an ethnic and external marker for settlers in general persisted. It corresponded to the view, already established in the Middle Ages, that all those arriving from the west onto Polish lands were Germans.[^14]

The term *kolonista* [colonist] derives from contemporary sources on settlement — both from the language of Polish administration and from colloquial speech — and refers to peasants settled on land as rent-paying tenants. Their settlements, *kolonie*, were often situated near older serf villages and named similarly, but with the addition of the qualifier *Niemiecki*, *Niemiecka*, *Niemieckie*, *Kolonia* [German/Colony]. The widespread use and entrenchment of the name *kolonia* was brought about by the Prussian period (1793–1806), when in the territories seized in the Second and Third Partitions of Poland, *königliche Kolonien* began to be established. From the late 1820s onward, as the enfranchisement of estates progressed, villages organised in the colonial manner appeared, often called *kolonie* for short. This name referred only to the socio-economic status of their inhabitants, who were rent-paying tenants. It had no connection whatsoever to the nationality of the householders, since Polish serf peasants were also converted to rent tenancy. The expression *deutscher Kolonist* — *niemiecki kolonista* [German colonist] — can today be treated as a kind of *terminus technicus*, a stylistic device in which echoes can be heard of once-suggestive historiographical concepts that demanded seeing in the colonist a farmer-householder, but above all a German. In the narrative layer I use this term, treating it as a synonym for the designations *osadnik* [settler], *gospodarz* [householder], *chłop-czynszownik* [rent-paying peasant], without disputing the character of the ethnos from which he derived. It is worth noting that the designation *koloniści niemieccy* [German colonists] was also extended to representatives of the first wave of Olęder settlers, who cultivated not only the Vistula valley but also the territories between the Warta and the Ner.[^15] At the close of the 18th century, only a few of them were direct descendants of Dutchmen (Frisians, Flemings). The majority constituted an ethnically mixed group. Over time, the names *Holender*, *Holendry* — used to designate the settler who differed in language and confession and who drained land, and the village in which he lived — underwent a dialectal transformation into *olender* / *olęder*, *Holendry*, and simultaneously ceased to be a term designating an ethnos, referring only to the legal and economic system represented by the settlers.[^16] The Prussian official August Carl von Holsche, who knew the vast expanses of Polish lands from personal experience, maintained that at the close of the 18th century the name *Holländer* was applied to *alle ausländische Kolonisten, welche sich in Polen niederließen und Brücher urbar machen*.[^17] In Polish administrative practice — for example, when drawing up settlement contracts or, later, notarial deeds — an Olęder was described as *uczciwy, sławetny, pracowity* [honest, reputable, industrious]. In German-language documents one most often found *ehrbare Holländer*, *arbeitsame Holländer*, but also *Landwirte*, and less frequently *Kolonisten*. The Olęders themselves regarded the designation *chłop* [peasant/serf] as an insult, since it was synonymous with a serf. They therefore preferred to call themselves Olęders or *gospodarze* [householders], to emphasise that they farmed independently.[^18]

A distinctive Polish-German historiographical dialogue on various aspects of the colonisation process developed over the years with varying intensity. The short-term, political entanglements of the image of German settlement in Poland did not serve attempts at objective, source-documented analysis, and for a long time determined the manner in which settlers' activities were described and assessed. The period attracting the most scholarly attention was that of the Kingdom of Poland (1815–1915), that is, the period in which the settlement network of German colonies stabilised at its maximum extent. Less attention was devoted to Olęder settlement, which by the time of the Third Partition had made progress in the former Łęczyca Voivodeship comparable to that in Greater Poland. Even less often did scholars concern themselves with German newcomers during the Prussian period (1793–1806) and the Duchy of Warsaw (1807–1814). The dynamism of the settlement process clearly weakened after 1846. The inflow of new settlers from outside the borders of the Kingdom of Poland almost entirely ceased, and established colonists only rarely decided to change their place of residence, most often contenting themselves with farming the inherited land and at most purchasing additional plots, usually within the boundaries of their own village. After 1864, a new phenomenon began: the departure from already overpopulated settlements lying to the west of the Vistula, and the search for new homes in the Lublin and Siedlce guberniyas. This new migration movement was caused by the growing overpopulation of colonist villages on the western side of the Vistula, and by lower land prices — at good quality — in the south-eastern reaches of the Kingdom of Poland.[^19]

The territorial framework of this work is closely connected to the process described. Polish scholarship has repeatedly signalled the difficulties that the concept of a region, divided by politicised administrative boundaries, poses for historical research.[^20] It is worth noting that the most recent historical works on socio-economic problems in areas characterised by frequent changes of administrative boundaries and lacking a tradition of regional distinctiveness refer to delimitations defined by natural boundaries.[^21] In settlement geography research too, the application of such criteria has an established tradition. During the period under study, state borders and the borders of administrative units changed many times. Therefore, taking the above considerations into account, the territorial framework of this work coincides with natural boundaries, some of which (the rivers Prosna and Pilica) also served as political borders. The northern boundary of the area under discussion is defined by the line of the border of the Netze District (*Obwód Nadnotecki*/*Netze-Distrikt*), running from Pyzdry to the Vistula at the height of Osiek. This line, which did not follow any significant natural features of the terrain, also formed part of the state border of the Duchy of Warsaw and the Kingdom of Poland with Prussia. In the pre-partition era, the area between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula encompassed the voivodeships of Brześć Kujawski, Łęczyca, Rawa, Sieradz, as well as the part of the Mazovian Voivodeship lying to the west of the Vistula and the part of the Kalisz Voivodeship lying to the east of the Prosna. During the South Prussian period, this territory was subordinated to the chamber departments of Kalisz and Warsaw, and its eastern marches — corresponding almost exactly to the area of the former Brześć Kujawski Voivodeship — belonged to the Poznań chamber. During the Duchy of Warsaw, the areas of interest to us formed part of the Warsaw department (to the west of the Vistula), the Kalisz department (to the east of the Prosna), and their northern extremities fell within the Bydgoszcz department. In the Kingdom of Poland, the area between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula belonged from 1815 to the Kalisz and Mazovian voivodeships, and after their renaming in 1837, to the Kalisz and Mazovian guberniyas, which were merged in 1845 into the Warsaw guberniya.[^22] In the German historical literature dealing with Polish lands, this part of them is designated by the term *Mittelpolen*. Like its Polish counterpart — *Polska Środkowa* [Central Poland] — the boundaries of this area have not been precisely delineated. It was sometimes identified with the Kingdom of Poland (*Kongreßpolen*, *Russisch-Polen*), and even more often with a territory of an irregular circular shape of varying radius, but always with Łódź at its centre.[^23] The borders of these variously named administrative units encompassed essentially always the same territory, within which before 1864 approximately 90% of all settlements inhabited by Germans in the Kingdom of Poland existed. The remaining ones were distributed to the east of the Vistula, in the north-eastern territories of the Kingdom, within the bounds of the Płock and Augustów guberniyas, in their extent as of 1837–1866.[^24]

The historiographical tradition, both Polish and German, has established the examination of German agricultural settlement in central Poland within several time intervals, defined by dates of political change. In the approach presented here, I adhere to the traditional divisions used hitherto in the literature on the subject. These have, however, only an ordering function for the narrative, since what interests me above all is the settlement process as a socio-economic phenomenon, the continuity of which was linked to systemic change — the transition from feudalism to capitalism. In the agriculture of central Poland this process lasted nearly 100 years. For continuity understood in this way, political changes, including changes of borders, were of secondary importance. The settlement process is therefore examined as a phenomenon of the *longue durée*, carrying with it also a number of modernising elements.[^25] Its essential characteristic feature was the spread of rent-based farming. The area between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula became the meeting point of two factors significant for its expansion: the knowledge of a new type of farming brought by newcomers from the north and west, and the striving of local landowners to improve (intensify) their farming — in which it is hard not to see the effects of Enlightenment thought. During the Prussian occupation (1793–1806) and during the Kingdom of Poland, this second factor gained further institutional support from the government. Above all, however, the settlement of German peasants on the lands of central Poland was a spontaneous, elemental process, determined by economic conditions, and stimulated until the decline of the First Polish Republic by landowners. Settlers came not only to private estates, but were also settled in royal lands and church properties belonging both to individual parishes and to monastic orders. This spontaneous current of settlement did not practically cease until the mid-19th century, although in its final phase it lost much of its intensity and no longer encompassed church properties. It was favoured by the supply of land and a relatively low entry fee. The final years of the First Polish Republic brought the first timid attempts at influencing the country's economy from above, a striving to intensify population growth, which, in accordance with the doctrines of the time, was supposed to translate into an increase in state revenues and power. This state of affairs was to be achieved by encouraging foreigners to settle within the borders of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth.

The beginnings of the process of conversion to rent tenancy are to be found in the 1770s, with the appearance of Olęder settlers on the northern and western frontier of the then Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth. The farming methods brought by newcomers from the Netherlands, and the settlements characteristic of those methods, had spread in Poland since the mid-16th century, initially encompassing the Żuławy Wiślane and the territories along the lower course of the Vistula. The Olęders settling in central Poland in the 18th century were a subsequent generation, already born in Pomerania, Greater Poland, and the Netze District. The organisational and legal form of creating new settlements characteristic of the Olęders persisted until the end of the 1840s. Onto the Olęder wave of settlement, further waves were superimposed. First came the planned wave, implemented from above by Prussia in the territory gained as a result of the Second Partition of Poland, which — alongside economic goals, mainly fiscal — also had a political objective: to create from South Prussia a land bridge between Silesia and East Prussia. The brief period of Prussian rule did not permit the realisation of this plan. The era of the Duchy of Warsaw, unfavourable to creative economic activity, confronted its authorities with the problem of continuing or abandoning — for lack of financial means — an already initiated process. The period of the Kingdom of Poland — first autonomous, and after 1832 increasingly harnessed into the political and economic system of the Russian Empire — brought an intensification of German population migration. One can speak of a certain priority given in the constitutional era of the Kingdom to the policy of attracting useful foreigners to the country. Legislation served this end, creating not only incentives but also giving newcomers guarantees of the protection of their rights. After 1832, competition from Russia — interested in attracting colonists — became more visible, although it had existed before.[^26]

The concluding chronological boundary of this study is defined by the definitive regulation of the legal and economic status of German peasants in the Kingdom of Poland, which was brought about by the imperial edict of 9 May 1866. Already following the declaration of enfranchisement on 2 March 1864, the distinction between colonist-rent-payers and serf peasants, and between *wsi okupne* [purchased villages] and *wsi zarobne* [earned villages], disappeared. However, the question of the subjects' status of the settlers remained a problem and required precise regulation. After 1866, the distinctiveness of German peasants farming in the Kingdom of Poland had only a cultural dimension. Beyond the sphere of material culture, it was visible in language (including toponymy, since *holendry* and *kolonie* remained), religious life and the education linked to it, and customs. This situation persisted until the years immediately preceding the outbreak of the First World War, when the tsarist administration perceived in subjects of German origin a potential vanguard of the Second Reich, which was preparing for war. The deportation, carried out in early 1915, of thousands of German peasants from the Kingdom of Poland into the interior of Russia, interrupted a continuity of settlement lasting more than a century.[^27] The return of the majority of colonists to their farms after 1918 initiated the last period of their stay on Polish lands. After 1945, the cultural continuity of German settlement in central Poland was definitively severed.

The factor that from the mid-18th century until the enfranchisement of 1864 distinguished all colonist settlements from serf villages was the different legal conditions governing land use. Settlers received land under the right of subordinate (usufructuary) ownership (*ius utile*), as hereditary tenants, rent-payers. Superior ownership (*dominium directum*) was retained by the treasury (in state properties), the landlord (in private properties), or the bishop, chapter, or heads of monastic orders (in church properties). Subordination of this type of ownership consisted in the obligation to pay rent upon the expiration of the free years stipulated in the contract, the requirement to obtain consent (*konsens*) for sale, and to pay *laudemium* (*jurium gabellae*). Within these limits, the rent-payer enjoyed the right of hereditary possession, use, and disposal of the land.

The basis of this study consists of sources of diverse provenance, of various types and categories, appropriate to the problems addressed in this work, gathered as a result of research in Polish and foreign archives. A detailed list of these is provided in the bibliography. The territorial and chronological scope of the work, the variety of problems discussed in it, and the dispersal of documentary material mean that fulfilment of the classical principle of exhausting the sources is not possible. Various matters relating to settlers interested both the highest state authorities — often becoming an element of their economic and social policy — and representatives of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church, which undertook the effort of institutionally caring for its faithful. For the reconstruction of almost all the realities of the settlers' daily life, notarial records are a source of inestimable value. Municipal records were also used — which might seem of little particular interest for rural problems. Yet in urban centres surrounded by villages inhabited by colonists, and especially in those that were seats of Evangelical-Augsburg parishes, matters relating to the otherness of German neighbours found their interesting documentary reflection. From the settlers' own milieu, in which written expression was rare, only a small number of testimonies survive — usually correspondence with the authorities concerning the most essential life matters. For this reason, the archival material used is dominated by accounts of settlers originating from officials of various levels or from clergy, presenting their own point of view. An important supplement to the picture emerging from the archival materials is provided by various 19th- and 20th-century accounts, memoirs, and diaries, whose authors, perceiving the cultural distinctiveness of German settlers, described it. Serious documentary material was also provided by German-language studies authored by Germans living in the Second Polish Republic who described various aspects of the past of their own national group.[^28] Given the destruction during the Second World War of many of the sources on which they were based, their documentary value is high. Cartographic sources appropriate to the topic undertaken were also used to a significant extent.[^29] These are supplemented in detail by maps and plans discovered by the author during archival research, published here for the first time. A discussion of the literature — which has accumulated in both Polish and German historiography around the settlement of German peasants on Polish lands — would necessarily exceed the bounds typical of an introduction. We are dealing here with a kind of historiographical dispute in which, especially in the past, alongside attempts to describe the phenomenon, a battle was waged to demonstrate German economic and cultural dominance. This abundance of studies — Polish and especially German — reflecting different perspectives on issues related to settlement / colonisation, determined the decision to discuss them in the closing chapter of the book.

The structure of this work derives from the characteristics of the settlement process described and follows a chronological-thematic arrangement. This allows both a broader context of the settlement process in each time period to be presented, and the specificity of those problems to be highlighted which, as characteristics of the *longue durée*, take on meaning only in a longer time perspective. The material illustrating the progress of the settlement process is contained in Chapters I–IV. Chapter five is devoted to the religious life and education of the settlers. These were the only areas of their life that from the beginning acquired an institutional dimension. The importance of both was very high, confirmed by the regulations contained in all known settlement contracts, and later in the legislation of the Duchy of Warsaw and the Kingdom of Poland. The final chapter presents the diversity of assessments of individual phases of rural settlement in central Poland, concentrating on the issues that once formed the axis of the Polish-German historiographical dispute: the numbers, origins, significance, and merits of colonists in raising the cultural level of the territories encompassed by settlement.

Sincere words of thanks for the opportunity to present partial results of my research and discuss them are due to all colleagues and associates who participated in the seminars led by the late Professor Józef Śmiałowski and Professor Wiesław Puś at the former Department of Social and Economic History at the University of Łódź. Warm thanks go to Professor Klaus Zimmer, Director of the German Historical Institute in Warsaw, for the opportunity to discuss the arguments of this work during a colloquium organised at the GHI. I shall retain in grateful memory the generous assistance afforded me by the staff of the Herder Institute in Marburg, and of Polish and foreign archives, during the archival and library research conducted there.

Wolf, Württembergische Rückwanderer aus Ost- und Südosteuropa in der ersten Hälfte des 19. Jahrhundert, [in:] *Migration nach Ost- und Südosteuropa...*, pp. 263–290.

[^5]: A letter is known from the Saxon manufacturer Gottlieb Krusche, settled in Pabianice, who in 1826 wrote to a friend in his native Reichenau: *Im Reichenau musste ich mit meiner Familie dem gänzlichen Untergange entgegensehen* [...], *dort war ich im gänzlichen Drucke und Verachtung, hier bey Kalischen Regierung* [Commission of the Kalisz Voivodeship — K.P.W.'s note] *in guter Aufnahme…* (E. Kunitzer, Der Vater der Stadt Pabianitz. Gottlieb Krusche (1769–1851) und seine Nachfolger, [in:] *Deutsche Gestalter und Ordner im Osten. Forschungen zur deutsch-polnischen Nachbarschaft im ostmitteleuropäischen Raum*, hrsg. von K. Lück, Leipzig 1942, p. 327). Krusche did not, it is true, come from a rural background, but the feeling expressed in the letter confirms the significance of the problem of alienation.

## Chapter I: Olęder Settlement. The First Attempt at Modernization in Agriculture

The introduction of rent-based farming on Polish lands was closely linked to the development of Olęder settlement. The transformation of the feudal structure of the serf economy through a change in the legal position of the peasant exceeded the economic imagination of the nobility. Conducting a rent economy was perceived in the mid-18th century as burdened with incalculable risk. The impulse to change this conviction came from several sources. In the second half of the 18th century, the ideas of physiocracy — with its criticism of feudal relations that tolerated wasteful use of the greatest national asset, the land — were not unknown in the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth. The most far-reaching concepts postulated the idea of creating large peasant farms based on hired labour, in which modern land cultivation methods would be employed. In domestic projects, the conversion of peasants to rent tenancy was proposed. Such views were held by pioneers of national economics: Antoni Popławski, Hieronim Stroynowski, Jan Ferdynand Nax, Józef Wybicki, Stanisław Staszic.[^30] At the same time, with the exception of Staszic, they were also advocates of an increase in the country's population. "Population has been considered the foremost and most important interest of every nation," argued J. Wybicki, and F. Nax added: "With a significantly diminished population, everything becomes worthless."[^31]

It is hard not to detect in this argumentation an echo of Prussian cameralism. In Staszic's view, there was no need to bring in rent-paying peasants from abroad. It would suffice to free the Polish peasant from serfdom and take him under the protection of the state.[^32] This idea arose partly from Enlightenment motivations and partly from observation of what was happening on the western and northern frontiers of the country. Already in the first half of the 18th century, the physiocratic impulse of transitioning from a serf economy to a rent economy had brought about transformations in Prussia. Before the First Partition of Poland, approximately 60% of landowners there had introduced rents on their estates. In the royal domain, this proportion was as high as 70%. In the wake of this change, the property-based stratification of the peasantry began to advance. The wealthier peasants, through purchase or lease, expanded the area of their holdings. Others grew poorer or lost their properties, becoming free-wage agricultural labourers. In this way, a large group of impoverished and landless peasants arose in Prussia, for whom emigration in search of better living conditions became a necessity. The combination of economic circumstances was thus the cause of the emigration wave directed towards eastern Europe, which in older German historiography was called the second German colonisation. This spontaneously unfolding process intensified after the First Partition of Poland. The seizure of Gdańsk by Prussia decisively inhibited the export of Polish grain and timber. Export revenues fell significantly. In turn, the loss of Wieliczka with its salt mines to Austria meant a fall in state revenues of nearly 50%.[^33] The king and the magnates tried to compensate for these losses by establishing manufactories. The middle nobility, lacking sufficient means for such large investments, found themselves confronted with the possibility of modernising their farming through the introduction of rent. The hope of obtaining income, after the expiration of the free years, from hitherto agriculturally unexploited lands, encouraged the founding of rent-paying settlements. The lack of any top-down conception meant that the establishment of new settlements with foreign newcomers took place in all voivodeships, without regional preferences. The centre of gravity of this settlement was found in the north and west of the country, between the Warta and the Vistula, not crossing the Austrian border on the Pilica to the south — that is, in the area where the middle nobility responded most actively to the expansion of the monetary economy.

The second factor in such localization was the regularity of the settlement process — the step-by-step movement in search of opportunities to settle. A significant role was also played by the economy of travel costs, which grew with the distance covered. An additional factor encouraging settlement on Polish lands was the considerable difference — compared to Prussia — in the level of rent and land prices.

Olęder (Dutch) settlement is usually associated with the process, occurring in the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries, of colonizing the Vistula Delta and the areas along the lower course of the Vistula, and with the colonization of the Lebus Land and Greater Poland a century later. Meanwhile, the economic capacity of the socio-economic system popularized by the Olęders proved large and attractive enough that this method of developing wasteland was applied in the following century across vast areas of the Kingdom of Poland. References to Olęder law were still encountered there in the 1830s and 1840s.[^34] It took just over half a century (1731–1787) for the Vistula valley between Wyszogród and Ciechocinek to fill with industrious Olęders. In 1731, J. Milewski founded the settlement of Osiek Łęg in the Osiek estate with 5 farmers.[^35] Seven years later, Krzysztof Antoni Szembek, Bishop of Kujawy, settled 12 Olęders die durch ihre Wirtschaft auf überfluteten Böden gut bekannt sind in Psiarzewo (now: Siarzewo) near Ciechocinek.[^36] In 1745, Kazimierz Dąbski designated 40 łans of forest in his estates of Dąb and Dobiegniewo for industrious Olęders. Four years later, Antoni, the brother of K. Dąbski, settled several families on Kępa Antonińska, leasing the land on a 40-year term.[^37] The contract with the settlers was renewed in 1789 by the next owner of the estate, the castellan of Sochaczew, Adam Lasocki, who granted a perpetual lease to the seven Olęders then farming the 4 łans of land measured out only at that time.[^38] The settlements mentioned were not large. Each was inhabited by 2 to 7 families.[^39] In 1759, the provost of Płock, Wolicki, acting on behalf of the Bishop of Kujawy, settled the fertile Kępa Tokarska near Płock. It is worth noting here that for the toponymy of the Vistula valley, characteristic names are the Kępy, designating islands lying in the river's current. The modifier accompanying the Kępa was formed from the name of the nearest settlement on the bank. In all Low German dialects, as well as in Dutch, the term Kamp, Kaempe always denotes some ground raised above its surroundings.[^40] In Old Polish, on the other hand, the term ostrów was used to denote an island in a river.[^41] Hence the conclusion that the Vistula islands owe their names to their first, Olęder inhabitants.

Another onomastic feature of the area is the widespread use of the modifier Niemiecki (German), added to the name of a locality in which Olęder newcomers settled, in order to distinguish it from the older, twin village inhabited by Polish peasants.[^42] In 1759, Ignacy Cichocki, the starost of Troszyn, founded two villages: Troszyn Niemiecki and Borki, located between the village of Troszyn Polski and the Vistula riverbed.[^43] Troszyn soon became one of the more populous settlements; by 1798 it already had a school.[^44] In 1760, Wiączemin Niemiecki was established, founded by the lord of the Świniary estate, Bogusław Stokowski.[^45] Almost simultaneously, the starost of Duninów, Józef Kretkowski, settled Olęders in Witka Królewska. Then in 1764, opposite Modlin, the settlement of Kazuń Niemiecki arose (now: Nowy Kazuń). In 1777, the starost of Kampinos, Ludwik Gutakowski, settled Hollanders in the royal domain near the village of Wilków in the Kampinos Forest. They formed Holendry Wilkoskie, Holendry Secomskie, Holendry Gniewniewskie, and Holendry Małowiejskie. On the map of W. Chrzanowski, 37 settlements named Holendry are visible in the area between Kozienice and Toruń.

In areas covered by extensive forest complexes, the beginnings of Olęder settlements were often provided by the farmsteads of pustkowianie, budniks, and szałaśniks (squatters in wilderness areas). The map of David Gilly shows numerous settlement points described as Pustkowie (wasteland) and Budy (huts). Many of these are still recorded on the Topographical Map of the Kingdom of Poland, drawn thirty years later. In such remote places lived peasants who had fled their villages for various reasons and did not submit to the existing legal and economic order. The existence of these settlements, remote from the administrative centers of estates and usually located on their margins, eluded all inspections and registers. Only the end of the eighteenth century brought the first censuses of them. This was the case, among others, in the Brzeziny and Opatówek key-estates of the archbishops of Gniezno. In 1788, a register was conducted of the wastelands scattered throughout the local forests. Agreements were concluded with the peasants farming them, specifying the conditions of land use, and names were given to the settlements. The former wastelands took names from the surnames or nicknames of their farmers. From that moment, Kazimierz Biernat farmed in Pustkowie Biernaty, Marcin Czarny in Pustkowie Czerniłów, Franciszek Dulęba in Pustkowie Dulęby, Roch Dzięcioł in Pustkowie Dzięcioły, and Wawrzyn (Wawrzyniec) and Wojciech Zaleśni in Pustkowie Zalesie.[^46] Over time, with the development of the villages, the designation Pustkowie disappeared from the names. In some of them, however, the designation Holendry appeared instead.

The 1780s brought the next wave of revival in the settlement movement. Between 1780 and 1783, the non-castle starost of Sobota, Franciszek Jerzmanowski, created Olęder settlements in Ksawerów and Szydłówka (Rochów).[^47] In 1781, Kajetan Dębowski, the lord of the village of Czermno in Płock County, founded within its boundaries, on lower-lying and unused lands, the settlement of Holendry Czermińskie, to which he brought in three groups a total of 17 Olęders.[^48] This settlement would later be called Wymyśle Nowe. In the same year, K. Dębowski founded in Płock County near Gąbin the settlement of Rumunki (Rumunek Hol.), settling in it the honest Hollanders: Jakub Koniarski,

Jan Koniarski, Wojciech Drews, Wojciech Golik, Dawid Górski and other free Polish people, granting them 1 łan and 15 morgs of land.[^49] In 1785, Adam Lasocki began an intensive colonization campaign. He first created the settlement of Arciechówek, in which one Olęder settled,[^50] and four years later organized several further small settlements: Bieniew — with three farmers, Kępa Bieniewska — with three colonists on 2 łans, Kępa Pieczyska, on which he allocated 1 łan and 10 morgs, Łęg Januszewski (Holendry Januszewskie), where three colonists farmed on 2 łans of land,[^51] and he renewed the earlier 40-year lease with seven Olęders farming on Kępa Antonińska.[^52] Stanisław Zabłocki, the cup-bearer of Gostynin and owner of the Świniary estate, showed similar activity, creating in 1787 the settlement of Sady with two Olęders, and in 1789 founding the settlement of Łęg Suchodół with four colonists on 3 łans. Also in 1789, Zabłocki settled Kępa Karolińska, placing 6 farmers on 4 łans of land.[^53]

In 1787, Świniary Holenderskie was established in Płock County, in which another initiative of A. Lasocki may be discerned, since the wyrumowanie (i.e., clearing of undergrowth) was undertaken by Marcin Datzlaw — a German citizen of the village of Sady.[^54] In the last decade of the eighteenth century, further islands in the Vistula current were also settled: Kępa Celejowska, Kępa Okrzewska, Kępa Popielarska, Kępa Sempławska, Kępa Skurecka, Kępa Suchodolska, Kępa Śladowska, Kępa Wolczańska, Kępa Wyszogrodzka, Kępa Zakroczymska, Kępa Zawadowska. It is estimated that in the years 1770–1795, in the areas of the Vistula valley between the Bzura and the Pilica, more than 30 villages inhabited by German peasants came into existence.[^55]

Alongside the organized movement there also existed a spontaneous settlement movement, whose participants were individual peasants. The economic significance of this current could not have been great, but such initiatives well illustrate the genesis of the formation of settlement points called in archival and cartographic sources pustki, pustkowia, rumunki (wastelands, clearings).

The actual moment of their formation was rarely documented. Known is the deed confirming the purchase in 1778 of land on Kępa Troszyńska by the Olęder Karl Hilisch. The seller was a peasant named Olk who was farming there. The essence of the document is the declaration of the buyer, who undertook the obligation of labor service at the rate of 8 days with draught animals and 4 days with a scythe, aber zum Holländerbruderschaft wird er nicht gehören nach seinen eigenen Willen.[^56]

The majority of settlers in the villages between Nieszawa and Wyszogród came from the territories along the lower Vistula, from the vicinity of Świecie, Grudziądz, and Toruń. They constituted a community differentiated in national terms, among which only a few were still direct descendants of Frisian and Flemish colonists, but the vast majority were Mennonites. In folk tradition the name given to them — Niedrunger, Niederunger (those settled in the lowlands) — became fixed.[^57] This community bound by religious ties, constituting one of the currents of Anabaptism, is regarded as pioneers disseminating techniques of land drainage. Having left the Vistula Delta in the seventeenth century — where they had arrived directly from the Netherlands, fleeing religious persecution — they undertook a journey up the Vistula. The influx of Olęder-Mennonites settling within the borders of the Commonwealth could also have been caused by restrictive Prussian legislation.[^58] The Mennonites' right to own, acquire, and even lease land was restricted, but above all from 1789 onwards they were subjected to compulsory military service, which their professed doctrine did not permit.[^59] And land was greatly needed by the Mennonites to provide for their usually numerous children. Moving to Polish lands provided protection from recruitment into the Prussian army, while simultaneously opening up the possibility of acquiring the much-needed land.

In 1796, the authorities of the Piotrków department made an attempt to settle

Mennonites from the Vistula valley in their territory. They took advantage of the circumstance that these Schnelläufer der Ostwanderung, as they were called, were just then setting out on the road to southern Russia, where favorable settlement conditions were being offered to them in the vicinity of Kherson.[^60] By 1795, more than 1,100 Mennonite families had emigrated from West Prussia, heading south. The main cause of leaving their previous homes was the doubling of the church tax and the imposition on Mennonites of the obligation to pay high fees to the Cadet Corps in Chełmno, as a substitute for exemption from military service.[^61] In the times of the Duchy of Warsaw, Mennonites were guaranteed freedom from military service, while in the Kingdom of Poland they were subject to a recruitment tax.

Civil registration documents from the first quarter of the nineteenth century only sporadically record the surnames borne by the first settlers on the Vistula islands and in the settlements on the left bank of the river. This points to the great mobility of the representatives of this first phase of settlement and their undertaking of a further journey, up the Vistula and in the direction of Ukraine and Volhynia.[^62] This phenomenon intensified after the tsarist decree of 1843 imposing compulsory military service on Mennonites. In small numbers, Mennonites also spread across the territories between the Warta and the Bzura. The authorities of the Kingdom of Poland drew attention to the Olęder-Mennonites settled in the vicinity of Gąbin and, bearing in mind their skills, outlined prospects for their even greater settlement on empty peasant lands or even on manor farms, however on condition that in terms of property they would be able to demonstrate the requisite qualifications.[^63]

In 1817, 21 Mennonites lived in the Sieradz district (in Rypułtowice, Rokicie, Retkinię, Wiskitno, Piątkowisku, Kudrowice, Wola Żytowska, Żytowice) and in the mill settlement of Rexul near Zgierz. In 1836, 16 Mennonites inhabited Holendry Żarzyńskie in the commune of Węglew, in the Konin district.[^64] In a compact manner they were settled in two villages: in Wymyśl Niemiecki (now: Nowe Wymyśle) near Gąbin and in Kazuń Niemiecki, which was also the seat of their religious community. Several dozen more families lived dispersed across the territories between Nowy Dwór Mazowiecki and Iłów.[^65]

Mennonites enjoyed a very good reputation as excellent cattle breeders, and moreover as prosperous, quiet, and industrious farmers. The nobility was glad to settle them in their estates, since they always paid their rent punctually.[^66] The role of the Mennonites in disseminating farming on flood-prone lands and in applying Olęder law is indisputable, despite their being a small community. In keeping with the principles they professed, the Olęder-Mennonites remained on the sidelines, not engaging in any public activities. They were a closed group in which religiously mixed marriages were not permitted. The factor weakening their distinctiveness was the incorporation of the Mennonites in 1849 into the organizational structure of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church. The Mennonite community, whose numbers were estimated in 1848 at 1,500 persons, has been the subject of numerous discussions in the literature.[^67]

Such a significant revival of the settlement movement in the second half of the eighteenth century was not confined to the Vistula valley. Northern Kujawy, the vicinity of Gostynin, and the Noteć river basin became areas of a peculiar ferment in farming practices. In it one can see the first, stronger accumulative impulse, which encompassed all types of estates. Although it was still a socially very shallow phenomenon, there was nonetheless a circle of the nobility who began to seek ways of increasing income without breaking with the traditional occupation — cultivation of the land.[^68] This important novum was the development of wasteland, of waterlogged ground, naturally fertile but requiring great expenditure of labor to bring it under agricultural cultivation. Unsuccessful proved the attempts to develop waterlogged areas undertaken in Mała Brużyca by Fr. Andrzej Reptowski, provost of the archcollegiate church in Tum, and in the Grzegorzew estate by Archbishop of Gniezno Antoni Kazimierz Ostrowski, using serf peasants for that purpose.[^69] Earlier attempts to settle Polish rent-paying peasants, also undertaken in the Vistula valley, had not met with success.[^70] The Olęders, however, thanks to their knowledge and skills, were able to drain the land, designating it for arable fields and meadows. Olęder drainage works proved very durable. Their traces are visible in many places to this day.[^71] Land brought to agricultural cultivation provided the means of subsistence for settler families over several generations. On the Kujawy Lake District, Olęders appeared in 1753 at the initiative of J. Kanigowski, the cup-bearer of Wyszogród.[^72] In 1760, Przystronie was established, founded by K. Zakrzewski, owner of the Mostki estate. In the years 1772–1792, close to 110 small settlements were established, of which only a few had more than 10 farms. The villages were concentrated around the region's towns: Sompolno, Babiak, Chodecz, Biesiekierz, Dąbie on the Ner. The characteristic form of construction in this area was half-timbered barns (Fachwerkbau), betraying the Pomeranian origin of the settlers.[^73] Still in the 1930s, Low German (Plattdeutsch) was spoken in these villages.[^74] The peasants living in them were regarded as true colonists, which was to manifest itself in the preservation of strong cultural distinctiveness vis-à-vis the Polish surroundings.[^75] Compared to other groups of the German population, settlers from Pomerania were the most conservative. No other community showed such strong attachment to the land, was so traditionally peasant in character. In the 1780s, Olęder settlement encompassed the area of the Gostynin Lake District, i.e., the territories lying between Gąbin, Gostynin, and Włocławek. The area abounded in sandy hills and waterlogged, peat meadows. Newcomers from the Noteć District (Margonin, Łabiszyn, Szubin, Wyrzysk) headed for this area. The first settlement to arise on bare ground, in the Strzelce starosty, was Strzelce Holendry. This encouraged the lords of the Sieraków and Sójki estates to found the forest settlements of Sieraków Holendry and Wierzbie Holendry.[^76] Before 1796, there were 12 small Olęder settlements in the vicinity of Gostynin.

From the 1760s onwards, an advancing settlement movement from the west could also be observed, encompassing a relatively compact belt described in the German literature as Warthebruch, stretching along the Warta valley from the Prussian border to Leśnik, lying to the south of Uniejów. There arose in total 63 villages inhabited by German-speaking settlers, scattered amid oxbow lakes, marshes, bogs, and sandbars. The oldest locality attested by sources is Holendry Węglewskie, founded in 1767 in the Sławsk estate, the property of the land judge S. Leszczyc-Zielonawski. Subsequently there arose: Rumin Holendry, Brzeźno Holendry, Paprockie Holendry, Drążyn Holendry, and Tury Holendry. Their first inhabitants came from the territories along the Noteć, the Oder, and in the lower course of the Warta (the vicinity of Gorzów Wielkopolski). This diversity of origins manifested itself in language. Among the Olęders of the Warta valley, the popular Upper German (Hochdeutsch) tongue could often be encountered, and in the vicinity of Leśnik, dialects typical of the German inhabitants of Pomerania.[^77] Harvested grain was stacked in the Warta valley in kopy (stooks), not in mendle, as one would have said in the Vistula valley.[^78] The wealthiest settlements lay to the west of Konin. Cattle breeding and poultry — geese and ducks — were well developed there.

The oldest Olęder settlement in the Kalisz Land is considered to be Łazińskie Holendry, founded in 1746 in the Trąbczyn estate of Paweł Prusiński. In 1764, in the estate of Morawski, Olędry Chwalborzyckie was founded.[^79] In 1772, the castellan of Sieradz, J. Wężyk, settled in his estate in Grodziec four villages: Borowiec, Konary, Grądy, and Wielołęka. After 1770, numerous Olęder settlements arose in the Ceków estates. By 1799, 34 farmers were living there on 24 łans of land.[^80] In 1782, the sub-judge of Smolensk, Marcin Wągrowski, founded in his estate three Olęder settlements: Piegonisko, Sobiesęki, Stoki.[^81] Two years later, the owner of the Gadów estate, Piotr Sokoła-Garczyński, signed a contract with honest Hollanders for the founding of the settlement of Gadów Holendry. The starost of Ostrzeszów, F. Stadnicki, and Fr. Prelate K. Lipski in the Chocz estate acted similarly. The zasadźca (locator) Georg Mueller settled there undertook to bring 20 Olęders to the newly established settlement of Józefów.[^82] In 1785, Kajetan Radoliński settled 10 Olęders in his Żelazków estate, which gave rise to Holendry Dębskie.[^83] Two years later, the starost of Ostrzeszów settled in a green forest (auf dem grünen Walde) the settlement of Białobłoty Holendry.[^84] Before 1795, the entire forested area between the Prosna and Teleszyna rivers was already inhabited by colonists. The waterlogged and sandy, agriculturally poor terrain meant that Olęder villages in the Kalisz region were of a dispersed character. With the exception of the villages of Józefów, Wielołęka, and partly Białobłoty, in which Pomeranian peasants settled, the remaining villages of the Kalisz Land were inhabited by immigrants from southern Greater Poland and the adjoining areas of Lower Silesia (the vicinity of Nowy Tomyśl, Wolsztyn, Krotoszyn, Wołów, Milicz, Brzeg). Evidence for this is provided by the dialect, close to the Silesian dialects of the German language.[^85]

In the territory of the former Łęczyca Voivodeship, Olęder settlement appeared in the 1770s, initially in the Kłodawa, Łęczyca, and Sobótka lease-holds, where large tracts of territory consisted of waterlogged land overgrown with timber of little value.[^86] Soon the owners of ecclesiastical estates appeared as initiators of colonization: the Kraków Chapter (the Pabianice estates), the Norbertine Sisters of Łęczyca, and the representatives of the Archbishop of Gniezno. The greatest scale, however, was taken on by the settling of Olęders on private estates. In the early 1790s, small Olęder settlements appeared in the vicinity of Łódź. In total, in the years 1779–1793, approximately 60 such villages arose in Łęczyca Voivodeship, numbering approximately 540 farms, inhabited by approximately 3,400 persons, which constituted close to 5% of the total population in that voivodeship.[^87] This settlement was dispersed, often only 2–3 farmsteads. Individual settlements were separated from each other by large forest complexes. In the Łęczyca territory, eighteenth-century Olęder settlement was almost the exclusive form of transition to rent payments.

Significantly smaller progress was made by the settling of Olęders in Sieradz Voivodeship and in the Wieluń Land. It did not extend beyond 3 villages in Ostrzeszów County, with the largest of them, Radziwiłły, founded in 1783 in the Grabów starosty and later disappeared, and 8 settlements in Szadków County.[^88] The numerous royal domains in this area abounded in compact forest areas of considerable economic value. The decision was not made to designate them for felling and agricultural development.

Settlement contracts reveal the full range of arguments used to justify the desire to found a new settlement. In royal domains, emphasis was placed on the significance of the expected revenues from rents for the state treasury:

in order to increase the benefit flowing to the country from the land and to increase treasury revenues (Dąbrowa, 1789); with a view to finding revenues which might increase both our treasury and that of our successors (Grabina, 1779); in order to improve the state of the starosty's prosperity (Wzgórze, 1782).[^89]

On private estates, attention was commonly drawn to the necessity of measuring the land and clearing it for new arable fields. It happened that a new settlement was located on the land of a previously existing and depopulated village, in order to restore the present wastelands to their former state (Ruda Bugaj, Wierzbno; 1782), or within the boundaries of a partly depopulated village (Brużyca Mała, 1791), or even in the place of a liquidated forest glassworks (Huta Bardzińska, ca. 1792).[^90] On ecclesiastical estates, lamentations were expressed about the impossibility of deriving any benefit from the wastelands (aus welchem niemals irgend ein Nutzen erwartet werden kann).[^91] In the last decade of the eighteenth century, such declarations are no longer encountered, which may indicate that the settling of Olęders was treated as an already widespread practice of developing wasteland. The hope of obtaining income from hitherto agriculturally unused territories encouraged the owners of all types of estates to found Olęder, rent-paying villages. Moreover, the risk of possible failure and losses was reduced to the foreign settlers alone, because the economic consequences arising from the introduction of rent would affect only the settlers and would not burden the settled serf peasants.

Settlement contracts from the last quarter of the eighteenth century frequently constituted confirmation of the already earlier occupation of land by Olęders on the basis of a fixed-term lease. This mode of land use, also called emphyteutic law (emphyteusis), had already been applied in the seventeenth century in the Gdańsk Delta.[^92] Over time, it spread across the Vistula valley, Kujawy, and the northern borders of Kalisz Voivodeship. In 1781, Kajetan Dębowski, lord of the Czermno estate, organizing in his estate a village of the same name, concluded a lease agreement with the settlers for 40 years.[^93] Similar were the origins of the settlements of Kobyle (1780) and Wzgórze (1782).[^94] In the years 1790–1791, Anna Modlińska, owner of the estates of Bytoń, Bieganowo, and Strużewo in Radziejów County, designated 10 łans of forest for Olęder settlements, which she established under 40-year lease contracts.[^95] In turn, the contract for the village of Besk (1786) was preceded by a 50-year emphyteusis. Emphyteusis occurred in two forms: long-term (long-duration), lasting 40–50 years, and short-term (short-duration), lasting 10–15 years. The rent-free years granted to lessees were counted toward the lease period. Upon the expiration of the contract, the next one was drawn up. This renewal of the contract entailed an obligation to pay Gottespfennig — double the annual rent value (Wzgórze, 1782).[^96] This was a payment deriving from the tradition of Roman law (denarius Dei), made to reinforce a concluded and legally valid contract.[^97] For the landowner it constituted additional income. As documents from the end of the eighteenth century demonstrate, such an arrangement was applied in settlements along the middle Vistula, but also in the vicinity of Kłodawa and Dąbie, both in royal domains and in private and ecclesiastical estates. When the lease period expired, a new agreement was concluded, already providing for perpetual lease.[^98] This attests to the economic stability of Olęder farms, which yielded a steady and reliable income. The new legal form of land use was also advantageous for the Olęders. As perpetual lessees they could be assured that the fruits of their labor would serve future generations. There is no doubt, however, that in the 1770s–1790s, the owners of landed estates were not fully able to assess and appreciate the scale of the profits flowing from expanding the area under cultivation. The rent-free years guaranteed to settlers pushed back in time the actual income from rents, which is why great importance was attached to the Gottespfennig payment, which constituted a perceptible influx of cash. Such a perception of this customary payment was expressed unambiguously in the laudum of the Kujawy nobility, adopted at the dietine in Radziejów in 1778. In view of the ever-increasing influx of Olęders, it was recommended that the 40-year lease contracts concluded with them be replaced by 20-year ones. After the expiration of that term, the settlers would be obliged to pay the Gottespfennig to the landowners.[^99] This plan was not, however, transformed into binding law.[^100]

Emphyteusis was a hereditary right, but also an alienable one. Upon notifying the landowner, the user (emphyteuta, lessee) could sell the right to use the land, however with the consent of the owner, who as a rule reserved for himself the right of first refusal and laudemium amounting to 10% of the sale price of the farm. Only in exceptional cases was the laudemium specified as a fixed sum. The landowner's care for the maintenance of continuity of farming was visible in the frequent specification of the conditions for selling a settlement. In Grabina (1779), an Olęder could sell his farm after paying rents and fulfilling obligations. In Ruda Bugaj and Wierzbno (1782), the buyers of land should be proper people. In Kiki (1791), only Catholics could purchase farms. This requirement was connected with the character of the colony, whose zasadźca was Urban Chyliński and whose inhabitants were Poles. In the event of concealment of the actual sale price, the laudemium was to amount to 20% (Rojków, Paprotnia, 1792). Even confiscation of half the transaction sum in favor of the manor was threatened. Sporadically it was decreed that the buyer and the seller must each purchase half a barrel of manorial beer and treat the neighbors with it. In Szczepanów (1792), this obligation was limited to the person selling their settlement. Olęders also had the right to pass their farm on to their heirs. All natural heirs of the deceased farmer had the right of inheritance, including those of the female sex, in the order determined by the degree of kinship.[^101] Settlers also had the right, as free people, to depart from the land they occupied, but only after the expiration of the rent-free years and upon finding a suitable successor in their place. Such cases were rare.[^102]

At the moment of drawing up the contract, settlers paid the landowner the so-called wkupne (entry fee), more rarely called okupne, and in German terminology: Grundgeld. This was an agreed sum for which the settlers acquired the right of perpetual lease of the land and its use in accordance with the principles of Olęder law. It was almost universally a sum paid in a single installment, only sporadically in installments, but for no longer than one year. Upon payment of the wkupne, the settler could take possession of the land. Contracts were as a rule merely a prelude to the settlement action. They were signed by the zasadźca (village headman), sometimes appearing in the company of 1–2 future inhabitants of the settlement, who usually took on the functions of jurors (Schöffe). Only rarely was a group of contractors a party to the agreement (their number was not always given).

An enormously important role in extending the reach of Olęder settlement and in propagating its advantages was played by the zasadźcy (locators), acting as intermediaries between the landowners and the settlers. As a rule they became headmen (sołtysi) of newly founded villages. Their task was to recruit, bring, and settle on the land a specified number of settlers, skilled in farming on terrain difficult to cultivate. The overwhelming majority of zasadźcy from the 1770s–1790s bore German-sounding surnames and first names. In the light of surviving sources, the question of their origin and national (ethnic) affiliation, with individual exceptions, cannot be resolved conclusively. An obstacle is posed by the official practice of that era, which permitted the alteration of the sound of surnames and first names by translation. Not always was a handwritten signature made. There were signatures made with a guided hand, from which one cannot judge, for example, the characteristic for the German language style of writing. It therefore seems permissible to conclude that, apart from cases in which the first names and surnames of the zasadźca and settlers have a Polish sound (Urban Chyliński, Johan Graboski, Lukas Rosiewicz — Kiki, 1791; Marcin Bilecki, Wojciech Kościeski — Mrówna, 1792; Błaszczyk, Zawacki, Pilnik — Paprotnia, 1792), or their religion was specified as Catholic (katholische Holländer — Kobyle, 1780), in the remaining cases these roles were played by persons originating from or connected with the sphere of German culture. Sporadically among the settlers one encountered Kolonisten jeglicher Religion (Chechło, 1784), which may indicate a mixed Protestant-Catholic (German-Polish) ethnos among the first inhabitants of the settlement.[^103]

The founding of an Olęder settlement was as a rule the result of cooperation between the landowner and the zasadźca. There were also intermediaries whose work involved the erection of several settlements. This attests to the fairly great interest on the part of landowners in the last quarter of the eighteenth century in acquiring Olęder settlers for their estates, as well as to the activity of zasadźcy who had good contacts in the regions from which they could recruit the needed farmers. In the 1780s, Gottlieb Kaczorowski, the headman of the Dutch art of farming (Schulze der holländischen Ackerbaukunst), showed particular activity in the territories of the upper Bzura and Ner river basins. In 1784, he brought about the founding of the settlement of Pustkowa Góra in the Chociszew estate belonging to the Łęczyca order of the Norbertine Sisters. In the same year, he brought settlers to the village of Słowik in the Strzelew estate of Jan Szczawiński. Five years later, he signed a settlement contract initiating the founding of the settlement of Dąbrowa in the Chojny estate of Benedykt Górski. Slightly inferior to him in energy was Chryzostomus Rudzyński, formerly headman of the settlement of Solne Holendry (Solnicer Holland). Judging from the toponymy, this settlement, which no longer exists and is not indicated on old maps, probably lay between Kłodawa and Ciechocinek, in the belt where brine springs occur. Perhaps the failure to establish this settlement induced Ch. Rudzyński to attempt founding a new one. At the end of 1789, he signed with Tadeusz Budziszewski, lord of the Witów and Konarzew estates in the Łęczyca region, a contract establishing the village of Swędów, to which he undertook to mehr Holländer herbeizubringen.[^104] This undertaking ended in success.[^105]

The strong drive to populate forests and wastelands with Olęders found expression in the issuing to zasadźcy of instructions to recruit further settlers. Counting on their community contacts and considerable mobility, zasadźcy and village headmen were even obliged to bring additional farmers. In 1779, the zasadźca of the village of Psary was obliged to bring at least 20 farmers within a year.[^106] A similar obligation was taken on by the zasadźca Georg Mueller of the newly founded settlement of Józefów near Chocz.[^107] A year later, the headman of the village of Kobyle then being established was ordered to settle at least 10 colonists in it and to bring further ones.[^108] In turn, before Jakob Prechie, the headman of the village of Wzgórze, a task was set in 1782 of bringing as many of his Olęder acquaintances as possible, but those arriving later had their rent-free period reduced by two years. In 1784, the Olęders of Pustkowa Góra and Słowik were obliged to recruit craftsmen to their settlements. The task of quickly attracting Olęders was also placed before the zasadźcy of Ruda Bugaj (1782), Dąbrowa (1789), Swędów (1789), and Domaradzyn (1792). The zasadźca of Radogoszcz (1793) was also to quickly bring further good farmers. For lack of appropriate sources it is difficult to determine to what extent the zasadźcy and headmen fulfilled these obligations. There are also no mentions of any possible sanctions for failure to meet these contractual conditions.

As can be inferred from many sources, the newcomers settling in the Vistula valley in the vicinity of Gąbin and Gostynin came mainly from Gdańsk Pomerania, Kujawy, and Greater Poland. The colonists brought by Kajetan Dębowski to Czermno were described in the lease contract as Polish people (ludzie polscy),[^109] which did not designate their nationality but referred to their origin from the territory of the Commonwealth. However, three of them were probably Poles, namely: Jan and Jakub Koniarscy and Dawid Górski; the remaining ones bore German first names and surnames. Since the majority of the Hollanders were German-speaking, the few Poles among them generally underwent Germanization. In daily life, in contacts with the local population, they probably used Polish surnames; in official matters they used interchangeably Polish or Germanized surnames. Among the colonists settled by

S. Zabłocki in Łęg Suchodolski, Piotr Jadyszka and Jakub Gołąbek were listed in the lease contract text. They signed the agreement as Peter Jedyske and Jakob Taube.[^110]

In Olęder settlements, the principle was to allocate plots to settlers equal in size. The acreage was usually specified in łans or włóki (unit of area), often also in huby, in which one should see the influence of the German system of area measurement. The Huba, a Polonized name from Hufe, encompassed 30 morgs, but the area of one Hufe varied regionally within the Reich in the range from 5.5 to 22.6 ha.[^111]

The Chelmno lan used in Polish lands until the 1820s, corresponding to one wloka, counted 30 morgs (17.95 ha). From 1819 it was replaced by the New Polish wloka, also counting 30 morgs, but with an area of 16.79 ha.[^112] Land allotments for the Olders usually encompassed 1 lan, although half-lan allotments also occurred. There were villages in which both full-lan and half-lan holdings were assigned (Wzgorze, 1782). Exceptionally, when land earmarked for settlement was scarce, allotments of other sizes occurred (21 morgs — Marcinow, 1785). It happened that in the division of land the wishes of individual farmers were taken into account: one lan, or half a lan, as desired (Rojkow, Paprotnia, 1792). Local terrain conditions had a great influence on the size of the allotment, especially the degree of afforestation. In many contracts one finds clauses about measuring and dividing the land among individual farmers only after the clearing of the area indicated to the settlers (Swedow, 1789; Domaradzyn, 1792).

In endowing the village headman (solty) with land, account was taken of his merits in founding the village, and the function he performed in it was also honored. Alongside the lan of land due to him, he most often received an additional half lan free of rents and obligations. Exceptionally, this additional allotment encompassed a full lan (Grabina, 1779; Ruda Bugaj, 1782; Bruzycza Mala, 1791). A case was recorded of rewarding the headman with an additional lan of land (Mileszki, 1790), but on half of that acreage he paid rent at the rate applicable to all villagers.[^113] When the land available for division was less, the headman was assigned on the same terms 1/2 or 1/4 of a wloka (Rojkow, Paprotnia, 1792), and it happened that even less (3 morgs in Marcinow, 1785). In 1783, in the village of Srebrna in the Strzeblew estate near Zgierz, the headman received 15 morgs of land on which he paid no rent, but was obligated to three days of foot-labor per year. A year later, in nearby Slowik, the headman had to erect at his own expense and within a short time a building for the convenience of travelers, by which was meant an inn (austeria).[^114] In Chechlo (1784) and Rydzyny (1791), the headmen received 15 morgs of land free of charge, but were obligated to pay hearth tax and tithe.

A frequently used solution was also to make the size of the headman's allotment dependent on his enterprise and effectiveness in attracting new village residents. In 1784, the very active founder (zasadzca) in the Leczyca region, Gottlieb Kaczorowski, was guaranteed in the contract drawn up for Pustkowa Gora an additional 15 morgs within its boundaries, as compensation for bringing in further settlers. The contract for Domaradzyn (1792) guaranteed the founder-headman one morg for each settled lan. The agreement with the founder of Swedow (1789), in turn, provided for compensation in the form of half a lan for 10–15 settlers brought in. At the moment of founding the village it had only five farmers. Exceptionally, in the contract for the village of Osiny Borek of 1788, additional land endowment was provided for a juror (lawnik). He was to receive three morgs of land.[^115]

The diversity of solutions adopted in assigning additional land to headmen testifies to the absence of customary norms, let alone legal ones, in this area. The rule remained the typically feudal gratification of land for specific services and the function performed, but the form and extent of this was decided by the landowner. The area of land additionally granted to the headman depended on the size of the entire acreage earmarked for the erection of a new village. In settlements created from scratch, and especially on as yet unsurveyed lands, such allotments could be larger. Encumbering part of the headman's land with rent indicates the growing role of a commodity-money economy and the attendant need of the landowner for cash. A good example of an attempt to break with the traditional, agrarian way of thinking may be the terms of the contract for the village of Slowik (1784). The podstoli of Brzeziny, Jan Szczawinski, who founded it on his estate, obligated the Olders to bring craftsmen to the village, and charged the headman with building an inn.[^116] This investment must after all have been advantageous not only for the village residents, but above all for the manor.

Great latitude prevailed in land allotments for farmers who were to serve as teachers in the village. In many contracts this matter was entirely omitted, while from existing records no regularity can be inferred, either geographically or chronologically. One does not encounter larger land grants for teachers than 1 lan of land free from rent and all obligations (Grabina, 1779; Kobyle, 1779; Bruzycza Mala, 1791; Radogoszcz, 1793; Lesnik, 1796). In the case of Radogoszcz and Lesnik, from the acreage designated for the teacher the settlers were to carve out an area for a burial cemetery.[^117] A similar solution, designating half a lan of land for the teacher and for the cemetery, was adopted in Grabieniec (1793) and Radogoszcz (1793). In Rojkow and Paprotnia (1792) it was decided that 1/4 of a lan each would be set aside for school and cemetery land.

Most often, however, the farmer serving as teacher received a quarter-lan of land. Sometimes a small pasture was added to it. In 1790 in Mileszki near Lodz, three rods of land (approx. 55 m²) from the village territory were designated for it.[^118] In Srebrna (1783) the teacher was required to provide 3 days of foot-labor from the 15 morgs of land allotted to him. In extreme cases the teacher's endowment amounted to only half a morg (Marcinow, 1785; Dabrowa, 1789).[^119] The acreage of the larger teachers' farms was usually sufficient to keep a few pigs and one cow. The arable land was cultivated collectively by the village residents. Additional compensation for the teacher consisted of payments in kind, the quantity of which was calculated for each farmer in proportion to the area of land he held.

In villages situated in the vicinity of Gostynin, from one lan a quarter-scheffel of rye was designated for the teacher (1 Scheffel = 54.7 kg),[^120] 1 pound (40 dag) of butter, and firewood. In the Older settlements near Ciechocinek, the teacher twice a year made the rounds of the village with his pupils. The pupils sang, and the teacher received the customary tribute (Kalende) in the form of sausage, groats, peas, beans, and bread.[^121]

In the Vistula valley the Olders developed a distinctive manner of spatial planning of their settlements and construction of houses. They populated the two lowest floodplain terraces, raised only 2–2.5 m above the water level of the river at normal water levels. These terraces were flooded by floodwaters at least twice a year (the so-called spring and July floods), which fertilized the soil.
