The river's overflows frequently reached localities several kilometers from its channel, which is why individual farms were situated on natural elevations or on artificially raised mounds (in German terminology: Wurten).[^122] Each of these had the shape of an elongated ellipse and was situated parallel to the river current. The face of the mound, directly exposed to the action of water, was piled with clay and reinforced with stones. In this way an effective barrier against the destructive floodwater was created. The mounds were piled at distances of 100–500 m from one another, usually in the middle of the settlement plots. An important element of space formation were also wicker fences woven from osier, whose primary role was to retain fertile humus during the river's overflows. In many cases they were also used as boundaries of individual parcels. The building site for the Older settlers in Wymysl Nowy was determined by reference to the boundaries, that is, the fences.[^123] The Olders chose the site for their buildings themselves, although in some cases the landowner tried to impose his own solutions. The castellan of Gostynin, Antoni Lasocki, when designating the Bieniewski Land for settlement, marked it with stakes, with the disposition: the buildings as they are to stand are also marked, so that the buildings be erected in line.[^124]

Significant changes in the Olders' economy came only with the construction of flood embankments along the Vistula, carried out in the mid-nineteenth century.[^125]

In the area between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula rivers there was great diversity in the spatial layout of villages, as well as in the siting of buildings on individual farms. The shape of a village depended primarily on natural conditions. In drained areas, row-type construction (a row village) was most commonly encountered, in which individual farms were located in a row along the river and a road running parallel to it. Each settler received a rectangular plot adjoining the road with one of its shorter sides. Sometimes the village road cut through all the parcels (Appendices II, III). Villages founded on cleared land usually took two forms. The older was the dispersed village, often described as a village with colonial-type settlement. The genesis of such a layout should be sought in the manner of forest clearing. If the landowner designated an unsurveyed area for felling, the settlers themselves chose the location for their buildings.[^126] It is worth noting that the author of a rural construction manual highly regarded in the 1780s, Fr. Piotr Switkowski, called Olders those settlements in which the farmers live separately, scattered, each in the middle of his fields, meadows and pastures.[^127]

At the close of the eighteenth century, attempts began to appear to give this form of settlement a more orderly character, both by indicating the order in which to conduct clearing — e.g., to begin from Wolka (Dabrowa, 1789)[^128] — and by requiring that buildings be placed in a line, which led to the emergence of a row (linear) village.[^129] Such an ordering disposition for construction appeared, among others, in contracts for Dabrowa (1789), Mileszki (1790), Dobrow (1791), Szczepanow (1792). Sometimes the restriction was limited to a general recommendation that buildings be erected according to a plan (Rochow, 1783; Chechlo,

1784; Rydzyny, 1791), for the village to be regularly built up (Srebrna, 1783).[^130] Exceptionally, settlers were obligated to erect fences (Ruda Bugaj, Wierzbno, 1782).[^131] Homesteads were situated as far as possible on one side of the road. Their loose construction was situated on strips of land wider than in the case of the typical row villages founded later.

In settlement agreements from the first quarter of the nineteenth century, greater attention was paid to the spatial layout of the village and the manner of construction, with more detailed recommendations being formulated. In 1811, Jozefat Stokowski, founding a new settlement on his estate near Parzeczew, obligated the headman to ensure the village was built in a line and in an orderly fashion, with its boundaries marked by mounds. He also specified the maximum dimensions of residential buildings. The length of their walls could not exceed 20 and 14 ells (11.4 m x 7.98 m), giving approximately 91 m² of floor area, while the height of the building — 12 ells (6.84 m).[^132] The village road was to be 12 ells wide (approx. 7 m). This size was consistent with earlier practice. In 1789 in Dabrowa near Lodz, a village road was planned at a width of 2 rods (approx. 8.5 m). The provision for Marcinow from 1785 anticipating a road 6 rods wide (sechs Ruten) seems unrealistic. It is difficult to imagine a road 23–26 m wide (depending on the unit of measurement adopted — the Prussian or Polish rod) running through a small village in which each settler had only a 21-morg farm.[^133] Numerous examples indicate, however, that practice could deviate from the expectations and recommendations formulated in settlement documents. In 1817, two villages already existing in the Wozniki estate near Zgierz were surveyed: Oledry Male and Oledry Wielkie. The fieldwork and maps were produced by the then-renowned surveyor of His Royal Majesty, August Wilhelm Dornstein, author of numerous plans, including the spatial layout of Ozorkow.[^134] In the case of both villages, the work was limited to marking their boundaries (partially marked with mounds in Oledry Wielkie) and drawing boundary lines between individual farms. The village roads, apparently already well established in the landscape, were left in their existing course without regulation,

Both villages were characterized by dispersed construction, with buildings along the road but at varying distances from it (Appendices II, III).

The size of a building plot depended on the total area of the farm, and in the vast majority of cases the following proportion was applied: from one lan of land, one morg for construction. For Older settlements founded in the years 1779–1793 this solution was almost the norm. The exception was two villages (Chechlo and Rydzyny) in the Pabianice estates of the Cracow chapter, in which farmers, despite holding one lan of land each, could designate for construction only 6 square rods each (approx. 1,200 m²). In the village of Kobyle, four morgs were designated for construction for all settlers, but the settlement contract did not specify their number. Apart from the three named by name, the document speaks of others (und andere), not necessarily numerous.[^135] Each homestead site would then have had slightly less than one morg. The area of the building plot was deducted from the total area of the farm and no rent was paid on it. Similarly, roads and cattle paths were not included in the acreage subject to rent.[^136]

In row villages a one-morg building plot also contained a section of the village road (Radogoszcz, 1793).[^137] The mere designation of a building plot did not yet determine the rapid and efficient erection of the necessary buildings. The first period after settling, the Olders spent in provisional sheds (Hüttungen), often semi-dugouts. It happened that they made use of manor buildings, as for example in Domaradzyn, where they were permitted to live in the new brewery, but no longer than one year. Anyone who had not built a house within that time would have to pay for lodging in the manorial building.[^138] The majority of settlement contracts guaranteed the Olders the possibility of erecting the buildings they needed from timber obtained through clearing of the land they had received. Exceptionally, with the consent of the manor, free procurement of building timber from other manorial forests was permitted (Grabina Holendry, 1779; Ruda Bugaj, Wierzbno, 1782).[^139]

Terrain conditions, the type and availability of building materials determined the forms of construction realized in Older villages. In the construction of individual farms one does not encounter, with the exception of the Vistula valley, the placement of the dwelling house (cottage) and farm buildings under one roof, that is, the layout characteristic of the so-called Frisian and German type of construction.[^140] A building roofed with a thatched straw roof was erected from wide poplar planks. A specialty of the Olders in the Vistula valley was the very smooth laying of thatch, in a manner known only from the territories of Frisia and Holstein.[^141] The construction of an Older's cottage allowed, among other things, in times of flood threat, to drive farm animals up to the attic.[^142] The walls of houses were whitewashed with lime, both inside and outside, which at first glance already distinguished them from the cottages of Polish peasants.[^143] Each farm had its own bread oven, as a rule situated away from the buildings, and its own well. Its construction, given the low level of groundwater, was not a complicated undertaking. In the remaining areas of central Poland the free siting of individual buildings on a square domestic plot dominated (the Polish type). This type of construction testifies to a departure from the original tradition of Older settlement, and indirectly to the already negligible share of settlers deriving directly from Frisia or from northern Germany in the colonization of the territories of central Poland. In the majority of contracts no greater attention was devoted to buildings, referring to them generally as: a dwelling building and a livestock building (Byszewy, Marcinow, 1785). It happened, however, that it was precisely indicated which buildings, beyond the dwelling, the settlers were to erect. In Besk (1786) these were: a stable, a barn, and a pigsty; in Ostrowek (1790) — a stable and a barn. In Grabina (1779) the requirement was limited to a recommendation that the buildings be as orderly as possible (möglichst ordentliche Gebäude zu errichten).[^144] Similarly in the village of Kobyle (1780): Alle ihre Gebäude sollen sie ordentlich bauen.[^145] A dozen or so years later in Kiki (1791) it was enjoined that the buildings be suitable for comfortable farming. There were also time constraints. In Rochow (1783), the Olders were to build before the expiry of the free years. In Chechlo (1783) and Rydzyny (1791), the time for construction was limited to three years, even though the settlers enjoyed an eight-year freedom period. Such a disposition must have arisen from the conviction that only farmers settled on their land would be capable of fulfilling the obligations imposed on them. In the agreement with the settlers of the village of Osiny Borek (1788) it was stipulated outright that anyone who did not build a house would forfeit the right to the land, and failure to fulfil other obligations would entail the necessity of leaving the village.[^146]

Only a few founding contracts for Older villages provided for the establishment of cemeteries by setting aside appropriate land for them. In 1779 in Grabina, only 10 square rods (approx. 187 m²) in a forest were designated for this purpose.[^147] In the small settlements of Osiny Borek and Dabrowa, cemeteries of half a morg each were designated, and in Rojkow, Paprotnia, and Lesnik, cemetery land was to be carved out from the allotments of local teachers. Judging from the relics preserved to this day, it appears that almost every Older settlement had a burial place, and in the case of several settlements located close to one another, they could have had a shared cemetery.[^148] Cemeteries were located on the outskirts of villages, at the edge of a forest, distinguished by a low earthen embankment. The problem of creating the organizational structure of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church in central Poland, its specificity and the role it played in the lives of the settlers, will be discussed in chapter five. At this point it is only necessary to mention the particular role of the cantor, who was also a teacher, and who could, among other things, perform baptisms and conduct funeral ceremonies. This constituted a sufficient premise for the establishment of small, rural cemeteries. Due to the enormous dispersal of settlements inhabited by Lutheran settlers, the concept of a parish cemetery, known from Roman Catholic tradition, did not develop among them.

Although many contracts mentioned land designated for the teacher, no attention was devoted to school buildings. By custom, the teacher's cottage served the function of a school; he was simultaneously the cantor. One room served as his living quarters, the other was a classroom, which on Sundays and holidays became a prayer room. One of the oldest in the discussed area was the school building in Slonsk, erected in 1781, of half-timbered (szachulcowa, ryglowa) construction, with a thatched roof. The interior consisted of two large rooms and a vestibule running through the axis of the building.[^149] The history of numerous Older settlements attests that schools began to function in them only 20–30 years after the founding of the settlement.[^150]

The fundamental privilege of the settlers was the freedom period (wolnizna), that is, the time during which they were exempt from paying rent and all other obligations provided for in the settlement contract on account of the land they were taking over. This principle, derived from the thirteenth-century colonization under German law, was very effective in attracting settlers to develop particularly difficult-to-cultivate lands. The free years allowed them to gain economic footing in their new settlements. The Olders settling on Polish lands in the last third of the eighteenth century and the early nineteenth century enjoyed, in the overwhelming majority of cases, a freedom period of 7 years. This was the case, for example, in the Older settlements along the Vistula, where such a period was the norm.[^151] In other areas, an eight-year freedom period occurred, but it never exceeded a period of 9 years. It should be assumed that the number of free years resulted from practical experience that had become established in local tradition. The objective premise for determining the time free from any obligations was undoubtedly the degree of afforestation of the land and the anticipated difficulty of clearing, although this assessment could be superficial. This is confirmed by the clause placed in many contracts stating that the final measurement of the land received by the settler would be carried out after the clearing of forest and undergrowth.

A factor enhancing the attractiveness of the land assigned to the Olders was the possibility of obtaining and making use of timber from felling and clearing. Settlement contracts provided for a whole range of ways of regulating this matter, while it is clearly visible that the landowners did not forgo the possibility of obtaining their own benefits in this way. The decisive factor in the scale of work commissioned to the Olders was the woodland coverage of the area, and more precisely the quality of the tree stand. With an abundance of timber, it was permitted to use it, sell it, give it away, or burn it (Paprotnia, 1792). The Olders generally had the right to free use of timber from their own plot, necessary for the erection of the necessary buildings. Oak trees were subject to a felling ban (Wzgorze, 1782), as their timber was usually intended for cooper's wares (Rochow, 1783). In Kiki, in 1791, settlers were permitted free felling of trees, on the condition that they had already used up the timber from their own lan. In Lesnik, farmers could carry out felling in designated places. The estate owner of Osiny Borek took a completely different stance toward the needs of the Olders. If anyone tried to obtain timber beyond their own lan, their cart and axe were to be confiscated, and these corpora delicti then delivered to the manor. In areas where building timber was not available in sufficient quantity, for the first buildings the Olders received it free of charge from the owner (Grabina, 1779). Material for subsequent buildings they had to buy (Dobrow, Bruzycza, 1791; Mrowna, 1792). Similarly in Pustkowa Gora (1784), where a shortage of building timber could be supplemented by purchase from the Norbertan Sisters' monastery in Leczyca. It was almost universal to guarantee the Olders an allocation of timber for rebuilding after a fire, sometimes specifying the extent of such assistance, e.g., two pine trees (Marcinow, 1785). Free collection of fallen timber for use as firewood was universally permitted, but for example in Mileszki, obtaining firewood beyond one's own lan required the manor's permission. Isolated in this respect was the provision in the contract for settlers in Srebrna (1783), forbidding the Olders from gathering brushwood in the forest.[^152] In heavily forested areas with valuable tree stands, the Olders were given the admonition to seek primarily the benefits of arable farming rather than of clearing and timber use (Pustkowa Gora, 1784).[^153] This warning testifies to the by no means uncommon practice of Olders abandoning the land assigned to them after clearing it and selling the timber or charcoal or ash (depending on the terms of the settlement contract).[^154]

An important privilege for settlers, especially in the first period of their farming, was the possibility of using pastures and meadows. Pasture lands, given the underdeveloped cattle, sheep, and goat breeding in central Poland, were not of particular value. Wastelands were designated for them, areas overgrown with shrubs, riverside osier. Among the Olders in the Vistula valley, especially on the islands, there was a custom of farmers arranging a common pasture (Allmende). The livestock was tended by a herdsman paid collectively.[^155] The possibility of grazing livestock free of charge was an attractive lure for the Olders, but economically it meant relatively little. The situation was different with meadows, whose maintenance in cultivation required expenditure, and whose economic significance was far greater than that of pastures. Meadows were treated as an area providing grass — as hay — for winter feeding of livestock. In numerous settlement contracts a clause was included permitting the use of existing meadows, or else guaranteeing the colonists freedom to establish them on the land they had cleared. Sometimes a more precise specification of the area designated for meadow occurred, as for example in the colony Wzgorze (1782), in which settlers had the right to establish their own meadows in the proportion: 2 morgs of meadow to 1 lan of land held.[^156] It also happened that meadows were leased to Olders (Marcinow, Niwki, 1785) for payment of 100–120 zlotys per year.[^157] Contracts were exceptional in which the location of the area designated as a meadow was precisely specified (one morg by the Ner river — Grabina, 1779).[^158]

Settlement contracts from the area between the Prosna and the Pilica confirm, as known from Older settlements in the Vistula valley, developed animal husbandry. The matter of their grazing was resolved in various ways. In the majority of cases the livestock was permitted to graze together with that of the peasants (Grabina, 1779; Ruda Bugaj, Wierzbno, 1782; Szczepanow, 1792; Radogoszcz, 1793), which indirectly confirms the thesis of the low quality of contemporary pastures. When these were lacking, the Olders could graze animals in the forest (Chechlo, 1784; Rydzyny, 1791) or quite without any restrictions, with the only proviso — without causing damage (Dabrowa 1789; Kiki, 1791; Mrowna, 1792). In nearby Byszewy near Lodz (1785), cattle could be grazed without restriction, while sheep and goats were grazed in the proportion of 30 head to one lan of land held. Such a regulation testifies to an awareness of the damage caused by grazing too large a flock of these animals. In 1790 in Mileszki near Lodz and in Ostrowek near Grabow, free grazing was permitted for Dutch cattle, which is direct evidence of the raising of this breed of animals also outside the Vistula valley.

Although the decisive majority of Older settlements was established on marshy lands drained by various watercourses, including larger rivers, the question of fishing was regulated only rarely in settlement contracts.

Fishing was, for example, completely free in Wzgorze, where Olders were permitted to dig ponds and breed fish.[^159] At the same time in Grabina, fishing in the Ner river was permitted, but without the use of nets.[^160] In Radogoszcz, fishing for fish and crayfish was reserved for the manor, and in Lesnik it was permitted in places indicated by the manor. In Older settlements lying on Vistula islands or on the bank of the Vistula, fishing was fundamentally free, sometimes only regulated by provisions specifying the types of nets permitted for use.[^161] Despite this, fishing was not of great interest.

Beekeeping must also have played a visible role in the economy of the Olders. It was considered sufficiently important that provisions specifying its conditions were included in settlement contracts. Typically the landowner permitted beekeeping without any restrictions within the bounds of the settler's farm. Gathering of produce from other locations could only occur after obtaining the manor's permission (Grabina, 1779). In 1791 in Bruzycza Mala it was declared that the bees that exist belong to the settlers[^162] and this applied to the entire forest complex in which the settlement was being established. Similarly, without detriment to the manor, this matter was regulated in Byszewy (1785), which means that in the surrounding forests there were numerous wild bee swarms whose produce was not being utilized. In Wzgorze (1782), it was permitted to make hives also in the forest belonging to the manor. In the decisive majority of cases such freedom was granted to a settler only after consultation with the manor. In contracts concluded after 1793 and in other documents concerning the Olders, beekeeping was mentioned only exceptionally. It should be assumed that this was connected with the disappearance of wild swarms and the construction of apiaries, although there is no evidence that the Olders engaged more strongly in this activity.

An important freedom of the Olders, although not included in the provisions of settlement contracts, was freedom of trade. It resulted from the principle of rent-based farming (gospodarki czynszowej), in which the settler had to have contact with the market in which he sold his products. The frequency and importance of commercial contacts with the town is confirmed by sources from the 1820s–1840s. Parents, maintained by children who ran the farms, reserved for themselves several trips to town per year at the expense of the persons obligated.[^163]

Recognizing the religious distinctiveness of the great majority of the Olders, their settlement contracts assured them of freedom of religion and guaranteed access to pastoral care. In almost all contracts from the years 1779–1793 provisions were included regarding the manner of pastoral care for the settlers. In the oldest documents (Ruda Bugaj, Wierzbno, 1782), settlers were permitted to bring a clergyman to the village twice a year, and simultaneously the fee for religious services (confession, three marriage banns, wedding, baptism) was established, at the level of 1 zloty for each of these acts. To Pustkowa Gora, the pastor was to come 4 times a year, while the Olders living in Dabrowa, Slowik, and Krzewo had the right to summon him as often as they needed. Given the still-organizing network of Evangelical-Augsburg parishes over the vast area between the Prosna, Pilica, and Vistula rivers, and with a small number of clergy, these provisions were purely formal in character. Practical significance, on the other hand, was carried by the burdens the settlers were obligated to bear for the benefit of the Roman Catholic Church. The Olders were frequently exempted from payments to the Roman Catholic parish in whose territory the settlement was located (Grabina, Wzgorze), invoking the constitutions of the 1768 Sejm.[^164] In several cases the tithe was taken on by the manor (Kobyle) or the starosty (Rochow). Because until the 1780s–90s baptisms, marriages, and deaths of Olders were recorded in Roman Catholic parishes, Catholic clergy collected fees for baptisms and weddings (Psary, Radogoszcz). It happened, however, that the Olders were burdened with the tithe, and at a relatively high sum. Farmers from Psary were obligated to give a "mendel" (15) of sheaves of rye to the parish priest of Chodecz, while the Olders from Besk paid 4 zlotys to the parish in Uniejow and 8 zlotys to the parish in Grabowo. Similarly, the Olders from Szczepanow contributed 1 zloty per year to the church in Uniejow, and farmers from Chechlo — 4 zlotys per year to the parish in Pabianice. In the contract for the village of Osiny Borek it was recorded that its inhabitants must come to an arrangement with the Catholic clergyman.[^165]

Analysis of eighteenth-century settlement documents convinces one that in burdening new settlers with payments to the Roman Catholic Church a certain rule was followed, which permitted the circumvention of the inconvenient provision — inconvenient because it diminished the income of the Roman Catholic parish — of the constitution of 1768.

If a new settlement arose on the site of an abandoned, depopulated village whose former residents had been obligated to pay the tithe, the new settlers were also obligated to it.[^166] On the other hand, settlers in villages founded from scratch were exempted from the tithe. There was no difficulty in obtaining acceptance of the tithe payment when the settlers were Catholic Olders (Chechlo, Kiki).[^167]

From the very essence of Older law, which respected the principle of divided ownership typical of feudalism, arose the dependence of settlers on the landowners. Possessing the right of use (ius utile) of the land they leased, the Olders were subject to the superior owner, who possessed a subjective right to the land (dominium directum). In the founding contracts of villages, this subordination was emphasized by provisions speaking of obedience to the manor and prohibition of seeking protection elsewhere (Grabina, 1779; Ruda Bugaj, Wierzbno, 1782). The headman and jurors of Dabrowa were expected to take an oath of loyalty.[^168] The settlers were to be good farmers, obedient to the manor. Those who farmed badly could be removed from the land (Grabina, 1779; Krzewo, 1790). If after clearing the land, an Older were to depart from his farm, he was to be sought by the landowner and pay for this search. In the event of a settler's failure to fulfil the terms of the contract, he could be removed from farming, but given the solidarity of the settlement's residents in bearing all burdens, such situations practically never occurred.

Regardless of what territory the Olders were settled in, their basic obligation was to bring the land entrusted to them into agricultural cultivation. The type and scope of the work necessary for this was determined by geographical conditions. In one case only, in Holendry Czerminskie, founded in 1781 by Kajetan Debowski near Gabin, the estate owner committed himself to bearing all costs associated with the improvement (melioracja) of the land. This declaration points to the enormous determination of Debowski in striving to increase the area of arable land and retain settlers on it. Another solution without precedent was the establishment of only 2% laudemium owed to the manor upon sale of the land.[^169]

Customarily this payment amounted to 10% of the sale price. The Vistula valley, and especially the islands in its current, required knowledge of land drainage techniques, appropriate construction and securing of buildings, and above all the skill of farming utilizing the silt (humus) brought by annual floods.[^170] The cultivated lands had to be fenced and appropriately protected against being covered with sand during the spring and autumn floods of the Vistula. Settlers often received detailed instructions.

And so that the Vistula water does not cover their lands and meadows with sand, they therefore undertake to fence a fence as quickly as possible, and moreover to plant the Vistula flats and all that land which the Vistula has already silted, with osier and brushwood, which brushwood, so that it may grow, they should not let cattle graze there, and this under penalty of five thalers.[^171]

Scant source mentions concern the cultivation of osier, which was used for weaving riverbank reinforcements, canal linings, and fences. Wild-growing osier was probably used for this, and its use was so obvious that it was not considered necessary to record it in documents. The cultivation of osier by the residents of the village of Slonsk is known from their petition addressed in 1850 to the Viceroy of the Kingdom of Poland, Ivan Paskevich. The Olders unsuccessfully sought the possibility of using the islands on the Vistula as compensation for osier cultivation completely destroyed by Polish troops crossing the Vistula near Slonsk in 1831.

Zu den Brückenschanzen wurden Faschinen aus Weiden genommen, die durch unsere Arbeit angebaut worden waren. Diese Weidenkultur wurde gänzlich ausgehauen.[^172]

In the sources there are also no mentions of the planting of willows, whose task was to stop ice during the spring thaw so as not to cause damage to buildings. The pollarding of these trees (cutting of the crown) promoted by the Olders caused their rapid and more vigorous regrowth.

Such a procedure, repeated every 2–3 years, supplied material for weaving basket fences (Hocken), which on floodplain lands allowed fertile silt to be retained in the fields.[^173] Wicker weaves from willow branches withstood the greatest floods thanks to their flexibility and permeability, and moreover part of the structure took root in the manner characteristic of willow. The system of fences together with a network of drainage ditches and canals constituted the most important component of the method used by the Olders for cultivating and farming the land.[^174] The characteristic pollard willow head forming during pollarding was a habitat for birds. To this day, pollard willows are considered valuable habitats for birds, insects, and small animals in agricultural areas. Willows growing in rows, low, pollarded, regarded as a symbol of the Polish lowland landscape, settled into it in this form only with the appearance of the Olders.

In the remaining areas of the vast territory between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula rivers, greater importance was attached to the skill of forest clearing and farming in marshy, low-lying areas, but ones not requiring such drainage skills as those demanded by proximity to a great river. Many Older settlements also arose in forest complexes growing on sandy, agriculturally low-yielding substrates. The basic obligation of Olders settled in such areas was forest clearing. The scope of this work was varied, dependent on the afforestation of the area. Sometimes the work was limited to indicating the places to be cleared of trees and shrubs, leaving the initiative regarding the scope and pace of work to the Olders. There were also dispositions for complete deforestation of the area, so that nothing would remain for successors to clear (Ruda Bugaj, Wierzbno, 1780) and so that profits could be had from the harvests (Domaradzyn, 1792).[^175] Olders were also frequently obligated to burn the cleared timber. As a rule, the landowner reserved for himself the right of first purchase of the ash, offering on average 1–1.5 zlotys per barrel of 40 garniec capacity (1 garniec = 3.77 l).[^176] Ash was used for the production of potash, in the eighteenth century still very profitable. There was also growing demand for charcoal, used among other things in clothes irons. Only exceptionally were settlers' contracts made to include an obligation for the full range of farming activities (to clear, to build, to raise cattle — Krzewo, 1790).[^177]

In settlement contracts with Olęders outside the Vistula valley, provisions concerning land improvement (drainage) or the construction of various types of water installations (embankments, ditches, trenches) were surprisingly rare. Perhaps such minor works of this kind were considered commonplace in the practice of Olęders and there was no need to enumerate them. Even to justify the increase in the value of land after years of use, a general statement was employed: *he carried out significant improvements*, without specifying the scope of the work performed.[^178] Probably the water conditions in newly established settlements did not always require more complex drainage works. For example, nothing is known of embankments in Pustkowa Góra that the local Olęders were obliged to repair in the event of flood damage.[^179] Only the Olęders of Leśnik near Uniejów, who took over waterlogged land, were obliged to construct appropriate embankments and a levee to protect the area against floods from the Warta river. The scale of this undertaking was so large, including among other things the raising of an earthen embankment on the river side, that the landowner declared he would provide 100 men to assist the Olęders.[^180]

The ability to collect rent in ready money was one of the stronger incentives in favour of settling Olęders. While guaranteeing the settlers a rent-free period, the landowners nevertheless sought to make it as short as possible, so as to derive the benefits of money rent as quickly as possible. Differences in the quality of the land transferred to Olęders were clearly recognised, which was reflected in the level of the rental payment. Those settling in Ostrówek Pokrzywno (1790) also received land that was partly already cleared, and this acreage was not covered by the rent-free period. They were obliged to pay rent on it from the moment of settlement.[^181] An additional rent of 15 złoty per łan of already cultivated land was paid by the Olęders of Brużyca Mała (1791), where the basic rent was 90 złoty per łan. This indicates that the colony was situated on partly cultivated, perhaps abandoned land. In the settlement contracts there are no explanations whatsoever for the rent levels set, which differ, often substantially, in relation to lands whose development required a similar outlay of labour. The material analysed does not allow any regularity to be established in the setting of rent levels, either with regard to specific estates or to broader geographic areas. Nor can any chronologically detectable tendency in the shaping of rent be identified. Probably the level was set at the landlord's discretion, perhaps in correlation with the size of the entry fee. In many Olęder settlements founded between 1779 and 1793, rent was calculated at 2 złoty per morga, which gave 60 złoty per łan. However, rates of 72 złoty (Swędów), 75 złoty (Domaradzyn), 76 złoty (Chechło, Rydzyny, Besk), 80 złoty (Krzewo) were also encountered. On the estates of the Kraków chapter in Pabianice, rent was set at 27 złoty per łan (Chechło, 1784). On the noble estates in the Szadek district — 36 złoty (Rojków, Paprotnia, 1792). The lowest rent was 8–9 złoty per łan (Swędów, 1789; Kiki, 1791). The customary date for settling rent obligations was the feast of St Martin (11 November). After 1791, a second date became widespread: St Adalbert (23 April) and the practice of paying rent in two equal instalments.

Similarly to rent, it is difficult to find any rule applied in imposing labour obligations (corvée, or labour rent) on Olęders. This was a universal obligation; only the farmers of Rochów, founded in 1783, and Brużyca Mała, established in 1791, were not subject to this duty. Olęders performed both foot labour and draught labour. In cases where one form could be substituted for the other, a conversion ratio was applied: two days of foot labour for one day of draught labour. The lowest labour requirement — 2 days of foot labour per łan — applied to Olęders in Mileszki (1790). Three days per łan were worked by farmers from Grabin (1779), Ostrówek Pokrzywno (1790); Kiki (1791) — during harvest with scythe or sickle, Szczepanów (1792) — with scythe. The Olęders of Słowik were to present themselves three times a year at the call of the manor. For the fourth day of work they received wages. Up to 4 days of hand labour were obligated of the Olęders of Byszewy (1785), Rojków and Paprotnia (1792), with the work to be performed during harvest. Five days of labour with tools were obligatory for the farmers of Chechło (1784) and Rydzyny (1791). In Psary, Srebrna, Niwki, Kobyłe and Mrówna, the labour requirement was 6 days with a scythe. In Domaradzyn the labour requirement was one day more. The labour obligation for the Olęders of Krzewo (1790) was set in an original way. For the first 20 years they were to perform it at the rate of 6 days of foot labour per łan, and thereafter — 12 days of foot labour. This was simultaneously the highest labour requirement known from the contracts for foot labour. A similar one appeared only in the village of Besk, where the local farmers could substitute the labour with a monetary equivalent of 2 złoty per working day.[^182] In Marcinów (1785), on the other hand, each of the six days worked with a scythe was compensated with a quart of beer.

Draught labour was required of Olęders considerably less frequently, and it was never the sole form of corvée. The highest draught labour requirements were found in Olęder villages in the Vistula valley. It reached up to 8 days per łan, but this was an expression of arbitrary behaviour on the part of the starosta of Troszyn, against which the farmers protested effectively.[^183] In the remaining areas between the Prosna and Pilica rivers, labour using the Olęders' draught animals usually amounted to 1–2 days per łan. The highest requirement, 4 days, was encountered only exceptionally. The Olęders of Wzgórze (1782) were obliged to spend such time hauling wood and hay, while the farmers of Leśnik (1791) were obliged to haul manure for 2 days and to plough for 2 days.[^184] Only sporadically (Domaradzyn, 1792) did the manor reserve for itself the right to call Olęders to extraordinary manorial duties. In such cases, compensation of one quart of beer and a portion of vodka was guaranteed in relation to one łan of land held by the employed Olęder.[^185]

The roots of the tradition of the landowner collecting dues in kind (natural rent, usually paid in grain) and occasional rent (usually in the form of poultry: capons, to a lesser extent geese, cockerels, and eggs) lie deep in the Middle Ages. A large dimension of such payments is usually treated as evidence of the archaic nature of farming, of the application of subsistence economy principles within the demesne or estate. Analysis of Olęder settlement contracts concluded between 1779 and 1793 in central Poland allows one to conclude that the share of natural rent among the total obligations of land users was relatively low. The inhabitants of approximately 45% of all Olęder villages were not obliged to pay any dues in kind.[^186] The remainder gave from 1 to 2 korczec of oats per łan and, on average, 2 capons and 1 goose. In three villages (Kiki, Mrówna, Leśnik), Olęders were additionally required to provide between 14 and 40 eggs at Easter.

According to the unanimous opinion of rural historians, long-distance carting (*podwody*) was one of the most burdensome feudal obligations of the peasantry. It was widely used in areas of intensive grain farming, providing cheap transport of grain intended for sale on distant markets. It is therefore unsurprising that the obligation of long-distance carting was readily extended also to Olęders, who possessed their own draught power — strong horses. This obligation is found almost universally in settlements located in the Vistula valley and in Kujawy. The Olęders of Wzgórze (1782) and Rochów (1783) were obliged once a year to provide long-distance carting with their own team to Włocławek or Toruń. The Olęders of Psary (1779) were to travel to Włocławek twice a year.[^187] A carting obligation once a year to Toruń, or payment in lieu of 2 złoty per day of travel, was the obligation of the Olęders of Niwki (1785). The colonists of Besk were obliged to provide two carts per year: once to Toruń or Warsaw, once to Włocławek. They could also pay the equivalent cost of both journeys, estimated at 20 złoty (Toruń, Warsaw) and 10 złoty (Włocławek). The value of the carting journey to Włocławek was similarly estimated for the Olęders of Mrówna (1792). The Olęders of Osiny Borek, in turn, were obliged to two cartings per year to the Vistula or to Toruń. The Olęders of Szczepanów (1792) were to travel once a year to Włocławek or once every two years to Toruń. The transport obligations of the farmers in Kiki (1791) were precisely defined. Once a year they were to transport grain to Włocławek, and on the return journey bring back half a barrel of salt or herring. This obligation also had its monetary equivalent, at 10 złoty per trip. The Olęders of Marcinów (1785), in turn, were required to travel twice a year to Tokary on the Vistula with a grain transport (12 Viertel per colonist; 1 Viertel = 13.9 l). In Ostrówek (1791) — once a year to Toruń with five korczec of grain. In Olęder villages located in the Pilica and Warta river basins, the long-distance carting obligation was encountered rarely, which was connected with the already slight use of both rivers for floating timber at the end of the eighteenth century.

Regulations concerning *propinacja* — that is, the production and sale of alcohol — constituted an important part of the settlement contracts concluded with Olęders. The constitution of the Sejm of 1768 stipulated that even on land subject to emphyteutic law, *propinacja* should remain *circa dominos directos fundi*.[^188] The profit from *propinacja* was always a by no means insignificant component of the total income of the landowner; thus any modification of this traditional monopoly can be seen as a symptom of changing thinking about estate management. The aim was, without relinquishing the monopoly on the production and distribution of spirits, not to discourage settlers from settling and remaining on the land through excessively harsh conditions governing access to alcohol. For this reason, the legal regulations contained in the contracts encompassed both obligations and rights accruing to the Olęders. In many Olęder settlements the absolute ban, typical of feudalism, on importing outside spirits was introduced, usually providing for a monetary penalty for its breach, often a very high one (100 złoty — Kiki, 1791).[^189] In the village of Czermno, Olęders could purchase vodka and beer exclusively at the taverns belonging to the lord of the manor.[^190] An exception to this tradition was the permission to bring in beer for occasions of baptism or wedding, but in that case the Olęder was obliged to pay for it in barley at a ratio of 2 Viertel of grain per 1 barrel of beer (1 barrel = 100 l) — (Marcinów, 1785). Regulations went still further in the direction of relaxing the *propinacja* monopoly, permitting Olęders to trade in manorial spirits. In 1792, in Rojków, Olęders were permitted to erect a tavern at their own cost in which they could sell spirits brought from the manor. A similar situation existed in Chechło (1784), where the innkeeper was obliged to pay a security deposit to the manor. At the same time in Słowik it was decided that during the rent-free years the village headman (*sołtys*) would distribute the manorial spirits for remuneration per every 20th barrel of beer and every 20th quart of vodka sold. Afterwards he could keep a tavern on conditions established in a separate agreement. In Kiki (1790), ½ łan was designated for a tavern in which, during the rent-free years, it was permitted to sell manorial spirits on conditions similar to those in Słowik. After the expiry of the rent-free period, the tavern was to become rent-paying. If manorial spirits were lacking, the innkeeper could bring them from outside, but only in agreement with the manor. Deviation from this rule was subject to a penalty of 100 złoty, of which 80 złoty went to the manor and 20 złoty to the informer. Numerous were also the regulations which, while maintaining the prohibition on importing outside spirits, permitted Olęders to keep a tavern independently. Settlers could maintain their own innkeeper (Kobyłe, 1780; Pustkowa Góra, 1784), sometimes endowed with ½ łan of land (Wzgórze, 1782; Swędów, 1789). In the settlement of Brużyca Mała (1791), established under difficult terrain conditions, each Olęder could produce alcohol for a fee of 10 złoty per łan per year and on condition that the *propinacja* would not be leased out to Jews.[^191] It sometimes happened that the tavern was run without rent payment (Chechło, 1784; Rydzyny, 1791), and in Dąbrowa in 1789 the manor undertook to erect a suitable building at its own cost. The obligation of one of the colony's inhabitants to sell manorial spirits was still encountered in the times of the Kingdom of Poland.[^192]

Although a large part of the Olęder settlements were located on rivers and watercourses enabling the construction of mills, source information about them is sporadic. The construction of boat mills in Olęder villages on the Vistula is confirmed.[^193] In the remaining area of central Poland, Olęders commonly used demesne mills, which they were obliged to do under the terms of the settlement contracts.[^194] The *propinacja* obligation and the obligation to use demesne mills, extended to Olęders, were sources of constant, though not as precisely calculable as rent, income for the landowner. This feudal monopoly survived in the Kingdom of Poland until the emancipation of the peasants and was one of the most enduring relics of the old order.

In addition to the above-mentioned obligations arising from the right to use land, Olęders bore other public burdens. In the settlement contracts these were rarely enumerated, with general reference made to the taxes and charges prevailing in the country. Olęders were undoubtedly obligated to pay the hearth tax (*podymne*). Between 1784 and 1791, its amount was 5 złoty 2 groszy per hearth (*dym*). A common obligation was also *szarwark* — that is, the maintenance of roads, bridges and embankments in a passable state. In Besk (1786) and Dąbrowa (1791), the farmers received half a barrel of beer from the manor for *szarwark* work. In Niwki (1785), the *szarwark* was set at three days per year per łan, with remuneration also provided — one quart of beer and one portion of spirits per day. In Marcinów (1785), the farmers were obliged to keep roads and embankments in proper condition. For each day of work they were compensated with a quart of beer. In Mileszki (1790), on the other hand, the farmers were to repair roads and guard the forest against fire. The question of military quartering was regulated relatively rarely. In the contract of 1791 for Brużyca Mała, the Olęders were exempt from it. In the same year in Rydzyny, on the other hand, the settlers were burdened with such quartering. In the scholarly literature it is argued that Olęders were obligated to quarter soldiers during wartime, but the lack of source evidence does not allow a definitive position to be taken on this matter. All other obligations of the Olęders towards the commune were recorded in the *wilkierz*.

*They may govern themselves by their own law; they bear obligations jointly* — in this motto, repeated in numerous settlement contracts, was contained the essence of the self-government of the Olęder village.[^195] The self-governance of Olęder settlements rested on a detailed and elaborately developed system of customary norms, written down in *wilkierze*.[^196] The preserved *wilkierze* allow one to reconstruct the scope of the areas of rural life covered by the regulations and the means of their regulation. One of the more extensive is the document written in 1804 in the Olęder settlement of Joanka, on the Iwanowice estate, in the Kalisz district.[^197] It collected customary rights, of which some, as the records show, had a remote pedigree (*wie es gebräulich ist*) and were treated as well known and universally applied in the Olęder community. Numerous similarities to the laws and good order recorded in it can also be found in *wilkierze* drawn up in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries and in force in Olęder villages in the Vistula valley.[^198] A number of regulations contained in the *wilkierz* from Joanka were detailed and locally adapted provisions of the General Land Law for the Prussian States (*Allgemeines Landrecht für die Preußischen Staaten*), in force in Prussia from 1794, especially its Part VII concerning the peasantry.[^199] It is obvious that later too, in the times of the Duchy of Warsaw and the Kingdom of Poland, Olęder customary law did not exceed the norms of statutory law.

The *wilkierz* confirmed the special position of the village headman (*sołtys*) and the aldermen (*ławnicy*), making them responsible for observing the norms binding on all inhabitants of the village, but also for the provisions of the law, if only through the obligation to supervise the correct use of weights and measures in the village. The sołtys and aldermen were also subject to the regulations in force in the village, with the additional restriction that any potential infractions on their part were subject to penalties twice as high as those imposed on other inhabitants.

The sołtys was almost always the organiser of the village, while the aldermen were elected by the inhabitants by majority vote, or the landowner indicated two farmers from among the candidates presented to him. The sołtys and aldermen, by virtue of their functions, were compensated by the village inhabitants in amounts calculated proportionally to the land held. In Joanka, 30 groszy per łan were paid to the sołtys and 25 groszy to each of the two aldermen. This charge fell on farmers who kept cows. Those who did not have them paid the charge at double the rate. In this regulation one must see economic pressure aimed at increasing cattle numbers. It is difficult to assess its effectiveness unequivocally. The only later information relates to 1870, when 113 cows in total were kept on 48 farms, thus on average just over 2 head per holding.[^200] The farmers also collectively and proportionally bore the costs of the sołtys's and aldermen's journeys outside the village boundaries, if such trips were undertaken in matters concerning the inhabitants. The colonists were obligated to present themselves immediately at every summons from the sołtys. Insolent opposition to the sołtys or aldermen was subject to the penalty prescribed by the provisions of the law of the land. All the most important matters concerning the village were discussed at general meetings. The summoning of inhabitants occurred through the passing of a guild token (*cecha*), that is, in a manner known since the Middle Ages from guild practice, and in Olęder settlements, especially the dispersed ones, carried out by young assistants of the sołtys.[^201]

The sołtys and aldermen had considerable judicial competences. They were obliged to assemble every 14 days to impartially hear the plaintiffs and the accused.[^202] The parties to a dispute had to be informed of the court date one day in advance. Only acts listed in the *wilkierz* as prohibited could be adjudicated upon, while in other matters involving the most serious crimes, described by the old four municipal articles, the manor was to adjudicate — it was also the court of second instance for persons dissatisfied with the verdict of the sołtys's court.[^203] There were also specific provisions. In the settlement contract concluded in 1781 by Kajetan Dembowski with the settlers of Czermno near Gąbin, it was stipulated, among other things, that a farmer found guilty of a serious crime had to sell his farm and leave the village.[^204] In the village of Ruda Bugaj, it was decided in 1791 that expulsion from the village would threaten the perpetrator of a crime who gave no sign of improvement.[^205] In Olęder settlements in the Vistula valley, theft was almost unknown. Cattle were left without fear on the pasture overnight, and houses were left unlocked. Perhaps the eradication of theft was achieved through the application of a drastic punishment, the memory of which survived in folk tradition as late as the 1930s. A perpetrator caught stealing horses or cattle was thrown into the Vistula in a tied sack.[^206] In order to exclude or at least limit the possibility of giving false testimony before the court, it was threatened with a penalty of 6 złoty payable to the sołtys's chest and 5 złoty to the court. A wide scale of penalties was envisaged for anyone who, on behalf of the village or commune, committed some dishonesty or behaved not as befits a righteous person. Such an act was subject to a penalty ranging from 2 groszy fine to imprisonment. From the settlement contract for the village of Osiny Borek (1788) a provision is known ordering the erection of a pillory in front of the sołtys's house as a symbol of judicial authority.[^207] For judicial activities the sołtys, and sometimes also the aldermen, were compensated, at 2 złoty and 1 złoty respectively (Pustkowa Góra, 1784; Dąbrowa, 1789).

In Besk (1786), Krzewo (1790), Rojków (1792), Paprotnia (1792), the sołtys shared the court fee equally with the manor. Sometimes half of the adjudicated penalty was transferred to the manor, while the other half served as compensation for the injured party (Wzgórze, 1782). Disputes between Olęders and peasants were judged exclusively by the manor (Dąbrowa, 1789).

A series of provisions of the *wilkierz* related to the organisation of the village's space. Each farmer had to take care of the condition of the road running through the settlement and plant it with trees along the length of his plot. Anyone who delayed in doing so paid a fine of 15 złoty per year. Each colonist was obligated to plant fruit trees in his garden. Those who did not comply paid a fine of 7 złoty per year. A very detailed provision concerned fencing. Each farm had to be separated from the neighbouring ones by a ditch and a fence. The width of the ditch was set at half a rod (2.23 m), while the height of the fence was a minimum of two ells (1.2 m). The size of both obstacles was derived from practice: a six-month-old calf must be unable to cross them. In the event of neglect in the construction of fences and resulting damage, the culprit was obligated to repair the fence and pay a fine of 2 złoty. A higher penalty (3 złoty) threatened damage to a neighbour's fence that had caused some further losses. The *wilkierz* recommended tying horses and cows grazing on meadows to stakes, so that neighbourly friendship would not suffer as a result of damage caused by cattle.

It also defined the size of yards designated for pigs, geese and ducks. Domestic poultry could be grazed freely, but with the proviso that if they began to cause damage to a neighbour, that neighbour had the right to shoot them without warning and to hand over the killed birds to the owner. The sołtys could also order the ringing of pigs. This procedure, once common, prevented animals from rooting (burrowing), thus limiting damage in the forests where pigs were most commonly grazed. Disregarding this order entailed a fine of 5 złoty. The elaborate regulations intended to limit losses resulting from the negligence or carelessness of individual farmers were further reinforced by one additional rule. The assessment of damage caused by cattle to crops and meadows was handled by the sołtys's court, which charged for this purpose a by no means insignificant fee of 12 złoty. Numerous provisions of the *wilkierz* suggest that more significant damage was not allowed to occur, with cattle causing damage being impounded. The person impounding the cattle was obliged to water the impounded animals, but was not permitted to milk another person's cows or harness impounded horses — all under penalty of a fine. If the impounding took place with the knowledge of the sołtys (and he could therefore take a position on the matter) and the owner of the animals did not collect them, the owner was obligated to pay for each night the animal was kept under a roof. This fee, set at 9 groszy for the first night, was doubled up to the fifth night, after which the matter of impounding and compensation for damages was passed to the manor for resolution. A widespread custom was to use the help of farmhands and maidservants on larger farms. Enticing such helpers away from someone with the intention of drawing them to one's own farm was subject to a fine of 2 złoty payable to the sołtys's chest. A high fine was envisaged for farmers who took in an unknown person under their roof without notifying the sołtys. Particular warning was given against persons of suspicious appearance and against Gypsies.[^208] Providing overnight lodging to such persons entailed a penalty in the form of funding one barrel (127 l) of beer for the village inhabitants. The same penalty and a fee of 10 thalers for the manor threatened anyone who discouraged an outsider from purchasing land in the village. Such an act was described as shameful for the village inhabitants and as contrary to the interest of the manor, which was seeking to attract new settlers.

The intention to sell land in the colony had to be reported to the sołtys and aldermen, so as not to give rise to suspicion of some impure motive connected with the transaction. Failure to observe this provision was to entail a penalty in the form of one barrel of beer to be funded for all the inhabitants. The *wilkierz* in force in the village of Sady Niemieckie in the Płock district, founded in 1787, regulated the conditions of farm sale in great detail. It restricted the circle of those interested in purchasing land:

> *iżby kupiec też Holenderski Człowiek był, któryby w Holenderskich Obyczaiach i sposobach się wiedział trzymać*
>
> [that the buyer should also be a Hollander Man, who would know how to conduct himself in the Hollander customs and manners][^209]

and described in detail the rules of purchase priority. The buyer had to be of good repute and accepted by the village inhabitants. Among those fulfilling these conditions, the priority to purchase a farm accrued to the two neighbours whose holdings bordered directly on the one being sold. If neither of them was interested in purchasing, the farm could be sold to a relative of the seller. If no one in the family circle was willing to buy, then other Olęders could seek to acquire it. The buyer was obligated to pay a deposit and to settle the remaining amount within a month, and also to fund one barrel of beer for the village inhabitants. This form of buying into the community of the village was a widespread custom among Olęders. An extremely high penalty threatened the breaking off of a sale. In the village of Joanka, if the parties had reached an agreement and completed the *litkup* (*und ist dabei Bier getrunken worden* — and beer was drunk on the occasion), and then one of the parties withdrew from the transaction, that party was obligated to fund 8 barrels of beer for the commune.[^210] It may be assumed that the threat of such a high penalty almost excluded the possibility of breaking the *litkup*. The lack of comparative material does not allow one to determine whether the appearance of such a norm was intended to guarantee the irrevocability of the transaction, or to effectively eliminate the occasional cases of it being broken.

A large part of the provisions contained in the *wilkierz* concerned mutual relations between inhabitants of the settlement. Using offensive language towards the sołtys, an alderman or a fellow inhabitant was subject to a fine of 8 złoty. No one was permitted to insult anyone, even while standing before his locked door. In such a case a fine of 3 złoty applied. If a man or a woman were to undermine someone's good name by word, and such an act were confirmed by two credible witnesses, the perpetrator would have to give 1 złoty to the sołtys's chest and pay court costs. If the same witnesses were to confirm lying in wait for someone in the village, in the field, or in the bushes, the perpetrator would have to pay 4 thalers, even if no assault had taken place. The provision did, however, contain the reservation that if the sołtys deemed it appropriate, the perpetrator could be bound and taken to the manor as a potential murderer.

The elaborate system of Olęder customary law norms extended also quite deeply into the private sphere, including family relations. The *wilkierze* determined among other things the legal and financial security of orphaned, minor children through the appointment of a guardian and the definition of his obligations.[^211] A problem whose consequences extended beyond the family circle was the succession of land after the death of the farming parents.[^212] There was a prohibition on dividing the farm among the remaining children. It was to be taken over by one of them, with priority belonging to the youngest. The new owner had to buy out his siblings. Such a regulation reflects a high awareness of the value of land and a striving to maintain it in the hands of a single user for as long as possible. It also effectively prevented the fragmentation of land and allowed farms of sufficient acreage to support a family to be preserved. The durability of this custom is confirmed by the rudiments of former settlement patterns that are recognisable to this day in the landscape.[^213]

The sołtys and aldermen exercised ex officio supervision over dams, sluices, levees, and generally over all the drainage installations constructed by the settlers. Their significance for the safety of the village and for maintaining the drained land in proper condition was immense. Most of the work ordered by the sołtys in cleaning ditches and streams, stabilising village boundaries, and improving pastures was carried out collectively. Anyone who did not take part had to pay a fine of 2 złoty and carry out the assigned task within 8 days. Joint and solidary bearing of burdens also applied in the area of providing assistance to those affected by natural disasters or criminal acts. The entire commune was then obligated to help the victim rebuild the farm, providing their labour, lending their draught animals and paying 15 złoty per łan of land. Moreover, in the event of theft, all village inhabitants were obligated to assist in identifying the perpetrator.

All inhabitants bore the obligation to extinguish fires and rescue property. If during a fire someone retained fire-fighting tools, they were obligated to return them to the sołtys. If they failed to do so, they exposed themselves to suspicion of wanting to misappropriate someone else's property and on this account had to bear a relatively high fine of 4 złoty. For the Olęders, accustomed to fighting water, the element of fire was undoubtedly the most dangerous. The means of preventing fires, the procedures during them, and above all the means of redressing damage and the scope of assistance to fire victims were set out in detailed provisions. Among the few preserved documents of this kind, particular attention is drawn to fire regulations (*Brandordnung*) drawn up in 1793 by the inhabitants of four villages in the Lipno district.[^214] These settlements, lying on the right bank of the Vistula and in its immediate vicinity, formally extend beyond the area covered by the present work, yet the fire regulations in force there were typical for the majority of Olęder settlements. The farmers residing in the villages of Włęcz (Lentzen), Stajęczynki, Nowa Wieś (Neudorf), Łęgi Osieckie (Osieker Lengden) voluntarily joined together and agreed to observe fire regulations, jointly to rescue the property of neighbours affected by fire, and equally jointly to assist fire victims.[^215] The voluntary nature of the agreement was underscored by a provision allowing any of the villages to withdraw from it upon payment of 15 złoty to those remaining in the association. The scope of these regulations deserves particular attention. It had a communal character, extending beyond the boundaries of a single settlement. The distance of Nowa Wieś from the other three excluded the possibility of providing effective assistance during a fire, from which one can conclude that the principal purpose of the agreement was to codify the principles of assistance to fire victims. The dimension of such assistance was set proportionally to the acreage held by the settler. Forty-two farmers, farming 34 łans of land, participated in the agreement.[^216]

Each farmer was obligated to possess a ladder enabling access to the roof, a fire hook, a lantern, a syringe pump and a leather bucket. Furthermore, in each village there had to be a large fire hook. Its indispensability resulted from the technique of extinguishing burning buildings used at the time. A long fire hook allowed the burning thatched roof or even elements of the roof structure to be knocked down to the ground and extinguished there by pouring water. Chimneys and hearths had to be cleaned once a week. The drying of flax on stoves was prohibited, as was the storing of straw near chimneys and smoking tobacco in the farmyard (permitted only in the dwelling house). In more restrictive regulations, for example in Brużyca Mała (on the estate of the Łęczyca chapter), even the drying of firewood near stoves was prohibited.[^217] At least four times a year the sołtysi checked the completeness of the fire-fighting equipment. A monetary fine was threatened for the absence of any of the necessary tools, even for a missing rung in a ladder. While its level (from ½ to 6 groszy) can be considered disciplinary, the fines imposed for improper handling of open fire, or for creating a fire hazard by drying flax and straw in unsuitable places, were already severe (from 1.15 złoty to 3 złoty). Procedures in the event of fire were regulated in detail. Regardless of whether it broke out during the day or at night, everyone who saw it was obligated to summon all the neighbours by shouting and to save what was possible. Anyone who failed to do so was fined 10 złoty. To extinguish a fire, one had to present oneself with the tools in one's possession. If someone failed to bring them, they incurred a fine of 15 groszy. If a fire broke out as a result of reckless negligence and carelessness, confirmed by credible witnesses, no one had an obligation to help the victim in dealing with the consequences of the disaster.

If an inhabitant of the village lost property in a fire, all others who had accepted the terms of the regulations were obligated to support the victim with a sum of 1 złoty per holding. When the victim's losses were smaller, this amount was 10 groszy. When those affected by a fire were a farmer and his family, they were to receive solidary monetary assistance that would allow the farm to be rebuilt. It was to amount to: 15 thalers for the purchase of a cow, 3 thalers for the purchase of a pig, 9 thalers for bedding, 18 thalers for clothing and an additional 9 thalers for shirts. In addition, those affected were to receive in kind 1 scheffel of rye, ½ scheffel of grits and 10 pounds (approximately 4 kg) of lard. Farmhands and maidservants were not forgotten either, if they lost their property in a fire. For them, support was set at 18 thalers (farmhand) or 15 thalers (maidservant), and moreover each farmhand and each maidservant living in the villages recognising the *Brandordnung* paid fire victims 9 groszy (for a farmhand) and 15 groszy (for a maidservant) respectively. If a fire devastated some holding between harvest and the end of the carnival period, the victim received for the sustenance of his family and livestock 15 scheffel of rye, 2 scheffel of grits, 2 scheffel of barley, 3 scheffel of oats, 7 cartloads of hay for four horses and straw for chaff — 3 Schock, rye — 15 sheaves, oats — 15 sheaves, for roof covering 6 sheaves of straw if it had burned completely, or 3 sheaves if it had partially survived.[^218] In rebuilding the fire victim's farm, each person was obligated to work for one day and to lend a cart for the same time. A separate provision concerned the teacher. If he lost his property in a fire, he would receive 15 złoty for the purchase of books, 24 złoty for clothing, 18 złoty for shirts, 18 złoty for bedding, 22 złoty 15 groszy for the purchase of a cow, 15 złoty for the purchase of a pig, and also 8 scheffel of rye, 2 scheffel of grits, 30 pounds of lard, and as much hay and straw as was needed for one cow.[^219] The school building was to be rebuilt by collective effort. The regulations also envisaged the possibility of losing flax accepted by the fire victim for weaving. If the person affected was someone belonging to the fire-fighting association, the fire victim was exempt from compensating that person for the loss. The assessment of damage caused by fire was handled by the sołtys together with the aldermen.

The self-governance of the Olęder settlement also manifested itself in efforts to organise a school and provide religious services for its inhabitants. The initiative in this area, as well as the Christian duty, lay with the sołtys and aldermen.[^220] They sought a suitable candidate and presented him to the pastor for examination of his abilities. The costs of maintaining the teacher, who was also the cantor, were borne by all the village inhabitants, proportionally to the amount of land held.

The provisions contained in the *wilkierze*, unchanged in their very essence and scope of village self-governance, took into account the peculiarities and minor differences arising from local tradition. In the Olęder settlements founded on the estates of the Łęczyca chapter, the sołtysi were obligated to submit a verbal report on their official activities once a month, while in extraordinary cases to inform the landowner immediately of the event.[^221] In the Olęder villages located in the area of the former starosty of Troszyn near Gąbin, the *wilkierz* drawn up in 1788 was in force, based on the earlier one of 1760. It stipulated that the village self-government consisted of a sołtys and two aldermen, having at their disposal a secretary and a commune servant. The function of secretary was usually entrusted to the teacher. The village inhabitants elected two candidates each for sołtys and aldermen, from among whom the starosta confirmed one and swore them in in the presence of the inhabitants. In the village of Ruda Bugaj, it was decided in 1791 that aldermen would be elected by majority vote of the farmers.[^222] The term of those elected lasted one year. The sołtys kept the communal chest, in which the book of court judgements, the testaments of inhabitants, payment receipts and money from adjudicated fines were stored. The key to the chest, however, was kept by one of the aldermen. On the feast of Pentecost (*Zielone Świątki*, *Pfingsten*), at the end of the self-government's term, the entire sum was divided into four parts such that one fell to the sołtys, the second was shared between the two aldermen, and the remaining two were allocated to the common need of the Hollander settlements.[^223]

The system of customary law norms in force in the Olęder community provided above all the economic stability of the village. Solidarity in bearing all the burdens arising from the use of land, solidarity in removing the effects of floods and fires, joint responsibility for the state of drainage installations, roads and fences contributed to the economic stabilisation of the village and allowed for successful farming. Olęder law also established the principles of social order in the settlement and stood guard over their observance. This tradition, lasting several centuries, strengthened the Olęder communes, but at the same time separated them from closer contacts with the outside world.

The character of the Olęder economy was determined above all by the geographical environment. The settlers owed their economic successes to a specific symbiosis with their surroundings, to the maximum utilisation of natural conditions, which was particularly evident in the Vistula valley. The economy of the Olęders living there was focused mainly on the keeping of dairy cows and the associated dairying, especially cheesemaking. It was precisely the Olęders who were the pioneers of the production of rennet-based, ripened cheeses in the Polish lands. They were given the shape of round loaves weighing approximately 3 kg. Almost all farms were equipped with appropriate dairy equipment: various types of bowls, sieves, strainers, copper cauldrons, presses and cheese moulds.[^224] While cheese production was directed even to distant markets, the Olęder settlements from Gostynin upriver along the Vistula supplied Warsaw with milk and cream. The quality of the dairy products must have been very good. This is confirmed by the decision of the owner of the Bobrowniki estate near Płock, Brzozowski, who in 1842 demanded from the Olęders settled there that instead of corvée they pay annually *2 Achtel Schönen Butter und 24 Pfund holländischen Käse*.[^225] Intensive dairy farming required the keeping of large numbers of cows. In the 1850s, on average from 5 to 14 dairy cows per holding were kept, described as *very beautiful, well-fed, giving much milk*.[^226] Such a considerable stocking rate was made possible by the large expanses of meadows, which also supplied the hay indispensable in winter. Already at the close of the eighteenth century it was noted that the cattle stock on Olęder farms was considerably higher than on peasant holdings. *Vorzüglich legen sich diese Leute auf Viehzucht*[^227], observed August Carl von Holsche, and a few years later Captain von Nothardt recorded that

*Die Colonisten halten doppelt soviel Vieh auf gleicher Größe des Ackerguts als die polnischen Bauern, düngen mithin besser*[^228].

The development of cattle breeding went hand in hand with the spread of clover cultivation. On Olęder farms it was still sown sporadically; it became widespread with the arrival in South Prussia of settlers from Württemberg. A marked change in farming methods occurred after the construction of flood embankments along the Vistula. In 1848 the Olęders of Wymysło Nowe criticised the embankments being raised:

The embankments on the lands at Świniary we did not erect, for the reason that the flooding of the river Vistula not only does us no harm but moreover fertilises our fields through the deposition of alluvial soil; were those meadows otherwise left, by nature unflooded, they would yield us no fodder at all; it follows therefore that the embankments are to our own ruin, not to our benefit.[^229]

The importance of pig-rearing then increased, since the land cut off from flooding by the embankment was manured with dung. Pork and lard were destined for own use. A prosperous farmer slaughtered 5–6 pigs in the course of a year.[^230] Chickens were also kept (on average 5–11 per farm) and geese (3–6 head), while Olęder farms had no sheep or goats. The Olęders were also good horse breeders, especially of heavy horses. They used them not only for field work but also for haulage services. There was great demand for such work in the vicinity of Ciechocinek and Nieszawa.[^231] The shaft harness (*szalejowy* hitch) was used, representing the simplest form of collar harness.[^232] Collar harness (*szleje*) was characterised by ease of repair and maintenance and great adaptability to the horse's build. The Olęders made no use whatsoever of ox-drawn ploughs.

Grain farming was of considerably lesser importance. Depending on the fertility of the soil, the crops grown were: millet, oats, barley, and on the most fertile soils wheat. Around 1836 potatoes appeared among the crops.[^233] Vegetables were also popular: cabbage, peas, and onions. Flax was sown for own use. In villages situated on the Gostynin Lake District, fruit growing developed on a very large scale. Plums were the main crop grown, destined for drying.[^234] In the second half of the nineteenth century they constituted a significant source of income for farms in that area.[^235] A culinary speciality was a preserve made from plums and sugar beets, known as *Kreide*.[^236]

Craft trades in Olęder settlements served to meet the needs of the inhabitants and nowhere became an economically competitive occupation alongside livestock breeding and farming. In Olędry Czermińskie at the beginning of the nineteenth century there lived 2 cobblers, a miller, a clockmaker, a sawyer, a potter, a carpenter, a cooper, two blacksmiths, 3 linen weavers, and 4 weavers. In Troszynek, in the years 1808–1829, among approximately 90 inhabitants there were 7 craftsmen (a cobbler, a wheelwright, two millers, 3 linen weavers). In 1818 in Sady there were blacksmiths, tailors, carpenters, linen weavers, and millers.[^237] The preserved inventories document good equipping of farms with tools for land clearing (axes, hatchets, saws, levers) and for drainage works (spades, shovels, spades).[^238]

The level of material culture visible in household furnishings, in elements of clothing modelled on townspeople's dress, testifies to greater diversity than among Polish peasants. Considerably greater than in Polish peasant cottages was the role of heating installations (an enormous, multi-functional stove around which the entire structure of the building was built; as a rule two cooking ranges, a warmer, a bread oven, a smokehouse).[^239] The source of these differences lies in the genesis of the ethnos and in connections with the farming way of life. It is for this reason that only in the Vistula valley did the most typical elements occur: a dwelling house and livestock quarters under one roof, the homestead situated on an artificially raised mound parallel to the course of the river, and wicker basket fences on the boundaries of the fields.
