## Chapter II: Settlement in South Prussia. The Lights and Shadows of Cameralism on the Fringes of the State

The annexation by Prussia of vast tracts of central Polish territory in 1793 and the creation of the new province of South Prussia signified important changes in economic relations. Through the secularisation of church estates and the nationalisation of royal domains (abolition of starostwa), two formerly active protagonists were eliminated from the arena of economic life. In place of individual, dispersed economic initiatives there appeared a centralised, state structure whose operation derived from the adoption of a certain economic doctrine.

From the end of the seventeenth century a variant of mercantilist doctrine known as cameralism began to spread in the economic life of the countries of central and eastern Europe. Its name derives from the word *Kamera*, denoting the office at the ruler's court that administered his estates and collected taxes and dues owed him. The activity of that office gave rise to an entire field of knowledge (*Kameralwissenschaften*) which at the beginning of the eighteenth century was elevated to the rank of a university discipline. Cameralism grew on the ground of the relatively backward economic, social, and political structure of Germany, becoming an instrument of change. The doctrine assumed full utilisation of human resources and productive capacities in order to increase the ruler's revenues, concentrating principally on fiscal matters. In Prussia the prime aim of economic policy was considered to be the enlargement of the sovereign's income, since the ruler's prosperity was to be equivalent to the prosperity of the country. In Austria, cameralism was based on the utilisation of the productive labour of subjects, and the role of the monarch was to consist in creating appropriate conditions for their work. Prussian cameralism also proceeded from the assumption that the well-being and strength of the state is a function of the size of its subject population. From the time of Frederick II, *Peuplierungspolitik* (population policy) became state practice. It harmonised with the aspiration to create a strong and numerous army, since the expansion of that army and meeting its needs required population growth and the development of crafts.[^240] Russia under Catherine II also referred to this model, through the recruitment of specialists in various branches and the initiation of agricultural colonisation. The Russian variant of cameralism also had a military dimension — the integration of territorial acquisitions from the time of the war with Turkey (1768–1774).[^241] Prussian cameralism, derived from the theory and practice of mercantilist and physiocratic doctrines, became the decisive driving force of the state. Consistently applied, it brought it growth in revenues. Whereas the western European countries built mercantilism on the basis of overseas trade, in Prussia it rested on the planned increase of population. The path leading to this was to be the development of trade, the stimulation of consumption, industrial production and trade, and the expansion of the army.[^242]

Already on 17 November 1793 Frederick William II issued to the Oberpräsident of South Prussia a directive on the question of colonisation policy for the newly acquired territory. In accordance with the principles of cameralism, the most important aim was to be the increase of population. Since South Prussia began to be treated as a kind of bridge linking Silesia with East Prussia, it was considered desirable to increase the number of Germans in the territories newly incorporated into the state, which were inferior in this respect to the neighbouring provinces. At the same time the king stipulated that this aim could not be realised through depopulating the old Prussian provinces. These were still relatively sparsely populated and large population movements could affect their economic position adversely. The monarch transferred the entire initiative in this sphere to the minister of the province, to which post the already tried official Otto Karl Friedrich von Voss was appointed. The authorities of South Prussia prepared a wide-ranging colonisation action through the establishment of war and domain chambers (*Kriegs- und Domänenkammer*) in Poznań and Piotrków. The problem, however, was a shortage of suitable land. In the first phase of colonisation, attempts were made to settle abandoned peasant farms. There were, however, few of these. The king's plan to divide the large landed estates taken over by the state into farms and assign them to colonists was abandoned under the influence of advisers. Within the borders of South Prussia there were extensive church estates and former royal domains. Initially the Prussian authorities had no idea what policy to pursue with respect to them. In accordance with cameralist doctrine the fiscal instrument was resorted to. The church was left its estates in exchange for surrendering half of its income to the state. The starostwa were left for life in the hands of the existing lessees, with a 25% tax imposed on their incomes. This position changed as a result of the mass support which the nobility and clergy gave to the insurgents of 1794. This attitude was assessed as an abuse of the trust which the Prussian authorities had extended to both estates. The Prussian authorities were concerned with the integration of the new province with the rest of the country. It was planned to achieve this through the introduction of an efficient administration. This was difficult, since in a short time it was necessary to appoint numerous officials knowing the Polish language, and these were in short supply. The Rising of 1794 showed that the great majority of the Polish nobility would not allow itself to be integrated into the Prussian state. The landowning class was alienated by the scale of property confiscations. The authorities did not want friction with the Polish nobility and did not restructure the peasant burdens, but were happy to extend credit, which was used, however, not for investment but for consumption.[^243] In such a situation Prussian state administration became the main driving force of economic change. After the fall of the Kosciuszko Rising, church estates were secularised and those starostwa whose lessees had participated in the rising were confiscated.[^244] The estates acquired in this way were transformed into state domains (*Staatsdomänen*). A change also occurred at the position of minister of the province. Otto von Voss was replaced by Karl Georg von Hoym, and the province was subordinated directly to the General Directory in Berlin. The economy conducted by K. G. Hoym was criticised by the Prussian government. Doubts were raised in particular by the distribution of estates, which ended up in the hands of undeserving people, intriguers, or outright thieves.[^245] This was possible because Hoym enjoyed enormous trust from the king.[^246]

The situation changed in 1797 after Frederick William III assumed power; he recalled Hoym and reinstated O. Voss. A year later the action of settling South Prussia with colonists from Baden and Württemberg was begun. Great credit for its development was owed to a captain von Nothardt, whose first name is not known today,

*Seiner Königlichen Majestät in Preussen unmittelbar bevollmächtiger Commissarius zur Annahme der Kolonisten im Reich*[^247].

Equipped with the full powers of the Prussian government, he organised an enormous recruitment apparatus, whose core was the *Reichs-Kolonisten-Annahme-Büreau* in Öhringen near Heilbronn in Württemberg. He also succeeded in convincing Frederick William III that precisely then a very favourable moment had arrived for carrying out this action, since in southern Germany and the Rhineland, owing to the economic crisis prevailing there, many peasants and craftsmen were intending to emigrate.[^248] Contemporary accounts described in a very vivid way the excitement at the prospect of emigration.

Everywhere in the towns, small towns, and villages it was swarming with emissaries and agents; the roads by day and night were full of wagons, carts, and barrows (with children) of families on the move; the highways resounded with the voices of those going to reconnoitre or returning — and the administrative officials were burdened with troublesome emigration matters.[^249]

The emigrants justified their decisions in a tone similar to that of a peasant from the vicinity of Karlsruhe:

*Nicht die Wollust (Abenteuerlust) und zu hastende Gemächlichkeit (übereiltes Tun), auch nicht hartes Traktament von meiner Obrigkeit, sondern die äußerste Not und bitterste Armut bringen mich…*[^250]

The Prussian authorities thus turned their attention to the small and overpopulated lands of the south of the Reich (Württemberg, Baden, the Palatinate). The growing from year to year emigration from these territories to America was directed, it was decided, to South Prussia and New East Prussia.[^251] From 1798 the recruitment of potential settlers from Baden and Württemberg was begun.

One of the first measures of fundamental importance for colonisation in South Prussia was the undertaking of a major settlement action in forested areas. An appropriate plan, making use of the hints and remarks of Captain Nothardt, was presented by O. Voss on 3 September 1800.[^252] Forests were treated in the Hohenzollern monarchy as a resource of considerable value, and so from the end of the eighteenth century rational management of them had begun to be practised.[^253] For agricultural use there could therefore be designated only so-called forest offcuts or forests growing on marshy, difficult to access terrain.[^254] Among such areas were the economic units of Łaznów and Tkaczew in the secularised estates of the bishopric of Włocławek. By 1806 the Prussians had established on their territory a dozen or so colonies, including the colony of Grömbach (Łaznowska Wola), which was treated as a model colony. In the Kalisz and Warsaw departments, colonisation only began to develop more vigorously from 1800. The Prussian authorities encountered there many Olęder settlements founded in the years 1782–1797. Attempts to give them a more orderly territorial shape, on the model of colonies, ended in failure, however. The Olęders, already accustomed to their own homesteads, would not agree to the straightening of their boundaries or the laying out of new roads. In Modlica and Paprotnia, situated on the former estates of the Krakow chapter, a revolt even occurred when the village headmen of both villages refused to carry out the orders of the Prussian local administration.[^255] Considerable progress was also made by the settlement of peasants from Württemberg in the territories between the mouth of the Pilica into the Vistula and Warsaw. There were then founded Stara Iwiczna (Alt Ilvesheim), Nowa Iwiczna (Neu Ilvesheim), Orężna (Schwenningen), Ludwinów (Ludwigsburg), Józefosław (Krapenest), Kanstadt (Kąty), and Szopy.[^256] These colonies stabilised, and in the 1830s the more prosperous farmers acquired lands from the surrounding parcelled landed estates.[^257] In this way the villages of Laski (Storchnest), Opacz, Chyliczki (Hilitz), Gorzkiewki, Dziekanów, and Gąski (Gonzig) were formed.[^258] Although the Prussian authorities endeavoured to observe the principle of recruiting settlers only from the south-western territories of the Reich, they were nonetheless unable to prevent the resettlement of land-seeking peasants from other Prussian provinces. An example is the colony of Wilhelmswalde near Łódź, founded in 1799. Taking advantage of a large area of forest knocked down by a hurricane, the Prussian authorities settled 60 colonists from Pomerania there.[^259] The attachment of colonists to their native places is testified to by the giving of such names to new settlements as were borne by the localities from which they originated. The Swabian colonies lying in the triangle Łęczyca – Łask – Łódź: Grömbach, Neu-Sulzfeld, Königsbach, Grünberg, Effingshausen, Neu-Württemberg, Hochweiler took their names from localities situated between Stuttgart, Heilbronn, and Karlsruhe. The village of Leonberg in Gostyń county took its name from the identically named locality near Stuttgart. Similarly Nagold, Luisenthal, and Neu-Diettlingen, and in the vicinity of Warsaw: Alt Ilvesheim, Neu Ilvesheim, Ludwigsburg, and Schwenningen.[^260]

At the beginning of 1802 Captain von Nothardt was appointed to conduct the Kalisz war and domain chamber, with the task of overseeing the settlement action also in the remaining chambers of South Prussia. Meanwhile the recruitment bureau in Öhringen was directed by the chamber councillor Gruner, who remained in constant contact with minister O. Voss.[^261] It was then decided that Württemberg immigrants setting out on the journey to South Prussia would receive subsistence allowance of 2 groschen per day per person, paid out until they could support themselves from their own farm. The breakthrough in Prussian colonisation came in the years 1802–1804, when approximately 7,500 immigrants from German lands settled in South Prussia.[^262]

The emigration movement reached such dimensions that Prussian officials began to fear that they would not be able to cope with handling it. The principles on which the settling of colonists in South Prussia was based were announced on 1 January 1803. A division into six classes was adopted, depending on the size of the sum that those willing to emigrate eastward could demonstrate. This sum determined the extent of state support provided. Regardless of this, all colonists were bound by the same legal regulations.[^263] Already the protocol of the contract attested that the settler named by name and surname received his land allotment by grant of the monarch, which must have built trust and strengthened the ruler's positive image.[^264] In official correspondence the term *königliche Kolonien* was used, thereby emphasising their special status.[^265] In the content of each of the contracts, attention is drawn to the placement already in Art. 1 of a guarantee exempting the settler and his sons from military service. The legislator must have been aware that in view of the prospect of long years of service in the Prussian army, this provision was very attractive to potential colonists and could prove decisive in the decision to resettle to the new, eastern province of the state. Settlers received land as perpetual leasehold tenure, from which they were to pay rent after the expiry of the free years (Art. 3). The free period (*wolnizna*) amounted to from 3 to 6 years and depended on the type of land and the extent of the work that needed to be done in bringing it under cultivation. The level of rent paid in half-yearly instalments due in advance was to change with the price of grain (Art. 3). In the event of arrears in payment, the government reserved the right to repossess the land (Art. 6). During the free period the settler was exempt from all taxes (Art. 2). Without government permission the settlement might not be divided or burdened with debt (Art. 11). Any possible sale of the land was restricted by several conditions. A colonist might sell land only after the expiry of the free years and upon obtaining the consent (*konsens*) of the government (Arts. 10, 12). For 10 years after the conclusion of the free period, the purchaser of the land could only be a foreigner. Only after that period was the sale of land to local people permitted (Art. 13). On sale, apart from sale to children, the settler was obliged to pay the treasury *laudemium* in the amount of half the annual rent (Art. 14). In settlements where buildings had been erected at government cost, the colonist had to insure them with a fire insurance company and look after their good condition. If the buildings burned down, the settler had to erect new ones at his own cost (Art. 8). Trees growing on the colonist's land were the property of the treasury; they could be sold to him by the forestry office (Art. 9). Other obligations of the colonist were also envisaged: payment of chimney tax (the Prussian equivalent of the old Polish hearth tax), provision of forage and transport services (Art. 4). If the land held by the settler was burdened with a tithe, it was to be paid (Art. 5). Several further provisions regulated the colonist's functioning in his new surroundings. If a church or school was being built in the vicinity, the colonist was obliged to help in their construction and thereafter in the maintenance of the buildings, the clergyman, and the teacher (Art. 7). At his own cost he was also to maintain the drainage installations established by the government, bridges, and embankments (Art. 17). He was also obliged to take part in communal works requiring collective effort (Art. 16). The settler was also subject to the compulsory monopoly of spirits: he could obtain drinks only from the local inn, distillery, or brewery (Art. 19). In addition it was expressly stipulated that no other benefits accrued to the colonist, not even the gathering of firewood in government forests (Art. 18). The settlement contract was issued free of charge, but the cost of entry in the mortgage register was borne by the settler (Art. 20). The authorities also withdrew from the subsistence allowance previously paid to colonists, in the amount of 2 groschen per day per person. This daily allowance was to be paid out until the farm could already maintain itself.[^266]

Colonists assigned to Class I (possessing from 30 to 500 Rhenish florins) received land allotments of 4 *Morgen* in area, exceptionally 6 *Morgen*. They were obliged to maintain the buildings erected at government cost. They were entitled to from 3 to 6 free years and compensation for land clearing. They also received reimbursement of travel costs in the amount of 2 good groschen per mile per person. The following farm implements became the colonist's property: a sickle, a scythe, rakes, pitchforks, flails, a chopper, a shovel, a spade, an axe, a carpenter's adze, a wheelbarrow, a brake (for flax), and a spinning wheel. The set of tools made possible not only the performance of manual field work but also basic woodcutting and carpentry tasks. The flax brake and the spinning wheel indicate that it was anticipated by the authorities that the least prosperous colonists would take up additional occupations in the processing and spinning of fibres (flax or hemp). Perhaps the 4 thalers given as a gift to the settler for garden seeds were also intended to be used for the purchase of linseed or hemp seed. Class I settlers received livestock from the government: 2 cows, 1 pig, 4 geese, 4 hens, and 1 cockerel. As the only group, settlers of this class were not required to provide forage and transport services.[^267] Class II colonists, demonstrating a cash holding of from 500 to 1,000 Rhenish florins, were directed to abandoned peasant settlements of from 15 to 40 *Morgen* in area. They received reimbursement of travel costs on the same basis as Class I settlers and were also compensated for land clearing. In addition they were assigned farm implements and livestock in proportion to the size of the given settlement.[^268] Class II colonists were obliged to provide forage and transport services. Settlers assigned to Class III (1,000–2,000 Rhenish florins in cash) received 3 Magdeburg *Hufen* of land as well as an allocation of timber for a house and farm buildings. They were extended a loan of 600 thalers for a period of from 5 to 15 years on which they paid no interest, as well as a sum of 200 thalers as a gift from the government. This group of settlers was equipped with neither livestock nor tools. Similar conditions were guaranteed to colonists assigned to Class IV (2,000–3,000 Rhenish florins in cash). The only difference in relation to Class III settlers was a larger land allotment, comprising 4 *Hufen*. Class V colonists (3,000–4,000 Rhenish florins) received 5 *Hufen* of land and a loan of 1,400 thalers, which they were obliged to repay during the free years. Class VI colonists (4,000–5,000 florins), who enjoyed the same benefits as Class V, received 6 *Hufen* of land. Particularly actively settled were the forests in the vicinity of Kłodawa, Gałkówek near Łódź; the colony of Rakutowo near Przedecz (municipality of Kowal, Włocławek county) was populated. The majority of colonists came from Württemberg.[^269] Prussian colonisation through individual contracts with settlers excluded the possibility of abuses through multiple applications for various kinds of support.

In disputed cases it was possible to appeal to the text of the contract (Annex IV). A characteristic feature of Prussian colonisation was the creation in colonies of farms of differing acreage. Alongside 30-*Morgen* farms there were 15-*Morgen* ones and farms of a few *Morgen*, assigned to peasants who were to support themselves from hired labour as day labourers (*Tagelöhner*).[^270]

The first large Swabian colony in South Prussia, at the founding of which in 1800 the Prussian authorities for the first time practically realised their theoretical colonisation plans, was Łaznowska Wola (Grömbach).[^271] In May 1800 ten Swabian families set out for South Prussia on five wagons drawn by 8 horses. They covered 152 miles (approx. 850 km). The Warsaw chamber determined that the newcomers possessed almost nothing beyond a small amount of cash.[^272] Among them were also craftsmen, but ones with the ability to do agricultural work.[^273] Initially they were directed to the forests near Zgierz. They were guaranteed daily subsistence allowance of 2 groschen a day for a man, 1 grosz and 6 pfennigs for a woman, and one grosz for children under 12 years of age.[^274] In addition the settlers received tools for land clearing free of charge and money for the making of agricultural implements. Already after two months the settlers were warning the Warsaw chamber that even 20 years would not suffice to transform land overgrown with dense forest into arable fields. They made use of the possibility of moving to the Łaznów forests, where the Prussian authorities planned to organise a settlement in which 33 families could live, each taking up 60 *Morgen* of land. In the end the terrain was demarcated for 50 farms.[^275] The survey was carried out by surveyor Kramm (Annex VI). In September 1800 von Colomb, who was directing the colonisation in the Warsaw department, proposed that every 2–3 families should build a temporary shelter for themselves and their livestock in order to survive the winter. For want of clay he recommended abandoning the construction of timber-framed buildings and building in wood, of which there was an abundance. Instead of tools for tree-clearing, settlers received money with which they were to acquire tools independently. Von Colomb judged that although the costs of purchasing livestock were high, each family should have a stock exceeding that specified in point 6 of the settlement contract: one horse, two donkeys, 2 cows, 2 pigs, 4 geese, and as tools: a chaff cutter, 2 scythes, hay forks, a plough, a churn, and milk vessels, two buckets, a dozen or so sacks, and for sowing — peas, millet, and linseed.[^276] The colonists were also offered payment for land clearing until September 1802 at the rate of 10 thalers a day. The subsistence allowance sufficed only for provisioning but not for the repair of tools or the purchase of clothing worn out during heavy labour. The soil was admittedly good but heavily overgrown with shrubs. At the beginning of November the settlers applied for an advance payment for land clearing, since the Olęders who had settled in the vicinity were ready to help them in the work.[^277] It was stipulated, however, that if the settlers worked as day labourers at the erection of dwelling buildings (which had been contracted out to an entrepreneur), then they would not be entitled to payment for land clearing.[^278] The village consisted of 54 farms, each of 60 *Morgen*. A 6-*Morgen* plot was also set aside for the pastor, 1 *Morgen* for a small church, 4 *Morgen* for a school, 3 *Morgen* for a smithy, 4 *Morgen* for a communal orchard for the village, and 2 *Morgen* for a cemetery.[^279] The 6 free years proposed for the settlers were shortened by the Warsaw chamber authorities to 4 years. The recommendations regarding house construction were also changed. Permission was given for the use of wood, but the buildings were to be erected on a masonry foundation; chimneys and hearths were to be made of unfired brick, while the chimney above the ridge had to be built of fired brick and additionally coated with lime.[^280] This decision was overruled by the Berlin authorities, who recommended timber-frame (*Fachwerk*) construction. The digging of clay and the firing of bricks were made the settlers' responsibility. It was also decided to dig one well for every two neighbouring farms. Fences were to be built by settlers from timber obtained on their plots. The total Prussian government expenditure on founding the colony of Grömbach amounted to 33,573 thalers and 23 groschen. The new settlements established by the Prussian authorities were characterised by regular building layout. Row villages (linear villages) were formed, with buildings situated along the road at equal distances from one another, a layout favoured by land allotments equal in area.[^281] Original plans of several colonies founded in the years 1799–1802 in the vicinity of Łódź have been preserved. In their design various solutions were applied (Annexes VI–X). From a village in which all farms were situated along a single road (Wilhelmswalde / Borowo), through a colony with several roads laid out (Grömbach / Łaznowska Wola), to the unique spatial arrangement of Nowosolna, in which the individual farms were located along four star-shaped intersecting roads. A certain analogy to this arrangement can be found in the layout of the centre of Karlsruhe.[^282] What is certain is that the plans of all the colonies established by the Prussian authorities were drawn up without regard to terrain conditions. In several cases (Krasnodęby, Lwówek, Markówka, Nowosolna, Tkaczewska Góra) this entailed the necessity of later corrections or effectively limited the possibility of territorial development of the villages.[^283]

Only fragmentary data tell of the extent of immigration from Württemberg to South Prussia. From August 1803 to June 1804, 194 families arrived, totalling 1,075 persons, and brought with them assets valued at 135,764 thalers. Per person this amounted therefore to 137.96 thalers, but per family — which seems the more correct indicator, since it determined the size of the settlement allotted — 648.82 thalers (extreme values: 531.50 – 1,063.70 thalers).[^284] It should therefore be assumed that almost all the settlers were classified into Class II. Other fragmentary documentary sources also indicate the decisive preponderance of this class of colonists in the settlement of South Prussia. From the preserved monthly reports of the resettlement bureau in Öhringen it emerges that in 1802, 263 colonist families comprising 1,325 persons in total were dispatched to South Prussia.[^285] Their prosperity was estimated on average at 444 thalers per family. The largest group (159 families) was directed to the Warsaw department. A further 67 families were assigned the Kalisz department as their place of settlement, and the remaining 37 families were assigned the Poznań department.[^286] These locations attest to the realisation of the political aim of Prussian colonisation, which was the saturation with German population of the frontiers of the state, where Germans were still few in number. There were also mentions of resettlements to the territory of the Płock chamber, but these contain no numerical data. It is known from other sources that in the years 1802–1804 the colony of Lwówek (Leonberg) near Gostynin was developing dynamically, with 78 colonists settling there.[^287] Nearly 86% of its inhabitants came from Württemberg, the remainder from Alsace, the Palatinate, and the principality of Baden-Durlach.[^288] In 1833 the village was enlarged through additional settlers arriving from Württemberg, partly at the cost of the parcelling of surrounding estates.[^289] In the future Lwówek was to become the third largest Swabian village in Poland by population.

In the selection of colonists, their qualifications were not always given attention, and often the provision requiring them to demonstrate specified funds was disregarded. A large proportion of the settlers thus arrived with exaggerated expectations, with the hope that without great expenditure of effort they would soon attain prosperity. An excellent illustration of the mood prevailing among immigrants from Württemberg was a popular song known among them, familiar already at the end of the eighteenth century, extolling moving to Poland as a land of Canaan abounding in all goods, where no one would want for honey (Annex XI).[^290] Little is known of the possessions which the immigrants brought with them to the lands between the Prosna, the Pilica, and the Vistula. Judging from the rich equipment of farms guaranteed by the authorities at the places of settlement, these were presumably only personal belongings and, in the case of craftsmen, tools. The man setting out in 1801 for South Prussia,

*Martin Jaeckle mit Frau und 3 Kindern […] hat nur 20. Fl. Geld mitgenommen hat aber 16 Stück theils hölzerne theils messingene Uhren mitgenommen, er war ein Uhrmacher wie es deren auf dem Schwarzwalde viele gibt*[^291].

From the seventeenth century the manufacture of wall clocks became widespread in the Black Forest (*Schwarzwald*). Initially these were entirely wooden constructions, including the mechanism. Craft production of clocks was an occupation carried on in autumn and winter, and constituted an important additional source of income for the rural population.[^292] Itinerant traders occupied themselves with sales, reaching with clock components as far as Transylvania. The timepieces had identical mechanisms and housings of similar size. They differed only in the decoration of the front panel, which allowed buyers to choose the specimen that suited them best. Black Forest clocks reached the lands of southern Poland via Silesia, while in central Poland they appeared together with arrivals from Württemberg at the beginning of the nineteenth century and became widespread among the rural population. They were recorded in numerous property inventories.[^293] Preserved to this day in museum collections and private collections, they are, apart from cemetery gravestones, the only material traces of the presence of German peasants in central Poland in the first half of the nineteenth century (Annex XIII).

The conditions which colonists encountered in their new places of settlement frequently diverged from the promises of agents. Above all, the newcomers were unable to carry out forest clearing. In recruitment announcements only a fleeting mention was made of the possibility of receiving forested land allotments. In many cases this circumstance was passed over in silence. An even greater disappointment fell to the lot of the cottagers who had hoped to become independent peasants in South Prussia. Complaints were also made about very severe winters and infertile soil. Many embittered settlers returned to their homeland, giving accounts of the hard situation of the colonists in the east. In 1803 the Württemberg press began to disseminate an account by one Georg Friedrich Beutelspacher, whose son had visited the vicinity of Warsaw in search of a suitable place to settle. His report was not encouraging as regards emigration. Besides the complaints familiar from other reports about bureaucratic sluggishness in directing settlers to particular colonies, Beutelspacher pointed to the lack of possibility of additional earnings, since the market for services and crafts was completely dominated by the local Jews.

*Er habe mehrere angetroffen, die Kürschner, Schneider und Schuhmacher in einer Person gewesen seien*[^294].

By publishing information of this kind, discouraging emigration from Württemberg, it was forbidden to disseminate the news sent in letters about finding better living conditions in South Prussia. When in the years 1803–1805, as the wave of emigrants intensified, the Württemberg authorities began to circulate an anonymous text depicting the Polish lands in the darkest colours.

Als der König von Preußen sein schlechtes Polen anbauen wollte, ließen sich auch zu diesem die Schwaben brauchen, wanderten zu Tausenden dahin, bauten gutwillig das elende Polen an und vertauschten das schöne kornreiche Schwaben mit den Bärenlande Polen. Die schwäbischen Ställe sind besser und bequemer als dortigen Menschenwohnungen[^294].

It is not known whether these warnings had the desired effect. The political collapse of Prussia in 1807 and Württemberg's accession to the German Confederation allowed it to introduce an emigration ban, which remained in force from 1807 to 1815.[^295]

The Prussian authorities issued a series of regulations designed to protect returning emigrants from being treated as vagabonds. Re-emigrants were provided with documents explaining their reasons for travel.[^296] In the years 1802–1803, wealthier settlers capable of taking on the largest farms appeared among the emigrating Swabians. However, the overwhelming majority were poor and even destitute peasants.[^297] In the face of the growing wave of emigration, the Prussian authorities began to observe the principles of farm allocation very scrupulously. As a result, settlements began to arise almost exclusively for Class I and Class II colonists. They were frequently directed to places abandoned by earlier settlers who had been unable to farm on the infertile soil. This occurred, among other places, in the settlement of Neu-Württemberg and Klein Gorne (Małe Górne) in the Warsaw department.[^298] Numerous settlements also arose consisting exclusively of 3–4-morgen farms, which raised the question of their capacity for independent existence.

The Prussian authorities endeavoured to disseminate information about the excellent conditions settlers had found in South Prussia. A particularly notable example is the letter of Johann Friedrich Meysenholder, sent in August 1801 to his brother-in-law living in Kochendorf in Württemberg. The colonist, living bey Petrikau, reported in detail on his situation, described his farm, praised the abundance of life — incomparably better than in his homeland:

Es ist doch besser ich kann meinen Kindern in Pohlen gute Nahrung geben und keine Schulden, als in Deutschland schlechte Nahrung und Schulden, und in Trübsal herrum schweben[^299].

The delivery of the letter to the addressee was facilitated by Captain von Nothardt. He ordered 20 copies to be made and sent to various parts of Württemberg, in order to refute the rumours circulating there about the difficult lives of emigrants settled in Poland. The enthusiastic tone of the letter differs fundamentally from other testimonies of South Prussian settlers — admittedly fragmentary, but numerous — which did not conceal their disappointment with the conditions they found and the difficulties of farming. Moreover, Meysenholder's letter contained only vague, and above all geographically incorrect, information about the place of residence (21 Meilen hinter Warschau auf der rechten Seite bey Petrikau)[^300]. This suggests that the letter was the work of Prussian administrative officials and was used as a propaganda tool. At the same time, those settled in the forests of the Łaznów economy, and thus bey Petrikau, complained:

die Klagen unserer Weiber nahmen mit der Noth zu, wir hatten nichts mehr, und die Hoffnung nahm mit jedem Tage ab, wir entschlossen uns daher ins Reich zurückzukehren, einige wollten über Berlin gehen um des Königs Majestät unser Unglück selbst vorzustellen […]. Wir verkauften unsere Hütten an alte Colonisten ich erhielt für meinige 4 rth. 6 gr. und zogen im größtem Elend ab[^301].

Complaints about the lack of the promised remuneration for clearing forest were frequent. Local intendants were accused of sluggishness and lack of interest in the newcomers. Settlers directed to Wiączyń (60 families) were housed in the local inn. The head forester of the Łaznów economy ordered them to build shelters in the forest and begin clearing trees. When they refused, arguing that their settlements had not yet been measured, they were accused of laziness. A year later, only 11 settler families remained in Wiączyń. The other 49 families had emigrated, partly to Russia, partly to Galicia. Of those who remained in Wiączyń, 5 families had decided to move to the nearby colony of Grömbach, and 3 more wished to go to Łódź.[^302]

In general, however, by 1804 the possibilities of providing land for settlers arriving in South Prussia began to be exhausted. A large proportion of them were sent to New East Prussia, which was also being settled with rural colonists, though on a considerably smaller scale. In mid-1804, the acceptance of new immigrants was halted. The scale of settlement in South Prussia is shown by the Generaltableau über den Fortgang des Kolonistenwesens in Südpreußens vom 13. Februar 1806.[^303] In the Poznań department, 518 farms were established, with 2,408 inhabitants and a total area of 12,283 morgens. In the Kalisz department: 352 farms with 1,745 inhabitants and 8,518 morgens of land. In the Warsaw department, 1,265 farms were created, with 6,140 inhabitants and 41,390 morgens of land. In total, therefore, 2,135 farms were established, with 10,293 inhabitants and covering an area of 62,191 morgens. The average size of a colonist's farm in South Prussia was slightly more than 29 morgens. These results were interpreted by the Prussian administration as very good. This is evidenced by the wide dissemination of the aforementioned Generaltableau, preserved to this day in numerous copies in many archival collections.[^304]

Looking at the expenditure that the Prussian government devoted to the settlement of South Prussia, the entire enterprise must be regarded as a costly undertaking. In the years 1798–1806 it consumed 1,841,083 thalers, and thus the average cost of establishing a single farm amounted to 862 thalers.[^305] Many expenses proved unexpectedly high. In the early years of colonisation, private contractors were commissioned to build farmsteads for settlers. The construction of buildings on a 60-morgen farm in Bukowiec (Königsbach) cost 881 thalers, in Starowa Góra (Effingshausen) — 763 thalers, and in Łaznowska Wola (Grömbach) — 676 thalers. According to the calculations of a government building inspector, the same buildings erected according to his plans should have cost only 343 thalers. It was therefore decided to entrust construction to the colonists themselves, paying them 343 thalers for that purpose.[^306] The early days of farming in the colonies were not easy, as the Prussian authorities did not always fulfil their obligations towards settlers, who were generally not very well-off financially. This is confirmed by the examples of Starowa Góra (Effingshausen) and Markówka (Hochweiler). Of the group wishing to settle in Starowa Góra, three colonists made false declarations about their financial standing, overstating their assets. They were punished with a two-week arrest, and at their place of settlement received the smallest plots, of only four morgens. During the first two years of the settlement's existence, only two dwelling houses and one barn managed to be erected. There were no stables or other farm buildings, indicating that livestock was kept in the open air. By 1806, construction of most farmsteads was completed. A school building was also erected. Before 1816, several colonists left the village, unable to endure the hardships of farming. They returned to their home regions in Württemberg. In 1816, the village had 24 farms with a total area of 1,092 morgens.[^307] These provided employment for farmers' sons until the 1860s. The beginnings of Markówka were also difficult. In 1803, the village headman asked the Berlin authorities for help, as:

Das Gras aus dem Walde essen wir wirklich schon. – Ja, es sind schon zwey Personen verhungert[^308].

There was also a shortage of food for livestock. As a result, some families returned to Württemberg.

As already mentioned, the Prussian administration found Dutch-type (Olęder) settlements in the new province. The opinion of local officials about their inhabitants was not the best. Setting aside the Olęders' reluctance to have their settlements reorganised spatially, attention was drawn to their rather unstable existence. The Olęders were described as a class of restless, pioneering farmers (unruhige Klasse von Primair Cultivateurs) who do not attach themselves to any place but seek lands they can use, cultivate them during the free years, then sell the farms and begin life, work, and speculation elsewhere.[^309] Their skill at clearing forest, however, the authorities fairly — and highly — valued:

Die Württemberger, welche nicht Gut mit dem Roden umzugehen wissen, Hauländer die mit dergleichen Arbeiten bekannt sind, und ihre eigene Ländereien schon urbar gemacht haben, für Geld zum roden dingen, sich selbst aber damit beschäftigen, das gerodete Land, welches noch immer noch voll Wurzeln steckt, und worauf doch vor Hand noch viel Bauholz stehen bleiben muss, umzugraben und zu reinigen, um so die vollkommene Ackerbaustellung möglich zu machen[^310].

For the Olęders in turn, the appearance of groups of Swabian colonists offered the opportunity of additional earnings, since the newcomers did not know the technique of forest clearing. They also quickly noticed the far more advantageous farming conditions in the colonies. In 1802, Andreas and Michael Dure, Olęders settled in Holendry Stokowskie near Łódź, addressed the Warsaw chamber with a question as to whether their farms could be converted into colonial settlements.[^311] The answer was negative, since this would have contradicted the principle of creating compact settlements in which all farmers, throughout the entire province, had the same rights and obligations. For similar reasons, the lands of the nearby serf village of Wiączyń were not incorporated into the territory of the Nowosolna colony, accepting a somewhat smaller extent for the new settlement than had been planned.[^312] The justification was the presence in Wiączyń of peasants obligated to labour services and dues, which in the classical rent economy that prevailed in the colonies could no longer have a place. Despite the ban on nationals purchasing farms in the colonies, such transactions occurred. In January 1801, Friedrich Bischoff, an Olęder residing in Pustków Górny, due to old age and poor health transferred his farm to his nephew (he had no children of his own). It consisted of 15 morgens in Pustków Górny and 15 morgens in Tkaczewska Góra (Neu Württemberg).[^313] It is not known how the Olęder came to possess land in a settlement established by the Prussian administration around 1801 and reorganised around 1805.[^314] Perhaps the land in Tkaczewska Góra was the dowry of his wife, Rozyna née Schwarz.

During the South Prussian era, Olęder settlement continued, carried out by private estate owners. It is, however, only sporadically traceable in sources. After 1793, on the territories occupied by Prussia, castle and land records were no longer kept. There was as yet no notary's office, in which — after its introduction in 1807 — settlement contracts were registered. The Prussian local administration also presumably showed little interest in such spontaneous initiatives, being engaged in the planned and centrally directed colonisation action from Berlin. Some light on the conditions then dictated to settlers by landowners is shed by a contract drawn up on 31 March 1802 by Michał Korabita Ostrowski, owner of the village of Kietlin near Radomsk, with the Olęder Johann Georgius Picz (Annex XII).[^315] This preliminary agreement defined the conditions for Olęders settling within the boundaries of Kietlin. The text of the document was formulated rather clumsily, without preserving the hierarchy of matters typical in such documents and with numerous errors, suggesting that Michał Ostrowski had no experience in drawing up such contracts. The settlers, all illiterate, likewise lacked such experience. The document, written in Polish, provides no information about the manner (or language) of communication between the two parties to the contract. The first names and surnames of the Olęders were recorded phonetically, allowing only speculation about their original form: Joan [Johann] Georgius Picz [Pietsch?], Georgi [Georg?] Friderych [Friedrich?], Samuel Picz [Pietsch?], Andrys [Andreas?] Chwic [?], Joan [Johann] Libeke [Liebeke?]. The lord of Kietlin allocated to the five settling Olęders an unspecified area of land, which was still to be measured by a surveyor. The cost of bringing in the surveyor and his work was to be paid by the settlers. Only then was the definitive settlement contract to be concluded. The area from the Kucharski road to the Łagiewniki boundaries was planned to be divided into farms of one łan each. The rent, dues, and obligations of the settlers were also calculated from this area. The entry fee, referred to as wchodne, was set at 72 złoty per łan. The rent was to be 115 złoty per year, payable in two instalments on St. Martin's Day and St. Adalbert's Day. At the first of these dates the manor required a payment of 4 capons, with the option of a monetary equivalent of 1 złoty 15 grosz per bird. Each Olęder was obliged to perform labour during the harvest — 12 days with scythe or sickle — and one day of ploughing with his own draught animals and plough. A further obligation was one long (6-mile) or two shorter (3-mile) cartage journeys with grain. Five korcy of rye (640 litres) were to be loaded onto each cart. If any colonist was unable to make this journey, he had to pay the manor 12 złoty, i.e. 2 Prussian thalers. All necessary buildings the Olęders were to erect themselves at their own expense. They could use the timber found on their land, with the exception of 30 pine trees already felled and processed. Once the timber from their own plots was used up, settlers could haul firewood from the manorial forests, but only one cartload per week and from places indicated by the manorial forester. The Olęders were entitled to pasture rights in the manorial forests, but no more than 8 head of cattle per łan. The keeping of goats — which usually caused considerable damage to crops and forests — was rigorously regulated. One of the contract's provisions stated that the keeping of goats is forbidden to colonists on pain of their being freely shot if any should be found in the forest.

The Olęders could not use meadows lying within the boundaries of Kietlin. The manor reserved the right to hand these over to settlers only after the free years had elapsed. Beekeeping was also restricted. It was permitted within the bounds of the Olęder farms, but for every 10 hives the manor required one quart of pure honey per year for itself. Settlers were subject to a ban on hunting, with the exception of shooting wolves. The lord, however, reserved for himself the right to hunt year-round in all forests of his estate, with the customary exclusion — on account of the animals' breeding season — of the period from St. Adalbert's Day (23 April) to St. Bartholomew's Day (24 August). The manorial propination monopoly also remained in force. Bringing in foreign spirits was punishable by a fine of 8 złoty per quart of vodka and 1 złoty per quart of beer. The agreement also provided four morgens of land for the village headman, free of rent. A further 1.5 morgens were designated by the lord for a school. The headman together with the aldermen was to settle disputes between colonists. In more serious matters, an appeal to the manor was available. Should any colonist abandon his land, the community was obliged to cover his debts to the manor or the tavern. An Olęder could sell his farm, but could not sell it to a nobleman or a Jew. He was required to pay 10% laudemium to the manor on the value of the transaction.

An interesting contribution to understanding the property relations developing in the South Prussian era is the contract concluded on 5 November 1802 by Bogusław Nieszkowski, hereditary owner of the Korzeniew estate in the Kalisz district, with a group of Olęder settlers. In the contract protocol (as the document was titled), Nieszkowski yields to the Hollanders on the colony the entire land and soil not yet measured by a surveyor, together in all with manorial and village fields hitherto held, gardens, meadows and forests, with full right of ownership.[^316]

An exceptionally high entry fee was agreed upon, amounting to 600 thalers (3,600 złoty) per one Chełmno łan (Art. 2). The lord secured 100 Polish złoty per year in rent from each łan, as well as 1 Viertel of oats and one hen — no more (Art. 10).[^317] The citation of only excerpts from this private contract in later records of the Kalisz district chief does not permit a fuller analysis. However, a significant deficiency is clearly visible, which may be evidence of B. Nieszkowski's poor knowledge of property law. Full right of ownership would exclude the possibility of subjecting the land purchasers to any fees or dues on account of their possession. On the other hand, the enormous entry fee, which has no equivalent in the source material known to me, could indicate that the Olęders were indeed becoming full owners of the land they acquired. A further premise for such a reading of the contract is the provision on the transfer to the Olęders of the entire area of the Korzeniewo estate, without reserving any part thereof for the seller. From this it follows that the seller must have had another place of residence. However, the imposition of rent on the settlers and the demand for dues in kind — admittedly symbolic by the standards customarily imposed in that era — point to the hereditary rent (wieczysto-czynszowa) form of land use by the purchasers. In 1849, all the lands were bought back from the Olęders by a certain Stawowski.[^318] In 1856, a dispute arose between the Olęders of Korzeniew and the parish priest of the Roman Catholic parish in Kościelec. The clergyman complained that the colonists were not paying the tithe. Under the law, it was due to him, since the Olęders had been settled on the lands of an estate from which the tithe had been collected. An explanation was provided to the chief of the Kalisz district by a 67-year-old Olęder, Gottfryd Piel:

When I came with my father to Korzeniew, at that time the Prussian ruled; I remember well that the colonists — it must be over 53 years ago — never paid more than 25 złoty in tithe to Kościelec, and although the late Reverend Binkiewicz wanted to introduce a tithe of 40 złoty, which came about at the time the Frenchman retreated, he took us to court and conducted [it] for a couple of years.[^319]

The outcome of this case, as well as the consequences of the complaint from the priest of Kościelec, are unknown, but this episode well illustrates the dimension of the feudal burdens weighing on the farmers of Korzeniew, as well as the peculiar reckoning of time in which only great events proved worthy of remembrance.

Prussian settlement brought with it the spread of the rent economy in its classical form, where apart from monetary rent and public taxes, farmers were not burdened with any other obligations. The planned layout of villages, on the other hand, was not a novelty in the territories between the Prosna, Pilica, and Vistula rivers. Recommendations to build in a line are found very frequently in contracts concluded with Olęders at the end of the eighteenth century. There is no doubt, however, that the Prussian authorities observed this principle far more rigorously, which was also favoured by the erection of buildings according to a single plan and by a specialist contractor. Despite the development of appropriate plans, the Prussian administration failed to popularise half-timbered (Fachwerk) construction, known and used by colonists in their homeland. There were buildings erected using this technique, such as the house of Jan Rohr, the sub-forester of government forests in Nowosolna, but the general body of peasants contented themselves with simpler houses.[^320]

Rural settlement carried out by the Prussian authorities also had an important political aim. Through instruments characteristic of economic policy, efforts were made to consolidate the presence of German peasants on Polish lands. In 1804, the war-and-economics chamber of South Prussia issued a Publikandum addressed to all colonists in the province. It prohibited the sale of a farm for a period of 10 years after its acquisition without the chamber's permission. Consent to a sale could relate only to a settler arriving from abroad, and the seller was obliged to declare how he would use the money obtained from the sale of the land. It could be spent only within the country, i.e. on the territory of South Prussia. Furthermore, during the free years a colonist was not permitted to sell livestock or incur debt on the farm without the chamber's consent.[^321] The immediate economic significance of rural settlement carried out by the Prussian government was not great. Colonists enjoying the free years contributed nothing to the state budget, yet frequently required additional financial support. They had no share in the fourfold increase in revenues from taxes collected in South Prussia between 1793 and 1807. The province's positive balance was due above all to the rapid growth of grain exports and a slight increase in cloth production (in the Poznań department).[^322] A fundamental restructuring of the economic system was not achieved. It proved impossible to revive internal trade, carry out road construction, or expand the towns.[^323] Bringing colonists from distant territories was a mistake, as Minister K. G. Hoym had already pointed out. The great distance to be covered on the journey raised migration costs. The climatic and soil conditions, and the manner of farming, were unfamiliar to settlers, differing considerably from what they knew in their home regions. Moreover, colonists had to be attracted with promises of significant benefits. The cost of settling each family was greatly increased by the need to establish a farm for it. In total, according to the estimates of Minister v. Voss, 10,293 settlers settled in South Prussia.[^324]

This result was contributed to by the centralised management of the colonisation effort, purposeful recruitment among volunteers, and a precisely defined method of land allocation. In November 1806, with the outbreak of the Franco-Prussian War, the colonisation effort in South Prussia came to an end. The Prussian experience was to be drawn upon in the future by the governments of the Duchy of Warsaw and the Kingdom of Poland.
