## Chapter III: A Difficult Legacy. German Colonists in the Duchy of Warsaw

A peculiar legacy inherited by the Duchy of Warsaw was the multitude of German peasants settled during the Prussian era, who required care. Two positions were characteristic of the settlement policy pursued after 1807 by the Duchy's authorities. The first recommended applying to the settlers the same means that the Prussian authorities had used to retain them in the country. Such a solution was proposed to Frederick Augustus by the State Council of the Duchy of Warsaw.[^326] The second placed emphasis on developing a new concept of settlement policy that would respond to the political and economic needs of the Duchy of Warsaw. As a result of the continental blockade ordered by Napoleon, the export of Polish grain collapsed almost entirely. The collapse of trade and the lack of capital caused widespread impoverishment and threatened the financial existence of the Duchy. Under these conditions, the economic policy pursued by the government was determined to a greater degree by practical aims than by references to economic theory.[^327] The maintenance of the Prussian colonisation principles quickly proved impossible. It is true that Frederick Augustus declared that:

For gain or loss, we have accepted under our management the colonies found in estates designated for the revenue of our crown,[^328]

but both the economic situation of the Duchy and the state of development of the colonists' settlements precluded any profits. Meanwhile, among the Württemberg peasants settled in the colonies, the conviction was spreading that the Duchy of Warsaw's authorities would not care for them as the Prussian administration had. The new division of the territory into departments corresponded largely to the earlier Prussian division into war-and-economics chambers (Kriegs- und Domänenkammer), which enabled a rapid and pragmatic reconstruction of the administration — all the more so as many Prussian officials remained in the service of the Duchy, not infrequently already having family ties with their new place of residence, especially in the provinces.[^329] This could give rise to hopes that the German settlers would find understanding with the local authorities.

Despite the difficult political and economic conditions, and considerable financial burdens, the government of the Duchy of Warsaw undertook the labour of the country's economic reconstruction. The promotion of industrial, craft, and commercial development was regarded as particularly desirable. In it was seen not only economic benefit, but progress in these sectors of the economy was defined as a political necessity. In the leading circles of the Duchy of Warsaw, the view was formulated that the collapse of the First Polish Republic had occurred partly because the importance of building a modern economy was not understood. The lack of industry, craft, and trade — and the associated group of people who in the western continent formed the support of governments — meant that Poland was the first to be eliminated from the ranks of European states.[^330] It was also pointed out that a large portion of the workshops and manufactories existing in the country owed their establishment to foreigners. This constituted an important premise for encouraging foreigners to settle in the Duchy. Of pivotal significance was the decree of Frederick Augustus of 19 December 1807, admitting foreigners to citizenship in the Duchy of Warsaw.[^331] The constitution of the Duchy of Warsaw, half a year earlier, in Article 4 abolished the personal serfdom of peasants and guaranteed them personal freedom.[^332] The elaboration of this imprecise provision (serfdom is abolished) was brought by the December decree. It introduced the principles of capitalist land ownership in place of the previous — feudal — one, thereby depriving peasants of the right to land. Article 3 of the decree permitted the lord to remove a peasant from the land at his own discretion, with the sole condition of half a year's notice of eviction. Under the December decree, a peasant could indeed move freely within the territory of the state, but was obliged to leave with his master what was called the załoga — that is, the buildings, tools, livestock, and also the crops sown. The consequence of the application of the new law was the economic dependence of the peasantry on landowners, since the vast majority of rural inhabitants had no possibility of finding employment outside agriculture. The government of the Duchy of Warsaw did not countenance the possibility of carrying out a deep reform of property relations in the countryside. This was justified in two ways. On the one hand, the view was expressed that the Polish lands were considerably backward in economic development relative to the developed countries of Western Europe, and therefore peasant land ownership would not fit the realities of the national economy. On the other hand, it was postulated that the peasants first needed to be educated so that they could understand the significance of the gift that land granted to them as property would be.[^333] The Duchy's authorities recognised the dire state of agriculture, the villages devastated by requisitions and military billeting, the rapidly growing number of abandoned peasant farms. Nevertheless, in an economy subordinated to the demands of wartime, these problems were pushed into the background. It was understood that intensive rural colonisation, modelled on the Prussian example and requiring considerable financial outlay, could not be carried out.[^334] The government therefore, in encouraging resettlement to the Duchy's territory, particularly emphasised the importance of non-financial incentives, such as the free years or the exemption of immigrants from military service. The State Council even postulated applying to new arrivals the same legal regulations that had been in force in Prussian times.[^335] These deliberations bore fruit in the decree of Frederick Augustus of 20 March 1809, which defined the principles of migration and settlement of foreigners in the Duchy of Warsaw.[^336]

Wishing that in our Duchy of Warsaw agriculture, factories, crafts, and every kind of useful industry might be brought to the most exalted state possible, upon the motion of our minister of internal affairs and with the opinion of our State Council, we have decided and do decide as follows: Art. 1. Every craftsman or farmer entering from foreign countries and settling in our Duchy of Warsaw shall be free for 6 years from all public burdens and charges of whatever name; Art. 2. Moreover, for farmers entering the country and settling on national estates on empty lands, exemption for six years from payments and rents for such lands is guaranteed; Art. 3. All foreigners arriving and their children brought into the country shall be free from military conscription; Art. 4. Colonists settled by the previous government shall likewise be free from conscription; Art. 5. Both these colonists and foreigners settled and settling in our Duchy of Warsaw we entrust to the special care of our minister of internal affairs and the departmental prefects, wishing them to facilitate the settlement of these persons in the country by all means and to preserve them in the peaceful enjoyment of the rights and freedoms granted to them.[^337]

The March decree grew from Enlightenment thinking, as well as from the principles of cameralism. The Duchy opened its borders practically to all newcomers, imposing no restrictions. Concerns therefore arose that such a policy constituted a kind of invitation for vagabonds and scoundrels, and above all for poor Jews.[^338] The growing emigration of colonists selling and abandoning their settlements was a cause of anxiety.[^339] The Duchy of Warsaw became a terrain of wandering by the local population, newcomers, and military marches. The lack of political stability and the economy subordinated to war aims were the reason why those most counted upon — useful foreigners — were fewest in number.[^340] Already from the spring of 1808, an intensification of peasant movements was observed in the Duchy, particularly in the Płock and Warsaw departments, involving both colonists and Polish peasants. Taking advantage of the benefits of the December decree, they were abandoning their farms — fallen into decline during wartime — or estates where they were badly treated. This considerable internal migration deprived the owners of numerous estates of part of their labour force, deepening the crisis prevailing in agriculture. Moreover, on the national estates there still existed unsettled territories, prepared even by the Prussian authorities for colonist settlement. There were also many settlements abandoned during the years of war. By extending the provisions of the decree of 20 March 1809 to include rural colonists, a means was sought that would allow these gaps in agricultural production to be filled.[^341]

The March decree gave colonists only legal privileges. Given the budget deficit, it was impossible to provide them with financial support, or support in kind. In this situation, only a small number decided to settle in the Duchy. The directorate of national estates, holding lands suitable for settling newcomers, was aware that their development would not be possible, since potential farmers were arriving without their own capital.[^342] Even in opinions derived directly from the principles of Prussian cameralism, the necessity of immigrants possessing a certain initial capital was emphasised:

Although the increase in the population of our country is very necessary and every newcomer, even a labourer, is useful, nevertheless […] it would be desirable that preference should be given to those who will be able to show some fund for the first establishment of a farm, craft, or factory.[^343]

Concern was also expressed that despite the many empty lots and abandoned farms on national estates, finding lands suitable for immigrants might prove difficult. It was recommended that even in such situations, newcomers should not be deprived of all hope of finding a home and livelihood here.[^344]

There were also other factors that influenced the slight resonance of Frederick Augustus's decree. The political uncertainty and economic crisis were deepened by the Polish-Austrian war, waged from April to October 1809. The content of the decree was disseminated, generally unprofessionally, only within the Duchy of Warsaw's territory. This prompted the minister of internal affairs to make changes in the manner of disseminating the content of the document, which was to be widely publicised. Not insignificant either were the difficulties placed by the Prussian authorities in the way of those wishing to move to the Duchy, by calling them up for military service.[^345] In November 1810, the minister of internal affairs Jan Paweł Łuszczewski proposed once more to announce the decree, simultaneously in Polish and German, to send it to towns and villages, to announce it from pulpits, and to affix the text to the doors of offices, churches, and inns.[^346] A month later he assured the State Council that in the German lands announcements are being made about the possibilities of moving to the Duchy […] those willing are applying to the Saxon envoy in Berlin.[^347]

The minister's optimism was premature, however. No larger group of German peasants appeared at the Duchy of Warsaw's border.

The following years brought the publication of further decrees by Frederick Augustus, which extended and supplemented the regulations in force. By the regulation of 18 January 1810, newcomers were permitted free choice of place of residence.[^348] To counter the rumours that the Duchy's authorities would refuse passports to immigrants who had decided to return to their homeland, a decree was issued on 17 December 1810 ordering the issuance of such travel documents, but only after the settlers had fulfilled their obligations to landowners.[^349] In turn, the regulation of 29 January 1812 guaranteed newcomers exemption from customs duty on imported movable property and livestock. To reduce the journey route and travel time as much as possible, the crossing of the border was permitted both at main customs posts and subsidiary posts, but immigrants were required to present a police certificate that they were not suspected in any matter.[^350] Despite such broad concessions and favourable legal conditions, the influx of settlers was small. It is estimated that during the existence of the Duchy of Warsaw, only 254 settlements were established on national estates.[^351] The settlement on private estates was not captured by any statistics. From the few sources available, one can infer its modest progress and its very traditional form, typical of Olęder settlement.

In February 1808, Józef Nasierowski, hereditary owner of the Kiszewy estate in the Konin district, concluded a contract with Krzysztof Hektus, residing in Holendry Dzierzubińskie, and with Wojciech Weinert, residing in the village of Kiszewy, concerning the purchase and sale of land in the Kępina colony, belonging to the Kiszewy estate.[^352] Both purchasers were Olęders, and the establishment of another such settlement was the lord's aim. He was settling them among the forests — still extensive — since in the contract he obliged Hektus and Weinert to bring in further settlers.[^353] The destination of the timber from the clearings was not specified, beyond the obligation to hand over 4 oaks per łan to the manor. It was also established that each farmer would hold no less than ½ łan and no more than 1 łan. The entry fee was set at 400 thalers, payable in three instalments by St. Adalbert's Day 1809. Failure to meet this deadline entailed a penalty of 100 thalers per łan. Settlers were entitled to 4 free years, after which they were to pay annual rent of 21 thalers per łan. In addition, the lord demanded from each łan 1 Viertel of oats, 1 goose, and 1 hen. The solidarity of the settlers towards the manor was contained in the provision: rent — all for one, one for all stands surety. Krzysztof Hektus, as the future village headman, was exempt from ¼ of the rent. The same concession applied to the teacher (schoolmaster), who, receiving ¼ łan, was also exempt from the entry fee. The manor reserved a monopoly on propination, under penalty of 1 thaler per quart of outside spirits. Construction of a mill was planned, with the help of the colonists, at the edge of the settlement. Its establishment was to take place on separately agreed terms. The manor also held fishing rights on the pond and a monopoly on hunting over all lands. Harming birds and game was threatened with a fine of 2 thalers per head caught. The contract also specified building conditions. Dwellings were to be solid and situated in a single line. In a second straight line, opposite the buildings, barns were planned to be erected. A problem for the settlement was the lack of pasture. The manor permitted livestock to graze in unenclosed forest, that is, in forest outside the young growth areas. It was stipulated, however, that the colonists must always content themselves with whatever pasture the manor would have. The settlers had the right of *gajenie* — that is, collecting firewood — in exchange for payment to the manor. They could collect wood twice a week, in designated places, working in return during the harvest: three days with a sickle, three days with a scythe, three days with rakes, and delivering 1 hen and 15 eggs. If the domain did not provide pasture or did not permit wood-gathering, the settlers were exempt from these labor obligations and dues. Other duties of the Olęders included: care of the stream flowing through the village, payment of all public taxes, and a payment to the Roman Catholic church in Tuliszków in the amount of 2 złoty per year per łan. They were also obliged to attend rites at the church in Tuliszków. It is unknown what denomination Hektus and Weinert belonged to — both of whom were, moreover, illiterate. Perhaps the considerable distance from the nearest Evangelical-Augsburg parish in Władysławów caused them to be assigned to the Tuliszków parish. A farmer from Kępiny could sell his holding by paying a 10% *laudemium* to the manor, which reserved the right of first refusal. When the property was sold to a son or son-in-law, no *laudemium* was payable. Understating the sale price would result in the forfeiture of half the transaction sum to the manor. It may be presumed that other new settlements in the private estates between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula were founded in a similar manner.[^353]

Great hopes were placed in the German peasants appearing on the frontiers of the Duchy from the direction of Silesia, ready to find some land for themselves. This emigration, caused by the beginning of regulatory reforms in Prussia, encompassed the poorest peasants, for whom the agrarian reform offered no benefits and indeed threatened them with complete pauperization. Nevertheless, for the authorities of the Duchy, every pair of hands for agricultural labor seemed valuable. The prevailing view was that every newcomer, even a day laborer, is useful.[^354]

In 1808, the minister of justice proposed facilitating the settlement of such newcomers in the Duchy.

> Farmers are beginning to enter the regions close to Silesia, who, having found a way of life and rights of freedom, wish to settle here and increase that class of people so greatly needed by us. The normal time for announcing the abolition of serfdom in Silesia is approaching; there is thus hope that from there this industrious people, bound to the land by former statutes, seeing here the opportunity through industry and labor to maintain a peaceful and good existence, will move to us in considerable numbers. Surely the administration is endeavoring how to show those moving to the country the protection of the government, the security of their persons and means of living, by exempting them for a time from conscription and ensuring the distribution of empty lands as hereditary holdings. From the side of the justice department, their arrival could be further sweetened by softening for them the provisions which the Code [of Napoleon — ed. K.P.W.] mandates with respect to contracting marriage. To conclude a marriage act, either a birth certificate must be submitted, or a deed of testimony signed by seven witnesses. Those arriving now from Silesia are unable to produce a birth certificate, because the local priests are prohibited under penalty of fortress imprisonment from issuing certificates to Poland. Not one of the locals will stand as one of the seven witnesses, out of fear of the persecutions to which they could thereby be exposed there, and so it happens now that they must either forgo marriage here, or take their future bride back with them to Silesia.[^355]

Despite this well-understood situation, the authorities of the Duchy of Warsaw took no position on the matter. It appears that in this case too, pragmatism and precise financial calculation prevailed. The border departments of the Duchy were better developed and more densely populated. There were almost no wastelands left there.[^356] This confronted potential immigrants with the necessity of undertaking a longer journey, exposing them to greater costs. Financial compensation for the hardships of travel might have been an argument for relocating deeper into the Duchy. No such incentive was offered. Consequently, the influx of German peasants from the direction of Silesia did not develop.

A much greater problem for the government of the Duchy of Warsaw, however, became the mass of settlers from the Prussian era whom the difficult wartime conditions had prevented from establishing themselves.[^357] The majority of settlers had taken possession of their land in the years 1802–1804, and consequently, in accordance with the contracts concluded, their rent-free years began to expire from 1808 onwards. A large portion of the settlements had been established on uncultivated, often wooded, terrain where at least several years of clearing work requiring great effort was necessary. Therefore, many farms were not yet organized and ready for independent existence. The phenomenon of abandoning settlements and emigrating from the Duchy of Warsaw appeared. Frederick Augustus ordered

> our ministers of the Duchy of Warsaw to immediately issue the necessary instructions to the border military and civil authorities, so that no colonist be allowed to pass outside the borders of the Duchy without a passport and permission.[^358]

These special security measures were justified in light of incoming information about the intensified activity of various agents who, exploiting the difficult situation of the colonists, recruited them for resettlement in Russia, promising material support declared by Tsar Alexander I.[^359]

This occurred even though soliciting emigration was punishable by 4–8 years in a fortress.[^360] In February 1810, the prefect of the Bydgoszcz department, Antoni Gliszczyński, informed the minister of the interior that

> many German inhabitants of this country, partly out of hatred for the present government, partly in order to obtain some office or means of living, having abandoned their homes, have departed abroad to Prussia,

asking what should be done with the abandoned dwellings.[^361]

This direction — to Prussia — was rarely chosen as a means of leaving the Duchy. Most often, settlers disappointed in their expectations headed eastward, to Russia. This was not always spontaneous emigration. In October 1808, the prefect of the Kalisz department alerted the minister of the interior that Russian commissioners had appeared in the vicinity of Krzepice, who with various promises were encouraging the colonists settled there to emigrate from the Duchy, and those who decided were sending them on their way across the Austrian border.

> Despite all caution, several families have already fled, since until now no one has applied himself to stopping them.[^362]

Several months later, the mayor of Inowłódz reported on the arrest of Councillor Spiegelberg, who presented an instruction from the Russian minister of the interior, Prince Aleksei Kurakin, authorizing him to conduct people out of the Duchy of Warsaw to Russia. In January 1810, the prefect of the Płock department, Rajmund Rembieliński, described Spiegelberg as

> a secret Russian agent, who roams around the Duchy of Warsaw [...] making very enticing promises to the colonists in the name of the Russian government, and showing them such promises in print, persuading them to emigrate.[^363]

In the same letter, R. Rembieliński informed about a schoolteacher a mile from Rawa who was spreading similar assurances. Though Spiegelberg had not succeeded in persuading anyone in the Płock department to emigrate, in the Warsaw department a dozen or so families were found who abandoned their farms and made their way to Russia. In recruiting the colonists, deceptions and disinformation were also resorted to, which were difficult to detect and explain. In 1809, Melchior Meinard, the village headman of Kolonia Inowłódzka, informed the Russian chargé d'affaires in Vienna that he had the permission of the Polish government

> to make arrangements regarding the removal of colonists from the Duchy of Warsaw to Russia, and already 16 families [55 persons — ed. K.P.W.] have signed.[^364]

At the same time, a general conspiracy of colonists from the Płock department to move to Russia was uncovered. The agents recruiting them were supplying passports issued by the Prussian consulate in Elbląg, for a fee. The practice was stopped by the arrest of the ringleaders.[^365]

> The scarcity of the treasury does not allow providing newcomers with a means of living,

it was warned in 1808 in correspondence between the ministry of the interior and the ministry of justice.[^366] The same applied to settlers already established. The poorest of them began to leave the country, and encouraged by their example, others, in a better situation but feeling a bond with their neighbors and countrymen, began to prepare to depart.[^367] The government's efforts to ease the financial burden on the colonists found expression in the decrees of Frederick Augustus of December 23, 1811 and November 28, 1812. These guaranteed the exemption of settlers from all taxes and dues until the end of 1812.[^368] The government also responded to numerous requests, often directed straight to Frederick Augustus, in which a reduction of rents was requested, justified by the poverty of the settlers.[^369] The primary causes of the settlers' poor material situation were the economic crisis and the presence of foreign troops in the Duchy, but there were also local causes. In 1811, settlers from Tkaczewska Góra (Neu Württemberg) in the Łęczyca district requested an extension of their rent-free years, as they had become greatly impoverished as a result of the war events.

> Our lands give us only a meager bread; we are even compelled to buy bread every year for our sustenance [...] buildings not built, for the war came.

The village headman maintained that if the colonists received no assistance,

> then there is no point in our staying and maintaining ourselves in this country, for we have nothing left to spend.[^370]

In 1814, Ludwig Nofer, on behalf of the colonists from Nowosolna, requested a lightening of the residents' lot through a reduction of the rent from 12 to 2 złoty Polish per morgen.[^371] That same year, it was reported of the settlers of nine colonies in the Łaznów economy that they intended to abandon their settlements and leave the country entirely, unless they were granted reductions in rent.[^372]

These complaints did not always correspond to reality, and the colonists, by insisting on their poverty, sought to elicit sympathy and obtain some additional support. The complaints that did arise had the effect, however, that the government had to meet the colonists' expectations so as not to lose them.[^373] The government's situation worsened further when the colonists turned to demand payment of sums owed to them. Extending the rent-free years and confirming existing privileges might calm numerous settlers and convince them to remain; however, in specific cases these measures were of little effect. As is known, the Prussian government had guaranteed financial assistance to the settlers. In addition to individual payments, expenditures were also planned for the construction of bridges or roads in newly established settlements, for example.[^374] In May 1812, the settlers of Ofterdingen (Michałów), in the Korabiewice estate, demanded

> payment to them of the subsidies which for each of the foreign colonists were designated and paid out by the former Prussian government.

This sum (for clearing and erecting buildings), some of them had not received, and the land had become a donation to General Claude Juste-Alexandre Legrand.[^375] The situation of the colonists from Ofterdingen must indeed have been difficult, for as the village headman testified in the spring shortage of 1812:

> Da nun auf unseren Collonisten Dorf sicher 33 Stück Vieh gefallen ist und eben auch unsere Kinder sehr absterben und dieses alles wegen Noth und Hunger.[^376]

In turn, the settlers from Borów in the Brzeziny district, signing themselves as poor colonists, requested in their letter to KRPiS the payment of the sum of 4,800 thalers granted by the Prussian government for road-making, or the deduction of this sum from the rent owed by them. The preserved documentation allows this matter to be clarified. The cost estimate for work carried out by the farmers amounted to 4,902 thalers. In 1806 they had already received a reimbursement of 3,000 thalers; thus 1,902 thalers remained, which they had been promised would be returned.[^377] The settlers from Zielona Góra (Grünberg) in the Łaznów economy complained about the unfulfilled promises of the Prussian authorities. They had waited in vain for the erection of residential and farm buildings, or a monetary equivalent for the land they had cleared.[^378] Frequently settlers complained of poor soil quality, prolonged drought, or the arbitrary conduct of local officials.[^379] The year 1811 was so unfruitful that the authorities feared whether the peasants would be able to survive. In March 1812, a decree appeared imposing on landowners the obligation to support the peasants living on their estates, but it made no mention of colonists.[^380] To the extent possible, the Duchy's authorities sought to sustain the Swabian settlements established in the final years of Prussian rule and to complete the investments begun in them. This was the case, for example, in the colony of Grünberg (Zielona Góra), which finally took shape only in the years 1809–1810. The year before, the expansion of the borders of the colony of Königsbach (Bukowiec) had been completed.[^381]

The policy of the Duchy of Warsaw's government favorable to the colonists found itself from the outset under pressure from various kinds of expectations on the part of the settlers. Official documents frequently noted that among the colonists there were often *schlechte und liederliche Leute*.[^382] This term described poor and impoverished peasants who owned little land and few livestock, but above all were not good farmers, which was the primary cause of their difficulties.[^383] Without outside help, their maintaining themselves on their farms was practically impossible. The difficult times of the Duchy of Warsaw affected the previously settled Olęders to a much lesser degree. Their farms had been organized earlier, and moreover the Olęders had never made use of state assistance. Only a few abandoned their homesteads, mainly those who had settled at the same time as the Swabian colonists.[^384] Yet even the long-established ones were susceptible to the propaganda calling for resettlement to Russia. The Olęders who had farmed since 1791 in Brużyca Mała on the Zdziechów estate, enjoying the rare freedom of propination, leased the propination for 3 years in 1811 to Tomasz Piasecki, a nobleman. The lessee was obligated not to extend credit greater than 6 złoty to a farmer or 2 złoty to a farm hand, so as to be able to accumulate 300 złoty per year in leasehold rent. Without waiting for the end of the lease — which was, after all, yielding some profit to the 24 Olęders — they sold their farms (the entire settlement) in 1814 to Ignacy Cieśliński, the head lessee of the Zdziechów economy, and moved to Russia.[^385]

Due to a lack of financial resources, the government of the Duchy of Warsaw was unable to meet all the colonists' requests. However, one cannot deny it a determined will to help. Attempts were made to convert settlers' obligations into rent-free years, but no significant success was achieved by this means.[^386] The contracts concluded by the colonists with the Prussian government were taken as the basis for such settlements. If for any reason a settler could not produce any document specifying the terms of land possession, the general principles of Prussian colonization were deemed applicable. In cases where obligations of the former administration had not been fulfilled, the settler's rent-free period was extended as compensation. If, on the other hand, obligations rested upon the colonist, these were spread into installments or written off entirely.[^387] A number of individual requests submitted by settlers were considered with a positive outcome for them.

In 1811, Frederick Augustus:

> graciously permitted the colonists in the two colonies of Lwówek, that is, Leonberg and Piotrkówek, as well as the colonist Hauser in the village of Słupie (Słup), in the Sanniki economy, whose 6 rent-free years had expired at Trinitatis 1809, an extension of two more years until the last day of May 1811, and a two-year exemption from rent, the chimney tax, as well as from requisitions, forage, and the provision of transport [...] due to their impoverished condition.[^388]

In September 1811, the councillor of state and director-general of National Estates and Forests, Marcin Badeni, alerted the minister of the treasury that

> the colonists of all nine settlements in the Łaznów economy, having been overburdened with taxes, have been brought to such a degree of dissatisfaction that they intend to abandon their settlements and leave the country entirely.

He requested that, in order to retain these peasants in the country, an order be issued so that the collectors of both arrears and current taxes would await further decision and refrain from all severity.[^389]

Two years later, Badeni renewed his request, this time specifying that: 1) the fee for use of pasture be reduced; 2) settlers be exempted from forage duties; 3) payment for delivered cattle be deducted from rent payments. Given the poor quality of the settlers' land, he also deemed the rent they paid of 2 złoty per morgen too high. He also asked whether, in view of the wartime conditions prevailing in the Duchy, additional concessions could be granted to the colonists so that they would not abandon their farms. He argued that the departure of the colonists would affect the budget revenues expected in future.[^390] The excessively high rent (usually 2 złoty per morgen) was the most frequent argument in the settlers' requests to the authorities for relief. In April 1814 and again in May 1815, the colonists from Nowosolna addressed a request to KRPiS for a reduction of rent and an end to the imposition of burdens. They considered the rent of 2 złoty per morgen too high.[^391]

Observing various shortcomings and irregularities in the reckoning of rent-free years, the minister of the interior issued in March 1810 a rescript addressed to prefects concerning the preservation and relief of colonists.[^392] In carrying out the recommendations contained therein, it was found, among other things, that in the Brysk economy in the Łęczyca district there lived settlers whose rent-free period had been shortened, and who therefore deserved three years' exemption from all taxes. This applied to 8 colonists from Orszewice, Podgórzyce, and Ambrożew. A similar relief was restored to five settlers from Balkow in the Zgierz district.[^393] In August 1811, settlers on the Zagórów estate, brought from Brandenburg by Prince Hohenlohe von Ingeltingen and living in the villages of Augustynowo, Aleksandrowo, Emitenhein, Sophienthal, Ludwigsthal, Friedrichsfelde, and Klein Engelfinger, complained that they did not have rent-free years. The freedom from public burdens granted to them by the Prussian government should continue to apply to them. They invoked a rescript from the former Berlin Finance Department of July 13, 1802 and December 13, 1803, stating that

> colonists settled on noble land are entitled to no other freedoms than exemption from the chimney tax for 3 years on land given to them without buildings, so that during that time they may be able to build the necessary buildings for themselves.

The complaint was examined, with the conclusion that if the colonists had paid this tax, it should be returned to them.[^394] A great deal of energy in coming to the settlers' aid was shown by Fryderyk Dawid Biederman, the head lessee of the Łaznów economy. In 1813 he interceded on behalf of the colonists from Nowosolna, Wiączyń Górny, and Wiączyń Dolny, from whom payment of the chimney tax had been unjustly demanded (they were enjoying rent-free years). He requested that

> the colonists be freed from the burdens unjustly imposed upon them, for otherwise they will abandon their settlements and go to Russia, which will cause the treasury great loss.[^395]

In cases of arrears in rent payments, the authorities did not hesitate to apply administrative enforcement. The settlers sought to forestall its announcement. In September 1811, the colonists from Andrespol in the Mileszki parish requested of

> the most illustrious minister of the treasury, our lord and benefactor, as our father, exemption from all burdens and protection from the announced enforcement of dues,

arguing that they had paid one installment of rent in the amount of 72 złoty, but could not afford the next ones.[^396]

Appeals were sometimes effective, at least partially, as evidenced by the exemption in July 1810 of settlers in the Gostynin district from the costs of enforcement, which was nevertheless carried out.[^397] Generally, attempts were made to help the settlers, but in a manner that would not expose the treasury to expenditure. This directive was recalled in numerous instructions concerning the leasing of state estates and colonists:

> The government will gladly receive from prefects proposals for assistance, as to how it could be given without ready expenditure from the treasury.[^398]

The military requisitions of 1812–1813 proved very damaging in their consequences. Settlers were stripped of the majority of their draft animals and wagons. This affected most settlements possessing animal-drawn equipment, but the most severely affected were the colonists in the Vistula valley and those living along the road leading from Silesia through Lubochnia and Rawa to Warsaw.[^399] Another cause of losses was pillaging carried out by undisciplined soldiers and stragglers. Such events must have been numerous enough that they were seen as the main cause of destruction in the Duchy of Warsaw.[^400] Little use was made of the proclamation of Alexander I's ukase of February 1, 1814, prohibiting military requisitions and designating march routes for the army.[^401] As requests for compensation for war losses and those resulting from quartering intensified, the Duchy's authorities were compelled to reject requests both for extensions of rent-free years and for any other form of assistance.[^402] It proved impossible to halt the departure from the country of embittered settlers. The government feared that freeing the colonists from their burdens would lead to neglect of farms and cause damage that could not later be repaired.[^403] Hope was held that the colonists would not abandon their farms and that the expected sums would flow into the budget.[^404]

A significant change occurred after the occupation of the Duchy of Warsaw by Russian forces. The period of wartime confusion and the lack of control over the former eastern border of the Duchy meant that movement across the vast territories of the Polish-Russian borderland encountered no formal obstacles. Russia's seizure of Bessarabia in 1812 opened up the possibility of settling its fertile soils. In the Duchy of Warsaw, gripped by wartime unrest, information about the favorable conditions that the Russian authorities were offering to settlers relocating there spread rapidly. In 1813, Tsar Alexander I permitted colonists living in the Duchy to move to Bessarabia, declaring that the costs of this resettlement would be covered. By August 1814, permission to leave the Duchy had been granted to nearly 1,200 families, numbering a total of approximately 6,000 persons. The Russian authorities acknowledged, however, that 400 families had reached their destination.[^405] A problem arose with the refusal to take the oath of allegiance, which the newcomers were unwilling to take in the absence of a clergyman. From the efforts undertaken by the Russian administration, it is apparent that among the Bessarabian settlers there were both Lutherans and Catholics, since clergymen of both these denominations were brought in to administer the oath.[^406] The settlers relocating to Bessarabia from the Duchy of Warsaw represented only a part of a larger movement that encompassed German peasants from Württemberg, Mecklenburg, Pomerania, and Greater Poland, heading eastward in hope of improving their lot. Local Russian officials noted their distinctiveness, calling them "Warsaw colonists," in contrast to "Rhine colonists."[^407] Emigrants from the territories between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula claimed to be abandoning their farms *aus wirtschaftlicher Not und wegen konfessionelen Gegensatzes zu den Polen*.[^408] The preserved source materials do not point to any problems of a religious nature with which the German settlers in the Duchy of Warsaw might have been struggling, and this is rather a later attempt at justifying their economic weakness. Alongside the objective sources of the settlers' difficulties, there were also causes residing in the colonists themselves. Those settled during the Prussian era came mainly from south-western Germany. In the course of only a few years of farming in new places, they had not yet succeeded in fully establishing themselves. Most of the newcomers had brought with them only a small amount of capital, relying on support from the authorities. When state assistance was lacking, they lost hope for a better future. They concluded that without government care they were unable to maintain themselves on their farms.[^409]

## Chapter IV: Continuity and Change in the Settlement Process in the Kingdom of Poland (1815–1866)

The Kingdom of Poland, from the moment of its establishment, had to grapple with poverty and economic crisis — a legacy of the era of the Duchy of Warsaw. In 1818, the Council of State estimated population losses at 634,810 persons.[^410] This represented a fall in population of approximately 20%. Military quartering and requisitions had brought many villages to ruin. Significant areas of land lay fallow, draft animals and seed were lacking. The collapse of agricultural economy was so deep that in the national estates it was difficult to find lessees. For a number of successive years, agriculture remained in a deplorable state.[^411] Modernization and improvement of farming depended above all on the landowners, but they too felt the effects of the crisis. Seeking a solution through peasant reform encountered the mental resistance of the overwhelming majority of the nobility. The average indebtedness of landed estates reached 62% of their value; every fourth estate was on the brink of bankruptcy or was indeed going bankrupt.[^412] The crisis in agriculture was further deepened by the introduction in the years 1815–1819 of protective tariffs on imported grain by the governments of Prussia, France, and Great Britain.[^413]

Pressing the program of rapid industrialization of the country, the government of the Kingdom of Poland recognized the dependence between urban population growth and increased demand for agricultural products, which seemed to bode well for a cohesive and harmonious economic policy. However, while the industrialization of the country was a work created almost from scratch, with necessary legal regulations in keeping with the spirit of the times, agrarian matters required the carrying out of a series of changes that went beyond the letter of the December decree — for which neither government circles nor landowners were prepared. Industrialization was not only a necessary condition for modernization, but also the only solution offering a chance for a relatively rapid balancing of the budget, and thereby the preservation of the political autonomy of the Kingdom of Poland. Alexander I made the continued existence of the Kingdom in its constitutional form of 1815 conditional on achieving a positive balance of payments. When approving the budget for 1819, he ordered that state estates be leased out in perpetual tenure to cover the deficit, which, however, was not put into effect.[^414] Turning to foreign craftsmen, the government of the Kingdom chose a method that was well-known and had been successfully implemented many times in various European countries initiating new branches of manufacturing. In the early nineteenth century, the crisis of overproduction struck the textile centers in the west of the continent, dominated by cheap English machine production, and forced impoverished craftsmen to follow the traditional market — eastward. The protective policy of the government of the Kingdom of Poland resulted, among other things, in the creation of favorable conditions for industrial settlement, and consequently in the development of the textile industry.[^415] In the area of agricultural economy, the government of the Kingdom of Poland was in a considerably more difficult position. It possessed national estates whose reform the Prussian authorities had attempted to carry out in the years 1793–1806, but the results of this work were modest. The land in many estates and key holdings did not even have reliable surveying, to say nothing of income assessments. Attempts were therefore made to carry out limited reforms in the national estates, including through the regulation of peasant lands and the conversion of corvée into rents, but this project was not consistently implemented.[^416] As early as 1816, the viceroy of the Kingdom of Poland, General Józef Zajączek, authorized KRPiS to settle colonists arriving from abroad in farms abandoned by peasants, in the event that there were no empty rent-holding settlements for the newcomers.[^417] The lack of close supervision caused this provision to begin to be exploited by already-settled colonists seeking better farming locations. Therefore, in May 1817, a further resolution was issued specifying the conditions under which only colonists arriving directly from abroad could receive land allotments on the right of perpetual lease.[^418] From the parliamentary podium, the necessity of bringing in foreign colonists was continuously proclaimed — colonists who, thanks to favorable legal conditions and government assistance in finding a suitable place to settle, would remain in the country, contribute to its development, and increase the population.[^419] These far-reaching expectations of the newcomers were formulated without regard to the often negative experiences of previous governments. The opinions of Fryderyk Skarbek, as well as of August Carl von Holsche, were known — both of whom did not conceal their criticism of previously arrived colonists. Most were bad, impoverished farmers, unfamiliar with the northern climate and the existing agricultural culture, who

> scheuten schwere Arbeit und trieben Gartenbau, wovon sie mit den Ihrigen nicht Leben konnten.[^420]

A large portion of them became beggars and a burden on society. Some returned to their homelands. Skarbek saw in all the colonists nothing but impoverished peasants whom poverty had driven from their homeland, and who were ready to wander and settle anywhere. After the expiry of their rent-free years they abandoned their settlements and moved wherever similar benefits were on offer.[^421] F. Skarbek's opinion was formulated on the basis of numerous reports from local authorities, alarming KRSW

> about the pressing into the country of idle people, having no certain means of living, and frequently of suspicious character.[^422]

For this situation, blame was partly placed on customs officials who did not know foreign languages, as well as on commune headmen who affixed their seals to often falsified documents, thereby legalizing them.

The deepening property differentiation of the peasantry in the German lands at the end of the eighteenth century led to the rapid growth of the group of the poor and landless. Baden and Württemberg were the countries with the highest population density in the Reich. This gave the sons of peasants no hope of feeding their families. Poverty was further deepened by the fragmentation of land. Dissatisfaction was aroused by the absolutist rule of King Frederick I, who as a member of the Rhine Confederation was obligated to supply recruits under French command. The situation was worsened by the crop failures of 1809, 1810, 1812–1813, and 1816.[^423] These were sufficient incentives to make the decision to emigrate. The *Schwabenzug* — as the emigration from Württemberg in the early nineteenth century used to be called — began.[^424] In Prussia, in turn, the reforms of Karl von Hardenberg confronted the poorest part of the peasantry with the alternative of seeking work with the owners or lessees of large estates, or seeking occupation outside the village. These circumstances, alongside growing tax burdens, the arbitrary conduct of officials, and rigorous military conscription, became a powerful impulse toward emigration. All the more willingly, therefore, were people receptive to signals coming from the Kingdom of Poland; propaganda developed extolling the charms of Polish lands. Once again the already well-known *Song of the Württemberg Emigrants* (Annex XI) became popular. The majority of those willing to migrate were poor peasants working as day laborers (*Tagelöhner*) and craftsmen. The former, in deciding to emigrate, counted above all on the possibility of acquiring land on favorable terms. The Government of the Kingdom of Poland did not have sufficient understanding of the economic and social conditions prevailing in the states of the Reich, and counted primarily on attracting settlers wealthy enough in capital to be able to begin farming without government assistance.

[^423] [^424]

The authorities of the Kingdom initially did not perceive the scale of problems associated with the influx into the country of useful foreigners — both manufacturers and craftsmen, as well as farmers. The conditions of settlement, defined in the decree of the governor-general, Józef Zajączek, of 18 March 1816, corresponded almost verbatim to the immigration decrees of Frederick Augustus of 1809 and 1812.[^425] This decree, known as the patent of freedom, created the legal framework — in force for nearly two decades — governing policy toward foreigners arriving in the Kingdom, including rural settlers. In a supplementary decree of 10 August 1816, the Administrative Council restricted the freedom of settlement in the Kingdom of Poland to only those newcomers who brought their own property or possessed certain technical skills. Agricultural colonists were required to present themselves in Warsaw, at the office of the Commission of Revenue and Treasury (*KRPiS*), where decisions about the place of settlement were made. Newcomers possessing 400 or more Polish złoty were assigned to the first class. They were entitled to a land allotment of 15 to 30 *morgen*. Second-class settlers, who could demonstrate assets of less than 400 złp, received fewer than 15 *morgen*. The third class consisted of immigrants without capital. They could be employed by lessees of national estates as day laborers.[^426] Newcomers who did not accept these conditions were deprived of state protection. In this way the Government of the Kingdom of Poland attempted to avoid the mistakes made by the authorities of the Duchy of Warsaw. This intention becomes even more obvious when one takes into account that colonists settled on national estates were obliged to pay rent from the very first year after settlement, without benefiting from rent-free years.[^427] At the same time, the KRPiS recommended the immediate compilation of inventories of vacant lands and buildings on national estates, so that they would be ready for the settlement of colonists. A common practice became the promotion of settlement principles in the Kingdom of Poland through printed announcements (*Flugblätter*) and newspaper advertisements. Already on 3 May 1817, the *Berliner Zeitung* published information about the rules governing the resettlement of German peasants and craftsmen to the Kingdom of Poland, and on 21 June 1817, also a Berlin — but supra-regional — paper, the *Voßische Zeitung*, carried this information on its front page.[^428] The results were not long in coming. Already in 1817, the first arrivals from Württemberg settled in government estates. In the years 1816–1817 alone, 785 families totaling 3,335 persons arrived in the Kingdom of Poland (mainly from the southwestern German lands). Of these, 3,041 persons settled on national estates.[^429] Among them were many poor and destitute newcomers.

The freedoms guaranteed to foreign colonists were intended to settle the vacant land and to bring new grounds in the government estates into cultivation; however, when their excessive influx, their poverty, and later disorder and trafficking in holdings disappointed the hopes placed in them, their access was further restricted, and supervision over those already settled was entrusted to head-lessees, while the benefit of new settlements was extended to domestic [subjects].[^430]

The provincial commissions informed the head-lessees of the stratagems employed by colonists:

> Some, under the pretext of poor health or inability to manage the settlement, declare that they intend to vacate it, and immediately indicate another person in their place — most often a foreigner who has long been residing in this country — who, having sold his own settlement, willingly acquires the vacated one under the same right of perpetual lease, from which he may enjoy the years of freedom, and later intends to vacate it by the same means […].

At the same time, the head-lessees were instructed not to lose sight of the moral and economic conduct of the colonists.[^431] It was then planned to distribute land for perpetual rent-holding to the most industrious peasants, but this intention was not realized.[^432] Meanwhile, work continued on a new organization for the administration of government estates, overseen by the Commission for Organizing Government Estates and Forests, established in April 1817 and operating within the structure of the KRPiS.[^433] The new surveys and valuations of estates carried out on this occasion revealed in the autumn of 1819 that almost all vacant settlements on government estates had already been occupied; consequently, settlers arriving in the Kingdom would be placed on private estates, as had already happened with the 58 families who had arrived in the first half of 1819.[^434]

In March 1819, it was decided to tighten the conditions for settlement in the Kingdom. From that point on, those wishing to move to the Kingdom of Poland had to present themselves to Russian envoys or agents in their countries of residence and obtain confirmation of their wealth or document their craft proficiency. These documents were forwarded to the KRPiS, which made the final decision. In this way, an attempt was made to limit the influx to only wealthy and credentialed colonists. The reason for this tightening was the unexpectedly large influx of immigrants and their impoverished condition.[^435] The new regulation quickly produced results. Of the 230 families who applied in 1819, 100 were refused permission to enter the Kingdom, as they did not possess sufficient means.[^436] In subsequent years the influx of settlers into government estates diminished sharply. In the period 1820–1823, 40 families arrived; thereafter the influx ceased entirely. Only in 1830 was the arrival of 31 families from abroad recorded.[^437] From mid-1819 the government disposed only of wasteland, because as a result of the ongoing regulation of national estates, numerous parcels of land had been allotted to peasants.[^438] When suitable colonists were once again sought, it turned out that there were no suitable candidates. It was then decided to extend the privileges guaranteed to colonists to include foreigners already settled on the land previously (local foreigners), i.e., the *Olędrzy*.[^439] This was a significant change compared to the settlement policy pursued by the Prussian authorities in the years 1793–1806 and by the government of the Duchy of Warsaw. At the same time, however, the *Olędrzy* were forbidden from leaving their farms before the expiry of the rent-free years. The *Olędrzy* were accused of buying forested fields, and after felling the trees and selling the timber, moving to other places, interested only in immediate profit rather than in farming the land.[^440] The lessees of national estates were made responsible for the re-settlement of vacated lands. This was a task difficult to accomplish, since after 1823 the influx of settlers to these estates had ceased entirely. The Government of the Kingdom of Poland did not create new settlements but rather populated abandoned farmsteads on government estates. In this method, called the colonial system and consisting of distributing plots of land distant from one another, the Commission of Internal Affairs and Police (*KRSW*) saw more drawbacks than advantages.[^441] It was acknowledged that in such a system a farmer must concentrate more on his own holding; however, peasant families living at a considerable distance from one another were threatened with losing contact with each other and falling out of the social life of the rural community.[^442] This manner of settling the estates resulted, however, from necessity. The government did not have at its disposal compact tracts of completely vacant land, while abandoned farmsteads — as well as wastelands on estates with an already existing structure of manorial farming — were very numerous. And only these free lands could be settled.

Tracking the official correspondence concerning the management of government estates in the 1820s–1840s, one gains the impression that the extent to which the lands within them were being utilized was not well understood. In 1826, the KRPiS issued a regulation ordering the compilation of lists of vacant rural settlements on government estates. Three years later it was rescinded, because the lessees of these estates were obliged to fill vacant holdings with capable farmers. At the same time it was noted that

> the extent of these vacant settlements cannot be determined with certainty, for the endowment of each settlement on the respective estates depends on the quality of the soil.

It was recommended that the largest settlements should not exceed 1 *łan* (30 *morgen*) in area, while the smallest were to comprise between 8 and 10 *morgen*.[^443] The vacant lands on government estates were, however, poorly suited for cultivation. They often consisted of narrow strips of land intermingled with those of other peasants and scattered across the entire area of the estate. There was no possibility of consolidating them into a single farm without reorganizing the entire acreage. Yet even such lands were scarce.[^444] The KRPiS predicted that

> when the plan to convert peasant dues to rent begins to develop, it should be expected that there will then be an opportunity to distribute spacious and well-appointed colonies on manor lands, which for lack of farm buildings will have to be converted into settlements.[^445]

Anticipating such a course of events, the viceroy of the Kingdom, Ivan Paskevich, stipulated the necessity of reserving

> a certain amount of land for foreigners who might wish to resettle in this country at a later time.[^446]

No decisive steps were taken, however, to convert peasant dues to rent, and the regulation of lands on the estates was not completed. Settlers arriving there with increasing frequency were disappointed by the conditions they found at the proposed places of residence. The result was an almost continuous movement of colonists in search of better living conditions. This peculiar drive to wander, characteristic of almost all colonists in the Kingdom of Poland up to the mid-1830s, frequently led beyond its borders, which encountered no formal obstacles on the part of the Kingdom's government. The animated movement of colonists was regarded by the authorities of the Kingdom of Poland as an unfavorable phenomenon. One may cite the opinion formulated in the KRPiS in 1828:

> The German colonists brought to the Polish country were intended only to complete those villages of the government, so that they would occupy the settlements abandoned due to the ravages of war, replace the population lost from the country, and through their industry and more orderly and better-adapted farming become a model for the country's inhabitants; this goal, however, was achieved only to a small degree, for those who arrived for the most part did not wish to live alongside Polish peasants, and therefore it was necessary to designate separate settlements for them and create colonies, providing them with assistance in timber. A significant portion of them moved from place to place and eventually emigrated from the country, and there were very many who, with little capital and without a will to work, turned into vagabonds. In general, throughout the entire course of the German colonization, it was observed that they imagined themselves to be coming to populate a wilderness, and thus were doing the country a favor by settling there, and had the right to demand everything from it. Occupied therefore by a single aim — that is, self-interest — regarding everyone outside the measure of their confession and origin as strangers, they left their own country but did not adapt to any [other].[^447]

The legacy inherited by the Government of the Kingdom of Poland from the Duchy of Warsaw included also a number of problems rooted in the time of Prussian occupation and left unsolved by the administration of the Duchy. The grievances of settlers from colonies founded by the Prussians, complaining about the non-fulfillment of promised investments, were still very much alive. Initially the settlers feared that the authorities of the Kingdom would leave them to their own devices. However, when it became apparent that letters directed to the KRPiS and KRSW did not go unanswered, hopes revived for a favorable consideration of the petitions and complaints sent from the colonies. In 1823, the inhabitants of Zielona Góra (Grünberg) in the Łaznów estate complained that the Prussian government had promised to build them dwelling houses and farm buildings, to clear the land for settlement, or to pay 1,000 thalers if they cleared the forest themselves. These promises the Prussian administration had not kept — or had not managed to fulfill — as a result of which the colony's inhabitants were forced into financial exertion and labor exceeding their capabilities. They complained about the poorly fertile, sandy soil, the lack of conditions for keeping horses, and furthermore that the cattle they had possessed had been lost during military marches. They requested the KRPiS to release them from the burdens and taxes imposed upon them.[^448] Settlers from Stara Iwiczna felt equally wronged for the same reason, as their headman, Jakob Schierle, reported in 1825.[^449] Perhaps the reaction of the Kingdom's authorities did not meet the settlers' expectations, since before 1830 many of them, living in Stara Iwiczna and the surrounding area, had emigrated to Russia.[^450] At least a dozen or more farmers also left the sub-Łódź colonies of Augustów, Bukowiec, and Nowosolna. From the last of these villages, in the years 1801–1838, a dozen or more farmers emigrated to Russia, and in the six-year period 1838–1844 several more departed to other places through the sale of their settlements to Catholics arriving from Rhenish Bavaria.[^451]

Nowosolna thus did not lose its economic stability; however, the local Evangelical-Augsburg parish was weakened through lower revenues from contributions and *iura stolae* fees. The high mobility of German peasants in the territories between the Prosna, the Pilica, and the Vistula is confirmed by notarial contracts of sale and purchase of land. Of the 13 buyers of settlements in Wiączyń near Łódź in the years 1808–1818, not one was still living there in 1846.[^452] Similarly in nearby Łaznowska Wola, where in 1846 none of those who had purchased land there in the years 1808–1818 remained. Only 14 colonists had been settled in Łaznowska Wola since its founding in 1800.[^453] The main reason for such great mobility among German peasants was the search for better conditions, by which was meant both more fertile soil and a larger acreage. Such an explicit declaration was made by three settlers who in 1824 set out from their farms between Kutno and Gostynin to find a suitable place for themselves in the colony of Tiefenbach (Nowe Boryszewo) in Płock County.[^454]

The attitude of the Kingdom's authorities toward the problem of the outflow of settlers to the east was very telling. The formulations used in private correspondence by the Viceroy of the Kingdom of Poland, General Józef Zajączek, shed interesting light on the broader context of the migrations taking place in the Polish territories. In a letter to Józef Radoszewski, president of the Commission of Kalisz Province and privately the husband of his niece, J. Zajączek addressed the concern described to him about the unrest prevailing in 1816 among colonists settled in Kalisz Province:

> Such significant emigration as you report to me is certainly worthy of reflection. Yesterday I spoke with the minister of internal affairs [T. Mostowski — note K. P. W.] on this matter. The opinion is that we are not permitted to detain either a foreigner or a native against his own will in the country; but if colonists wishing to emigrate have not fulfilled the contracts under which they have been sitting and making use of the land, then one may refuse them a passport and hold them until they have fulfilled the obligations they have undertaken: among these [obligations] there shall be the one that a person abandoning land and a homestead does not have the freedom to leave until he has found another peasant to take his place; this question will occupy the [Administrative] Council on Saturday next. What is strange in this matter is that the Russian ministry made an appeal to His Excellency Novosiltsov that he should not at this time send any colonists to Russia, while here there are those who mislead colonists settled with us and incite them to cross over to Russia, where they will be given nothing either for the journey or as an allowance, as the minister explicitly states in his letter to Novosiltsov. It seems to me as well that there was nothing wrong in your having arrested the three ringleaders of this agitation among the colonists, once you had handed them over to the court […].[^455]

That this was not an isolated episode is confirmed by the correspondence of provincial authorities from the years 1819–1820. In October 1819, the Commission of Masovian Province reported to the KRSW that in the commune of Stok, a certain Stendzko was operating who, having illegally obtained a passport himself and serving as a guide, had helped several colonist families flee to Russia.[^456]

Against the backdrop of the government's efforts to create a legal framework for the swelling wave of settlers heading toward the territories of the Kingdom of Poland, and to develop mechanisms facilitating their adaptation in their new places of settlement, a behind-the-scenes rivalry was ongoing with similar efforts of the Russian administration. In these maneuvers, a significant role was played by the Secret Councillor Nikolai Nikolaevich Novosiltsov. The correspondence he conducted, among others with Alexei Andreyevich Arakcheev — one of the closest associates of Tsar Alexander I and formally head of the military affairs department in the State Council — reveals the tortuous meanders of Russian policy on the question of receiving foreign subjects. The appearance in Russia in 1819 of a group of 19 settler families from Württemberg, holding passports of the Kingdom of Poland, aroused suspicion that the Polish government was pursuing some broad-based immigration policy, including on behalf of Russia but without its knowledge. In response to the insinuations coming from St. Petersburg, Novosiltsov reacted firmly, at the same time invoking the Tsar's protection over the mission he was carrying out:

> Respect for the holy will of our monarch, esteem for the government of the empire, and the unwavering reason marking all the activity of the government here do not allow even the supposition that it could make declarations on behalf of Russia; not to mention that from the position I occupy here, signifying a certain degree of authority delegated to me by His Imperial Majesty, there follows the necessity of showing enough wisdom to prevent such declarations, if necessity required it. […] The Government of the Kingdom of Poland not only made no declarations concerning the resettlement of colonists to Russia, but did not even think of it at all.[^457]

At the same time, on the same matter but bypassing Novosiltsov, the little-known Warsaw informant Osip Kozaravsky was in contact with A. A. Arakcheev, writing among other things:

> […] the position of the Committee of Ministers […] the substance of which is presented verbatim in my note, is intended in my view solely for the attention of the Secret Councillor Novosiltsov, and not for formal submission to the Government of the Kingdom of Poland.[^458]

Highly eloquent was the report of Nikolai N. Novosiltsov of 17 July 1819, addressed to Alexander I:

> Since the proclamations inviting foreign colonists to resettle to the southern regions of the Russian Empire, entire groups or individual families have been passing through Warsaw, choosing their destination independently or receiving it from the Russian mission. In this way Warsaw became a gathering point for colonists. The local government, occupied with issuing passports to those who had received them only to Warsaw, turned to me, and I consulted with the minister of internal affairs. On this basis, at the beginning of 1818, several families of German colonists crossed into southern Russia. After the establishment of the main office for colonist affairs in southern Russia, approved by His Imperial Majesty on 20 March 1818, the minister of internal affairs, the chief curator of the committee for colonist affairs, was instructed to outline more convenient principles for settling colonists and to publish them, of which the foreign missions and consuls were notified. However, numerous colonist families had already received passports and, having departed from their homes, did not cease arriving in Warsaw. [The newcomers] requested passports and caused me a great deal of trouble. Although they all wished to resettle at their own expense, although many of them did not ask for land, intending to settle with their compatriots who possessed an excessive amount of land, while others demonstrated quite considerable capital — notwithstanding all this, they were not permitted to enter Russia, and this prohibition was confirmed by a resolution of the Committee of Ministers of 19 April of this year [1819]. In such a situation I endeavored to take care of these unfortunate people who had abandoned their ancestral home and sold their property, settling them in the Kingdom of Poland in agreement with the local government. This was possible as long as individual families or a small number of them were coming. Now 92 families of Württemberg colonists have arrived; having been referred to me, they have placed me in genuine difficulty. Due to their numbers I have no means of settling them in the Kingdom of Poland. On the other hand, the elders of this group claim that they had the honor of presenting themselves to His Imperial Majesty in Aachen and received the most gracious protection, in proof of which they presented an invitation received from Potemkin, the plenipotentiary of His Imperial Majesty at the Stuttgart court, to appear in Stuttgart to have the conditions explained to them under which they would be permitted to resettle to Russia following their most humble petition. In response to questions put to them here, they explained that they intend to resettle at their own expense to Georgia, and that they belong to that sect of Separatists, part of which has already settled there. Not knowing how to act in this situation, I have taken the liberty of referring it to His Imperial Majesty. His Highness the Tsesarevich [Grand Duke Constantine], who took an interest in the migrants and visited them encamped in a field outside Warsaw, permitted it to be communicated to me that, taking into account all the above-mentioned circumstances, their crossing to Georgia may be permitted, all the more so as the committee for colonist affairs established in Georgia under the command of Infantry General Yermolov has already commenced its operations. Informing His Imperial Majesty of this most humbly, I take the liberty of asking how to proceed in similar cases, which are to be expected, judging by various reports appearing in foreign newspapers. It will not be difficult for His Imperial Majesty to instruct the ministers and plenipotentiaries at foreign courts not to issue passports to colonists wishing to resettle to Russia and the Kingdom of Poland without the issuing [of a permitting order].[^459]

It is worth noting that almost throughout the entire nineteenth century, rumors periodically circulated in the Kingdom of Poland about the possibility of favorable settlement for farmers in the territory of the Russian Empire. Some of these had their source in reports of colonization actions actually undertaken by the Russian administration, usually carried out on the empire's borderlands, while some were unfounded inventions. In 1848 considerable unrest was caused among colonists settled in Warsaw Province by the news of a possibility of resettlement to Russia in connection with the depopulation of large areas due to the cholera epidemic. This information was reportedly disseminated from the pulpits of Evangelical churches. Each colonist ready to emigrate was to receive 30 kopecks per day for the journey and a cart, and at the destination a farm with livestock and equipment. Declarations of those willing were collected, among other places, in the marketplaces of Łęczyca, Kutno, and Gostynin. Twenty-five colonist families from the villages of Borowo and Wiklice in Rawa County declared their readiness to depart. The source of the rumor was soon revealed. It was a misunderstanding of news conveyed by pastors about the introduction in the Kingdom of Poland of the Code of Major and Corrective Punishments, which, based on the principle of deterrence, provided as one of the penalties the sending of convicts to more distant provinces of the Empire.[^460] When a similar agitation among colonists recurred in 1861, the KRSW informed the secretary of state at the Administrative Council that

> it is not the first time that a collective demand has arisen from local inhabitants for resettlement with the granting of privileges to the Empire.[^461]

Previous emigrations had taken place in the years 1813–1816 (to Bessarabia), in 1831 and 1832 to the Great Russian and New Russian provinces, and in 1839 to Georgia and the Transcaucasian Region. It was explained that

> the local authorities, in each of the above instances, took a generally passive role — confining themselves only to executing the supreme orders that had been issued upon the representations of the Imperial government, having the economic interests of the state as their aim.[^462]

Meanwhile, in the Kingdom of Poland, German settlement was also developing around the industrial towns of the forming Łódź district. In 1829, Dionizy and Joanna Biernawski, owners of the Maszkowice estate in Zgierz County, founded the colony of Helenów, settling 24 farmers in it. This group, alongside German Evangelicals, also included 7 Poles. Among the land buyers was also a woman, Anna Rewerowa, a widow, residing in Ozorków. The newcomers were settled on small plots of 2–3 *morgen*, from which they were to pay an annual rent of 24–36 złoty respectively. Threefold failure to pay would result in eviction from the land. The headman was endowed with an additional *morgen* of land free from rent payment, while the schoolteacher was assigned 1 *morgen* of land and 72 square *pręt* of meadow. The lord also granted land for an Evangelical cemetery, but its area and location were not specified. Within the boundaries of the village, a free pasture was designated for working oxen; in addition, the settlers had the right to graze one cow per *morgen*. The colonists could also dig clay for building chimneys. The harvesting of timber and the collecting of dry wood were prohibited, while the raking of cones and needles for use as animal bedding was permitted. A rarely encountered restriction was the requirement to obtain the manor's permission for baking bread and selling provisions.[^463] The landowner probably wished in this way to reserve the profits from the sale of his own foodstuffs on the rapidly developing markets of the industrial towns: Ozorków, Zgierz, and Łódź. In 1835, Mikołaj Krzywiec Okołowicz, who already had experience in settling craftsmen-manufacturers in the factory settlement of Konstantynów which he had founded, granted four colonists 5 *łan* of land on perpetual lease within the territory of Rszewo, called Dąbrowa.[^464] The settlers were obliged to pay an entry fee of 1,140 złp per *łan*, corresponding to 25 years of rent, of which they paid half at the time of concluding the contract, and the remainder after two years. They enjoyed three rent-free years, after which they were to pay an annual rent of 45 złoty per *łan*. No labor obligations or any other dues were required of them. The right of propination belonged to the lord. They could sell their settlement with his permission and upon payment of 10% laudemium. On similar terms, in the years 1819 and 1827, Feliks Skórkowski, owner of the Wielka Wola estate in Opoczno County, settled Evangelical farmers, founding two settlements: Feliksów and Joanów.[^465] In addition to the rent, colonists farming on 15-*morgen* allotments were obliged to provide 6 days of foot labor annually and dues consisting of 1 capon and 15 eggs. In the 1840s it was written of the farmers of both villages that

> at all times, when they wish, they have the opportunity of earning wages at the lord's estate and in the nearby vicinity, and they avail themselves of it.[^466]

The most interesting initiative of this kind was the founding on government lands between Pabianice and Łódź of the settlement of linen spinners — Ksawerów. Laid out in 1825, it was settled by German-speaking newcomers from the Bohemian Sudetes. In 1829 it numbered 81 farmers, benefiting as craftsmen from an allowance paid from the manufacturing fund.[^467] The five-*morgen* plots were to be used for the cultivation of flax. The soil of the settlement proved, however, unsuitable for this crop, and its area too small to ensure the livelihood of the settlers' families. The colonists were poor. Before 1830,

> they would go to nearby towns for piecework, spinning for more significant manufacturers.[^468]

After 1832 the settlement declined.

The period of the November Uprising and the Polish-Russian War brought German peasants in the Kingdom uncertainty about their future fate. Although military operations on the left bank of the Vistula lasted briefly, for many villages in the counties between Warsaw and the Prussian border, the military marches meant requisitions and losses.[^469] Many colonists decided to leave their settlements:

> In the Kujawy district there is a great desertion to Prussia, especially of colonists; to remedy this, not only has the battalion garrison been transferred to Włocławek, but additionally proposals have been made for the assignment of a larger number of officers,
