wrote in a report of 16 March 1831 to the National Government the president of the Commission of Masovian Province, Rajmund Rembieliński.[^470] In areas more distant from the theater of military operations, the events aroused a more lively response only among German journeymen in the factory settlements of the Łódź district.[^471] It did happen, however, that German peasants exploited political uncertainty to seek some form of support that would compensate them for failures in farming. In 1834, the chief of Kalisz County received a petition from settlers of Sobiesęki:

> During the revolution the colony was ruined to the point of utter destitution by various impositions, dues, and unprecedented requisitions.[^472]

The farmers also complained of destruction in the forests, where there was no construction timber. In 1838 another group also set off for Russia: Mennonite peasants from Gostynin District, as well as Württemberg colonists from Stara Iwiczna and Józefosław.[^473]

In January 1833, the Viceroy of the Kingdom of Poland issued a bilingual, Polish-German proclamation addressed to those intending to move within the borders of the Russian Empire. Agricultural settlers were indicated as their potential place of settlement Bessarabia, though formally the Russian authorities had already concluded its colonization. Lands were to be found there for 180 families. At the same time, a warning was given that emigrants would receive no support from the treasury, and therefore those setting out on the journey must have their own financial resources.[^474] Local conditions were extolled, with mention made of good water occurring at shallow depth, and soil excellent for cultivation and haymaking.[^475] Half a year later, the KRSW announced the principles for applying for passports by foreigners residing in the Kingdom of Poland who wished to leave its borders. Confirmation of the settlement of all obligations, both public and private, was required.[^476] Given the numerous irregularities of a formal nature committed during the procedure for issuing certificates to emigrants, the KRSW in July 1833 issued a detailed regulation governing in minute detail matters related to the issuance of passports. It was stipulated that an official guilty of dilatoriness in preparing the necessary documents

> shall pay the emigrant one złoty per day for each day needlessly lost, calculated per head.[^477]

The provincial official gazettes reported on the then-considerable emigration of agricultural colonists to Russia.[^478]

The political changes and the curtailment of the Kingdom of Poland's autonomy after 1832 brought modifications in settlement policy. Above all, Russian diplomats in the German states now quite openly conducted recruitment of colonists without distinction as to whether they were to move to the Kingdom of Poland or to Russia. The stimulus for the authorities in Warsaw to engage more actively with the problem of attracting settlers to the country was the activity of the Saxon envoy to Russia, Lembke, who informed the Russian foreign minister, Karl Robert Nesselrode, of the readiness of many Saxon subjects to resettle in the territory of the Russian Empire.[^479] Almost immediately K. Nesselrode informed I. Paskevich of this. Shortly thereafter, Saxon diplomats sought from him the possibility of the resettlement of Saxon subjects to the Russian Empire, asking at the same time

> whether resettlement to the Kingdom of Poland can be permitted to them and on what terms.

In response, reference was made to the decrees of the Viceroy of the Kingdom of Poland of 2 March 1816 and 3 May 1817. The second of these guaranteed those settling on uncultivated and unbuilt land 12 rent-free years on condition that a settler wishing to take 4 Magdeburg *morgen* of land should possess at least 600 Rhenish złoty in assets, and from ½ to 3 *łan* — at least 1,500 Rhenish [złoty].[^480] Settlers possessing a minimum of 100 Rhenish złoty could take occupancy settlements with land from 2 to 3 *morgen*. The first post-November Uprising announcement about the acceptance of foreign colonists to the Kingdom of Poland appeared in the *Stuttgarter Zeitung*, no. 121 of 28 April 1832, as a result of which 4 families arrived from Württemberg — but without funds.[^481] Two weeks later, on 13 May 1832, the same — and most widely read in Württemberg — newspaper published the 1817 decree on the conditions of settlement in the Kingdom of Poland. The old text was supplemented with information that potential settlers were obliged to leave the declared cash in the Russian legation. They would receive it back upon arrival in Poland. At the same time it was recalled that those wishing to resettle who possessed less than 400 Rhenish złoty would not receive a farm. A single male colonist deposited a sum of 200 Rhenish złoty.[^482] The announcement did not produce a wider response. Only 4 families in Württemberg expressed interest in settling in the Kingdom.[^483] This weak reaction was presumably connected with the uncertain political situation in the Kingdom of Poland following the November Uprising. A subsequent announcement published in 1832 and repeated the following year in several German newspapers had a better result, as 86 families came forward willing to leaving Württemberg and Baden.[^484] According to official data, in 1834 a total of 350 families declared their readiness to settle in Poland, yet only 67 decided to emigrate.[^485] Discrepancies also emerged between the emigrants' declared wealth and their actual financial means. Those wishing to settle in the Kingdom deposited the sum of 70,029 złp in the bank of Stahl et Federer, of which only 29,046 zł was paid out in Warsaw — less than half.[^486] The KRPiS did not conceal its disappointment with the results of the press campaign. Assessing the potential gains from securing such farmers for the country, it was sceptically noted that one cannot attach great importance to their resettlement in any respect.[^487]

Notwithstanding this scepticism, drawing on the experience of the years 1816–1819, the local authorities were ordered to settle newcomers directed to the various provinces as quickly as possible, and to remove all obstacles so that they would have no cause for complaint or dissatisfaction. The district commissioners received instructions from the KRSW to extend broad care and provide every assistance to colonists travelling from beyond the western border of the Kingdom to Warsaw or making their way to already designated settlement sites on government estates. All information they required was to be provided immediately and with full accuracy. Any failures in this regard on the part of local administrative officials were made subject to professional liability.[^488] This concern for new arrivals was not, however, matched by efforts to calm the mood and build confidence among already settled colonists that the changed political situation of the Kingdom meant no changes to their way of life. The exodus of their compatriots — artisans who, following the introduction of a customs border between the Kingdom and the Russian Empire in 1832, began en masse to leave towns and factory settlements and move to Russia — must have made a powerful impression.[^489] In March 1832, Georg Adam Mak and Bogusław Szreder, on behalf of

69 peasants of the Piotrków district petitioned the KRSW for permission to permanently resettle to Podolia gubernia,

> because having sold our land before the revolution and being utterly ruined on account of the present war, we cannot recover our former state; we already wished to settle in Russia in 1817, but by the command of the monarch and king, the late Alexander I, we remained in Poland.[^490]

The group of colonists prepared to leave the country numbered 69 persons. The Polish authorities had no means by which to keep German peasants in their settlements. They could, however, require that all outstanding obligations be settled. Every settler who intended to leave the Kingdom was required to pay the rent for the rent-free years and the charge for timber used to build houses, as well as to settle all possible private debts. Creditors were to be notified by means of an announcement in the Provincial Gazette.[^491] This obligation was also communicated in the Evangelical churches of the Kingdom of Poland.[^492] No statistics were kept on emigrants, but fragmentary data indicate that the phenomenon was of considerable scale, affecting both colonists from government and private estates. From the account of Pastor David Bergemann of Pilica it emerges that the German colonists settled in his parish did not remain long attached to the land on which they worked.

> Die Zeiten haben uns überzeugt, daß die Kolonisten unseres Glaubens, nachdem sie die Wälder geräumt und die Sümpfe trockenlegend haben, die sauberen Felder an Kolonisten römisch-katholischen Glaubens verkaufen, woraus ein Nachteil für unsere Pastoren und Unbequemlichkeiten bei Sammeln der Kirchensteuern entstehen.[^493]

It sometimes happened that an entire village (e.g. Wiączyń near Łódź) resolved to move to Russia, particularly when it was situated on poorly fertile or still heavily forested land requiring clearing. In the case of the colonists at Wiączyń, an additional factor was their awareness of their own distinctiveness from their surroundings (all were Calvinists).[^494] It cannot be excluded, however, that the political motive was used to conceal personal difficulties or weaknesses in farming, since such an argument would contradict the reputation of colonists as capable farmers. There were also cases of applying for a passport and leaving the Kingdom for personal reasons, albeit of an economic character. In 1836, the colonist Martin Schwartz from the settlement of Eufeminów, in the commune of Bedoń in Łęczyca district, was relocating to Prussia to inherit a life-tenancy property there. The district commissioner described the emigrant as follows:

> of good conduct, took no part in the revolution, did not serve in the rebel army, faces no criminal charges, and has no debts or obligations; throughout his residence in this country he engaged solely in farming and has no property beyond a few hundred złoty in cash.[^495]

Yet another phenomenon was also observed. Fairly often, particularly at the beginning of the 1830s, the centrally located territories of the Kingdom of Poland were becoming the permanent place of residence for German peasants who had left their homelands with the intention of travelling to Russia. In 1835, a few residents of Leszno-Wola were reported to have

> arrived in the Kingdom of Poland in 1832 with the intention of resettling in Russia, but expressed a wish to remain and have lived in the village of Leszno-Wola for three years.[^496]

Detailed regulations governing the conditions and procedures for the settlement of foreign colonists in the Kingdom of Poland after the November Uprising were contained in a resolution of the Administrative Council, approved by viceroy I. Paskiewicz on 10 May 1833.[^497] The earlier legal acts of J. Zajączek simultaneously lost their force. Under the new regulations, the procedure for the transfer of foreigners to the Kingdom was to be as follows. Every person wishing to settle in the Kingdom of Poland was required to report to the Russian ambassador, resident, or agent in their country of residence and to present proof that they were a craftsman, manufacturer, or farmer. The declaration of the applicant had to include information about their property, the size of their family, and their choice of place of residence (village or town). At the same time, they had to prove that they were free to leave their native country — an obstacle could be, for example, the obligation to perform military service — and to present a certificate of good conduct. All these documents were forwarded to the government of the Kingdom of Poland, whose relevant commissions made the decision regarding the acceptance of the immigrant and set a date for their arrival (art. 2). Such a procedure applied to those wishing to settle in towns or on government land. In the case of settlement on private estates, however, colonists negotiated only with the landowners, presenting proof of consent to leave their previous country of residence (art. 3). All arrivals were required to report in Warsaw to the KRSW, while the place of settlement on government land was in each case decided by the KRPiS. Those heading to private estates could at any time and in any place negotiate with their owners regarding the conditions of farming, which had to be confirmed by a written contract. The government of the Kingdom of Poland upheld its obligations towards colonists, as contained in the resolutions of viceroy J. Zajączek of 2 March 1816 (art. 6) and 26 November 1822 (art. 7). The guarantees included duty-free import of movable inventory, exemption from military service, and exemption for a period of 6 years from all fees and public burdens. Both the obligations of the settler and the privileges guaranteed by the government had to be included in a written contract (art. 9). Anyone who was dissatisfied with living conditions in Poland had the right to leave the country, provided they had fulfilled their obligations to the landowner or under any legal agreement concluded (art. 5). In the case of land ready for cultivation, a settler received 6 years of freedom from rent. If the land required clearing or drainage, the period of exemption from rent was 12 years. The requirement to hold cash was maintained, with the amount determining the size of the land allotted. A minimum sum of 100 Rhine złoty entitled a settler to apply for 5 morgen of land, i.e. to take on a smallholding. Only those possessing more than 400 Rhine złoty could receive an allotment of 1 Magdeburg Hufe, i.e. 30 morgen. Following the announcement of the Administrative Council resolution of 20 May 1833 prescribing the rules of colonisation, a list arrived from Stuttgart of settlers coming to the Kingdom of Poland. It comprised 86 families, collectively holding 56,823 Rhine złoty in cash,

> but no one reported to the government estates, so it is unknown whether they arrived at all.[^498]

After 1832, the Kingdom's authorities wanted to place over 1,000 settler families on government estates, including up to 150 families in abandoned peasant settlements, up to 500 families in forests designated for clearing, and up to 500 families on land drained by the Bydgoszcz Canal.[^499] Information about this was announced in the press of Württemberg and Baden.[^500] The Prussian and Saxon consuls in Warsaw expressed interest.[^501] According to estimates of the KRPiS, in 1835 some 1,165 settler families arrived, and in the following year 1,151, totalling 9,117 persons, which does not mean, however, that all of them took up any land.[^502] Not all arrivals intended to settle on government estates. Increasingly, they were heading to private estates, where they did not have to present documents demonstrating their wealth and could hope to negotiate more favourable conditions. The government offer was also increasingly perceived as unattractive. Between 1832 and 1838, a total of 497 families settled on national estates.[^503] The authorities of the Kingdom of Poland were disappointed by such meagre progress in settlement. In August 1834, the KRSW decided to issue a rescript on the free entry of colonists into the Kingdom of Poland. Customs offices were instructed to inform immigrants that they need not purchase farms, because

> they will receive such farms free of charge with the benefit of rent-free years, if they settle on government estates.[^504]

This was a significant change, since previously those settling in this category of estates had been required to pay rent from the first harvest. At the same time, attention was drawn to the fact that many newcomers, owing to a lack of or erroneous information, were wandering the country, which exposed them to additional costs, and some were returning to their homelands, blaming the Kingdom's government for the disappointment they had suffered. In November 1834, the presidents of the provincial commissions sent out for publication in frequented places (inns, churches, municipal town halls) a bilingual notice for arriving colonists about the procedures preceding settlement. A very important piece of information was the indication that several families could delegate a single representative who could handle all formalities at the KRPiS office in Warsaw.[^505]

Soon, however, it was noticed that German peasants were again arriving in the Kingdom, wishing to become farmers with large holdings, but having no means to invest, to purchase livestock or tools. Therefore, in 1839 the government tightened the conditions for resettlement. Permission to move to the Kingdom was granted to families that could demonstrate cash holdings of at least 400 Rhine złoty. From single persons, 260 Rhine złoty were required.[^506] Emigration was also deterred by a regulation applied from 1835 in Württemberg, and extended in 1839 to the remaining lands of the Reich, which allowed for refusal to issue return passports to emigrants. This cut off the route home for those emigrants who had been disappointed at their intended place of settlement. Many of them became vagrants. Those apprehended were directed to military correctional companies.[^507] As late as 1845, the Bavarian envoy in St. Petersburg requested that the principle be observed whereby Bavarian emigrants residing in the Kingdom of Poland be permitted to return to their homeland only after prior confirmation that they would be received in their own country.[^508] There are grounds to infer a certain kind of understanding in this matter between the administration of the Kingdom of Poland and the states of the Reich, presumably inspired by the Russian authorities. At the end of December 1839, viceroy I. Paskiewicz decided that colonists from the Duchy of Weimar who had arrived in the country for permanent settlement should not be issued return passports, as upon returning they would become a burden on the communes they had voluntarily left.[^509] At the same time, the Russian minister plenipotentiary to the German Confederation was disseminating information about the inconveniences to which German peasants migrating to Polish lands were exposed. These concerned above all settlement on private estates. The tsarist diplomat pointed out that the lack of any limit on the number of colonists a private landowner could accommodate on his estates was leading to excessive fragmentation of land.[^510] He warned against estate agents who committed various abuses. Indeed, such a problem existed and was known to the Polish authorities, who tried to remedy it. The procedure for arriving on the territory of the Kingdom of Poland was twofold. Colonists determined to settle on government estates deposited a sum of money in their country of origin and, with proof of holding such assets, crossed the border of the Kingdom. They then proceeded to Warsaw to withdraw the deposit at the Bank of Poland and to receive from the KRPiS a referral to the place of settlement. This procedure practically excluded the possibility of any fraud to the detriment of the arrivals. The situation was different for immigrants heading to private estates. No monetary deposit was required of them, only the presentation of proof that they would be received on specified estates within the Kingdom. This situation was exploited by agents holding powers of attorney from landowners and permissions to bring foreign subjects into the country. They were accused of

> trafficking in certificates of acceptance, multiplying their number, or colonists on their part made arrangements with these agents for sums which they were subsequently unable to produce on arrival.[^511]

This practice is unsurprising, given that agents were remunerated for each colonist brought in, while the inflated resources the immigrants purportedly possessed were, in the agents' view, an advantage when applying for larger land allotments.

Already from the mid-1830s, complaints from colonists settled on private estates about landowners' failure to honour contracts began to multiply. The complaint lodged in December 1835 by colonists from the village of Tłokinia near Kalisz attracted widespread attention. They complained about the village's landowner, Radzimiński, who had taken their money (an entry fee) but had not given them any land. The matter reached Paskiewicz himself, who ordered that the colonists be assigned a court-appointed lawyer and be exempted from court costs.[^512] The KRSW took the position that the conduct of the administrative authorities towards colonists should be, as far as possible, most encouraging for them.[^513] Landowners were required to obtain the KRSW's consent for the recruitment of colonists and were required to provide detailed information about the investment needed for the land offered to settlers.[^514] Compliance with these provisions was to be overseen by the local administration. It was in the local administration that colonists were meant to find a friendly partner and defender against the arbitrary conduct of landowners. The provincial official gazettes recalled the regulations applicable to the colonisation of private estates.[^515] In 1837, the KRSW introduced the obligation to record the rights and duties of colonists in the form of annexes to the mortgage registers of estates. Landowners were not permitted to allot a settler family less than 30 morgen of land. In addition, the period for resettlement was restricted to three months: March, April, and May.[^516] Two years later, the Administrative Council imposed on civil governors the obligation to appoint officials for special assignments, whose task was to oversee relations between landowners and colonists. These officials were required to carry out inspections that were to be unannounced and periodic.[^517] Despite this, settlement contracts were violated, as evidenced by complaints directed to representatives of the Kingdom's government administration. From the surviving correspondence it appears that the greatest objections raised by settlers concerned manorial monopolies which, already unknown in the territories of the Reich, were regarded by them as archaic and, above all, as restricting their freedom. The Kingdom's authorities were aware of this problem. In 1844, the KRSW informed the Kalisz Gubernial Government that the colonisation documents of private estates presented to it contained provisions

> aimed at obliging colonists to a certain form of compulsory servitude, namely not to purchase vodka or other spirits from outside the lord's tavern, under penalties for smuggling,

and defined them as formulated contrary to existing law, specifically art. 686 of the [Napoleonic] Civil Code.[^518] This article stated that

> owners are permitted to establish on their properties or in favour of their properties such easements as they see fit, provided however that the established easements are imposed not on a person or for the benefit of a person, but only on an estate and for the benefit of an estate, and provided that these easements contain nothing contrary to public order […].[^519]

The practice of economic life was, however, different, and the propination monopoly persisted on the lands of the Kingdom of Poland until the enfranchisement, remaining throughout its existence one of the most widely applied and most tenaciously defended easements.[^520] It is estimated that in the mid-nineteenth century in the Kingdom of Poland income from the sale of alcohol constituted approximately 25% of total annual profits on private estates.[^521] It sometimes happened that colonists who had been disappointed in their expectations and were embittered returned to their homeland, as occurred for example on the Chocz estate in Kalisz province, from which 8 colonist families returned to the Palatinate. The accounts of returning migrants deterred potential emigrants from undertaking the journey, and even caused some to abandon a journey already begun eastward. Württembergers heading to Poland in early 1833 reported to the authorities that

> nur bis Kröpfelbach im Oberamt Hall, kamen, wo ihnen bereits solche Auswanderer begegneten, die einige Monate früher nach Polen gezogen waren, aber kein Unterkommen gefunden hatten, und welche über ihre Aufnahme in Polen die traurigsten Schilderungen machten, und ihnen den Muth zur weiteren Fortsetzung ihrer Reise benahmen.[^522]

Other returning migrants went so far as to lodge complaints at the Russian mission in Frankfurt am Main.[^523] In 1839, the Russian envoy reported on the difficulties that were emerging with the resettlement of Württemberg colonists to Poland. The mission's office was besieged by people who had no means to develop a settlement and often could not even cover the cost of the journey. The desperation of prospective settlers was evidenced by the fact that they were prepared to undertake the journey in late autumn. The envoy proposed the necessity of migrants proving they held a specified sum of money and the introduction of a ban on resettlement during winter months. These proposals, however, did not find favour. It was limited to the requirement that agents confirm their powers of attorney at Russian missions in the Reich. A good way to avoid disappointment at the place of settlement was to conduct a reconnaissance, during which delegated scouts could select a place and obtain information about the conditions for taking up land. In 1836, five newcomers from Hesse and Nassau reported to the provincial authorities in Kalisz. They were placed under the care of district chiefs, who were to find them settlements; if they did not wish to accept, they were to be directed to private estates.[^524]

The willingness to provide this far-reaching assistance was presumably also due to the fact that the immigrants held cash of 390–400 Rhine złoty, which allowed them to set up. They did not, however, find suitable land for themselves on government estates, since a year later, at Bechcice in the commune of Lutomiersk, Piotrków gubernia, on the estate of Kwiryn Okołowicz, some 50 families settled who had come directly from the Grand Duchy of Hesse (Landgrafschaft Hessen-Darmstadt).[^525] The immigrants did not travel together. They entered the Kingdom of Poland through the customs offices at Podgrabów, Szczypiorno, and Wieruszów.[^526] A year later, a similarly large group arriving from Hesse settled at Łobudzice near Szadek in Kalisz gubernia.[^527] Their settlement was also preceded by a reconnaissance, and they crossed the border through the customs offices at Wieruszów and Szczypiorno.[^528] In that same year, another 10 families from Hesse made their way to Sławoszewo near Konin. At the opposite pole of relations between the manor and colonists were cases in which colonists were cared for in a manner that constructed the image of a solicitous landowner, while at the same time violating the applicable law. In 1837, Kazimierz Fremkel, the lord of the Chocz estate, refused to provide the number of Evangelicals residing on his lands, on the grounds that

> the lord cannot allow them to be disturbed and possibly burdened with additional obligations.[^529]

The explanation would be convincing were it not for the circumstance that the colonists belonged to the Roman Catholic parish in Chocz, which had been stipulated in the settlement contract to which Fremkel referred. It may therefore be supposed that the landowner in some way persuaded the newcomers settling on his estate to become parishioners of the church in Chocz, even though at least some of them were Protestants. Despite the clearly formulated legal regulations concerning the conditions for resettlement to the Kingdom of Poland, questions also multiplied from prospective settlers, to which both the KRSW and the KRPiS endeavoured to provide exhaustive answers. In 1839, it was communicated, for example, that in order to be recognised as a colonist one had to be at least 21 years of age, and that the right to hunt on one's own land was unrestricted on government estates, whereas on private estates this right customarily belonged to the lord. Information was also provided about the possibility of importing into the Kingdom and owning firearms, subject to detailed permission from the military commander of the relevant gubernia, upon payment of 2 złp per firearm, earmarked for the fund of rewards for killing predatory animals.[^530]

In 1843, given the absence of free land for settlement on government estates, the KRPiS proposed radical restrictions on the influx of colonists, while the viceroy of the Kingdom proposed directing newcomers to private estates.[^531] In the wake of this, the local administration conducted a survey regarding the feasibility of such a plan. Reports sent from commune headmen in the areas of Kalisz and Piotrków gubernias to district chiefs were unambiguous:

> there is no land; does not possess sufficient land to cede to colonists; Wrząca Wartska does not wish to receive foreign colonists for settlement; likewise not one of the landowners around Błaszki.[^532]

In July 1844, the Administrative Council notified the Russian missions in the German states to cease issuing emigration passports, as the government of the Kingdom of Poland no longer had any land it could designate for colonists. It was even alarmed that

> owing to the large number of foreigners arriving who wish to settle on government estates — it is impossible to accommodate them there.[^533]

In the opinion of the KRPiS, on government estates only forest remnants remained as unsettled land. Across the entire Kingdom their area was estimated at approximately 2,100 łan. On private estates, the area of free land was estimated at 2,633 łan, which is very little, and even these do not present a promising prospect for those arriving.[^534] Indeed, the influx of German peasants interested in farming the land in the areas between the Prosna, the Pilica, and the Vistula ceased almost entirely towards the end of the 1850s.

The government's stimulation of the migratory movement did not escape the notice of private landowners. By promising more favourable settlement conditions, they endeavoured to attract colonists to their own estates.[^535] Already in the 1820s, the dogma popular among the landowning nobility of the Kingdom of Poland concerning the complementarity of the Polish economy with the West had begun to collapse. Since the lifting of the Continental Blockade (1814) and the systematically tightened British Corn Laws (1815–1846), imports of Polish grain had been declining, which entailed a fall in its price. In this situation, interest began to emerge in the development of the domestic market, towns, industry, and no less in the modernisation of agriculture. The idea of reforming peasant relations was at the same time suppressed by fear of a drain of labour from the manorial farm.[^536] For the advocates of economic progress, the fundamental dilemma rested in the question: how to revitalise individual initiative, where to seek sources of capital accumulation.[^537] The conversion to rent on private estates had its origins in the Enlightenment undertakings of a small number of magnates, who in the final period of the existence of the Commonwealth had sought its reform and the improvement of the profitability of their estates. In the times of the Kingdom of Poland, ready cash was sought in the form of rent payments to compensate for the fall in income caused by the effects of the Napoleonic Wars, and not infrequently also to cover losses incurred through dishonest leaseholders or to avoid disputes over the measure of serfdom. Both entire estates and individual villages were converted to rent, sometimes only parts of them.[^538]

An important reason for the transition to a rent-based economy was the spread of new crops (clover, vetch, potatoes, sugar beet), which required moving away from the three-field system to four- and eight-field cultivation. The intermingling of manorial and peasant fields did not allow the new system of cultivation to develop, so regulations of land, the ordering of pastures and forests, and the eviction of peasants became necessary. It was assumed that incorporating peasant land into the manorial farm would bring more benefits than the serf labour rendered from it.[^539] After the November Uprising, economic progress in agriculture was achieved generally through land regulations (separations) and evictions. Peasants evicted to inferior land frequently abandoned their farms. Deserted homesteads and empty fields were encountered on almost all estates.[^540] It was precisely on such land that German colonists were willingly settled, as they were happy to take on poorer land while negotiating the most favourable conditions possible.[^541] Around 1830, close to half of the peasants farming on government estates had already been converted to rent. On private estates, this proportion was lower. A new category of villages began to emerge, called rent-and-serf villages, though the name is misleading if one takes into account the proportions between these two types of obligation.[^542]

The share of rents in the total income from the majority of rent-and-serf villages was negligible. Its application was a consequence of the inability of landowners to exploit the potential serf labour resources that the estates possessed. In such cases, a portion of the labour services was converted into rent. Numerous examples from across the entire area between the Prosna, the Pilica, and the Vistula demonstrate that in villages situated close to manorial farms, serf labour alone was maintained, satisfying the farms' labour needs, especially foot-labour. In villages situated far from the estate centre, or within the bounds of newly created manorial farms on poor land, wastelands, virgin land, and clearing sites, farmers were required to pay full or partial rent.[^543]

There was, however, one factor which, right up to the enfranchisement of 1864, distinguished all colonist farms from serf farms, namely the different legal conditions governing land use. Colonists received land under subordinate (usufructuary, *ius utile*) ownership, being hereditary lessees — rent-payers. Paramount ownership (*dominium directum*) was retained by the treasury (on government estates) or by the landowner (on private estates). This type of subordinate ownership entailed the obligation to pay rent after the expiry of the rent-free years specified in the contract, the requirement to obtain permission (*konsens*) for sale and to pay *laudemium* (jura gabellae). Within these bounds, the rent-payer enjoyed the right of hereditary possession and use of the land and its disposal.[^544] The above-noted distinctiveness of conditions under which settlements were established corresponding to the various waves of rural settlement is most clearly visible in the example of the Olęder villages, still fairly numerous in the Kingdom of Poland in the 1840s. Before 1806, the Prussian authorities warned farmers in the colonies founded at that time that in the event of a sale of land they should present a foreign settler as the buyer. This was aimed at the Olęders, who as specialists in forest clearing were frequently hired for this work by inexperienced colonists — Swabians. The numerous benefits from which colonists settled by the Prussian chambers enjoyed (livestock, seed grain, grants for setting up) constituted a powerful temptation for Olęders to abandon their own farms.[^545]

From notarial records it emerges that despite the prohibition, Olęders moved to the colonies.[^546] This contradicts the thesis that it was colonists who were drawn to the Olęder settlements, finding there neighbours close to them in language and confession.[^547] The Olęders' desire to take up residence in the colonies becomes obvious when one compares the economic situation of both categories of settlers. The obligations of colonists towards landowners were, after the expiry of the rent-free years, limited to payment of rent, the level of which was usually determined according to average grain prices, and to the upkeep of roads and bridges within the colony. Olęders, settled mainly on private estates, had to bear numerous burdens in favour of their owners. The scope of these feudal duties could be very wide. A good example is a contract concluded at the end of 1818 between Antoni Płonczyński, lord of the Niewiadów and Zaosie estates in Brzeziny district, and the honourable Olęders Bogusław Handt and Chrystian Tayfer, representatives of 37 settlers.[^548] The Olęders received the perpetual right of possession of land in the colony of Lipinki and Wykno. After three rent-free years they were required to pay rent of 78 złp per Hufe (30 morgen). Timber remained the property of the manor. Only for construction could the Olęders obtain 20 logs per Hufe. From fallen timber they were permitted to burn charcoal. The manor also allowed cattle to graze in the forest outside the colony. For each Hufe of land held, the Olęder was obliged to provide free labour of two days with draught animals or four days by hand at the manorial farms, or wherever the manor pleased and at every request for ploughing, hauling manure, and timber. One day of draught labour was valued as the ploughing of 200 square rods (2/3 of a morgen). After the expiry of the rent-free years, obligations increased by one day per Hufe for planting or hoeing potatoes. To these burdens was added the obligation to build a tavern, the propination compulsion, and the compulsion to mill at the manorial mill under penalty of a fine of 60 złp. The transfer of settlements was restricted. An Olęder could not sell the land either to a nobleman or to a manorial official. In the event of an undervalued sale price, on which 10% *laudemium* was to be paid, the manor reserved for itself the right to reclaim the settlement.

An interesting example of the flexible adaptation of long-established Olęder settlements to changing farming conditions is the history of the Prażuchy colony, founded in 1780 as an Olęder settlement by Krzysztof Celiński, owner of the Stropieszyn and Ceków estates in Kalisz district. In 1821, the owner's son, Maksymilian Celiński,

> resolved to secure [the colonists] in perpetual possession of the land [...] but under different conditions from those hitherto, so that they should be better adapted to present circumstances.[^549]

The change consisted in replacing labour services and dues with a fixed monetary payment. Each of the four days of labour services to which colonists were obliged was valued at 1 złp 12 gr, and the annual tithe of 2 capons was converted to 2 złp. Only the village headman was exempted from these payments during the period of his office. In the new agreement, the manor withdrew from its guarantee of providing building timber to fire victims, without, however, preventing colonists from insuring themselves with the Fire Insurance Society. The accompanying obligation of colonists to collectively extinguish fires in the manorial forest clearly indicates the growing value of timber in the vicinity of the Prażuchy colony. The manor also showed concern for proper land management in the colony. The land user had the right to transfer it without payment of *laudemium* to a son or son-in-law. If, however, the successor managed the farm poorly, the father was permitted once more without payment of *laudemium* to cede the land to a second son or another son-in-law. This renegotiation of the former contract very well reflects the changes in the perception of the value of money, and demonstrates the readiness of the estate owner to switch to an already capitalist form of farming, in which there is no longer payment in lieu of feudal obligations. The initiative for such a change came from the estate owner, but it was also beneficial for the settlers in Sobiesęki. Their dependence on the manor diminished, they could dispose of their time without restriction, and received yet another incentive to participate in the market. This type of amendment to settlement contracts, concluded at the end of the eighteenth century and the beginning of the nineteenth century, was already common in the Kalisz region in the 1830s.[^550] The process advanced more slowly in the northern part of the area under study.

In the 1820s, the remaining wasteland in the Vistula valley was also being settled. It is known that Olędrzy were being settled on the estates of the Potocki family at Wilanów, on an island in the river current at the height of the village of Zawady. The first, no longer extant, contracts were signed in 1819 and 1823.[^551] A subsequent contract, concluded in November 1824, stipulated a 40-year lease of lands which, not yet measured, the settlers were to clear of shrubs and undergrowth. In 1832 the agreement was confirmed, obliging the Olędrzy to erect, within 4 years, in suitable locations, residential and farm buildings.[^552] A house of sawn timber, thatched, was to measure 24 x 14 ells (approx. 110 m²), while a barn measuring 20 x 11 ells was to be built from softwood timber.[^553] Five years later there was already a chapel and a school on Kępa Zawadowska, a cemetery had been established and a road laid out.[^554] The fertile soil, enriched by the Vistula floods, was used for the cultivation of rye and, above all, potatoes supplied to the Warsaw market.[^555]

On private estates, settlers were placed not only on contiguous areas of greater extent but also very willingly on various kinds of wastelands, forest clearings, or in corners along the boundaries of villages and estates, where even marking out a reasonably regular plot was itself a challenge. An excellent example of the siting of such a settlement is the plan drawn up in 1819 for measuring one Chełmno hide for the settler Jozef Klinger at the junction of the boundaries of the settlements of Czerwonka, Zielone, and Dziurdzioły on the estate of Kazimierz Buczyński in Rawa district (Annex XIV). The plot had the shape of an irregular trapezoid, one side of which was formed by a watercourse. Within the plot there was also a fragment of forest of about 1 morga, from which the farmer could obtain building and fuel materials.[^556] Dispersed settlement, also called single-homestead settlement, was very advantageous for estate owners, as it eliminated troublesome wastelands at the lowest cost. Only some such settlements grew in size. Most disappeared, and the lands brought into agricultural use were incorporated into new structures in the course of the division of estate areas. The reclamation of wastelands in the area between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula continued until the mid-1850s.[^557] Not always traceable in cartographic materials, it left traces in archival documents. In 1840 the head of Kalisz district mentioned three such settlements inhabited by Evangelicals from the parish of Sobiesęki: Pustkowie Daniel, Pustkowie Pośrednie, Pustkowie Borek.[^558] This manner of cultivating wastelands survived until the years preceding enfranchisement. In 1854 Hieronim Bojakiewicz, the heir of the Skarżyn estate in the commune of the same name, founded the Kolonia Marianów, consisting of a single farm on land left over from a felled forest still requiring clearing.[^559]

Cartographic sources from the first half of the nineteenth century frequently reveal interesting details concerning newly founded villages. A map made in 1817 of the newly founded colony of Mariampol in Zgierz district covered only the outline of the boundaries of the projected village (Annex XV). Its area, cut through by a watercourse and an accompanying field track, had no internal divisions from which one could infer the planned number and size of farms. In subsequent years the colony was settled and in 1827 Mariampol had 19 houses and 137 inhabitants.[^560] A map of the village of Kotwasice in the Piętno estate (Kalisz district and voivodeship), drawn up in April 1822, attests to a new spatial organisation of a previously existing and partially depopulated village (Annex XVI). The area demarcated in the field by mounds received a regular division into hides (łany). Alongside the old road network, several new, straight-running roads were laid out. Just beyond the village boundary there remained a small cleared space called huta stara [old glassworks], which testifies to the earlier existence of a glassworks on that spot. At some distance from the village boundary, next to the manor meadow, a common meadow for all farmers was set aside. It bordered on overgrown land that had once been used as a meadow by a draniczarz [bark cutter], a wheelwright, an innkeeper, and one Walenty. At the opposite end of the village, at the edge of the forest, the only buildings apart from the demesne buildings were marked. The absence of other additions indicates that the owner of Kotwasice was only beginning to populate the settlement.

A great deal of new light on property relations in German settler colonies was shed by the analysis of notarial acts attesting to the purchase and sale of individual farms. One hundred and fifty randomly selected transactions from the years 1808–1819, concluded between settlers living in Brzeziny and Łęczyca districts in colonies founded during the Prussian occupation, were examined.[^561] Land turnover was characterised by great dynamism. The most common subject of purchase and sale were 60-morga settlements (40% of all transactions). Settlements of 30 morgas were the subject of 11% of contracts. Farms of 6 and 10 morgas attracted less interest. The value of the land was determined primarily by its fertility and the state of development (clearing or drainage that had been carried out). In the majority of cases the price of one morga of land fell within the range of 18 to 30 Polish zlotys. In more than half of all the transactions analysed, the subject was land only. In the remaining ones, buildings were listed, and their type and number indicate differences in the prosperity of the colonists and in the state of development of the settlements. The most frequently recorded building configuration was: a house, a cowshed, a barn, not infrequently described as being under one roof. A shelter (buda) was mentioned several times (also shelter and barn, shelter and cowshed), which testifies to the makeshift character of the residential building. Timber, apart from oak, was not at that time a factor raising the value of a settlement. The lack of roads, the lack of navigable rivers, and the lack of demand for timber amid its abundance made this raw material almost worthless. Cleared stumps lay for years in the fields, hindering agricultural work.[^562] Characteristic was the narrowing of the circle of contracting parties to almost exclusively the settler community. Representatives of other social groups made up 6.7% of the total in this group, and among them were: 4 noblemen, 3 townspeople, 3 Olędrzy, a miller, a forester, and the owner of a glassworks. Similarly, the area from which the contracting parties came was limited, describable as a circle of approximately 35 km radius around the settlement to which the transaction related. Great activity prevailed, however, within individual colonies and between those situated in neighbouring areas. Telling here is the example of Wiączyń, in which of the 15 purchasers of settlements in the years 1808–1818, not one remained on his land until 1846.[^563] Frequent changes of ownership, arising from the search for better farming conditions, did not, however, hasten the moment of achieving stability.

This is confirmed by the opinion of Rajmund Rembieliński from 1820, that in the vicinity of Łódź

> settlers are extirpating the superfluous forests there, but pay their owners little for them, and have not yet attained a good state.[^564]

Greater stability of farming characterised the western and north-western margins of the Kingdom of Poland. Endowed with relatively good soils and settled early — already in the 1770s and 1780s — by Olędrzy, they entered the sphere of leasehold farming most quickly of all the lands of the Kingdom. The majority of settlements established in that era did not change their acreage or number of inhabitants for at least half a century. Martin Arndt, who lived in the Holendry Maślane settlement near Kleczew in Konin district, founded in 1779, declared in 1833:

> I have been living here for 50 years since I paid my way in [...] I must honestly testify that for 50 years I found 12 hearths here, which exist to this day; there were no more, and no change either in the buildings or the lands has occurred during that time.[^565]

Numerous similar materials confirm the opinion about the German agricultural colonies of the Kingdom of Poland:

> Die deutschen Dörfer Ungarns sind kulturell selbständiges Organismus, die Kongreßpolen kleine Splitter, die nur durch Zusammenschluss lebensfähig werden.[^566]

Settled residence and economic stability are usually accompanied by prosperity. The generality of German colonists in the countryside was perceived as living in comfortable circumstances. This impression was already created by the mere sight of a colony, most of which were built regularly and with similar buildings. Interesting information on the material situation of agricultural settlers is provided by farm inventories drawn up after the death of the owners. Analysis of these sources allows one to make observations about the origin and mobility of the settlers, their contacts with the surrounding world, and above all their property status. Forty-five inventories from the years 1843–1849, relating to agricultural farms in colonies that in time came to lie within the boundaries of Łódź, were analysed.[^567] Their merit is that they show the state achieved after 25–35 years of farming. The inventories revealed a wide range in the value of individual farms. The most prosperous was estimated at 4,290 Polish zlotys, while at the opposite pole were two indebted farms: one by 720 Polish zlotys, the other by 93 Polish zlotys. In none of the homesteads were there brick buildings. The thatched residential buildings differed only in the number of rooms, which in no case exceeded two. The number and size of farm buildings depended on the livestock kept. Only three farms recorded draught animals: twice two oxen, and in one case two horses and one ox. The number of cattle and young stock ranged from one to five head. Two farms kept flocks of sheep numbering 12 and 17 animals. Three homesteads had gardens with fruit trees. The equipment of individual farms with agricultural tools also varied. One could encounter the plough already archaic in this area (płużyca), but there were also wheeled ploughs with iron fittings. Carts — both unshod and half-shod as well as fully shod with a box-wagon and half-baskets. Almost all farms were well equipped with woodworking tools, two or three axes each and the same number of saws. This can be explained by the needs of clearing. In one of the better-off farms there was even a fire pump. Household furnishings reflect property differences to a lesser degree, and in any case until about the mid-nineteenth century they were not accorded great importance. Pine furniture predominated, in a few cases painted red. Striking is the small quantity of kitchen utensils and the relatively modest clothing. The inventories unfortunately lack information on the type of heating devices in the residential buildings (an iron stove was mentioned only twice), and on floor coverings (sand, daub, or boards). These two components of the inventory have been considered in ethnographic research as significant indicators of the standard of rural living. In five farms the inventories recorded flax-weaving workshops, two spinning wheels each and brushes for combing flax. It is difficult to infer, however, to what extent they were used — whether they served to satisfy one's own needs or whether weaving was done on them for pay. From the fact that they were found in more prosperous farms, better equipped in other tools and implements as well, one may suppose that they were used to a limited extent.

Settlement developed with great commitment on government estates could act as a stimulus for the private initiatives of owners of private estates, but it was certainly not a model for them. The nobility continued the form of modernising their own estates that had already begun at the close of the First Polish Republic, and government settlement could only accelerate this process. In formal and organisational terms, both settlement streams ran in parallel and independently of each other. In any case, all restrictions on the conditions of settlement on government estates, or the periodic lack of suitable land within them, gave rise to greater interest in settling on private estates. A factor facilitating the nobility's decision to organise leasehold farming on their estates was presumably the activity of the Land Credit Society founded in 1825, whose influence on the formation of a new, capitalist treatment of land is difficult to overestimate. Already in the years 1828–1829 significantly greater interest by colonists in private rather than government estates became apparent. Of the 7,580 settler families present in the Kingdom of Poland in 1830, 2,816 families lived on private estates and 4,764 on national estates.

Data from 1840 indicate that the number of colonists settled on private estates was three times greater than the number of settlers living on government estates. Of 10,507 colonist farms, 7,971 were on noble estates and 2,536 on national estates. These data also clearly show that in the years 1830–1840 there was a marked outflow of settlers from national to private estates. In the years 1840–1859 the number of colonist families settled on private estates grew from 7,971 to 10,185, while on government estates it increased by only 12 families.[^568] This means that in the two decades indicated above, the entire inflow of colonists was directed towards private estates. Already in the mid-1830s the Commission of Revenue and Treasury was noting that many colonists from abroad who wished to settle on private estates did not appear in Warsaw at all.[^569]

The reduced inflow of colonists in the years 1840–1859 was undoubtedly significantly influenced by the abandonment of government agitation after 1844. The years 1842–1848 also marked the peak period of colonist inflow to private estates. This revival of settlement in the 1840s may have been favoured by the ongoing process of evictions and, simultaneously, the modernisation of agriculture. It is estimated that approximately 60,000 morgas of land passed into the hands of German settlers from evicted serf peasants. Estate owners needed cash for the modernisation of farming, the introduction of new crops (sugar beet, expansion of potato cultivation). One of the sources of obtaining the required funds could be rents and entry payments. Evicted peasants were placed within the manor farm buildings. The growing demand for manual labour in the cultivation of root and fodder crops freed the estate owner from concern about providing them with lands greater than a kitchen garden plot. These circumstances explain the numerically significant wave of German settlers arriving in the territories between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula in the years 1846–1849.[^570] At that time 1,154 farms were established, among which were some of greater acreage taken up by more prosperous settlers for whom there was no longer room on government estates.[^571] From the 1850s onwards, landowners had fewer and fewer lands they could designate for settlement. Migrations within the Kingdom almost ceased, and the influx of land-hungry settlers from abroad became minimal.

In contrast to the model of settlement implemented by the authorities of the Kingdom of Poland, which consisted of settling newcomers on empty lands, private settlement initiatives were from the outset oriented towards the creation of colonies. For this purpose, collective contracts were concluded with settlers, providing for the creation of spatially compact villages. Collective contracts defined the legal relationship between the settlers as a community and the landowner. The mutual rights and obligations were specified in detail, from the size of individual settlements to the level of rent and mutual obligations. The contract was simultaneously the founding document of the village.

The internal structure of a colony was determined above all by the collective responsibility of its inhabitants, especially in respect of rent payment. This type of contract crystallised in the second half of the eighteenth century with the development of Olęder settlement and survived in the territories between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula until enfranchisement, although the term Olęder itself and its synonyms disappear from documents at the turn of the 1840s–1850s. Despite the tradition maintained for decades in the understanding of solidarity in land use, in 1846 a model text of a settlement contract was published, in which the concept of collective solidarity was explained in detail.

> Solidarity is to be understood as meaning that, regardless of whether one, two, or more perpetual leaseholders are unable to pay [the rent], the community of colonists always pays the rent at the specified amount into the coffers of the landowner.[^572]

Given the date of publication, one must see in this model a confirmation of the practice applied for decades, not a normative recommendation. Solidarity strengthened the colonist community, allowed the overcoming of individual farming failures, and when necessary the presentation of a unified position in disputes. It ensured attention to the common economic interest but also helped regulate the internal life of the village (maintenance of a cantor, a teacher). Comparing both streams of rural settlement from the years 1815–1864, one must point to its considerably wider scope on private estates, on which five times more colonists settled than on government estates.[^573] Settling on private estates gave the colonist freedom of choice of residence and did not require financial outlays from him. The settlement agreement was signed on the spot and was frequently preceded by an inquiry into the location and value of the lands to be taken up. Above all, however, estate owners were willing to create separate colonies inhabited only by foreign newcomers. This was an advantageous alternative to the government proposals for dispersed settlement. The conduct of the landowners was therefore more flexible and corresponded to a greater degree to the expectations of the colonists. One must also note that despite the considerable outflow of colonists from government to private estates, the authorities of the Kingdom took no steps to halt or limit this process.

The question of the numbers and demographic development of the community of German agricultural settlers (as well as industrial settlers) in the Kingdom of Poland has, over the decades, been covered by an extensive literature. This literature was based on a fragmentary, far from sufficient evidential base for definitive conclusions, and the formulated findings depended both on the nationality of the author and on current political tendencies.[^574] For the first three decades of the nineteenth century, valuable material remains the so-called Warsaw list, compiled before 1939 by Eugen Oskar Kossmann on the basis of documents from the former Archive of Old Records in Warsaw that no longer exist today.[^575] The data included in the registers must, however, be treated with great caution. The criteria by which the voivodeship authorities were guided in compiling the colonist registers in 1835 are not known. They encompass rural settlers — farmers, but also craftsmen connected with the wave of industrial settlement and forming specialised textile settlements (Kamienica Polska, Huta Stara, Czarny Las, Ksawerów, Zelów).[^576] There were also Jewish colonists in the Kingdom of Poland whose farms were counted in 1835.[^577] Above all, however, at the beginning of the nineteenth century the nomenclature of many settlements was not yet established. Setting aside errors in records made by officials, this nomenclature evolved with changes in the character of the village. This applies especially to Olęder settlements founded as Holendry, later losing this designation, or even completely changing their name, as happened for instance with Holendry Dębowieckie. Founded under this name in 1779 on the Dębowiec estate in Konin district, they were already called Holendry Maślane in the times of the Duchy of Warsaw, to adopt in the 1840s the name Maślaki Holendry, or simply Maślaki.[^578] Similarly with the settlement first named Holendry belonging to the village of Piegonisko, later Piegońskie Holendry, and in the 1850s — Piegonisko Kolonia.[^579] Considerable doubts also arise in establishing the number of hearths and the number of inhabitants in localities that underwent division, such as Kolonia Stocka separated from the settlement Marcinów Holendry.[^580] Considerable confusion is also caused by the occurrence between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula of localities with identical names (e.g. Mariampol on the Guzów estate and Mariampol in the commune of Piaskowice). The numerical data are considerably more difficult to verify. This applies especially to population figures in individual settlements. These can differ from each other very considerably over a period of a few years, and the fragmentary source accounts neither allow the verification of these figures nor the seeking of reasons for the discrepancies. A further weakness of the statistics from the Warsaw list is the inclusion in the registers of all localities in which colonists were found and the treating of them as German colonists. Meanwhile from other sources it is known that there were also colonies inhabited by a mixed national and denominational population, such as Joanka, Poroże, or Tłokinia in the Kalisz region. Similarly in the Łask estates, where among the forests there were several settlements inhabited by German and Polish peasants (Kolonia Rokitnica or Olędry Rokitnickie, Olędry Anielińskie, Olędry Teodorowskie).[^581] Nevertheless, the Warsaw list constitutes telling documentation of the spread of German settlement across the vast expanses of the Kingdom of Poland and, when compared with later materials, including cartographic ones, it confirms the permanence of the settlement of German peasants in central Poland.

The legal basis of the settlement of German colonists consisted of settlement contracts concluded with the landowners. These regulated the rights and obligations of the settlers and determined the fundamental framework of their economic activity. The form prevailing on private estates was more advantageous. Private landowners corresponded more to the expectations of the settlers, but in doing so they were of course also pursuing their own aims. The principle of collective responsibility of the settlers was undoubtedly of primary importance. In 1846, Seweryn Biernacki, keenly interested in the commutation of peasant obligations, noted that the solidarity of the colonists entailed an increase in group discipline, for the farmers

> watch carefully over the conduct of their neighbours and influence them to farm better, or compel them to leave the colony.[^582]

On government estates the principle of collective responsibility was not applied. Personal responsibility prevailed. All rights and obligations rested on the shoulders of the individual settler. Weak farmers who made poor use of their lands had, however, to reckon with the possibility of eviction from their farms. The principle became established of eviction from the land if a colonist had not fulfilled his obligations for two consecutive years. The settlement of German peasants on noble estates was in no way dependent on the prosperity of the newcomers. Those wishing to settle on estates had to pay a one-time fee (entry payment, Grundgeld), the amount of which depended on local conditions and the location of the lands to be taken up and amounted usually to between 10% and 30% of the annual rent. A form of assistance was free building timber, but as a rule it was guaranteed only for the first construction. For any subsequent repairs or rebuilding after fires, the colonist had to purchase the timber he needed. Numerous examples demonstrate that from the time of the Prussian period onwards, government assistance and care for settlers on national estates, just like the substantial support given to them by the owners of private estates, did not guarantee the long-term occupation of a farm and farming success. To a large extent these were determined by the individual characteristics of the colonist, his commitment and industriousness, skills, and also his ability to adapt to prevailing conditions.[^583]

> Order [Rządność] is the first step on the path of agricultural improvement. Prosperity follows in its wake, it is its natural consequence,

wrote Ludwik Górski in 1845, a man well acquainted with the realities of rural life and farming.[^584]

This orderliness, by which was understood the careful, conscientious, and rational management of a farm, was presumably a significant factor in the success of the colonists. Another factor, no less important and in large measure independent of agricultural effort, that had an influence on the effectiveness of farming was the quality of the lands. Contemporary observers' accounts point to this unambiguously.

> [...] Einige dieser Kolonisten, welche unvermischt aus dem südlichen Deutschland stammen und zufälliger Weise guten Acker erhalten haben, sich jetzt in blühenden Umständen [befinden], doch ihre Zahl ist nur geringe.[^585]

Landowners transferred to colonists lands not used for agriculture, often not located within the boundaries of the demesne farms and of little value. Only in the 1830s, with the growing interest in expanding the area under cultivation, was attention turned to wasteland.

> In recent years more than ever the desire has awakened among landowners to bring colonists from abroad and settle them on their properties. This occurrence is to be attributed to exact knowledge of their own interest, which flows from well-organised farms and the utilisation of extensive lands hitherto lying fallow or overgrown with forest.[^586]

At the same time, landowners tried to exchange lands in such a way as to incorporate all cultivated lands into the demesne farm. This was associated with the eviction of peasants, including colonists, onto poorer lands requiring improvement to agricultural productivity.[^587] The bad consequences of this practice for the peasant economy in the Kalisz region were pointed out, among others, by Bonawentura Niemojowski as early as 1831.[^588] It happened that lands cleared by colonists were not suitable for agricultural use, being too sandy. In 1823 settlers from the colony of Paproć Duża in the Płock region complained:

> The cause of our poverty is the land, which initially appeared useful but is in reality infertile, as even the best farmers from the neighbourhood can attest [...]. Moreover our area is distant from the town where we could sell our produce.[^589]

In the face of poor land quality, the question of farm size acquired special importance. Only farms of large acreage could sustain themselves on poor soils. Unfortunately, this circumstance was not taken into account, especially on government estates. The size of land allocated to a settler was consistently made dependent on the capital he brought with him. The situation on private estates was different. Settlers received there as a rule large allotments which were measured out when the village was founded.[^590] The conviction prevailed that it was better for a village to have 30 equally matched farmers than for 10 prosperous ones to coexist alongside 20 beggars. Landowners set aside in each colony plots of varying sizes, from 4–5 morgas to 1 New Polish hide.[^591] Farms on lands already previously cleared usually had between 15 and 20 morgas in area, while those established on forested lands requiring clearing were smaller (7–10 morgas). The average size of a colonist's farm in the Kingdom of Poland was estimated in 1842 at 31.6 morgas, and in 1859 at 19.9 morgas.[^592] This change indicates a clear diminution in farm size, mainly as a result of family divisions, and explains one of the fundamental reasons for the appearance of the next settlement wave, in which after 1864 the sons of colonists from villages lying to the west of the Vistula set out eastward (Lublin region, Volhynia) in search of areas to settle.[^593]

Until the promulgation of the enfranchisement edict in 1864, settlers could not become owners of the land they used. This situation corresponded to the expectations of the landowners, who were primarily interested in receiving rent over a longer time perspective. The settlement of colonists as leaseholders reduced the risk associated with farming within the framework of the demesne farm. Although this form continued to exist, a factor of steady cash inflow appeared in the economic calculation. The widespread granting of rent-free years for the improvement of the land demonstrates that private estate owners were not interested in quick profit. It must be noted, however, that the absence of rent revenues in the first period after the settling of colonists was to some extent compensated by the entry payment, paid at the time of taking up the land, usually in the full amount, less often in instalments over 2–3 years. Numerous purchase and sale transactions of land by colonists before 1864 were in essence transactions for the purchase and sale of the right to perpetual lease. The contracting parties settled upon a price between themselves, of which 10%, as laudemium, was received by the landowner, who also had to formally consent to the transaction taking place. An essential element of it was the expression of consent by all farmers in the colony to the dwelling among them of the new occupant. With varying degrees of diligence was observed the issuance by estate managers of consents, which unambiguously determined that the colonist had accepted all obligations arising from the use of the land.

The financial obligations of a colonist were described in detail in the settlement contract and consisted, after the expiry of the rent-free years, of a rent payment to the landowner and public taxes. On private estates, however, the form of feudal rent payment deriving from the initial period of introducing leasehold farming survived. It consisted of a twofold, and sporadically threefold, mode of payment. In addition to rent in money, colonists were also obliged to perform foot or draught labour service during the harvest or at the peak of other field work (haymaking, potato digging). The type and extent of this obligation was precisely specified in the settlement contract. Most frequently colonists were obliged to perform foot labour, with a scythe. Customary form was taken by in-kind dues (capons, geese, eggs), required by the manor in a strictly specified quantity, usually at Easter. The level of rent was determined in accordance with the acreage of the land, though one can also speak of certain regional patterns. In the oldest contracts relating to settlements founded on forest lands, provision was made for a final measurement of the land after the completion of clearing. In Prussian times a bureaucratic solution became widespread whereby the basis for calculating the rent was the annual value of all harvests made on the farm. It was envisaged that the colonist had to allocate approximately 2/3 of his income to the maintenance of his family and the satisfaction of needs connected with running the farm. A further approximately 10–16% of income was reserved for unforeseen farm expenses. From the remaining part of the colonist's income the rent was calculated, which in villages situated on poorer soils was described by the inhabitants as crushing.[^594] These bureaucratic rules were changed in 1819. The quality of the lands was then adopted as the basis for calculating the rent, and the level of rent was to remain fixed for 30 years. After the expiry of this period, the government of the Kingdom of Poland declared new calculations of rent charges. Their basis was to be the average prices of rye in the preceding thirty years and the quality of the land. The new rent rates were to be in force for the next 30 years.[^595] On private estates the level of rent calculated per morga was the same for all farmers in the colony. Its value was stated in the settlement contract as binding on all, regardless of differences in land quality between individual colonists. The calculation of the rent amount resembled the method used on government estates and was presumably modelled on it. In 1846, in the model settlement contract recommended to landowners, Seweryn Biernacki proposed determining the level of rent on the basis of current rye prices and recalculating it after 20 years. At that point, however, the average rye prices from the preceding 20 years obtained on the markets in the two towns located closest to the given colony were to be taken into account.[^596] The reference to the local grain market, above all to the prices of the universally cultivated rye, explains the very great differentiation in rent levels. Significant differences existed even within the same estates.[^597] In Płock district the level of the annual rent was 50% higher than the average in the Kingdom of Poland.[^598] This compelled many local smallholder colonists and cottagers to seek another place of residence.[^599]

Colonists were subject to public taxes, of which the chimney tax (podymne) had the oldest pedigree, being levied on each chimney projecting above the ridge of the roof. In the times of the Duchy of Warsaw an increased chimney tax was in force — a burdensome legacy from the Prussian period but one that augmented the treasury's revenues. Under the decrees of 24 March 1809 and 23 December 1811, a farmer using one hide of land was subject to a 50% surcharge on the chimney tax, the user of two hides paid double the chimney tax, and the farmer working three hides paid the chimney tax at 200% of the basic rate.[^600] The regulations did not, however, apply to newly founded colonies.[^601] The tsarist edict of 1858 reformed the chimney tax, establishing new rates and introducing two annual instalments, payable in March and September. In the times of the Kingdom of Poland the szarwark also came into force, that is the obligation to maintain the passability of local roads, enforced to varying degrees, usually 2–4 days.[^602] Farmers were obliged to perform draught szarwark, while cottagers were obliged to perform foot szarwark. If the lands on which a colony was situated were subject to tithe, the tithe also applied to the colonists.[^603] This tax was abolished in 1864.

Particularly severe, especially in the initial period of farming, were various kinds of natural disasters, such as spring floods, frequent in the valleys of the Vistula, Warta, and Pilica. Their effects were often as described in 1840 by the pastor David Bergemann from Pilica.

> After the flooding of the Vistula [the colonists] lost everything, they fled from their homes in order to save at least their lives; now they are forced to beg for bread.[^604]

Despite fairly rigorous fire protection regulations, the red cockerel visited villages with some frequency, destroying, like floods, the settlers' life's work. In the settlement contracts of the Olędrzy there was usually recorded a clause providing for assistance from the landowner in rebuilding the farm in the form of free timber. In estate inventories from the 1830s and 1840s one can find mentions of fire-fighting equipment owned by the colonists: boat hooks, ladders, fire buckets.[^605] Colonists also tried to insure each other against the effects of fires, but these were isolated cases.[^606] Of wider scope, however, was the creation of regulations (fire ordinances), compliance with which was obligatory for all colony residents. Such regulations required each household to be equipped with appropriate equipment, obligated mutual assistance, and also precisely defined the scope of compensation to be contributed by the farmers who had not been struck by misfortune.[^607] Various kinds of assistance rendered by landowners to colonists affected by some misfortune or elemental disaster often depended on what kind of farmers those in need of support were. Assistance was usually not refused to good farmers. The successful establishment and functioning of a colony could be expected above all in private estates. Social control of the inhabitants and the principle of solidarity in regulating dues to the landowner made it possible to fulfill obligations.

*Der Erste hungert tot, der Zweite leidet Not, erst der Dritte bekommt Brot*
(The first dies of hunger, the second suffers poverty, only the third has bread).[^608]

This proverb from the eighteenth century, known among German settlers in southern and eastern Europe, aptly captures the material situation of three generations of colonists. Even if the time from the founding of a settlement to the building of a profitable farm did not always fill three generations' lives, the beginnings of farming were undoubtedly a serious trial for the settlers. They did not always manage to overcome their hardships and remain in their first place of settlement. For the vast majority of settlers, the only source of income after taking possession of land was its clearance. The funds obtained from the sale of stumps and potash derived from burning lower-grade timber were sufficient only for the most modest existence.

In the years 1842–1848, the total number of colonist farms in the Kingdom of Poland increased by 12.9%, while the area of land they cultivated decreased from 281,164 morgen to 261,565 morgen, that is by 6.9%.[^609] (For the years 1842–1859 these figures are: 11.2% and 4% respectively.)

Between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula, this phenomenon occurred on a considerably smaller scale. In the years 1842–1848 the number of colonist farms decreased by 1.6%, and the area of land by 3.4%. The decrease in the area of land remaining in colonists' use was explained by the dishonest practice of landowners, who enlarged their manor farms at the expense of parts of colonist settlements.[^610] This practice could develop especially when the colonists' land was forested, or comprised less accessible wasteland (floodplain or waterlogged terrain). Although such cases are attested in the sources, they are exceptions. The incorporation of wastelands into manor farmland took place primarily at the expense of the land of serf peasants. In this way, in the years 1846–1864, approximately 5% of this category of land passed into manor use.[^611] The colonists' holdings were fairly well protected by the provisions of the settlement contracts.
