To every attempt to violate the terms, the colonists responded with the threat of abandoning the village.[^612] Friction arose most frequently at the sale of estates in which colonists were settled, since new purchasers often tried to treat their rights as non-binding.

The decrease in the area of arable land cultivated by colonists in the years 1842–1848, combined with the simultaneous increase in the area of forests and especially clearings, testifies to the great activity of colonist-clearers who, aiming for quick profit, cleared forests and undergrowth without erecting residential or farm buildings. They took up small allotments of 4–6 morgen, usually adjacent to one another, which they cleared of trees and undergrowth. After completing the clearance they sold the entire acreage, ready for cultivation. In this way, among other things, the last larger forest complexes in the Duchy of Łowicz were deforested.[^613] Similarly, on the southern borders of Kalisz gubernia, in the upper course of the Warta and Pilica rivers, landowners settled Polish peasants as leaseholders on land cleared by the colonists.[^614] On the scale of the Kingdom of Poland, between 1848 and 1859, there was an almost precipitous decrease of 56% in the area of forests used by colonists. In the area between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula, where Olęder settlement had earlier made significant advances, 36.3% of forests were lost.[^615] Deforestation was closely related to the growing demand for timber in increasingly rapidly developing towns. The price of building timber rose quickly, which caused the colonists to begin managing rationally those forests that still remained in their use. In the 1830s it was written of the inhabitants of the Grabieniec colony in Zgierz county:

> That these small forests have been maintained until now must be attributed to the orderliness of the colonists, who seek help in them only when a fence, house or other building requires repair.[^616]

The surviving statistical materials allow us to characterize the agricultural economy of colonists in private estates between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula in the years 1842–1859.[^617] In 1842 there were 4,581 colonist farms in this area, constituting 57.5% of all such farms in the Kingdom of Poland. They encompassed 67.9% of all land used by colonists (190,048 morgen). The average farm size of 26.3 morgen was somewhat larger than the average for the Kingdom of Poland, which stood at 21.69 morgen, but there were significant differences between the western part (Kalisz gubernia) and the eastern part (Mazovian gubernia) of the described area. In the Kalisz region a colonist's farm averaged 18 morgen in area, while in Mazovian gubernia it was more than twice that — 39 morgen. There were several reasons for this differentiation. The Kalisz region had been settled by leaseholder-colonists much earlier, hence appropriate sites had been exploited sooner. The relatively good quality of the soils, particularly in the central and northern parts of Kalisz gubernia, allowed for the allocation of relatively small land allotments. The fragmentation of land within a single farm could also have been affected by family divisions, given that in the 1840s the second generation of settlers was beginning to farm. The structure of land use was also different.

In the Kalisz region, arable land predominated, occupying on average approximately 73% of the farm area. In Mazovian gubernia this figure reached just over 57%. The almost threefold predominance in the extent of clearings in Mazovia (25% of farm area, compared to 8.4% in the Kalisz region) indicates a process of acquiring land for agricultural use that was not yet complete. The absence of statistically comparable material for later years, comparable geographically and chronologically, does not allow us to trace the further course of the process of land differentiation in colonist farms.[^618] Both the number of colonist farms settled between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula, and the quantity of land concentrated within them, convincingly confirm the dominance of this area on the scale of the Kingdom of Poland. From this predominance also followed a very high share of individual types of land among the total area occupied by the corresponding categories. In 1842 arable land constituted 69.7% of all arable land cultivated by colonists in the Kingdom of Poland, meadows — 56%, clearings — 78%, fallow land — 66.5%, forests — 50.6%.[^619] The figures for 1848 reflect minor changes. Land used by colonists in Warsaw gubernia then constituted 75.7% of the total area of this category of land in the Kingdom of Poland. The share of arable land rose to 76%, and the share of clearings to almost 89%. The area of meadows increased decisively — to 77%, the percentage of forests decreased slightly — to 49.4%.

The basis of the agricultural economy of colonists between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula, as in the whole Kingdom of Poland, was grain cultivation, primarily rye and, to a lesser extent, wheat on better soils.[^620] In areas abounding in sandy soils, profits from crops were sufficient only for a very modest existence. Grain production on colonist farms faced significant competition from landowners who increased the acreage devoted to rye and potatoes, used in manor distilleries for the production of vodka.[^621] The mood prevailing among the colonists was well characterized by a contemporary observer: the colonies are well appointed, the land cultivated, and many settlers strive to arrange things as well as possible. Yet the slightest reason suffices — the sight of some undefined, easier earnings — for them to sell all their property and emigrate.[^622] Colonists farming on larger areas generally made successful attempts to adapt their farms to market requirements. This is demonstrated by the very small percentage of transactions selling farms with an area of more than 30 morgen.[^623] In the years 1842–1848 on colonist farms between the Prosna and the Pilica, the area of meadows increased by 2%, the area of clearings by 1.8%, and the area of fallow land by 0.3%. The last of the mentioned figures can be misleading. By fallow land, wasteland, one then understood not only land in no way utilized, but often also vegetable gardens, orchards and even pastures.[^624]

The strongest tendency, however, was the growth of livestock raising, which became an important source of income in the colonists' economy. Records from the 1840s also unambiguously indicate the growing role of vegetable growing and fruit growing, especially in colonies located near Warsaw. To a somewhat lesser extent this phenomenon concerned the surroundings of Łódź and Kalisz. In the mid-1840s, in the territories between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula, crop rotation began to become widespread. Large-scale cultivation of root crops (potatoes, sugar beets) and grasses was considered the basis of sound farming. In propagating new plants and methods of cultivation, estate leaseholders, educated estate managers (economists), and sheep breeders played a large role. For many of them the Kingdom of Poland constituted an ideal place of permanent residence. They themselves sought employment opportunities, cited their many years of experience, and presented references.[^625] An eloquent declaration was made in March 1855 by Johan Boenken, born in Prussia in the Regency of Gabin (Gąbin). He was an estate manager, married, with four children, and possessed no property:

> that since I have married in this country and already have several children [...] and apart from that having no prospects in the Prussian country, I therefore wish once and for all to remain in the Kingdom of Poland [...].[^626]

It should be noted that interest in administering estates in the territory of the Kingdom of Poland appeared already in the 1820s, which was undoubtedly connected with the expanding leaseholder settlement, which had rules already known to those offering their services. Advertisements from persons willing to work on estates between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula began to appear in the official press.

> A person furnished with sufficient experience, who has already been engaged in estate management in Silesia and here in the Kingdom of Poland for 20 years and possesses sufficient knowledge both in agricultural and forest economics, is fluent in the Polish language and in accounts, wishes to enter service in this country from St. John's Day of the current year as a commissioner of noble estates.[^627]

Examples of leaseholder farming were also drawn from the bordering Prussian provinces.[^628] In turn, estate owners developing sheep breeding were very interested in acquiring shepherds, especially those familiar with the breeding of finer races. Already in the 1830s there were many of them in the territory of Kalisz province, among others in the communes of Milejów and Dominikowice.[^629] In many noble estates, particularly along the northern border of the Kingdom, rational forest management was undertaken in the 1840s. In 1843, twelve colonists from Prussia decided to settle in the newly founded colony of Marzyczyn in the commune of Wierzbie, in Kujawy county. Each of them received 5 morgen of land. Almost all were sawyers and timber cutters.[^630]

Livestock raising developed in German settlers' farms first as an occupation accompanying crop farming, up to and including becoming an alternative to it. Colonists drew upon the patterns that had once been practiced in their homeland and upon those practiced in the Olęder farms that had existed since the end of the eighteenth century. Dairy cattle breeding combined with the production of butter and cheese rose to the status of a symbol characterizing the colonists' economy, especially in villages located in the Vistula valley.[^631] For example, in villages lying in the Gostynin and Sochaczew counties, the breeding of valuable cattle of the Żuława breed was widespread on a large scale.[^632] It constituted the main source of income for the inhabitants of these areas. In the 1840s in Sochaczew county, one colonist farm had on average 19 cows.[^633] The colonists' farming is well characterized by the number of draft animals per farm. In 1842 there were nearly 10,000 horses on colonist farms in the Kingdom of Poland, which meant an average holding of 1.23 horses per farm. This figure decreased slightly in 1848 (1.1), to reach 1.37 in 1859. In the area between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula, where 67.7% (6,657 head) of all horses kept by colonists were found, the same tendency was observed. In the years 1842–1848 the horse population fell by 17.2%, which meant an average decline from 1.45 to 1.03 horses per farm.[^634] A strong regional disproportion was visible in this. In Mazovian gubernia an average of just over 3 horses fell to each farm, whereas in the Kalisz region this average was 0.36 horses. The cattle population of oxen followed a similar pattern. Almost three-quarters (4,627 head) of these animals kept by colonists in the Kingdom of Poland were found in the territories west of the Vistula. In 1842, in Mazovian gubernia an average of 2 head of these animals fell per farm, in Kalisz gubernia — 0.36 head. In the years 1842–1859, the population of oxen kept by colonists in the Kingdom of Poland increased by 25%, and in the territories between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula — by 15% (up to 1848). It may be assumed that the lower number of draft animals in the western part of the Kingdom of Poland was connected with the smaller average acreage of farms there. Keeping an ox, and all the more a horse, was expensive. In the case of smaller farms it was more rational to use neighborly assistance than to maintain draught animals year-round. Cow keeping remained at an almost constant level until the end of the 1840s. On the scale of the entire Kingdom of Poland, colonists kept 15,500–16,000 of these animals. About 70–73% of this number were on farms between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula. In the years 1848–1859 the cow population almost doubled, which was connected with the growing demand for dairy products among the population of developing towns. The dynamic development of cow breeding is also indicated by the growing number of calves

(3,800 head in 1848 and 8,100 in 1859).[^635] A similar growth dynamic was achieved by pig keeping on colonist farms. In 1848, 16,400 pigs were kept in colonists' farmsteads in the Kingdom of Poland, of which 65.7% fell to the territories lying west of the Vistula and Pilica.[^636] In the following decade the increase in the population of these animals was even faster, but the absence of statistical data does not allow its estimation in relation to Kalisz and Warsaw gubernias. The growing importance of pig keeping and the rapid growth of the population is demonstrated by the sale-purchase contracts for land in the Kalisz region analyzed further below, in which almost all maintenance clauses indicated the necessity of keeping pigs.[^637] The branch of livestock raising that developed fastest was sheep farming. From 16,900 sheep in 1842 and 21,100 in 1848, the sheep population on colonist farms reached 168,000 in 1859, of which approximately 72–75% were grazed in the territories between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula. As the most spectacular example of colonists' adaptation to market conditions is considered the extraordinarily dynamic development of sheep breeding in Lipno county, on the right bank of the Vistula (Płock gubernia), where in the years 1848–1859 the sheep population grew by nearly a hundredfold (from 1,526 to 145,542 head). A better breed of fine-voiced sheep brought in from Silesia was propagated, and additional meadows and pastures were leased from manor estate owners.[^638] This sudden increase was a reaction to the slowdown in cotton imports for the textile industry of the Kingdom of Poland, caused first by the crisis of 1857–1858, and after it by the American Civil War. In these conditions, the breeders of the Lipno region became important suppliers of wool to the Łódź industry, finding buyers also in the Białystok region (Supraśl, Choroszcz) and in Toruń, Poznań, and Wrocław.[^639] After the January Uprising and the resumption of cotton supplies, the sheep population fell sharply. On a smaller scale, but with a similar dynamic, sheep farming developed in the counties of Łęczyca, Konin, and Rawa, in which already at the beginning of the 1840s the sheep population exceeded 16,000 head.[^640] This dynamic development of animal husbandry was connected with the expansion of the area of meadows and pastures and the growing care for their quality. It should also be remembered that a common practice was the grazing of pigs in forests. Contemporary commentators drew attention to the advantages of the multifaceted development of German colonist farms, which ensured the achievement of profits, contrasting them with peasant farms focused almost exclusively on the poorly profitable production of grain.[^641]

Already in the 1840s, land in the Kingdom, and especially in the territories situated west of the Vistula, had become a commodity in the capitalist sense.[^642] In the 1830s–1850s the average price of land was approximately 30 rubles per morgen, meaning the price of one włóka (a larger land unit) was approximately 900 rubles. There were, however, very large price fluctuations (500–1,200 rubles), even within the same county.[^643] In the Kalisz region land prices in the 1830s–1840s ranged from 32 to 120 złoty per morgen, and in the 1850s and 1860s from 20 to 70 rubles per morgen. Analysis of land sale-purchase contracts in the colonies indicates that here too, as in the centrally located counties of the Kingdom, the value of a settlement was not affected by the buildings situated on it. Here too one can point to differences of up to 100% in the valuation of plots of equal area located in the same colony. Analysis of land sale-purchase contracts in the colonies of Kalisz county in the years 1832–1864 proves that it was an almost universal phenomenon for land to be transferred in this form to successors: sons or sons-in-law of the farmers.[^644] This can be called inheritance through the purchase of leasehold rights. A constant element of such contracts was the imposition on the purchaser of the obligation of lifelong maintenance of the selling party (parents, parents-in-law).[^645] The usually very wide scope of maintenance obligations was specified in detail. The basic provision was rye, averaging 6 Viertel per year (1 Viertel = 13.9 litres) and oats — averaging 2 Viertel per year. Barley (1 Viertel) appears less frequently and in small quantities, wheat, millet, and spelt only sporadically. Beyond grain, the guaranteed delivery of peas was common — averaging

7 garniec per year (1 garniec = 4 litres), buckwheat groats (over 4 garniec) and millet groats (over 3 garniec). An important item in maintenance provisions was salt, of which an average of 3 garniec per year was demanded. In almost all agreements the seller of the farm demanded for himself each year a young pig or its cash equivalent (in the 1850s and 1860s this was an amount from 1.3 to 2.0 rubles). A common maintenance provision was the year-round upkeep of cattle, generally 1, less often 2 cows. The agreement stipulated that these animals would become the property of the maintaining party after the decent burial of the last of the parents or parents-in-law. Sporadically one can find the requirement of keeping sheep, or several head of poultry (hens, geese). A frequent requirement was the delivery of rye and oat straw. Sale-purchase contracts for land from the 1830s and 1840s abound in maintenance provisions obligating buyers to allocate to sellers a portion of land for cultivation: potatoes, beets, cabbage, swede, flax, hops, with the stipulation that the strips intended for this purpose be just as fertile as the strips of the new owners. A frequent practice was also the securing by the sellers of land of such provisions as: a cart ride to church, to the mill, to the town, sometimes with the specification of frequency (2–4 times per year), more often without limitations. With the requirement of providing accommodation, often a separate room, was associated the obligation to supply firewood and kindling. In individual cases, maintenance agreements mention the division of cherry, plum, and pear fruit. Several trees of the mentioned varieties were cultivated and the provisions extended even to the fruits of wild pear trees. This confirms the opinions of the county authorities about the low level of fruit growing in Kalisz county.[^646] Detailed provisions concerned the payment of compensation to siblings. These were realized in cash, often in installments. Minor siblings received their share of the inheritance upon reaching adulthood. An interesting customary practice was the obligation on the person inheriting the farm to pay for one day's wedding celebrations for each sister, and to purchase appropriate clothing for each brother. Only in just under 1% of the examined sale-purchase contracts for land in Kalisz county in the years 1832–1864 were farm implements (a cart, plough, cultivator, harrows, hoes, forks, barrels) listed as the subject of the transaction. Not once in the body of alienated property were household items or clothing mentioned. This may serve to establish that the only thing of real value to the colonists was the land and the crops harvested from it.

Official government documents for a long time spoke only of the expectations held of colonists, in whom one wished to see orderly and hardworking peasants. The actual results of their farming were only mentioned in passing. Only from the mid-1850s did opinions begin to appear more frequently — both official and colloquially expressed — on the state of the economy and the prosperity of colonists. Their authors sought in various ways to explain the causes of the relatively good situation of the German peasants and to contrast them with Polish peasants. Characterizing in 1859 the state of rural farming in Kalisz county, its head wrote:

> The condition of the nobles in general, with very few exceptions, is not merely not prosperous but in the vast majority of cases critical, and that of the serf peasants is wretched; they cannot be supported by the nobles in cases of misfortune, and thereby decline and must abandon their farms. The condition and prosperity of foreign colonists as well as native leaseholders cannot be compared with the situation of the serf peasants; for the colonists as well as the leaseholders work at the cultivation of the land more assiduously, and indeed with a different conviction, and therefore derive greater benefits from agriculture; besides, in cases of misfortune, of disappointment in the expected benefits from agriculture, they still have the opportunity of earning a living through whatever they have easier access to, as nothing stands in their way since they depend on themselves alone. [...] Those rendering serf labor, having no personal interest in improving the farm, being unsettled, can barely manage to feed themselves and pay taxes.[^647]

It should be remembered that this opinion referred to what was considered one of the economically leading regions of the Kingdom of Poland, with the highest ratio of colonists and leaseholders among the peasantry overall, maintained in the years 1853–1864 at the level of 55%–56.5%.[^648] In a similar tone were expressed in 1863 views on the colonists inhabiting Płock gubernia:

> their existence is incomparably better than that of the peasants; this should be attributed more to industry [i.e., enterprise — note K.P.W.], diligence, and a better understanding of the benefits of ownership.[^649]

In the method of soil cultivation there were no significant differences between colonists and Polish peasants: the manner of tilling the soil is ordinary, with a plough, cultivator, and iron harrow.[^650] The benefits of technical progress were used throughout the area between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula to a similar extent.[^651] The success of the colonists was therefore not a derivative of technical progress or the introduction of new crops, but resulted from a different approach to farming, treating one's own farm as an enterprise that must generate income in the form of means to maintain one's own family and livestock and to supply goods intended for sale. It was estimated, for example, that in villages inhabited by German peasants, in the years 1843–1862 the value of livestock tripled.[^652] In February 1865, just after assuming the presidency of Łódź, Edmund Pohlens characterized the German settlers in Łódź county as follows:

> they are universally distinguished by diligence, orderliness, good farm management, morality, and are for the most part prosperous people.[^653]

Such an opinion accompanied the German peasants in central Poland from at least the 1850s, when, having settled, they were achieving visible results of their labor. The Polish serf peasant could be and was perceived as a component of a larger whole: the manor farm on private or crown estates. This image was not altered by the leaseholder-peasants, whose number, from the 1830s onward, grew slowly but steadily, since their tie to the estates in which they lived was stronger than the aspiration to greater economic independence.

Colonists participated in the market, differed in appearance, dress, and language. Villages inhabited by German peasants drew attention by the distinctiveness of their buildings, very legible in the landscape and arousing interest in the inhabitants. Oskar Kolberg, quoting an opinion from 1863 about the areas between Łęczyca and Łódź, wrote:

> Leśmierz [...] and beyond it some other village; the rest are colonies scattered here among meadows, there on a hill, characteristic with their straight-cut gable ends. In this style are built all the houses of the German colonists.[^654]

In almost all the assessments of older historiography, the evaluative effect regarding German settlement was achieved through comparison of the settlers' economy with that of the local population. This was not justified, and for the pre-emancipation period is in principle unachievable. Until the emancipation reform, settlers were privileged leaseholders. They were therefore in a considerably more favorable situation than the serf peasants. The years of freedom from dues was a great relief, as was the absence of constraints imposed by the three-field system. It is considered that the more favorable results visible in the economic balance were achieved by the settlers not so much thanks to superior techniques of soil cultivation as thanks to the more versatile profile of their farms. The use of new tools or methods of cultivation was a result of general economic transformations, not the result of individual innovative solutions.[^655] It seems that the finding of W. Rusiński, referring to Olęder farms, can be justifiably extended to all German colonies and the entire pre-emancipation period. According to Rusiński, the assertion about the greater productivity of colonist farms would be justified if it could be shown that under identical conditions and with the same feudal burdens, colonists achieved better economic results than Polish peasants.[^656] The emancipation abolished this difference, but the disparities in the level of farming could still persist, since colonists were not infrequently three generations deep into practicing and accustomed to a different system. One must also point to the resistance of Polish peasants to changes favorable to them. In 1861, the peasants of the village of Olesiec in the Chocz estate of Count Raczyński refused to agree to the leasehold conversion of their farms, claiming that

> they want no change and prefer and wish to remain as heretofore under serf conditions.[^657]

This was not an isolated stance. Very soon, however, this situation changed. The emancipation edict of 1864 granted land ownership rights to all peasants, including colonists.[^658] The interventions undertaken with the authorities by landowners who had settled colonists shortly before the issuance of the edict and who demanded the return of the granted land or payment for it, as well as the ambiguities concerning settlers possessing foreign citizenship, meant that the ownership of land was ultimately regulated by the edict of 9 May 1866.[^659] It stipulated that those colonists who were Russian subjects or who applied for acceptance of Russian subjecthood would become owners of land under the terms specified in the emancipation edict. The final deadline for submitting such applications was 1 May 1868. Colonists who were Russian subjects confirmed their status by a resolution of the commune assembly.[^660] German farmers almost without exception accepted Russian subjecthood, becoming owners of the land they cultivated. Very favorable for the colonists was the granting to them of ownership also of land leased from the manor.[^661] The emancipation reform regulated their legal and property situation and freed them from various forms of feudal dependency. After 1864, intensive migration to Lublin gubernia, Chełm gubernia, and to Volhynia began. An important asset of those territories proved to be the large areas of forests, which already in 1865 attracted settlers from the Kingdom who were experienced in the felling and clearing of trees.[^662] The landowners who in the large-scale felling and sale of timber saw a rescue from bankruptcy were interested in acquiring them. Meanwhile, for the next generation of German settlers, who could no longer find land between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula, the opportunity opened up to acquire cheaper land beyond the Vistula and to farm profitably.

## Chapter V: Religious Life and Education in the Lives of German Colonists

Religious life and education constituted two areas of the settlers' life that acquired an institutional dimension. Their rank in the life of the colonists was very high, as is confirmed by the regulation of these matters in all settlement contracts without exception concluded in the last decades of the existence of the First Polish Republic, as well as in the later Prussian legislation, that of the Duchy of Warsaw, and of the Kingdom of Poland. Among colonists and settlers living for a long time in the diaspora, a certain pattern of existence developed, concentrated on the immediate surroundings and separating them from the external world. The efforts to preserve the mother tongue and religious tradition — predominantly Pietist, inherited from ancestors — combined with the concern to preserve cantor positions and schooling, were, unlike in the territories of the Prussian parish, rather an expression of traditionalist consciousness than a sense of national identity, or indeed of nationalistic separation from the Polish or Slavic surroundings, marked by a sense of mission.[^663]

From all relations of contemporaries without exception, the image of an isolated way of life of the colonists emerges.[^664] The vast majority of rural settlers belonged to the Evangelical-Augsburg Church. Other Protestant denominations were represented to a small degree. Reformed Evangelicals (Calvinists) coming mainly from Franconia arrived in greater numbers at the Pilica estates of Prince Adam of Württemberg, where pastoral care was exercised over them by a Reformed pastor from Warsaw, and in the area of Konin (Holendry Kiszewskie) and in several settlements in Łask county (Pożdżenice) and Brzeziny county (Wiączyń).[^665] The Mennonites, inhabiting the Vistula valley and scattered point-by-point in settlements between the Warta and the Bzura, then the Herrnhuters (Moravian Brethren) — in Lwówek, Nowa Solna (Nowosolna) and several nearby villages — and from 1854 the tentatively appearing Baptists, sharply combated by Lutheran clergymen, completed this confessional mosaic. Among colonists, Catholics were almost never encountered.

Apart from the economic factor (the lower price of land redemption), a strong incentive encouraging migration to Polish lands was the freedom of religious worship and public rights for non-Catholics (dissenters), adopted by the Polish Parliament (Sejm) in 1768. The so-called Warsaw Treaty guaranteed dissenters the possibility of founding parishes, building churches, houses of prayer, and schools.[^666] The Constitutions of the Sejm of 1768 stipulated precisely:

> dissenters of both confessions are henceforth declared free from paying the said *jura stolae*. Catholic Roman clergy shall not under any pretext demand any donation, whether from the nobility, from townspeople, or from dissenter people of the rural estate.[^667] Marriages between persons of different religion [...] and evangelical of both confessions, shall not be hindered or impeded by anyone. Offspring born *ex mixto matrimonio*, sons are to be educated in the father's faith, and daughters in the mother's faith.[^668]

While consensus reigned in the case of inheriting confession from a parent, in matters of payments, the collection of tithes, and Christmas offerings, disputes arose on numerous occasions. In 1801, the Prussian authorities changed the obligation of tithe paid in kind to a church tax in cash.[^669] In the same year the Evangelical population was freed from all payments and dues to the Catholic Church. After 1807 there was a return to the tradition based on linking ecclesiastical obligations to the land, not to the person occupying it (their confession).

The virtually explosive increase in the number of Olęder settlements, visible in the years 1768–1793, could have been connected with the proclamation of religious freedom. It had already happened in the first half of the eighteenth century that owners of landed estates and leaseholders of royal lands allowed Evangelical newcomers to build a house of prayer, which also served as a school. The distinguishing feature of the building was a cross placed on the roof. If conditions permitted, a wooden bell tower was also built.[^670] Not infrequently the settlers received free building materials.[^671] There were, however, also long-lasting disputes with district governors (starostowie) or Roman Catholic clergymen who were hostile to non-Catholics.[^672] Antoni Dąmbski, the hereditary lord of the Iłów estate, having regard for the interest of the local parish priest, obligated the Olęders living in villages near Iłów to enter into matrimony and have their children baptized in the Catholic church in Iłów. In practice, the majority of colonists fulfilled these rites in Toruń.[^673] Dąmbski declared the establishment of an Evangelical parish in Iłów, but it was founded only in 1775, after the estate was purchased by the Sochaczew castellan, Adam Lasocki. The new owner founded it on the territory of the village of Iłów and endowed it with one łan of land, which comprised 20 morgen of arable land, 6 morgen of meadows and pastures, and 4 morgen of garden. Moreover, the parish received land for building a church and for use as a cemetery. Lasocki also undertook to pay the pastor a salary of 90 taler per year and to supply him with firewood.[^674] The Evangelical-Augsburg parish in Iłów was the first and for a long time the only one in central Poland; it was therefore later called the mother parish (*Muttergemeinde*) of the Vistula region. It was attended by Evangelicals living dozens of kilometres from Iłów and equally far from the Vistula valley.[^675]

Meanwhile their numbers grew, primarily as a result of the influx of new settlers.

In the oldest settlement documents (Ruda Bugaj, Wierzbno, 1782), the Olęders were permitted to bring a clergyman to the village twice a year, and at the same time a fee was established for religious services (confession, three marriage banns, wedding, baptism), amounting to 1 złoty for each of these acts. The settlers regarded the limitation of pastoral visits to twice a year as not fully consistent with the principle of religious freedom. This was probably not justified, since the pastor of the then only parish in Iłów covered an enormous territory, in which the faithful lived in great dispersal and at distances exceeding 100 km. The pastor was constantly travelling, and visiting the same settlement twice a year required no small effort.[^676] In 1797, the Olęders of Sadów, Wiączemin and Czermno complained that *um jetzt eine Taufe oder Nachtmahl abzuhalten, wir 20 bis 30 Meilen reisen müssen*.[^677]

They travelled to Iłów or Toruń. The more distant Słońsk was rarely visited by the pastor, who did not lead services, attended funerals only sporadically, and left the preparation of children for confirmation to the cantor. From time to time the pastor would inspect the school.[^678] Many settlement contracts specified the purpose of clerical visits (baptism and holy communion, usually combined with confirmation). Due to the absence of nearby Evangelical churches, baptisms, marriages and deaths were registered in Roman Catholic parishes, with baptisms and funerals permitted to be conducted by cantors.[^679] In February 1786, the cantor of the Ruda Bugaj settlement delivered to Pastor Johann Christian Bücher, rector of the Evangelical-Augsburg parish in Iłów, a letter from Walenty Chobrzyński, owner of the Brużyca Wielka estate, concerning the provision of pastoral care for the Olęders living in his lands. The congregation had identified J. Ch. Bücher as its pastor and confessor. Since Chobrzyński had guaranteed in the founding contract of the settlement that an Evangelical clergyman would be able to visit its inhabitants twice a year, Bücher agreed to fulfil this role. The Iłów rector also stipulated that the congregation must arrange its marriage ceremonies and children's baptisms in such a way *that it is I who can administer them*.[^680]

The Olęders also taxed themselves for the benefit of the church at the rate of 4 złoty per holding, payable in two annual instalments. Fees in respect of *iura stolae* were to be the subject of later arrangements. These customary fees were set according to the wealth of the parishioners. It was not until the 1850s and 1860s that the social differentiation of these fees became smaller. In the Pabianice parish, relatively prosperous owing to the presence of a group of local textile entrepreneurs, a schedule of fees was introduced in 1864. A funeral with a farewell address in church and the registration of the death certificate cost 5 roubles; a funeral with a prayer in church — 3 roubles; a funeral with a graveside prayer — 2 roubles; a wedding — 2.55 roubles.[^681] In the colonies it was common practice to charge for pastoral services (baptisms, weddings, funerals) a basic fee plus a supplement proportional to the area of land held.[^682]

The religious life of the Olęders was concentrated almost entirely within the settlements they inhabited. In almost every settlement contract, settlers were guaranteed the possibility of building a school, which served as a prayer house. In Prussian times, a plan for such a building was even drawn up. In smaller settlements, gatherings for services were held in the house of one of the farmers. Almost all religious functions were performed by a lay preacher (cantor), who was also the schoolteacher. This organisation of religious life (the cantoral system) was a distinctive feature of the structure of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church in the lands of central Poland, but it also existed in sparsely populated Scandinavia and in Estonia.[^683] It arose from the dispersal of settlements inhabited by Lutherans and met their spiritual needs reasonably well. In 1804 a parish was established in Płock, a year later in Wyszogród, and in 1821 in Włocławek. In 1827 a Lutheran parish was erected in Gąbin, and two years later in Gostynin, which was also connected with a lively influx of cloth-makers and the establishment of factory settlements in those towns. In 1837 a parish was established in Pilica, and five years later its filial in Stara Iwiczna.[^684] A year later the Evangelical-Augsburg parish was founded in Nieszawa and in Nowosolna near Łódź, where Lutheran craftsmen and the surrounding colonists had their own church and parish already from 1827.[^685]

In Kujawy the first parish was Babiak, founded in 1796. This was connected with a plan to establish a cloth-making settlement by Bonawentura Raczyński, owner of the Ozorzyn estate. The landlord endowed the parish with arable land (7.5 morgen) and a meadow (2 morgen) and assisted in the construction of a wooden church. He also undertook to supply the pastor with 6 bushels of rye, 6 barrels of beer and 6 cartloads of firewood annually.[^686] At the turn of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries there were schools in 12 villages of the parish. In 1865, in the same area there were 8 cantorships and 7 German elementary schools. In 1801 the Chodecz parish was established, in 1827 Przedecz, in 1840 Sompolno, and six years earlier Holendry Maślane, as a filial of Konin, where a Lutheran parish had been founded in 1826.[^687] In the Kalisz region, the oldest parish was established in Władysławów in 1776. Its first rector, Martin Marggraf, travelled through settlements inhabited by Evangelicals not only in the Kalisz area but also in Kujawy. In 1780 a parish was erected in Stawiszyn. In Prussian times, thanks to the considerable commitment of the landlord Stadnicki, a parish was established in Grodziec.[^688] In general, the Prussian authorities were not interested in the formation of Evangelical congregations, even where the number of potential parishioners was significant.[^689] In 1808, through the efforts of the landlord Krzysztof Celiński, a parish was established in Prażuchy. It encompassed the settlements of: Ceków, Stary Ceków, Prażuchy, Pławnia, Zakrzyn, Kuźnice, Czachulec, Przespolew, Jaszczury.[^690] In the 1830s, Sadykierz and the colony of Poroże were incorporated within its boundaries.[^691] By the mid-nineteenth century, Prażuchy was a prosperous and well-organised parish. The pastor had a brick house, a barn and a cowshed. The cantor had a house and a barn; the sacristan — a small house and a building for storing the hearse. Within the parish there were 8 cemeteries. Of the faithful, the local rector wrote: *the people attend church diligently and observe the religious prescriptions*.[^692] In 1808 the landlord Wężyk founded a parish in Iwanowice. In 1818 it was transferred to Sobiesęki.[^693] The Prussian administration did not complete the construction of any Protestant church. One of the attempts was made in 1804 by the mayor of the Pabianice commune, Georg Mattner. Estimating the number of faithful at approximately 1,000 persons, he planned to erect, at a cost of 16,000 thalers, a church accommodating 600 people.[^694] The idea met with understanding from the provincial authorities, and Minister Otto von Voss, in a rescript of 9 August 1804, deemed it advisable to also build a rectory and a school building.[^695] The construction was never begun, however, and the idea was not revisited until the era of the Kingdom of Poland.[^696]

The organisation of almost all Evangelical-Augsburg parishes in central Poland encountered various difficulties — from the delimitation of their boundaries, the apportionment of the costs of building the church and maintaining the pastor, to the fees due to him in respect of *iura stolae*.[^697] This occurred despite the considerable, including financial, commitment of the government authorities of the Kingdom of Poland, who recognised that the still small number and great dispersal of Evangelicals made it difficult to incorporate them into the construction of churches.[^698] In parishes of a mixed, urban-rural character — and these constituted the decisive majority — friction sometimes arose between the faithful from both of these communities. In January 1817, Superintendent Karl Michael Sinell of Kalisz informed the Commission of the Kalisz Province that within a radius of 1 mile (1 mile = 7.5 km) from Pabianice there were 250 Evangelical families numbering 1,230 souls. He proposed uniting them within a church organisation by creating an independent parish. The Pabianice Evangelical-Augsburg congregation was erected in September 1818. It was composed of the inhabitants of the town and 27 surrounding villages, totalling 1,300 souls.[^699] In 1827 the parish numbered 320 families, of whom only 16 lived in Pabianice. The more significant concentrations of Lutherans at the time were the villages of: Bukowiec (64 families), Olechów (39), Pawlikowice (31), Markówka (25), Starowa Góra (24).[^700] In total, the Pabianice congregation at the end of the 1820s brought together Evangelicals from the town and from 32 villages and mill settlements. The numerical preponderance of the rural population persisted in the Pabianice parish until the mid-1860s. This was a phenomenon typical, until 1914, of all urban Evangelical-Augsburg parishes in the Kingdom of Poland, with the exception of Warsaw and Łódź. The establishment of the parish in Pabianice provoked protests among the rural settlers living at a greater distance from the town. They had indeed participated in the preparatory work and committed their own equipment to the hauling of construction materials, but they considered the financial contributions towards the building of the church an excessive burden. At the end of 1828, the village headman of Olechów stated that the contribution required from the farmers was almost equal to their annual rent. *We never truly intended to build such a church, from which even later, living more than two miles away, we can derive no convenience, as in every need we must bring the pastor from the nearer towns*.[^701]

The headman of Starowa Góra expressed a similar view, adding that

*the local colony, with its peaty and infertile soil, is in the worst and most wretched situation*.[^702]

In turn, the inhabitants of the Bukowiec colony demanded separation from the Pabianice parish. They explained that they had a school building with a spacious hall, in which there was a pulpit and an organ (presumably a harmonium).[^703] They themselves brought the pastor when he was needed.[^704] The Commission of the Kalisz Province, to which these complaints were referred, did not grant them, fearing similar actions from elsewhere.

The position of the highest government authorities of the Kingdom of Poland was unambiguous and resolute on the question of Evangelical congregations:

*providing religious service for the foreigners arriving in the country is one of the most powerful means contributing to the growth and stabilisation of settlements* [...].[^705]

In the summer of 1828, the mayor of Pabianice informed the General Consistory in Warsaw that in places distant from Pabianice, baptisms were being performed by unauthorised persons.

*Evangelical parishioners come from various parts of the world, it is indispensable that in the shortest time, in order to maintain religious order among the licentious parishioners, a suitable man be designated as shepherd of such a numerous flock as quickly as possible*, appealed to the ecclesiastical authorities the mayor, J. Sprzęglewski.[^706] The first permanent pastor of the Pabianice congregation was, from 1819, Samuel Fryderyk Jaekel. The brief period of his tenure fell at the height of the dispute over the location of the church. Not only did differences of opinion among the parishioners come to light, but also the pastor's temperament. During his sermons, which were often sharp, he was not averse to striking his fist on the pulpit or the altar, which displeased the Pabianice townspeople. *The townspeople of Pabianice, as people of greater refinement and education, were dissatisfied with the method of delivering sermons, which in their opinion were aimed at the tastes of the surrounding colonists*.[^707] *I am in a position to convey the truth to my audience without striking my fist on the pulpit or altar, as a tipsy man or a drunkard does in a tavern*

— argued the pastor, not accepted by the Pabianice townspeople despite the approval of the surrounding colonists.[^708] In 1825, Reverend Karl Gottlieb Bartsch, a member of the Evangelical Consistory in Warsaw, submitted to the KWK an opinion on the regulation of Evangelical parishes. In it he emphasised the reluctance of Evangelical settlers to bear the burdens associated with the erection and subsequent maintenance of parishes and clergy:

*[...] they do not wish to do anything until a regulation to which they must submit obliges them to do so, despite being convinced that such institutions are indispensably necessary for their own good*.[^709]

It must be added that these burdens were not onerous when distributed among the individual members of the congregation, since the budget of the KRPiS provided annually a sum of 20,000 złoty for the regulation of Evangelical parishes. Evangelical clergy were also paid from the treasury of the Kingdom of Poland.[^710] It is difficult to accuse the author of the report of lack of objectivity when he wrote:

*it is necessary to put an end once and for all to the long-entrenched abuses regarding the burial of the deceased in remote and self-established cemeteries*.[^711]

The regulation of cemeteries and the question of their supervision would continue to occupy the attention of the consistorial authorities into the 1840s, although the regulations on the appointment and composition of cemetery boards were formulated by the consistory in 1839.[^712] The construction of churches was accompanied by a collection announced through all the provincial commissions in the Evangelical parishes of the Kingdom of Poland.[^713] The Evangelical-Augsburg parishes established before 1830 in the area between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula were founded primarily with government assistance and the help of owners of private estates and towns, and were located — with the exception of Prażuchy (1808) and Sobiesęki (1818) — in towns.[^714] Only in the 1830s and 1840s, with the demographic growth of the German villages, did rural filiały begin to emerge (e.g. Holendry Maślane in the Konin parish; Józefów in the Stawiszyn parish, or Błędów in the Stara Iwiczna parish), and exceptionally independent parishes (Nowosolna, 1838). The decision to become independent was determined by the new congregation's ability to finance the parish, and one had also to reckon with the opposition of the local pastor and the consistory, generally disinclined to divide and financially weaken existing parishes. For several years the colonists of Nowosolna, the most populous Swabian village in the Kingdom of Poland, had been seeking independence. The Prussian authorities, in founding the colony, had promised to build a church and endow a pastor. This investment was never realised. Possessing their own prayer house, the colonists refused in 1826 to participate in the costs of building the Evangelical church in Łódź. A powerful argument in favour of the creation of their own parish was the neglect of the inhabitants of Nowosolna by the Łódź rector, Fryderyk Metzner. He was obliged to hold a service in the village every 2 weeks, but did so twice as infrequently,

*and in such haste that he would have only two verses sung to the glory of God, and likewise delivered a sermon, and in this way conducted services in several places on the same day*.[^715]

The consistory appointed an administrator in Nowosolna, who began teaching the farmers' children, and the farmers themselves began building a church. It was completed in 1838, equipping the church with all the necessary furnishings, bells and an organ.[^716] This attests to the wealth of the inhabitants of Nowosolna and its surroundings, among whom there were approximately 100 families owning no land but living *by some form of labour or craft*.[^717] As already mentioned, almost all Evangelical parishes located to the west of the Vistula were of a mixed, urban-rural character. Tkaczewska Góra, founded in 1804 under the name Neu-Württemberg on the lands of the Tkaczew estate economy, between the villages of Chociszew, Pustkowie Górne and the mill settlement of Rexul, was assigned by virtue of this location to the Evangelical parish in Ozorków.[^718] The Ozorków parish was founded and endowed through the efforts of the local landlord, Ignacy Starzyński, in 1817. To the same church the Evangelical-faith Olęders living in the Rexul mill contributed their dues.[^719] In turn, the inhabitants of Konstancja, numbering 100 persons at the time, declared payment in kind equivalent to 5 thalers for the maintenance of the Ozorków pastor, while those settled in the nearby Słowik undertook to pay 100 złoty in kind rather than in cash, *since the priest will collect various natural produce from us at the new year anyway*.[^720] The Evangelical parish in Brzeziny was also of a dual character. Already in 1818, local cloth-makers and settlers living in nearby colonies declared a contribution towards the maintenance of a pastor. The notarial document approving it was signed by colonists from Felicjanów, Żakowice, Teodorów, Głogowiec, Bogini, Laski and Adamów.[^721] Shortly afterwards, the inhabitants of Łaznowska Wola — who had possessed a prayer house since 1801 — were incorporated into the parish. In the 1850s they began efforts to separate from the Brzeziny parish, but were refused by the consistory and the gubernial authorities. Only after several years did the authorities change their position and decided on the construction of a church in Łaznowska Wola, but as a filial of the Brzeziny parish.[^722]

The organisation that drew colonists more actively into parish life was the church boards (dozory kościelne). The composition and principles for the appointment of boards at Evangelical parishes were regulated by the decree of the Governor of the Kingdom of Poland of 6 January 1824. A board had to consist of a minimum of three persons, and in such a composition, with the participation of the pastor, could make decisions concerning the parish. In parishes extending over many, often distant settlements, efforts were made to ensure that each of them had its representative on the board. This was the case, for example, in the Prażuchy parish, whose church board numbered six members in 1835, four of whom were farmers who had been settled for 12–20 years.[^723] However, despite the legal requirement, in many rural parishes boards did not exist.[^724] Nor was the matter of cemeteries settled everywhere; these, especially in small settlements, were established without the appropriate permits and remained unsupervised, as for example in Stare Grunty in the Grodziec parish.[^725] Only in the years 1837–1840 were the principles governing the internal organisation of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church in the Kingdom of Poland formalised. Vocations (appointments) for all positions became universally obligatory, methods of election were defined, as were the principles for drawing up documents and conduct certificates, tables of endowments and parish incomes.[^726] In 1839 an instruction was issued for the organisation of cemetery boards. In villages, these were to include: the local landlord, the commune headman, the village headmen of the Evangelical faith from those settlements which made use of the cemetery; and if there were only one village, the cemetery board should include two trusted farmers.[^727]

The cantor was a figure and an institution at once, unknown in German lands with a dense network of rural parishes, but existing — outside the Kingdom of Poland — in the sparsely populated Scandinavian countries and in Estonia.[^728] In villages and settlements distant from Evangelical churches, the cantor to some extent stood in for the pastor in religious functions. Initially, the cantor (and teacher) was the farmer most proficient in reading.[^729] The ability to read printed texts in German and Polish, proficiency in calligraphic writing — though without the requirement of orthographic correctness — was sufficient to become a cantor. *Das Brot kommt ihnen liegend*, it was often said of cantors, charging them with a lack of striving to broaden their knowledge and skills.[^730] The cantor could not in principle administer the sacraments recognised in the Protestant religion, that is baptism and holy communion. In urgent cases, such as danger to the life of a child, he could baptise it. The so-called emergency baptism had to be subsequently confirmed by the pastor. As late as the 1830s, owing to the infrequent visits of pastors to villages, the *wywód* (churching of women) was practised by cantors. Among the settlers, it was the custom that after childbirth a woman should direct her first steps to the church or prayer house. Without having undergone the churching ceremony, she could only undertake light domestic work, but was not permitted to leave the home. From the 1840s, pastors began categorically forbidding cantors from performing churching ceremonies. Nor was the cantor permitted to deliver addresses at funerals or to conduct pre-marital instruction. However, his presence, together with his family, was required during the catechisations conducted by the pastor in the summer months. The religious functions of cantors included presiding over services, reading passages from the Bible, and intoning hymns. In the cantorships, reading, writing, knowledge of the so-called Small Catechism of Luther, singing, and to a small extent arithmetic were also taught. The cantor was thus also a teacher, often the only person in the settlement able to read and write. By virtue of possessing these skills, cantors provided support to village headmen, a large proportion of whom were illiterate until the mid-nineteenth century.[^731] Usually the cantor enjoyed the respect of the village community. It was hard to imagine a wedding or a funeral meal (*Trauermahlzeit*) without his participation. Relations between the rector-pastor and the cantors did not always run smoothly, but the reasons for most of the various frictions cannot be explained today due to the lack of sources. The opinion prevailed that many pastors treated cantors with contempt, not caring about their living conditions and not giving them the opportunity to improve their teaching qualifications.[^732]

The ecclesiastical law introduced in the Kingdom of Poland in 1849, regulating relations within the Evangelical-Augsburg Church, formally legalised the cantorships, which until then had existed by force of tradition.[^733] The organisation of religious life in the parishes began to be put in order, and instructions appeared defining the duties of cantors. The duties of the cantor (teacher), confirmed in this position by the ecclesiastical authorities (intendancies), were specified in a detailed instruction, which particularly highlighted activities related to teaching. In 1850 the cantor in the village of Wytrzyszczki in Łęczyca County was obliged to obey the pastor and to fulfil all instructions received from him. Proper conduct was not only a necessary condition when applying for the function of teacher, but was to be binding in all circumstances. Above all, the cantor was to teach the children of Evangelicals who paid the contribution for the maintenance of the school. The method of teaching was also specified. The teacher was required to be continuously present at school and was forbidden from engaging in anything else. Every departure from the place of residence required the pastor's permission. He was to conduct lessons personally, daily (with the exception of those weeks and times in which pupils may be released from lessons), in accordance with the timetable, while maintaining *calm, decorum and morality*.[^734] The diligence and attentiveness of pupils were to be monitored. Lessons each day began and ended with a prayer recited by one of the pupils.[^735] The instruction issued two years later (1852) for Fryderyk Lehner, teacher of religion and cantor in the colony of Pustkowa Góra, at the prayer house existing there, contained a considerably longer list of duties, augmented mainly by religious functions.[^736] The justification for these could have been the distance from the parish and the small number of inhabitants of this old Olęder settlement. The local cantor was obliged to conduct services every Sunday and on the second day of major feasts. Each service was to begin with the singing of a hymn from the *Warschauer Gesangbuch* hymnal,[^737] followed by the reading of a sermon from a book, the Lord's Prayer, and a prayer for the clergy. The service was to be concluded with the singing of the appropriate hymn. School instruction for children was also to include singing lessons conducted by the cantor twice a week and exercises in responsories sung customarily after services. An additional duty of the cantor was the care of the local cemetery. He held the keys to its gate but also conducted the correspondence and accounts of burial fees (*pokładne*). In 1854 a similar instruction was issued for the cantor and teacher in Holendry Stare Paprockie.[^738] The cantor there was to conduct a service also on Whit Sunday, but excluding those days on which the inhabitants should be present in the parish church (this presumably refers to Good Friday, the most important feast in Protestant churches, as well as Christmas and Easter). He would read the sermon from the collection *Hirtenstimme* (Voice of the Shepherd) by Gottfried Kleiner. This was the most popular, perhaps throughout the entire nineteenth century, collection of sermons, meditations and commentaries, intended for Lutheran clergy.[^739] By 1854 this collection had gone through 30 editions. The scope of the cantor's teaching work was also precisely defined. Lessons were to take place twice a week, regardless of the number of pupils. Children were to learn to read, and above all to perfect themselves

*in the knowledge of melodies and church hymns and in singing the responses, that is the so-called responsories used during services in church*.[^740]

Confirmation candidates were prepared for confirmation in the second year of instruction, over eight months. In the first year they were taught the principles of the Evangelical-Augsburg religion from Martin Luther's Small Catechism.[^741] A thorough knowledge of these was a condition for proceeding to confirmation. The cantor could be released from his duties after a month's notice communicated to the pastor and on obtaining the consent of the ecclesiastical authorities.

Also among the beneficiaries of the freedom of religion prevailing in the Polish lands were the Moravian Brethren, often called *morawczycy* or Herrnhuters (*herrnhuterzy*). They probably arrived in the territories of central Poland at the end of the eighteenth century. The Protestant congregation named Herrnhut — which can be translated as *the Lord's Watch* — was established in 1722 on the initiative of Ludwig Zinzendorf on his estate in Upper Lusatia, on the border of Saxony and Bohemia. It gathered representatives of various Protestant denominations seeking refuge from the increasing persecutions by the Catholic Habsburgs in Bohemia and Moravia. The Herrnhuters did not attach great importance to dogmatic questions. Members of the congregation were required only to believe in redemption through Christ and to hold communal services. The congregation (assembly), divided into small choirs, lived in a community of goods. Over time, the Moravian Brethren developed extensive missionary activities, reaching South America and Greenland. Already from 1800, the Moravian Brethren lived in Nowosolna, which soon became the centre of religious life for this community. A significant role in spreading their convictions was also played by the village of Lwówek in the Sanniki estate near Gąbin, founded in 1802 and entirely inhabited by Herrnhuters.[^742] On the basis of a tsarist decree, in 1825 the Moravian Brethren in the Kingdom of Poland were exempted from military service in exchange for a special tax paid annually. This was a very significant concession, enabling the development of the community's religious life, given that military service lasted 25, and later 7 years. In 1829, Karl Friedrich Domke, the guardian (superior) of the Moravian Brethren in the Kingdom of Poland, settled in Nowosolna. He laid the foundations for the activities of most of the congregations, and reformed the rules concerning the observance of church feasts.[^743] After Domke's death in 1840, Jeremias Scholz continued his work. The Moravian Brethren were distinguished by the strictness of their customs, and a strong sense of community and brotherhood. They promoted ecumenical ideas, thereby gaining sympathisers. The principles of their public activities were regulated by the decree of Governor Ivan Paskevich of 31 July 1846. This obliged mayors and commune headmen to guarantee the Moravian Brethren freedom of assembly and freedom of worship.[^744] Not without significance, presumably, was the circumstance that they had ardent supporters in the highest circles of Russian authority. These included, among others, von Grimm, the chamberlain of Tsar Alexander I, and Count von Oppermann, adjutant to Ivan Paskevich. The decree of 1846 removed from the Moravian Brethren the suspicion of heresy and sectarianism.[^745] It is worth recalling at this point that in the Kingdom of Poland in the years 1828–1849, both Evangelical confessions — the Augsburg and the Reformed — were united in an administrative union.[^746] This came about on the initiative of representatives of both churches, who were seeking ways to reduce the disparity between the number of Lutherans and Calvinists. The considerable influx of members of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church into the territory of the Kingdom in the first third of the nineteenth century had made them nearly 15 times more numerous than the Reformed Evangelicals. The hope was expressed

*that the Lutherans would receive a portion of the old glory [of the Reformed — K.P.W.'s note], and the Reformed would feel stronger and reinforced in numbers*.[^747]

Although the aim of the union was not achieved, the climate it created was favourable for the activities of the Moravian Brethren. The tsarist decree of 20 February 1849 introducing the new ecclesiastical law in the Kingdom of Poland dissolved the union, creating separate consistories for both churches. The Moravian Brethren were incorporated into the structure of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church.[^748] The first general superintendent was Adolf Teodor Ludwig, who held this function in the years 1849–1874. He saw in the activities of the Moravian Brethren dangerous competition for the Lutheran church, and therefore treated them as a sect and combated them. Only his successor, Paul Woldemar von Everth, discerned in the Herrnhuter doctrine elements desirable for religion and the church.[^749] In the villages of German colonists, apart from Lwówek and Nowosolna, the Moravian Brethren did not leave a stronger mark of their presence and distinctiveness. The community in Lwówek was systematically visited by preachers from the centre of the Moravian Brethren in Bertelsdorf near Herrnhut.[^750] The considerable autonomy of the choirs did not create incentives for establishing a parochial structure. Beyond the two rural parishes mentioned, the Herrnhuters had churches in Łódź, Pabianice and Zduńska Wola.

Already in 1854, the Warsaw gubernial government had sent to the district heads in the Kingdom of Poland a circular informing them of the appearance in various places of clandestine preachers associated with a new religious movement, Baptist. As examples, Friedrich Bogusław (Gottfried) Alf was mentioned, as well as Friedrich Bülow, a teacher at a school in the village of Kochanów in Rawa County, who delivered sermons

*from concepts composed by himself [...] gave instruction from memory, delivered addresses at the burial of the deceased, admitted women to the churching ceremony*.[^751]

At the turn of the 1850s and 1860s, the Evangelical-Augsburg consistory undertook numerous efforts to counter, together with the district authorities, the propagation of Baptist principles, especially among the colonists who were *leaving the Evangelical-Augsburg church*. Such cases were frequently noted. German peasants were attracted to Baptism by the slogan of religious renewal, and contact with a travelling preacher — closer than that with an often distant pastor — built a stronger sense of community. In 1862, public propagation of the faith was prohibited, which meant that before 1864 the Baptist movement did not develop more widely. Religious conversion remained a personal matter, so the Kalisz superintendent, Pastor Stockman, could assure the head of the Kalisz district that whether anyone had converted from the Evangelical-Augsburg confession to another Christian denomination was unknown, since such persons were not accustomed to registering themselves as having left their faith.[^752]

Education was, alongside religion, the second fundamental factor uniting and sustaining the group bonds of the colonists. The only practical organisational form was the cantor school (*szkolnictwo kantoralne*), since the repeated attempts in the 1840s–1850s to transform these into elementary schools with a government-appointed teacher — wholly foreign to the community — ended in failure.[^753] The cantor schools had the character of religious schools in which, over the course of two years, pupils were taught the fundamentals of the faith, reading, writing, and arithmetic. Until the 1840s–1850s there were no curricula, and the scope of instruction depended on the abilities and inclinations of the teacher. The beginnings of formalising instruction in cantor schools can be observed only in the second half of the nineteenth century. The most important task set before pupils was mastery of Martin Luther's Small Catechism, which was necessary in order to be admitted to confirmation.

Many of the founding contracts of Olęder villages provided for the endowment of land for the teacher and the school. Moreover, the Olęders undertook to remunerate the teacher in kind. Usually, however, some twenty years elapsed between the founding of a settlement and the establishment of a school. In smaller settlements of a few or a dozen or so farmsteads, schools did not arise at all, since their upkeep would have exceeded the financial means of the settlers. In 1820, Andreas Rosin, the village elder of Sobótka in Łęczyca district, petitioned for the revival of the school that had been founded in the settlement some thirty years earlier and possessed its own building. Also living in Sobótka was the teacher Jan Bogumił Lange, but children were obliged to attend school in Chełmno, which was practically impossible owing to the great distance separating the two villages.[^754] The earliest information about the existence of schools in Olęder settlements was recorded by Pastor Beniamin Gottlieb Witthold of Iłów in his notes from inspection tours of the parish area. In 1798 he listed schools in Słowik, Brużyca, Radogoszcz, Kały, Dąbrowa, Swędów, Głogowiec, Mileszki, and Domaradzyn in the former Łęczyca voivodeship. A total of 212 children attended them.[^755] The report from one year earlier by Pastor Friedrich Heinrich Grimm of Stawiszyn spoke of 15 cantor schools active within the parish area.[^756] The clergyman drew attention to the insufficient endowment of the schools and the not particularly high level of skill of the teachers. The number of cantor schools on the western borderlands of the First Polish Republic must have been considerable, since in an opinion formulated at the close of the eighteenth century it was said:

> Many non-Catholics have been brought in from Brandenburg or from other neighbouring states and settled in wildernesses, forests, and deserted villages; and before they settled they obtained from the lords half a hide or a quarter for a schoolmaster, and therefore there is not a single dissenting village among us in which a schoolmaster for the instruction of children in reading, writing, and the catechism is not to be found.[^757]

By virtue of the General Law of the Land (*Powszechne Prawo Krajowe*), compulsory schooling was introduced in South Prussia on 1 June 1794. Initially this regulation applied only in royal estates, and from 1797 it became universally binding.[^758] To standardise the curriculum, a *Lesebuch* — a kind of primer compiled by Fischer, a councillor of the Warsaw chamber, in both Polish and German — was published in Warsaw in an edition of 10,000 copies, intended for pupils of elementary schools.[^759] The introduction of compulsory schooling in the countryside meant the necessity of maintaining a school and teacher by all householders, regardless of whether they sent their children to school or not. In 1806 the authorities of the Warsaw department lamented that:

> the majority of schools in the countryside of our department have already been organised, but unfortunately cannot be made use of, the reason for which lies in the fact that the subjects are unwilling to send their children to school.[^760]

Critical assessments were made of the progress of expanding the school network, particularly in small towns and villages, noting that even in centres as large as Skierniewice it had not been possible to organise an educational establishment. The nearly complete failure to establish schools in Polish villages was pointed out. In 1799 the Kalisz department had 11 Evangelical rural schools, and the Warsaw department had 72; the greatest numbers were in Gostynin district — 29 schools, Łęczyca district — 18, and Zgierz district — 11. This corresponds to the data on the proportion of the Protestant population.[^761] It seems, however, that for the Prussian administration not all cantor schools were deemed worthy of the name of schools, and the above estimate is probably an undercount. At the same time, the number of pupils per school was estimated. In the Kalisz department, half of the rural schools were establishments attended by between 51 and 100 pupils. The next group (30% of the total) comprised schools in which between 21 and 50 pupils were taught. In the Warsaw department, by contrast, the smallest schools predominated (86.5% of all rural schools), with fewer than 21 pupils. In only one school did more than 50 children study. This confirms the thesis concerning the progressively eastward phenomenon of greater dispersal and smaller population density in German colonies. From fragmentary data on teachers' remuneration it emerges that the decisive majority of those teaching (82% of the total) in rural schools of the Kalisz department received salaries not exceeding 20 thalers. The remuneration of the rest did not exceed 50 thalers. The situation in the Warsaw department was similar.

The condition of school buildings throughout the province was unsatisfactory. It happened that one of the peasants would make a room in his cottage available for the school's use, or that lessons were held in rotation in the homes of all the householders. A project was therefore drawn up which efforts were made to implement above all in newly founded colonies. The school building, erected in half-timbered construction (the so-called *pruski mur*, or Prussian wall), consisted of two parts divided by a centrally situated corridor running through the full width. On one side was the schoolroom with a stove; on the other was the living quarters, comprising the teacher's living room, kitchen, dining room, and two storerooms (pantries).[^762] The building was 65 feet long, 32 feet wide, and 8.5 feet high. The cost of its construction was estimated at 637 thalers. In another design differing slightly in the size of the building (50 x 30 feet and lacking one living room and one pantry), the cost of construction was estimated at 600 thalers.[^763] The surviving design of the school in the colony of Hochweiler (Markówka) demonstrates that a building with dimensions of 45 x 30 feet was planned, giving approximately 110 m² of floor area, of which approximately 37 m² was allocated to the schoolroom (Annex XVII). The cost of building one school amounted to just over half the annual salary of an averagely paid Prussian official (500 thalers), meaning that the reinforcement of the material base of the schools that occurred during the South Prussian period required relatively modest financial outlays.[^764] Around 1800, the Olęders of Rydzyny near Pabianice appealed for the establishment of a school. They wrote that in the vicinity there was neither school nor church, and that their children were growing up like wild animals.[^765] A similar petition was submitted in 1803 by the inhabitants of Wiskitki. The assignment of a given village to a school district was preceded by an analysis of its location. Residents' arguments citing various difficulties for children on their way to school — such as the need to skirt around marshes (Kowale Kościelne in Turek district, Krzyworzeka in Wieluń district) — were frequently given weight. The problem of finding suitable candidates for teacher positions was significant. Many of them possessed the requisite skills but did not know the German language. In the Württemberg colony of Louisenthal in Gostynin district, the chamber insisted that one of the nearby householders from a Polish village be employed to teach Polish for four hours a week.[^766] This is probably the only such case among German settlements. The establishment of interdenominational schools was considered — an idea to which part of the Prussian administration inclined, and to which both Protestant and Catholic clergy were decidedly opposed. In the plans for colonies for settlers from Württemberg, space was allocated for a school that was simultaneously to serve as a house of prayer (*Betsaal*). Where the number of settlers was sufficiently large, the school building arose quickly (e.g. in Wola Łaznowska in 1802, in Starowa Góra in 1803, in Lwówek in 1804).[^767] Many settlements were reached by Swabian colonists who brought their own teacher with them. In others there were difficulties in finding a person with the appropriate qualifications. The efforts of the Prussian government in the field of education were highly praised in 1807 by the Educational Chamber of the Duchy of Warsaw:

> The former government rendered the country the most important service by largely bringing to completion the statute that the Commission of National Education had intended and begun but that national calamities had interrupted — namely elementary rural and urban schools dedicated to the enlightenment of the people.[^768]

The educational policy of the Prussian authorities was a consequence of the idea of unifying South Prussia with the rest of the country, and of the most rapid possible integration of both communities — Polish and German.[^769] The advocate of such a course was Chancellor von Goldbeck. In 1795 he appealed to Minister K. G. von Hoym to support Protestants in the province under his administration. He argued that qualities such as enterprise, culture, civic sense, and a sense of duty were more widespread among Protestants than among Catholics, and that by mixing the two elements these desirable values would be disseminated. He recommended that provincial funds be used to support the construction of churches and schools, since such investments exceeded the means of the local population, and that the maintenance of pastors in poorer parishes be subsidised. Hoym agreed with this reasoning. He provided support for the maintenance and residence of a pastor in Piotrkow, but for more intensive activity there were insufficient funds, and efforts had to be confined to the most difficult cases.[^770] The most important step in educational matters was the introduction in 1800 of a compulsory school levy, covering not only parents but also all householders, including those without children. This method of financing lower education was retained in the Duchy of Warsaw, when a progressive tax was maintained with a division into four property classes. The payment of the teacher by all members of the local community was also maintained in the Kingdom of Poland, though the 1817 ordinance regulating this matter could not be fully implemented before 1830.[^771] Those concerned excused themselves on grounds of poverty, crop failure, military requisitions, or non-use of the school's services. Towards the close of Prussian rule, school societies also began to function. The society was to be composed of the landowner, the pastor, and representatives of the town or rural commune.

The idea of school societies was upheld in the times of the Duchy of Warsaw. The society was formed by the entire local community, which from among its members elected a school supervisory board (*dozór szkolny*). In practice, in the German rural environment the supervisory board was composed of the pastor, the village elder, and several householders enjoying authority. The supervisory boards were to ensure the regular remuneration of the teacher and that all parents sent their children to school.
