This body was also maintained in the Kingdom of Poland, with the pastor placed at its head, since owing to his pastoral duties, knowledge of his parishioners, and continuity of his office, he was deemed most suited to this role. The Educational Chamber of the Duchy of Warsaw strove for universal instruction. Article 1 of the school statute of 1808 stipulated that no child, regardless of denomination, could be excluded from school.[^772] Political unrest and the difficult economic situation of the state did not permit the consistent realisation of this objective. The times of the Kingdom of Poland brought a further attempt to spread education, this time with consideration given to the presence of inhabitants differing in language and denomination. The public elementary school was, in the intention of Minister Stanisław Kostka Potocki, to be a school for all children regardless of the denomination and nationality of the pupils. The educational authorities perceived the drawbacks of organising separate schools in German colonies, since these would sustain the settlers' distinctiveness. Children born in the colonies, even many years after the founding of a settlement, did not know a single word of Polish.[^773]

In the first years of the Kingdom, school organisation was guided by general assumptions deriving from the school regulations.[^774] On the question of education among the settlers, two types of solution were proposed. It was postulated that colonies should be incorporated into schools being organised or reorganised, or that separate schools for settlers' children should be maintained but organised in accordance with the applicable regulations.[^775] Instruction was to be conducted in Polish and German, and if the teacher had been appointed by the ecclesiastical authorities, religious duties were to be performed outside of school hours. The implementation of the second model presented difficulties. Above all, there was a shortage of teachers with knowledge of both languages — Polish and German. The authorities consented to the further training of the teacher and the consequent deferral of school reorganisation, or to the teacher maintaining an assistant with knowledge of Polish.[^776] In practice this solution was not applied. The lack of consistency in implementing the school reform as envisaged was linked not only to Potocki's dismissal but above all to the absence of a social climate for addressing the problem of the education of peasant children. In 1821, by decree of the Viceroy of the Kingdom of Poland, General Józef Zajączek, elementary schools were to be maintained from voluntary contributions.[^777] This brought about a decline in the number of schools. There was a widespread belief that an educated peasant might begin to demand his rights, and that school might alienate children from their life role. Fear of losing labour power outweighed the hope of benefits arising from education.[^778]

With the establishment in 1828 of the General Evangelical Consistory in the Kingdom of Poland, denominational school matters were also subordinated to it. Decisions taken in this area were to be consulted with the Government Commission for Religious Denominations and Public Enlightenment (*Komisja Rządowa Wyznań Religijnych i Oświecenia Publicznego*).[^779] By virtue of the ukase of 15 July 1833, the Public Education Council was established, subordinated to the KRWRiOP. By the ukase of Ivan Paskevich of 24 March 1834, both presidents of the General Evangelical Consistory became members of this Council. In this way, the Evangelical-Augsburg Church strengthened its influence in educational matters. The authorities did not obstruct the establishment of new schools in German settlements, which before long, as religious schools, came under the exclusive supervision of the Evangelical Consistory, in accordance with the new church law introduced in 1849.[^780] Earlier, at the end of 1839, the separateness of the school system in the Kingdom of Poland was abolished, with the Warsaw Educational District established subordinate to the Minister of Education in St Petersburg. In August 1840 the school statute of Sergei Uvarov was issued, which brought no changes to the organisational structure of elementary schools but introduced into them the teaching of the Russian language.[^781] Russian interference in the sphere of education in the Kingdom of Poland became still more visible in 1847, when the Viceroy of the Kingdom issued a rescript:

> with the intention of strengthening the German language as well as the religious spirit among the Germans of the Protestant denomination settled in this country.[^782]

It contained provisions concerning the organisation and financing of denominational schools. Instructions were given that in establishing initial Evangelical schools, both urban and rural, guidance was not to be taken from the inclination of gubernatorial authorities — who tended towards combining German and Polish children in one school — but rather account was to be taken of the will of the Consistory, which sought to establish separate Evangelical schools even in situations where their separation would reduce the income of Polish schools.[^783] Evangelical children attending interdenominational schools were to be transferred from them to existing or newly established cantor schools, even in cases where the staffing establishment (*etat*) of the latter did not exceed 45 roubles per annum. This sum may therefore be treated as the minimum cash remuneration of a teacher. Evangelicals were also prohibited from contributing to the levy for Polish schools.[^784] The governors of the Kingdom of Poland in the 1850s proposed reforming the cantor schools by transforming them into initial schools. It was assumed that the implementation of this project would take 10 years, and during that period the Russian Ministry of Education would have the right to regulate the activities of the cantorates in accordance with the interests of the state. At the same time, for a period of 10 years no new cantorates would be permitted to open.[^785] The aim of the directive was clear. The objective was to weaken Polish education, but above all to create a legal framework for separating the German, Evangelical community from its Polish surroundings. The recommended subordination of the cantorates to the Consistory took place in 1849. The 1850s brought a more profound decline of elementary education. Rural children were released by tsarist decree from instruction in popular schools, and communes from contributions towards them. The tsarist ordinance was elaborated in the rescript of the Curator of the Warsaw Educational District of 23 December 1851. It stipulated that parents who paid levies could not be compelled to send their children to school, and created a range of formal obstacles to the establishment of new schools or the raising of dilapidated schools to a proper standard. School buildings could be repaired only when the school levy sufficiently ensured the school's existence and the school society expressed consent for the investment. These regulations did not directly concern schools in German colonies, but they nonetheless created a climate not conducive to more vigorous engagement with education. Many necessary works were undertaken only as a result of decisive interventions by the Evangelical-Augsburg Consistory, as exemplified by the repair of the house of prayer and the cantor's cowshed in Holendry Dębskie, carried out in 1856.[^786] That settlement was not among the prosperous ones; it comprised 23 householders who did not pay the school levy. The teacher-cantor maintained himself by farming a few morgen of land.[^787]

In the early 1840s, efforts were made to put educational affairs in order in the Kingdom of Poland, through, among other things, an inventory of existing elementary schools. The Curator of the Warsaw Educational District, in a circular addressed to the gubernatorial governments, announced that:

> in all towns and colonies inhabited by Evangelicals, the inhabitants select more or less capable persons to teach religion and reading in German [...] clandestine schools arise of which the authorities know nothing.[^788]

In 1835, the President of the Kalisz Province Commission, Wilhelm Szmidecki, could find no information about the school in Rajsk in the Marchwacz estate. Its fate was clarified only in 1847 in correspondence between the Warsaw Gubernatorial Government and the head of Kalisz district: the school had collapsed after the inhabitants of the village of Marchwacz withdrew from the school society.[^789] Also in 1847, the matter of organising an Evangelical school in the colony of Zborowo came to light. The head of Kalisz district could not find it within the territory under his administration.[^790] The educational authorities, exercising no supervision, had no clear picture of the number of cantor schools in existence.[^791] The most complete knowledge of cantor schools was possessed by the pastors — the parish priests — if only by virtue of the annual confirmations they conducted. Similar knowledge was presumably held by the Consistory, whereas for the district and gubernatorial administration the affairs of cantor schools were a peripheral area of interest, not within the competence of those offices. The Ministry of Spiritual Affairs in St Petersburg had only a very superficial knowledge of the scale of the cantorates functioning in the Kingdom of Poland. This is evidenced by the lists of cantorates preserved in its archival records (Annexes XVIII, XIX). Setting aside the errors in place names, these reveal enormous discrepancies in relation to the fragmentarily preserved tallies compiled by the district offices (Annex XX). The school reform of Aleksander Wielopolski of 1862 did not encompass the cantor schools, which remained under the control of the Evangelical-Augsburg Consistory, which consolidated their ties with local communities and, in ethnically mixed environments, sustained the isolation of Evangelical children from daily contact with their Polish peers.[^792]

The main factor determining the good functioning of a school was securing the financing of its activities. Colonists in larger settlements managed relatively well, where the contributions of the householders forming the school society were sufficient to maintain the building, pay the teacher, and equip the school with the necessary furniture and teaching aids. The levy was calculated proportionally to the amount of land held, but its level differed in almost every school society, connected as it was with soil fertility and the incomes achieved by the householders.[^793] The best-placed were those settlements in which estate owners held land and, by virtue of this, contributed to the levy and frequently also financed repairs to school buildings, or supplemented the school and teacher with some extraordinary donation, as was the case, for instance, in Korzeniewa in Kalisz district.[^794] A good example confirming such a pattern is the history of the school in Prażuchy in the Ceków estate, belonging to the Celiński family, who from the turn of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries had been establishing German colonist settlements on their lands. In founding the parish at Prażuchy in 1808, they also took care to erect a building designated as a school.[^795] Its source-attested activity falls in the 1820s–1830s, when the cantor Karol Szyltzne taught there.[^796] In later years its fate is unknown. From 1843 a school society existed, to which belonged householders from Prażuchy, Starocekowskie Holendry, Plewiska, Kuźnica, Zakrzyn, Lipicze, Celestynów, and Będzikowo.[^797] The contribution of the society members together amounted to 59 roubles 85 kopecks, of which 45 roubles were allocated to the teacher's salary. This post was held by Daniel Schubert, appointed by the Curator of the Warsaw Educational District.[^798] Born in 1801 in Silesia, he had completed the three-year Teacher Preparatory School (*Szkoła Preparandzka Nauczycielska*) in Mąkoszyce and subsequently the Teacher Training Seminary in Wrocław. He passed the examination for teacher of an Evangelical elementary school at the inspector of the District School in Łowicz.[^799] From 1823 he was cantor and teacher at the Evangelical school in Wieluń, later a private tutor in the manor houses of central Poland. In 1839 he began work at the elementary school in Kutno, and three years later — with a good reference: conduct exemplary and moral — he became teacher and cantor at the parish in Prażuchy.[^800]

D. Schubert, who came from Silesia, belonged to a fairly numerous group of teachers active in the rural and small-town German environment of central Poland, who — educated at academic institutions in Berlin, Wrocław, and Gniezno — were already in the early 1820s seeking employment among the German diaspora.[^801] The majority of teachers active before 1864 in cantor schools in the Kingdom of Poland to the west of the Vistula came from Silesia, Greater Poland (*Wielkopolska*), and Brandenburg.[^802] A characteristic feature of their professional careers was great mobility, frequent changes of place of residence and employment. The main motive was the search for better remuneration, which in German colonies was not always satisfactory and regularly paid.

In 1854, 88 children were taught in the school in Prażuchy, making it one of the largest cantor schools between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula rivers. The school building was the property of the commune and also housed the teacher's residence. The annual levy, which in the years 1856–1864 hovered at a level of 60–64 roubles, made it possible to ensure a livelihood, the more so since the teacher himself drew up the list of levy debtors and demanded his remuneration.[^803] The householders belonging to the school society were subject to the following rates: those farming over 30 morgen — 60 kopecks per annum; from 15 to 30 morgen — 40 kopecks; below 15 morgen — 20 kopecks.[^804] The budget of the school existing in the former Prussian colony of Markówka (Hochweiler) near Pabianice was similar. Of the projected 1860 income of 69 roubles 18 kopecks, the following expenditures were planned: teacher's cash salary 37 roubles 20 kopecks; his due of 10 *korce* of rye — 16 roubles 18 kopecks; use of 4 morgen of land — 6 roubles; cultivation of the garden — 1 rouble 50 kopecks; writing materials — 1 rouble; prizes for outstanding pupils — 1 rouble. After paying the caretaker (1 rouble) and the chimney sweep (52 kopecks), barely 4 roubles remained in the school fund.[^805]

In the years 1843–1849 a dispute flared up around the attachment of children from Sadykierz and Jaszczury to the school in Prażuchy. The settlers in Sadykierz held a plot for a school, donated by the estate owner, and in the 1820s jointly with the householders of Jaszczury financed a teacher. The basis of his maintenance was payment in kind (2 *Viertel* of rye, 2 fathoms of firewood) and the income from farming 7 morgen of land belonging to the school. Since the farmsteads in both colonies were small, the annual levy yielded only 35 roubles 75 kopecks. The number of children studying, however, was large — in 1835 a total of 25, confirming the oft-repeated assessment of the district authorities that the poorest have the most children.[^806] In the 1830s the school ceased to exist, and the school land was leased out without notifying the estate owner of this.[^807] The colonists were unwilling to send their children to Prażuchy, particularly in winter on account of the great distance, and in summer on account of their employment in the farmstead.

> And if the government were to compel us absolutely to send our children to school in Prażuchy, we feel obliged to state here, compelled by such necessity, that we prefer that these children should know neither how to read nor how to write, seeing that they will at least not be exposed thereby to danger to their health, which could easily follow from attending from so great a distance.[^808]

The commune mayor (*wójt*) confirmed that the children could not attend school daily, as it was too distant. In 1850 the Warsaw gubernatorial government pronounced the necessity of both colonies — Sadykierz and Jaszczury — joining the school society in Prażuchy if they did not themselves establish a school.[^809] The pressure must have been effective, since the school budget for the years 1850–1852 included contributions from 8 householders from Jaszczury and 18 colonists from Sadykierz. The description of the school in Prażuchy from March 1852 speaks of a half-timbered building (walls plastered and rough-cast with clay), with three rooms, sufficient to accommodate both the school and the teacher's residence.[^810] A brick stove, in good condition, was not installed until 1856. An inventory of the furnishings of the school in Sobiesęki from 1855 has survived. In the schoolroom measuring 10½ by 6½ ells (6¼ m by 3¾ m) there was a sheet-iron stove with a matching flue pipe, one bench 7 ells long, a table 6 ells long, and one black wooden board. The teaching aids were: 14 alphabetical boards in Polish and Russian, a book of conversions of Polish to Russian units of length and vice versa, a similar book dealing with units of weight, one copy of Teodozy Sierociński's *Zasady poprawnego czytania i pisania po polsku* (*Principles of Correct Reading and Writing in Polish*),[^811] and a guide to treating cholera in the absence of a physician.[^812] The furnishings of the school in Sobiesęki did not differ from the equipment of other cantor schools, but there were schools maintained and equipped considerably less well. In 1835, the Kalisz superintendent Johann August Modl, inspecting the cantor school in the settlement of Joanka, noted that:

> Each child possesses its own book, but not the same. There are no educational collections whatsoever.

There was no blackboard, and the school building itself required repairs.[^813] In 1845 the school in Sobótka contained a table, 4 benches, a blackboard on a stand, 7 printed Polish boards, 7 Russian ones, and one book.[^814] The school building was in a condition no longer suitable for repair, and the inspector of the district school in Łęczyca reported that: *rain pouring into the schoolroom does not allow the children to study.*[^815] Shortly afterwards the school was closed down and the children assigned to the school in Dąbie. In 1856, the school in the former Prussian colony of Markówka (Hochweiler) had 5 tables, 13 benches, a wall board, and 14 boards with letters. Other teaching aids comprised the following books: *Przypadki Robinsona* (*Robinson's Adventures*), *Początki języka rosyjskiego* (*Beginnings of the Russian Language*), *Wiadomości o ratowaniu osób w pozornej śmierci będących* (*Information on Rescuing Persons in a State of Apparent Death*), and volume five of *Pamiętnik Religijno-Moralny* (*Religious-Moral Memoir*). In 1859 the following appeared: *Przewodnik do nauczania rachunków z pamięci* (*Guide to Teaching Mental Arithmetic*) and a Russian primer (*Bukwar*). In 1864 the number of letter boards increased to 25, and the printed items were supplemented by a copy of *Kmiotek.*[^816] Noteworthy in this inventory is the absence of books in the German language. It should be supposed that, being well known to the pupils, it did not require improvement through reading. Similarly, religious instruction could be conducted only by the memorisation method. Another reason may have been the absence of appropriate books. It is known that the Olęders living on the Gostynin Lakeland and in the former Łęczyca voivodeship made use of Bibles and hymnals published in Szczecin, by the printers Johann Franz Strunk and David Bolhagen.[^817] The Płock superintendent Johann Karl Hevelke reported in 1842 that the faithful in the vicinity of Chodecz used the hymnbook of D. Bolhagen.[^818] Sporadically encountered were explanations of M. Luther's Small Catechism, published in Dresden in 1842.[^819] The content of the book was addressed to pupils aged 12–14. In accordance with the title, the work allowed the material it contained to be mastered in the course of one year of study. Sixteen lesson hours were allotted to the revision of its contents. Two types of print were used: Gothic type (*Schwabacher*) in the section intended for ordinary lessons, and Latin type in the section intended for confirmation candidates. However, in 1841 the Consistory observed that:

> for many years now there has been a keenly felt shortage in the local parishes and filial congregations of a hymnbook for divine service in the German language for Evangelical-Augsburg believers, whereas foreign hymnbooks could not suffice.[^820]

Although no justification was given for this opinion about hymnbooks published in Germany, it seems that the main reason for the reservations was the song repertoire contained in them, which differed from that known and performed in the churches and cantorates of the Kingdom of Poland. In 1842 a hymnal compiled by Julius Ludwig, pastor of the parish in Warsaw and superintendent of the Warsaw diocese, was approved. Based on another, unknown and more extensive hymnal, it contained up to 700 hymns selected from it for every occasion and for divine service.[^821] The Consistory also perceived the urgent need to publish a primer from which Evangelical youth could learn the rudiments of the German language according to one consistent method. Such a primer was compiled under the supervision of Rev. J. Ludwig and introduced into school use.[^822] It is also worth drawing attention to the above-mentioned printed work on methods of rescuing people deemed to have died.[^823] It was frequently encountered in rural schools, from which it must be inferred that cases of the burial of living persons occurred. Echoes of such events can be found in the folk demonology of the nineteenth century.[^824] Cantor schools were also to disseminate practical knowledge not covered by any formal subjects but useful in everyday life. The pastor of Sobiesęki, who oversaw the school in the settlement of Joanka, lamented in 1842 that not one of the pupils was perfecting himself in handicrafts (*roboty ręczne*).[^825]

In 1846 the organisation of an Evangelical school in the settlement of Piskory, in the commune of Brudzew, was undertaken. The driving force behind its creation was the owner of the Jarantów estate, who wished the school to serve children from Piskory, Jarantów, and Dębniałki. In the first of these settlements there were 16 householders, in the second — 14, in the third — 6, a number therefore sufficient for the joint levy to remunerate a teacher and cover the necessary school expenses. Through joint effort, in the years 1850–1853 a school building was erected in Piskory, but it could not be completed, since the participation of the settlers from Jarantów was small. Only the imposition of administrative enforcement of obligations, through the billeting of a soldier — a Cossack — whom the village was obliged to maintain, compelled the householders from Jarantów to take part in the work on the school building. It was also decided jointly pay for the cantor Johann Gottlieb Fietze, residing in Piskory, who had been approved by the Kalisz superintendent for the purpose of teaching children religion and reciting prayers at the cemetery during burials, in return for which each of the 36 farmers would give 15 kopecks of Christmas gift on New Year's Day, 15 kopecks for firewood, 15 kopecks per burial, 4 quarts of rye grain, and 30 kopecks in cash.[^826]

Barely a year later, the settlers from Jarantów refused to participate in the agreed remuneration for the cantor, claiming that in Piskory there was a school, not a prayer house. They also argued that they had become very impoverished due to a flood that had struck them.[^827] The agreement with the teacher in Piskory illustrates the tendency, emerging in the 1840s, to abandon payment in kind in favour of cash payment. This attests both to the expansion of the internal market and the growing role of money, as well as to the greater practicality of such a solution. Disputes over the quality and measure of the goods owed to the teacher were avoided, while the teacher, having cash at his disposal, could seek a more rational and cheaper means of supply.

The remuneration of teachers in cantoral schools was a very serious problem, affecting not only the functioning of the schools but often determining their very existence. A low fund from the school subscription and poor premises were factors that led to the closure of schools due to a lack of candidates willing to take them on. In the period before enfranchisement, no uniform principles for remunerating teachers in German colonies had been developed, and the contracts concluded with them encompassed a very wide range of solutions. Their common feature was payment in cash and in kind, with the share of money growing over time. It occasionally happened that children from tiny settlements, whose inhabitants did not belong to any school society, were sent to cantoral schools. In such cases, the children learned for a fee agreed with the teacher.[^828] Problems with collecting the school subscription meant that in the 1850s, in many colonies, the idea arose of endowing the teacher with land, on which, by farming it, he would become independent of the remuneration from the school society. The consistory was decidedly opposed to such solutions. It was feared that teachers working the land would have no time for classes with children.

In Prussian times, in the opinion of officials of the Warsaw Chamber, the remuneration of an elementary school teacher should consist of a minimum fixed annual salary of 30 thalers and 18 scheffels of rye, 6 of wheat, and the same of millet, barley, and buckwheat. He was also to receive for his use a dwelling in the school building, firewood, a piece of land, and two cows with the right to graze on the communal pasture.[^829] The sources contain no confirmation that such a plan was actually realised. Concerning the teacher who taught around 1800 in Pustkowie Smolnik on the estate of the justice of the peace Bielski in Kujawy, it is known that he received 6 scheffels of rye, 1 scheffel of peas, half a cartload of hay. He lived in a shepherd's hut (Hirtenhaus), farmed half a morgen of land, and additionally worked as a tailor.[^830]

In 1808, in the Prussian colony of Markówka (Hochweiler) near Pabianice, the teacher's remuneration was to amount to: 216 Polish zlotys 15 groszy, 9 fathoms of wood, 9 viertels and 2 quarts of rye, 6 viertels of barley, 3 viertels of buckwheat, 3 viertels of peas. All the goods in kind were worth 378 Polish zlotys 18 groszy.[^831] For a colony inhabited by 13 farmers each holding 1 włóka and 12 morgens of land, such remuneration was unrealistic. It is true that the school was endowed with a 4-morgen garden, manured by the village inhabitants under the supervision of the village headman, but it was difficult to expect that the crops harvested from such an area would noticeably improve the teacher's livelihood. As late as 1836, the Pabianice pastor Daniel Biedermann, who supervised the school, informed the commissioner of the Sieradz district that the colony could not be enlarged, as it was surrounded by noble estates, and without adding land to the farmers, it was difficult to expect from them a higher school subscription than that which was already paid and was insufficient for its needs. The situation of the school and the teacher improved when in 1849 the farmers from Wymysłów and Wysieradz joined the school society in Markówka.[^832] Both settlements together counted 38 farmers, and each contributed 50 kopecks and 4 quarts of rye per 15 morgens of land. A further strengthening of the school came in 1852 with the attachment of children from Kolonia Majówka, Wola Żytowska, and Kudrowice. Two years later the school building was repaired, and on its land a small cowshed and barn were erected for the teacher's use. In 1819, Jan Diffenbacher, teaching in the Olęder settlement of Pustkowie Górne in the Łęczyca district, received 200 Polish zlotys in cash and 12 Polish zlotys for chopping firewood. He also had the use of a two-morgen garden.[^833] In 1835, the teacher in the colony of Joanka received as his remuneration: 12 viertels of rye, 4 viertels of barley, 2 viertels of peas, 6 fathoms of soft firewood, and 300 Polish zlotys in cash.[^834] Four years later, in place of barley and peas, the teacher was given 6 viertels of spring grain.[^835] In 1845, the teacher's remuneration in kind was half what it had been 10 years earlier and comprised 6 viertels of rye, 1 viertel of peas, 2 viertels of barley. The wood allocation remained unchanged: 6 fathoms. In subsequent years, payment in kind to the teacher was abandoned and replaced with a cash subscription proportional to the area of land held by the farmers who sent children to school.[^836] In 1854, in the colony of Karolew, situated within the boundaries of the village of Gać Kaliska, the teacher-cantor was remunerated by the estate owner Karol Osterloff and by the colonists, who collected in three instalments a sum of 50 rubles. Additional income came from fees for preparing children for confirmation and for funerals at which the cantor sang.[^837] Very often, however, reality diverged from the letter of agreements. In 1850, pastor Johann Gottlieb Ehrentraut of Sobiesęki reported that, in writing through the village headmen, verbally, and even five times from the church pulpit, he had urged the school society to pay the subscription, which had not been done, and the teacher, receiving no salary whatsoever, was in a sorry state.[^838]

This difficult material situation of the teacher-cantor developed into a conflict during which, in 1852, the pastor was accused of causing a deficit in the treasury of over 100 Polish zlotys.[^839] The affair, which shocked the parishioners and could not be explained in detail, ended two years later with the handing over of the school treasury to the teacher, Juliusz Buchwitz. When in 1857 pastor Eduard Fiedler took over the parish in Sobiesęki, control of the school treasury returned to the incumbent.[^840] In 1860, the local school society consisted of 181 farmers, a tavern keeper, a miller, a blacksmith, and a cobbler. The subscription collected must have been sufficiently large that in 1863 the sum of 5 rubles was deposited with the district treasury. The school in Sobiesęki was the only cantoral school in the Kalisz district to have remitted a surplus.[^841] The few schools with stable funds could be attractive places of employment for unmarried and older teachers. In the case of teachers burdened with large families, providing for their livelihood was difficult. In 1848, the school in Sobiesęki was taken over by Jan Gottlieb Moderan, a teacher with 28 years of experience, who had arrived from Turek with excellent references from the local pastor.[^842] He was accompanied by his wife and eight children. Already after a few months, Moderan began complaining about his low remuneration. He soon decided to move to Wiżajny in Sejny County, but delayed his departure for several months, having no means for such a long journey.[^843]

There were repeated complaints about the level of teachers' competence and about children's academic progress. Critical remarks were made by both representatives of the local administration and clergy who inspected the schools. This problem persisted throughout the entire period described. Across the vast area between the Prosna, Pilica, and Vistula rivers, there was not a single enclave in which questions of education in the rural environment did not give rise to concerns.

> Elementary schools, which today bring little benefit, partly due to their small number, and partly due to the poor remuneration of teachers and the failure to send children to school — particularly in summer — to such a degree that in many schools children do not learn at all

— reported the head of the Kalisz county in 1859.[^844] The consistorial authorities complained throughout the nineteenth century about the shortage of suitable candidates for teacher posts in cantoral schools.[^845] In the harsh opinion of Revd K. G. Bartsch from 1825, the teachers

> are in general limited individuals who, although they have no qualifications for anything, are still considered by the unenlightened to be quite capable of being teachers. Among them one will find cloth-makers, cobblers, and the like — I even know one near Władysławów who was previously a farmhand.[^846]

Johann Georg Seegemund, the pastor from Gostynin, expressed a similar view:

> [Die Lehrer] mit selenem Ausnahmen besitzen sie selbst nur geringe Schulkenntnisse und Lehrfertigkeit, viele sind höchst unwissend,

adding that those coming from abroad teach better, but are not always morally upright (sittlich).[^847]

Johann Christian Schmid, who was teaching around 1800 in Pustkowie Smolnik in Kujawy, was liked by the inhabitants of the settlement, but in the assessment of pastor Johann Gottlieb Maske from Babiak his teaching was deplorable.[^848] Of Johann Krause, the teacher from Mariampol on the Guzów estate, it was written that

> although he does not possess full proficiency in writing, it is difficult to find another candidate due to the meagreness of the fund.[^849]

The rector of the Łowicz Teacher Training Institute assessed Jan Bogumił Lange, the teacher from Sobótka, as follows:

> he demonstrated mediocre ability; although he expresses himself verbally in Polish and reads quite well, he writes with insufficient confidence.[^850]

Jan Fryderyk Krombin, proposed in 1830 as his successor, did not gain the acceptance of the church supervisory body, and furthermore lacked qualifications.[^851] In 1839, the Kalisz superintendent Johann Modl, supervising the school in Joanka, noted:

> the present teacher is entirely unsuitable for the method of teaching, and moreover his conduct is of the most immoral kind, and he performs his duties with the greatest negligence [...] he fairly often gives himself over to drink. The school guardian found the teacher absent from lessons.

These unflattering opinions made the need to find a successor inevitable, in which the pastor of the Sobiesęki parish became engaged. However, the candidate proposed by him was rejected by some of the colonists. Only the intervention of the village headman and aldermen of Joanka caused the village assembly, in July 1839, to elect Fryderyk Hintz by vote. He soon became known as diligent, but more suited to teaching German children, to whom he imparts higher instruction.[^852] In the opinion of the school guardian,

> he employed a slow, gentle method of teaching, with praise for the excellent and reprimand for those who fell behind in their studies.

A few years later he was assessed as diligent and quite zealous, running the school reasonably well. Hintz's dedication to his work was exceptional compared to other cases known from the sources. In November 1842, Hintz brought to the school in Joanka 25 copies of German primers. The curriculum covered: reading, writing, arithmetic, natural history, geography, moral instruction. Classes were conducted in a planned manner. From 9:00 to 10:00 — Polish and German reading; from 10:00 to 11:00 — elementary calligraphy; from 11:00 to 12:00 — Roman Catholic catechism. After a two-hour lunch break there followed a further two hours of instruction, covering: reading in Polish and German, arithmetic, and preparation of conscience for Holy Mass. The change of teacher resulted in an increase in the number of children attending school. In 1838 there were only 17, while 20 others — mostly girls — of an age suitable for beginning their education were not being sent to school. In 1839, the number of pupils at the school in Joanka reached 20, and a year later — 45. In 1843, 42 pupils attended the school.[^853] However, the academic results they achieved were not impressive. Outstanding progress was shown by 1–2 children, moderate progress by 4–6, while the remaining pupils were described as weak.[^854] In the settlers' communities, many teachers worked who possessed the minimum of necessary skills but had not been formally appointed to those posts. An example is Bogusław August Neumann, teacher in the colony of Sadykierz on the Kuszyn estate. Born in 1807 in the colony of Brzoza in the Grand Duchy of Poznań, after completing elementary school in Gniezno he took up work as a private teacher. In 1835 he passed the examination for a teacher of an Evangelical elementary school before the inspector of the Kalisz County School.[^855] Teachers in Czachulec and Poroże held similar qualifications. In 1847, a positive assessment was given of Bogumił Lechner, teacher in the colony of Żabieniec in Łęczyca County, who

> possessed Polish, German, and the rudiments of Russian, and obtained the qualifications necessary for the teaching profession at home from his parents.[^856]

From the 1830s, teachers in cantoral schools were required to fulfil two conditions: holding Russian subjecthood (they could not be foreigners) and confirmation of qualifications by the inspector of the county school, and from the mid-1840s by the director of the gubernial gymnasium. The requirement to document adequate educational attainment came into force from 1836.[^857] In many cases, particularly in small colonies, a vocation letter issued by the pastor supervising the school and confirmed by the commune head sufficed.[^858] Pastoral oversight of schools was sometimes ineffective. Farmers, for reasons known only to themselves, changed teachers, appointing persons about whose abilities and qualifications the incumbent had no knowledge whatsoever.

> Die neugewählten jetzigen Lehrer sind mir aber sowohl der Person als den Kenntnissen nach völlig unbekannt,

complained in 1833 pastor Johann Gottlieb Ehrenkraut of Przedecz in a letter to the superintendent of the Płock diocese.[^859]

Many critical remarks concerned pupil attendance. Children attend school irregularly, it was noted in 1842 in Holendry Dębskie, where a group of 30 was taught by the almost 70-year-old teacher Samuel Norlitz.[^860] In all cantoral schools, instruction was limited to a few months per year. Most parents were inclined to send their children to school only for 2–3 months before confirmation, arguing that the children were needed on the farm. In 1820, in the settlement of Sobótka, 27 children were enrolled in instruction, of whom 18 missed a quarter of the lessons, four children missed 73 days, and two missed 55 days. Parents explained the absences in detail:

> he was kept at home to tend the geese; the parents kept them at home to tend the livestock.[^861]

The Evangelical-Augsburg Consistory endeavoured to ensure that teaching in schools ran from St Martin's Day (11 November) until Easter, but in practice this was difficult to enforce, and children learned from Christmas until the first warm days of spring.[^862] Already in the 1830s, opinions emerged from clergy supervising Evangelical schools about the inadequate preparation of pupils presenting themselves for confirmation. In connection with this, in September 1839 the consistory issued to the superintendencies a recommendation specifying the conditions that needed to be met in order for young people to be able to complete this religious act. It was determined that a candidate for confirmation must be at least 14 years of age, be able to read printed texts, and know the principles of faith according to the so-called Luther's Small Catechism. Pastors were required to keep a register of confirmands and to issue each of them a certificate.[^863] At the same time, cantor-teachers were disciplined to ensure that religious instruction took place twice a week, at the time most convenient for the children, and for at least 4 months a year.[^864] The cantor received from the pastor-incumbent a vocation — that is, a call to perform his functions. The vocation had to be approved by the commune head. This was not merely a formality. In 1851, the commune head of Przespolew refused to approve the cantor at the prayer house in Poroże, giving extensive reasons for his refusal.

> The person called does not know either the Polish or the Russian language, one of which he ought to possess, inasmuch as in our country German is not needed, and the incoming German colonists ought to accustom themselves to the national language; furthermore, on the colony of Poroże it is not only Germans who live, but also Poles, who have a greater right to benefit from the instruction.[^865]

This very resolute position of the commune head indicates the scale of the problem of mutual relations in communities of colonists of varied national composition.[^866]

Given the considerable distances separating most inhabitants of German colonies from parish churches, cantoral institutions as religious-educational establishments played an important role. They allowed the cultivation of language and religion — those elements of culture of the greatest significance for the building and sustaining of ethnic consciousness. This was reinforced by a sense of otherness in relation to the surrounding population, while language and confession allowed that otherness to be defined. The advantage of cantoral institutions was that the cantor-teacher was a person well known in the village. By performing religious services, he simultaneously ensured upbringing in the Evangelical spirit and in the German language. The cantoral institution was an institution that integrated the village, even if its educational results left much to be desired. The level of education in German colonies was slowly but steadily declining. It is estimated that at the end of the nineteenth century barely 45% of the entire German population in the Kingdom of Poland could read and write. In rural communities this percentage was presumably even lower.[^867] Illiteracy and rare contact with clergy meant that in many settlements the level of religious observance was low. In the mid-1840s, prayer books and hymnals were encountered frequently in the homes of colonists, but already in the 1860s attention was drawn to the fading Evangelical custom of daily Bible reading.[^868] There were homes in which no copy of the Bible was to be found.[^869]

Apart from the parish organisation and the school structure closely linked to it, there existed before 1866 no other institutions uniting peasants from German colonies in the area between the Prosna, Pilica, and Vistula rivers — with one exception: in 1853 the statute of a Shooting Society (Schützengilde) was approved, bringing together inhabitants of 19 colonies situated around Aleksandrów Łódźki.[^870] The justification for granting permission for the society to operate stated that the authorities were aware of the loyalty and attachment of the colonists to the throne.[^871] Shooting societies, which traced their origins to the late medieval tradition, had come to be regarded as typically urban organisations satisfying the cultural needs of artisan and petty-bourgeois milieus. They were widespread in western Europe, particularly in countries of the German cultural sphere, and beyond it wherever a numerous German bourgeoisie existed. From the early 1820s, along with the influx of craftsmen into the towns of the emerging Łódź industrial district, shooting societies founded in them gained acceptance in accordance with the guidelines of the Kingdom of Poland's government policy, which stated that

> allowing the German factory population to maintain the customs widespread in their country of origin may all the more accustom them to the place of settlement, and thereby increase the number of the country's useful inhabitants.[^872]

Practising target shooting in the nineteenth century had a sporting character, and shooting competitions organised several times a year became a pretext for communal celebration, with the largest accompanying the king's shooting (Königsschießen) on the second day of Pentecost. The initiative of rural colonists testifies to a very great vitality of tradition, but also to a need for communal entertainment and the sustaining of neighbourly bonds. It also attests to the trust of the authorities, as each member of the society had the right to keep one firearm at home. In 1854, German colonists living in the commune of Nowosolna attempted to organise themselves in a similar society. The request did not receive approval. The petitioners were directed to the possibility of membership in the nearby Łódź Shooting Society (Lodzer Bürger-Schützengilde).[^873] The refusal of registration was all the more incomprehensible in that it was precisely the German peasants of Nowosolna who had in 1831 given proof of their loyalty to the throne.
