## Chapter VI: German Colonists and Their Neighbours. Influences and Interactions

The growing number of German rural settlers over the decades could not go unnoticed. Germans were the second nation, alongside Jews, with whom Polish peasants came into direct contact; however, we have no peasant accounts of the consequences that this neighbourly coexistence produced up to the time of enfranchisement. In the common Polish perception, all foreign colonists were simply Germans, constituting a homogeneous group differing from the Polish population in language and confession. In reality, however, this community was strongly differentiated. From the reliable estimates of Albert Breyer, supported by field research, it emerges that among all German peasants living in the Kingdom of Poland to the west of the Vistula, 36% came from Pomerania, 28% from Silesia, 28% were colonists associated with the Vistula valley, known as Niederunger, and approximately 8% were settlers from Swabia.[^874] Regional differentiation was attested by various elements of folk culture, among which the most easily observable were dialectal differences and those in the sphere of material culture (architecture, clothing). Documents of official provenance, which constitute the majority of those relating to the colonists' community, only sporadically provide information about manifestations of traditional folk culture and forms of folklore. This does not allow conclusions to be drawn about its character.[^875]

German colonists from villages on the Kujawy Lake District were called Kashubians (Kaszubi) by the other Germans settled in the Kingdom of Poland. Moreover, even those who in the first quarter of the nineteenth century emigrated from the Kingdom to Bessarabia were called Kashubians.[^876] In Kujawy this term had a derogatory meaning, but in German villages situated further to the south, the expression "stupid Kashubian" (dummer Kaschube) was the most commonly encountered insult.[^877] In reality, the Kujawy settlers had arrived from the Netze District (Obwód Nadnotecki), Pomerania, and the New March, and had nothing in common with the ethnic Kashubians. The expression harter Kaschube (hard/tough Kashubian), firmly rooted in tradition and used in rural communities, was meant to denote a certain kind of backwardness and unwillingness to make contact with outsiders. The German inhabitants of Kujawy were considered distrustful of all novelties, not very hospitable, and closed in on themselves. An analysis of parish register entries carried out by A. Breyer in the 1930s showed that among the Kujawy Kashubians, settlers from Pomerania predominated. This was attested by the surnames (ending in -ow: Modrow, Kreklow, Rekow, Karow, and those ending in the syllable -ke: Janke, Kotke, Schmidke, Jeschke, Tulke, Wudke). The boundary between the two dialect varieties ran along the upper Noteć. To the east of it, Prussian Low German (preussisches Platt[deutsch]) was spoken; to the west — Pomeranian Low German (pommersche Platt[deutsch]).[^878] Furthermore, those originating from Pomerania called potatoes Tüffke, while the inhabitants of the Vistula valley called them Schoke. Hence came the dialectal distinction of the population into Tüffkeleute and Schokeleute. The Pomeranian origin of the Kujawy settlers is also attested by the hymnals and Bibles they used, published in Szczecin and Greifswald. Colonists originating from Pomerania spread their characteristic method of barn construction, using the timber-frame structure popularly known as Prussian wall (pruski mur). Such buildings were encountered even quite far from the area of compact settlement by Kashubians, which was favoured by their relatively high mobility.[^879] In the area of southern Kujawy, settlers from the territory of Alsace, bordering on Württemberg, settled in considerable numbers, as was attested by the surnames of the settlers: Philipp, Durier, Courbiés, Kommand. In 1812, Alsatian colonists from Jasieniec near Przedecz served as interpreters for the French soldiers of the Grande Armée marching eastward.[^880] From the account of Eduard Heinrich Busch it emerges that as late as the 1860s there lived in Kujawy colonists holding French citizenship.[^881]

As completely alien (landfremd), closed in on themselves, distrustful, and even fearful in relation to newcomers from other parts, the Olędrzy long settled in the Vistula valley were described as late as the 1850s.[^882]

> Es ist ein ganz absonderliche Menschensorte – der deutsche Ansiedler an der Weichsel,

it was written about the settlers in the Vistula valley, with reference above all to their manner of farming, which differed from that practised by Polish peasants and aroused their astonishment.[^883] The sole objects of their concern were the good of the family and the good of the village. Their hospitality was also distinctive.

> Ein Holländer […], wenn er auch gastlich ist, wird einen Fremden doch niemals nötigen, Platz zu nehmen und sich zu bedienen, und wenn der sich nicht selbst setzt und zugreift, kann er in Ewigkeit stehen und hungrig bleiben.[^884]

The Olędrzy were also very proud of their prosperity and boasted of it before others.[^885] A particular occasion for demonstrating wealth were weddings, which frequently lasted as long as eight days. In the 1860s, even a not particularly wealthy farmer would devote to wedding feasting a cow, 2 pigs, 4 rams, 20 geese, as well as 2 barrels of beer and 16 quarts of vodka.[^886] An essential condition for preserving and maintaining prosperity was the non-division of farms among heirs. Farmers strove to accumulate a sufficient sum of money in order to buy an appropriate amount of land for those sons who had not married wealthy girls.[^887] The Olędrzy considered themselves superior to others, particularly to Jews. In this context, attention was drawn to the looser economic ties of the Olędrzy than of other German peasants with Jewish intermediaries and tavern keepers. The Olędrzy, offering good quality products on the market, were able to defend their high prices. Moreover, they were not burdened by the propination obligation, which freed them from the duty of frequenting the tavern. In many cases the Olędrzy were permitted to brew beer and distil vodka for their own needs.[^888] The Olędrzy also maintained a considerable distance from pastors and cantors, not holding them in particular esteem.[^889] There are grounds for thinking that the Olędrzy in the Vistula valley, cultivating the tradition of their Frisian and Flemish ancestors, differed from the other colonists in the Kingdom of Poland in their manner of inheritance. Customary law, practised in north-western Germany, Schleswig-Holstein, and also in the Netherlands and Brittany, made the youngest son the heir to the family estate. This principle of minorat (Minoratsrecht) was attempted to be disseminated by Catherine II among the colonists settling in Russia after 1764, among whom there were initially many peasants from the northern lands of the Reich.[^890] This attempt did not succeed, as the dominant group of emigrants soon became settlers from the south-western parts of Germany, who did not know the right of minorat. Neither was it applied by colonists from those parts who settled between the Prosna, Pilica, and Vistula rivers. In their tradition, as among Polish peasants, the eldest son remained the heir to the estate.[^891]

For immigrants from Württemberg and Baden, a serious obstacle to communication even with the German-speaking environment was the Swabian dialect they used. The considerable distinctiveness of the speech of Swabians settling in South Prussia was already noted in 1801 by Colonel von Puhle, who supervised their placement in the colonies of the Łaznów economy. He considered it essential to bring in clergymen and teachers who were fellow-countrymen of the colonists, as only such persons would be able to communicate with them and gain their trust.[^892] He also warned that Württembergers scattered across South Prussia would gradually become polonised through contact with the local, uncultured people (rohe Menschen), and it was therefore necessary that the settlers remain in their own clusters.[^893] As is known, the Swabian colonies established by the Prussian administration fulfilled the condition of self-sufficiency, which reduced contact between their inhabitants and the surrounding environment to a minimum. A quarter of a century later, observing the Swabian settlements in the Sanniki economy, Julian Ursyn Niemcewicz wrote:

> a different language, faith, and separate settlements will never allow this people to become polonised.[^894]

Equally sceptically, pointing to the same reasons, the possibility of the Olędrzy in the Kalisz region assimilating was assessed in the mid-nineteenth century.[^895] Württemberg Swabians settled in a more compact manner in the settlements of the Kujawy Lake District.

the Gostyniński Lake District, a strip on the right bank of the Vistula from the Pilica to Warsaw, and the territories of the former estates of Tkaczew and Łaznów in the vicinity of Łódź.[^896] Close contacts between neighbouring villages inhabited by Swabians and newcomers from Pomerania were rare. The individual groups spared each other no barbs or ill-will. The Pomeranians were portrayed as physically strong but not particularly sharp-witted, the Swabians as weak but full of peasant cunning.[^897] The inhabitants of the Vistula valley, meanwhile, had a reputation for being conceited and arrogant.

Smaller differences were also observed — ones that did not constitute communication barriers but nevertheless divided the settler community and provided pretexts for cutting jokes. This was experienced, for instance, by the Swabians settled in Kujawy, who had no aptitude for crafts whatsoever and whose thrift bordered on miserliness.[^898] The inhabitants of the old Olęder settlements west of Łódź were called *Hockerlingen*, from their characteristic dialectal features and from the wicker fences (*Hocken*) with which they enclosed their farmsteads.[^899]

Confessionally mixed marriages were a rarity in the first half of the nineteenth century among the German colonists, since partners coming from alien milieus were not tolerated.[^900] There were no marriages between Herrnhuters and adherents of other Protestant denominations.[^901] The Olęder settlers of the Vistula valley were almost entirely closed to such unions, which was presumably connected to the fact that some of them belonged to the Mennonite community.[^902] In the Olęder villages between Warsaw and Płock, over the course of half a century only a single marriage between an Olęder man and a girl from outside that milieu was recorded.[^903] In the Kalisz region such unions were unheard of altogether.[^904] Mixed marriages were vigorously and consistently opposed throughout the nineteenth century by the Roman Catholic Church, whose clergy refused to read the marriage banns in their parishes. Records of such cases can be found in the marriage registers of the Evangelical-Augsburg parishes, for their pastors placed no obstacles in the way of mixed marriages conducted according to the Lutheran rite.[^905] These observations seem significant in the context of searching for the reasons behind the social insularity of German settler villages. The examples cited confirm the thesis about the inhibiting role of confessional congregations in the process of social consolidation.[^906] In the popular perception of the Polish surroundings, all the German colonists were Evangelicals. They did not go to *kościół* (church) but to *kircha*, and for the neighbouring Polish peasants the inferiority of the colonists' faith was beyond any doubt. Mutual contacts between the two communities, when they occurred at all, did not serve to acquaint one with the intricacies of a neighbour's faith, since that belonged to the strictly personal sphere. Meanwhile the question of confessional community among the colonists was, on the scale of the entire Kingdom of Poland, more complicated than could be inferred from observation within a single parish. The territorial and ethnographic diversity of the colonists' origins was accompanied not only by differences in the organisation and administration of parishes, but also by liturgical divergences. It may sound surprising that an Evangelical who found himself at a service in another — sometimes even a neighbouring — parish felt as though he were in a church of a different denomination.[^907]

The reason lay in the different orders of service, composed at the discretion of individual pastors; differences in the observance of feasts, celebrated in some parishes but not others; and, during services, the use of different hymnals — so that even when the hymns were the same, they often differed in their melodies. The persistence of this diversity was also possible thanks to the specific nature of the Protestant confessions, which granted individual parishes relatively broad autonomy. The Lutheran faith of the colonists — mockingly called *psia wiara* (dog's faith) by the Polish surroundings — nevertheless aroused interest, though not enough for anyone to explore the theological nuances of their neighbours' confession.[^908] There prevailed a conviction that in matters of piety the German farmers were no worse than Polish peasants, but were more superstitious.[^909] This opinion was based on observation of the practices employed by the Olęder settlers in cattle-rearing, many of which were borrowed from them by our peasants.[^910] The Olęder settlers enjoyed a well-deserved reputation as excellent dairy-cattle breeders, as evidenced by the large herds grazed on the Vistula sandbanks and along the river's banks. It was astutely noted that the principles of the Evangelical religion required the ability to read. Lutheran clergy were perceived as better educated than Catholic priests, whereby the prayers and sermons they delivered were held to carry greater power of influence. Attention was also drawn to the different way in which the German peasant spent his holidays:

> The Evangelical villager returns from the congregation and, having taken refreshment, passes the rest of the day reading Holy Scripture or resting; the Catholic returns from Mass and most often goes to the tavern, where he spends Sunday — and frequently Monday too — in debauchery and drunkenness.[^911]

This somewhat schematic comparison nonetheless contained a measure of appreciation for the rules propagated and practised in daily life by the Evangelicals. The concept of the Protestant work ethic, usually applied to urban milieus, can with full conviction be applied to the characterisation of German rural settlers.[^912] The good organisation of parish work in this community was also noticed by Catholic clergy. Father Adam Szelewski, parish priest of Brzeziny, who was in constant contact with the Lutherans settled in the town and its vicinity, drew attention in 1853 to their discipline in collecting subscriptions for their church. The amount gathered was the basis for applying for an additional fund granted to Evangelical parishes by the government, and this circumstance seemed to arouse the envy of Father Szelewski.[^913] The importance of this support was, however, exaggerated, as is shown by the subsidy for the Łódź Evangelical parish of the Holy Trinity: to its budget for the years 1851–1853, the sum of 15 roubles silver was added from the treasury of the Kingdom of Poland.[^914]

The German settlers attracted attention by the distinctiveness of their physical type.

> The colonist Prussians — even without speaking to them — can be recognised by the features of their faces, which always preserve a distinct physiognomy, and by their clothing, which is ungainly but neat,

noted Oskar Kolberg, himself sensitive to the particularities of different ethnographic groups, yet even so describing the entirety of the colonists by a single, and moreover erroneous, label.[^915] The Swabians living on the Gostyniński Lake District described with great precision the different outward appearance of the Olęder settlers neighbouring them:

> *Die Holländer, das ist ein ganz anderes Geschlecht von Menschen. Sie haben alle ganz kurze Hälse, auch den Leib etwas kürzer und viel dicker als die Schwaben, dafür aber sehr lange Beine.*[^916]

The anthropological characteristics must have been sufficiently distinctive for them to be taken into account in personal descriptions. In 1842, on the road between Łask and Piotrków:

> two persons of the male sex were murdered by unknown criminals […] and left by their murderers in the following state: one completely naked with only a blue handkerchief around his neck, the other in a shirt with a yellowish handkerchief around his neck; both these men resemble Germans, but it is not yet known who they are.[^917]

Those handkerchiefs around the neck — neck-cloths (*Halsstück*) — point to yet another element identifying foreigners: namely, their dress. *A cloth jacket of German cut, navy blue*[^918] — this was the most stereotypical description of the German colonist's attire. A navy-blue frock coat, a *szpancerek* of navy-blue cloth, or a *sukmana* of the same colour, were the most commonly encountered outer garments among men. A colour other than navy blue was a rarity. Waistcoats, the already-mentioned neck-cloths, and black, round hats — entirely unknown in such a form to Polish villagers — were in common use.[^919] Less is known about women's dress. Among the Mennonites living in the Vistula valley and on the Gostyniński Lake District, the men wore long blue or grey homespun coats (*sukmany*) made at home, and the women wore colourful woollen skirts and aprons.[^920] One cannot fail to mention the image of the devil, deeply rooted in Polish culture — not only folk culture — characterised as dressed scantily, *po niemiecku* (in German fashion).[^921] Even if the story recorded by O. Kolberg about the murder in the marshes near Łęczyca of a German *Regierungsrath*, who dressed in the German manner appeared to a Polish peasant to be the devil, is a literary fancy, it nevertheless appeals to the collective image of the German.[^922]

The otherness of the German colonists meant that it was already being noticed at the beginning of the nineteenth century, and attempts to characterise it were almost always made in opposition to the traits possessed by the Polish peasant. An interesting opinion on the settlers from the turn of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries was expressed by Fryderyk Skarbek:

> The German colonists, who were then streaming into the country in large numbers and establishing proper settlements in places recently covered by forest, found in the Polish peasants neither imitators nor people envious of their prosperity. The proximity of the German settlements had no influence whatsoever on the way of life and farming of the Polish peasant; all the agricultural improvements in the colonies drew not even his attention. The German was always a German, and for that very reason could not gain the respect of the Polish peasant, who indeed levelled at them the charge that in establishing a settlement they cleared the forest.[^923]

Skarbek's opinion drew on the stereotype of mutual Polish-German perception, but did not touch the essence of the matter. The life and farming of the rent-paying settlers differed in a fundamental way from the daily practice of the serf, for whom the very idea of economic calculation or the modernisation of farming was utterly alien, since he was not an active participant in the commodity-monetary exchange. All the risk associated with farming was borne by the landowner. The peasant was merely the executor of orders, often not even invested in carrying them out properly. Several years later this was perceived by Julian Ursyn Niemcewicz, who, on one of his journeys undertaken before 1828, stopped to rest at an inn between Gostynin and Kowal:

> I found in the room a crowd of our Polish peasants and German colonists. Never had the difference between the farmers of two nations struck me more forcibly. The Polish *kmiecie*, treating themselves generously to spirits, were shouting and singing, kissing one another most tenderly; on the other side, four colonists, having pooled their money for a glass of beer, were all slowly sipping it, accompanying this gloomy sipping with harmonious conversation in their tongue. Better surely is German temperance than our immeasurable drunkenness; but if our *kmiecie* too were on rents, if our clergy taught them as the Evangelists teach theirs, they would always drink more than the Germans, but would not get drunk.[^924]

In this brief sketch from life Niemcewicz encapsulated the entire essence of the difference between the Polish villager and the German colonist. He assumed, however, that the granting of property and freedoms to the Polish peasants would mean that in a short time they would be better farmers than the foreign newcomers.[^925] From observations made in the 1860s by Eduard Heinrich Busch, as well as from the conclusions reached by Adolf Eichler more than half a century later, it emerges that the agricultural economy of the German settlers developed very slowly. While in the first half of the nineteenth century it did not differ — especially in the Vistula valley — from the level of farming in the German lands, by the early twentieth century the colonists in the Kingdom of Poland were lagging far behind in terms of technical progress and cultivation methods.[^926] Complaints were made about conservatism and stubborn adherence to traditional forms of life, visible both in farming and in the sphere of culture and custom.[^927] This enclosure within old forms of life meant that the colonists' skills — good and innovative at the time of their settlement — had lost their value, which led their farmsteads into a certain stagnation. The conservatism proper to peasant culture was reinforced by a sense of foreignness, for the breaking of which there were no stronger impulses.

Records of relations between German colonists and the Polish population began to appear only around the 1850s, initially confining themselves to citing the mutual opinions each held of the other. These were formulated on the basis of observations encompassing the most easily perceived elements of culture. In accordance with the theory of the downward drift of cultural products, their formation can be pushed back several decades.[^928] Polish ethnographic and publicistic accounts of German farmers consisted to a large degree of a record of an already established stereotype, in which the German colonist reproduced a number of traits of the German in general — as the tradition stretching from the Middle Ages would have it — supplemented only partially by observations arising from direct observation. Industriousness was undoubtedly one of the traits most frequently attributed to the German colonists. Their relatively better material position compared to that of Polish peasants, their care for order in the farmstead and the appearance of their buildings, attracted attention and made them worthy of description.

> The Olęder settlers are also very industrious, though as regards work they seem to differ little from our peasants,

wrote Stanisław Ciszewski in characterising the Olęder settlers from the vicinity of Stawiszyn.[^929] Of the Olęder farmsteads along the Vistula it was written that their *Hof und Haus sind stets blitzblank* (farmstead and house are always spotless), while the houses are neatly kept, spacious and comfortable.[^930] In Oskar Kolberg's view:

> one can say with certainty that it is a German travelling, if one encounters a cart drawn by a pair of plump little horses. The colonists are fond of trading in them, fattening and selling them while buying others.[^931]

J. U. Niemcewicz drew attention to the love of work and enlightenment when searching for the sources of the superiority of the German people over ours.[^932] An objective factor in the colonists' farming success was certainly their larger land allotments, developed livestock-rearing, and domestic crafts. In the 1850s in the Kalisz region, Olęder women were almost universally engaged in weaving — an occupation already being abandoned by Polish peasant women. As S. Ciszewski noted, only the dyeing of woollen yarn from their own sheep was commissioned in town, while the weaving itself was done at home.[^933]

In mutual contacts between Germans and Poles, language was a significant barrier. In individual Polish assessments, it was at times harmonious and melodious, at times barking, but always incomprehensible. Hence the Polish peasant:

> does as much as he can to adapt to them and twists his tongue to pronounce at least a few words in their dialect, which is quite difficult to understand even for those with some knowledge of German.[^934]

> *Mit Hände ist hier nichts zu verdienen, denn die polnischen Leute können ja nicht mit uns sprechen* —

the colonists of Wiączyń and Nowosolna complained in 1801, as they tried to earn money outside the colony.[^935] Ten years later, Bernard Eherissman from another Swabian colony, Bukowiec, declared before a notary who spoke good Polish, on the occasion of leasing part of his land: *a mi sobie komoro sidzely i swoy chleb wyrobkiem zarabialy* [and we had lodgers sitting with us and they earned their bread by day-labour].[^936] In the 1820s settlers who spoke Polish were encountered, but they were exceptions.[^937] This skill was found relatively often among shepherds, who were very mobile and changed their place of work easily over the vast expanse from the eastern borders of Prussia to the western provinces of Russia.[^938] Yet the great majority of colonists, even in the third generation, had no command of Polish or Russian, communicating exclusively in German.[^939] Of the German settlers in the Kalisz region it was written that they maintained no contacts with their neighbours who spoke other languages.[^940] Knowledge of Polish among the inhabitants of Lwówek remained meagre even in the 1920s.[^941] In the 1850s one encountered German peasants who had served in the tsarist army and spoke Russian quite respectably, but knew no Polish.[^942] An excellent, almost anecdotal example of the language barrier being maintained across generations was given by Oskar Kossmann — a German geographer and historian, born in 1904 in the colony of Ruda Bugaj near Aleksandrów, into the family of the local schoolteacher. In the early twentieth century, his grandmother, when asked something by Polish market-women at the market in Aleksandrów, would answer in fright with the only Polish sentence she knew: *Nie umiem po niemiecku!* (I don't know German!) — she was convinced she was saying: *Nie umiem po polsku!* (I don't know Polish!).[^943]

At least until the mid-nineteenth century, the colonists' contacts with towns were sporadic. An obstacle was the distance of most colonies from larger urban centres, and the poor state of the roads compounded the hardships of travel. Settlers from villages situated near an Evangelical-Augsburg urban parish to which they belonged visited towns more frequently. Contacts with the town only rarely served the purpose of procuring craft goods. For a defining feature of peasant farmsteads was self-sufficiency, which in the case of the colonists took on the dimension of a life necessity. The settler needed skills in carpentry, wheelwrighting, saddlery, blacksmithing, and cobbling. In many settlements, from the very beginning of their existence, there were also craftsmen — including the indispensable blacksmiths — who farmed on small plots of a few morgen. Mutual aid was extensive in the colonist villages, harking back to the tradition of the Olęder settlers' collective responsibility. It was precisely the rural craftsmen who were the first inhabitants of the colonies to seek contact with the town, as it offered them a broader market. As early as the 1820s, Brzeziny — the private town of Izabela Lasocka — became attractive to the inhabitants of the surrounding colonies. In 1816, Jan Gloss from Nowosolna settled in Brzeziny, purchasing a house from the local physician Jan Gottlob Vogel.[^944] In the same year, Frydrych Lenk, a linen-weaver from Nowosolna, bought a house in Brzeziny from Bartłomiej Gniotkiewicz for 920 złoty Polish.[^945] Three years later, Johann Zeiser, a saddler, moved from Nowosolna to Brzeziny, purchasing two houses for the sum of 3,000 złoty Polish.[^946] Two tanneries were active in Brzeziny at that time and a cloth-making settlement was beginning to develop — possibly he had some plans connected with that. Among the masters of the cobblers' guild active in Brzeziny in 1825, there were four inhabitants of nearby colonies: three from Łaznowska Wola and one from Kolonia Gałkowska.[^947] Three years later, among the 44 cobbler journeymen in Brzeziny, there were 8 Evangelicals.[^948] Those leaving the colonies were mainly the smallholders and, from the 1830s onward, representatives of the second generation of settlers for whom there was no land or opportunity to practise a craft in the village. In 1830, the cotter Henryk Zerfass left Wiączyń, moving to Tomaszów.[^949] Three years later, Georg Muenster, with his 12-year-old daughter, left Wiączyń to seek work in Tomaszów.[^950] In 1835, Karol Arendt, 33 years old, left Nowosolna, also going in search of work in Warsaw. When he found no work there, he settled in Tomaszów.[^951] The inhabitants of Nowosolna were also drawn to nearby Łódź. Two of them, in 1828, took up building plots in the Łódka settlement.[^952] At the same time, Michael Reps, a roofer from Wiączyń Górny, was covering the roofs of Łódź's houses.[^953] There is no doubt that the proximity of the developing factory towns constituted a powerful magnet for rural colonists seeking earnings or better living conditions. Many Olęder settlers in the Vistula valley, known for their horse-breeding, also engaged in carting — including, among other things, delivering salt from Ciechocinek.[^954] Those colonists who lived in close proximity to towns and supplied them with their produce also had increasingly strong contacts with urban centres. Warsaw and Łódź were avid markets. Already in the 1830s, Swabian settlers from villages to the south of Warsaw were specialising in potato cultivation, destined for the urban market. Complaints arose that as a result of this:

> they move away from their homes every day, and assign their field work to farmhands or hired labourers, as was the case on the Wilanów estates.[^955]

From the Olęder villages in the Vistula valley, dairy products went to the Warsaw market. The dairy economy was also developing well in the Łódź industrial district, finding a ready outlet in its towns for its products.[^956]

The insufficiency of source research did not permit a more detailed characterisation of the ties between German farmers and the town; opinions were therefore formulated on the subject dealing with individual cases or limited to the inhabitants of a small area. In the 1830s, many German peasants from the Warta valley are said to have set off to the towns of the Łódź district, finding employment there in the textile industry.[^957] With respect to the Kalisz region, on the other hand, it was stated that *eine Verbindung zum Lodzer Industriegebiet besteht fast nicht*.[^958] The phenomenon of the exodus of peasant sons from the overpopulated villages around Łódź was also mentioned by O. Heike.[^959] The prime example was to be the career of Friedrich Wilhelm Schweikert, grandson of a Württemberg peasant who had settled in 1802 in Starowa Góra (Effingshausen) near Łódź. His son ran a smithy and a craftsman's nail workshop in Pabianice, while the aforementioned F. W. Schweikert founded a cotton spinning mill in Łódź which, by the turn of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries, had developed into one of the largest local enterprises in that branch.[^960] There are grounds for formulating the view that it was only in the second and third generation that German village inhabitants turned toward the towns to seek employment there. This is confirmed, among other things, by an analysis of the birthplaces of weaving apprentices and apprentices in other trades in Pabianice in 1836. Of 34 weaving apprentices, 20 had been born in colonies situated in the immediate vicinity of the town. Among 137 apprentices in other trades, a similar origin was attested by 94 apprentices.[^961] To complete the picture of contacts between the German inhabitants of Łódź and the colonists close to them in language, one must note the numerous cases — attested in the sources for the early 1860s — of weavers moving to the countryside.[^962] This phenomenon must be linked to the consequences of the mechanisation of weaving in the towns of the Łódź district.

There is no doubt that, with the passage of time, the intensity of contacts between German colonists and their Polish rural and urban surroundings increased. The economic development visible both in agriculture and in industry meant that these relations became ever more varied. They can be illustrated by the examples of the connections that three colonists from Antoniew near Łódź had with the surrounding farmers and Łódź craftsmen. The first of them, Michał Stejnke, was a creditor of several farmers from his own colony as well as more distant ones: Dąbrowa, Rokicie, Budy Stokowskie, Kusy Kąt.[^963] Among his debtors were also inhabitants of Łódź, including four weavers, one dyer, one mechanic, and one wheelwright. It is impossible to establish under what circumstances their debts arose — whether they were unpaid dues or loans. The sums owed ranged from 180 to 810 złoty Polish. Smaller in scope and value were the obligations owed to the Schmiedke spouses, who also lived in Antoniew.[^964] Among their debtors, alongside farmers, were two weavers from Łódź: one owed 45 złoty Polish for grain, the other had borrowed 150 złoty Polish in cash. It should be added that the events described took place during the first serious crisis in the Łódź district (1848), when the impoverished artisans of the towns could seek support from the inhabitants of the surrounding villages, who — despite the poor harvest — were better supplied with food. Another practice still was reported in 1855 by the mayor of the commune of Olesiec to the chief of Kalisz district:

> In this commune, the condition of the villagers is adversely affected by the Holęder settlers, for they purchase from the villagers grain still standing in the field, hay and crops not yet mown, and in addition lend them money, taking produce as interest. And there are several such persons in this commune who have leased out their farmsteads and occupy themselves exclusively with such a grain trade.[^965]

Such a report — and it is not an isolated one — undermines the received wisdom about the strong commitment of the Olęder settlers to farming and livestock-rearing. It is rather evidence of their resourcefulness and entrepreneurship — their ability to exploit a situation to augment their own income.

Analysis of extensive source material makes it possible to question the view found in older literature, that changes in the spelling of the surnames and given names of German settlers might testify to a process of advancing assimilation. Indeed, in numerous official documents from the nineteenth century one encounters transcriptions that polonise German surnames through translation:

> Marcin Wesołowski, namely Frehlich;[^966] Georg Bille, namely Wojciech Billak;[^967] Gottfried Sztein, namely Kamiński.[^968]

This phenomenon was of an almost universal character. In many cases this new form can be compared with the handwritten signature of the person in question — though one must note that literacy was declining in the colonist community over time. Such a comparison reveals that the change in a name's form almost always took place on the initiative of an official, not at the will of the person concerned, who signed himself Martin Frehlich, Georg Bille, Gottfried Stein — to refer to the examples cited above.

One might suppose that a certain opportunity for Germans and Polish peasants to draw closer to one another was provided by a shared place of residence. Such circumstances began to arise in the 1840s, when in newly created colonies — mainly in the Kalisz region — Polish and German peasants were settled alongside one another. The advancing process of converting estates to rent-based tenure also meant that Polish rent-payers were settled in existing German colonies. The forum at which formalised contacts then took place were the Lutheran parishes and school associations. It is difficult to call them fruitful. In 1849, the inhabitants of Holendry Sobiesęckie and Holendry Piegońskie appealed against the enforcement of a school levy imposed on them, arguing:

> We did not send our children to study in the Evangelical school and among Evangelical believers we neither wish nor are able to send them in the future. We are children of our Mother, the Catholic Church; on her bosom we wish to live and die with our children, and we wish to educate our little ones in the pure teaching of our holy faith.[^969]

At the same time they acknowledged that some of the farmers had sent their children to the school, but that these children had been mocked by the children of the Evangelical colonists. In the 1840s, a dispute flared up in the parish in Sobiesęki over the level of the parish levy and the fees for religious services, which in the perception of the Polish parishioners were too high. The pastor was also reproached for not knowing the Polish language, as a result of which the Polish inhabitants of the colonies of Kakawa and Przystajnia derived no benefit whatsoever from the teachings.[^970] The intervening Government Commission for Internal Affairs recommended that a service in the Polish language be held every fourth Sunday. Both events demonstrate that a confessional community did not create bonds strong enough to overcome the sense of distinctiveness rooted in linguistic and national consciousness.

The older historical literature disseminated the view that a measure of progress in the integration of the German newcomers into Polish society was their attitude to the national uprisings.[^971] It was rightly observed that the distance of time between the two insurrections — corresponding to the active life of one generation — influenced the Germans' approach to the question of Polish aspirations for independence, making them more open in the years 1863–1864. More thorough source research does not undermine this thesis, allowing it to be supported by further examples which nevertheless show that the reality was more complex. For the majority of those who had arrived from beyond the borders of the Kingdom of Poland, the November Uprising was an incomprehensible event, perceived as a rebellion against the lawful monarch whose subjects they were and to whom they had sworn homage. The Polish national movement therefore did not deserve support.[^972] From the report of the commissioner of the Sieradz circuit, it emerges that alongside the inhabitants of the Pabianice New Town, the German colonists from the villages of Ksawerów, Rydzyny, and Pawlikowice:

> at the first summons of General-Police-Commissioner Lessowski in 1831, armed themselves to fight in the cause of the rightful monarch […] and performed service, advancing towards Łask at the first order of the military authorities — they did not, however, have the opportunity to distinguish themselves in action, since […] during patrols they did not encounter the enemy.[^973]

The inhabitants of other villages formed several companies, the same commissioner reported, but the picture of the settlers' activity appears to have been exaggerated. The underpinnings of this demonstrative support for the lawful authority are indicated by correspondence from several years later. Recalling their conduct of 1831, the colonists from the aforementioned villages expressed the hope that:

> were they to be freed from rent for a couple of years, that would have given them the right to a distinguished rivalry in loyalty to the throne.[^974]

The request went unanswered. The inhabitants of Nowosolna, for their part, behaved ambiguously during the uprising. Compelled to provide fodder and to equip two cavalrymen, they fulfilled this obligation, estimating the costs incurred at 2,226 złoty Polish.[^975] Somewhat later, noticing two volunteers passing through the vicinity on their way to join the Polish army, they seized them and handed them over to the Russians.[^976]

An unprecedented event took place in Pabianice. In 1831, two members of the church council accused the local pastor of the fact that:

> during the rebellion in the country […] Pastor [Daniel Gottlieb] Biedermann was guilty of treason against the throne of the rightful monarch, not only through contemptuous speeches, but also set a bad example to his congregation, since not only Evangelicals but also the greatest part of the Catholics, namely all the Germans, not wishing to fight against the imperial-Russian troops, indeed armed themselves against the insurgents and rebels, that same unworthy priest [...] plotted a conspiracy with other rebels to overthrow the throne, was present in the church with a saber and pistols, threatened everyone that they should take up arms, and when they did not do so, refused holy baptism for children, and later wandered through forests and woods with partisans, thereby forfeiting the trust of the parish.[^977]

The accusations leveled against Pastor Biedermann were investigated by Rev. Johann August Modl, superintendent of the Kalisz diocese. Among the authors of the accusation, one did not appear for questioning, while the second and the summoned witnesses became confused in the details of their testimonies. Biedermann himself did not deny that he had delivered patriotic sermons. The Evangelical consistory did not publicize the matter; it was quickly forgotten, and the pastor of Pabianice remained in his office until his death.

There is no information about the direct participation of colonists in the insurgent units of 1863–1864. On the other hand, many Germans living in the cities and representing almost all social strata — from workers and craftsmen to merchants and physicians — supported the January Uprising.[^978] Pro-Polish sentiments were also openly expressed by numerous Lutheran clergymen, such as the Łódź pastor Gustaw Manitius, who collected funds for insurgent purposes and held services for fallen insurgents.[^979] He paid for this with his removal from office and his transfer to a provincial parish in Łomża. Many of the German participants in the uprising fell in battle. Others, captured by the Russians, were deported to Siberia.[^980] There are indications that allow us to speak of the hostility of German rural colonists toward the uprising. Fragmentary source accounts provide information about the murders of traitors — colonists bearing German-sounding surnames.[^981] It must be remembered, however, that this account may be misleading, since from the 1840s onward the term "colonist" encompassed a broader group of rural inhabitants, among whom were also Poles living in villages organized in the colonial manner.[^982] The opinion formulated in the 1880s in the St. Petersburg department for the affairs of foreign religions must be regarded as quite telling. It was noted that the settlement of the German element in the Kingdom of Poland had been supported by the Russian authorities since 1815, because in the newcomers they saw people who did not engage politically. With a certain satisfaction, it was noted that this attitude of the immigrants had been confirmed in the course of the Polish national uprisings, by which the Germans had alienated the Poles from themselves.[^983]

The decades-long presence of German peasants living in diaspora across the vast territory of the Kingdom of Poland led to the formation of a peculiar symbiosis among all participants in rural life. Whereas between the mobile Jews, the itinerant traders, and the manor and the village, a certain model of mutual relations had developed, compelled by economic interest, there were insufficient grounds for the formation of a similar relationship between the German colonists and their Polish surroundings. In the first case, the mobile and dynamic Jews broke down the language barrier because necessity dictated it. It was not always trade that required this — often intermediary roles, even in matchmaking.[^984] In the second case, such contact was not necessary. *She does not associate with me; she is from the Olędry, and I am from the village*, said a Polish peasant woman from the vicinity of Stawiszyn at the end of the nineteenth century, thereby attesting to the isolation of the colonist community that had lasted for generations.[^985] It may be taken as a certainty that the contacts of the German settlers with their alien surroundings did not, in the pre-enfranchisement period, extend beyond economic necessity. Among the German peasants there existed a peculiar solidarity, built on the specific mentality of the colonists (*Siedlermentalität*).[^986] Family ties, common rural origins, shared religious community, the same tradition, and an almost exclusively German marriage market (*Heiratsmarkt*) meant that mixed marriages — in which assimilation is most easily achieved — occurred very rarely. The phenomena typical of the process of assimilation/acculturation with regard to all communities of Germans in central Poland began to appear in various forms and with varying intensity only in the second half of the nineteenth century. Knowledge of them remains fragmentary to this day.[^987]

The folk culture of German colonists in central Poland was not a subject of research. In the interwar period, only a small number of folkloric materials were gathered.
