## Chapter VII: The German-Polish Historiographical Dialogue on Settlement in the Kingdom of Poland

As with few other questions of Polish-German contacts and the neighborliness of representatives of both nations, the problem of German rural settlement in the Kingdom of Poland has accumulated an enormous literature: German and Polish. The output of both historiographies in this area has already been subject to discussion.[^988] The historiography of the topic of German colonization in Poland constitutes a German-Polish dialogue, but one that is asymmetrical both in terms of the number of publications (a decisive majority being German) and the assumptions and research objectives that guided them. Settlement studies, particularly medievalist ones, have always enjoyed great popularity in Germany, especially since the appearance in 1907 of the monumental, now classical work by Raimund F. Kaindl on the history of the settlement of the German population in the Carpathian countries.[^989] It inaugurated a veritable flood of publications on settlement in German scholarship. The thematic scope of these studies encompassed, alongside major syntheses devoted to various waves of colonization, also monographs on the settlement of individual villages or regions. The development of settlement-related issues was also connected with the expansionist policy, first of the Prussian state and then of Germany, ultimately serving to document and justify the conquests carried out by the Third Reich.[^990] The specific character of settlement questions allowed individual, minor issues to be treated, which did not require broad or specialized preparation from the authors, nor an elaborate scholarly apparatus. The leading role in these studies fell to historians and regional scholars residing outside the borders of Germany. Their publications preserved the tradition of individual national groups (*Auslanddeutschtum*), while simultaneously constituting a fragment of the broad panorama of German settlement in Europe. The relatively lively, multi-stage settlement movement involving the German population on Polish territory, connected both with rural and urban environments, provided an objective incentive for its description and scholarly analysis.

Despite many common features, the German literature addressing the issue of the influx and presence of Germans in the territory encompassed by the borders of the Kingdom of Poland showed differences in certain periods. Five such periods can be identified, in which the publications produced were characterized by certain distinctive features: 1) up to 1860, 2) 1860–1918, 3) 1920–1944, 4) 1945–1990, 5) after 1990.[^991] As can easily be observed, these turning points coincide with the most important political events for Central Europe, which points to a fairly close connection between historical research in the field discussed here and current politics. In the oldest works, published before 1860, the settlement of German peasants in territories lying to the east of the Reich was criticized. With regard to Polish lands, the achievements in South Prussia were evaluated negatively. The main objections concerned the overly hasty founding of settlements, which caused their incomplete preparation and the settling of colonists who lacked the necessary qualifications.

*Der Augenschein kann uns alle Tage belehren, dass aus dem Auslande, mit vielen Kosten herbeigezogenen Kolonisten lange das nicht leisten, was man sich von ihnen versprochen hat* [...], wrote the Prussian official Carl August von Holsche, who was well informed about the contemporary realities.[^992] From the 1860s onward, a new way of interpreting the phenomenon can be observed. The strengthened German national consciousness, reinforced by unification, provided a favorable framework for reflections on the settlement of Germans across Europe. A visible tendency emerged to build the glory of Prussia in a patriotic-nationalist spirit. The extensive work of Max Beheim-Schwarzbach provided the figure of 300,000 persons settled to the east of the Oder during the reign of Frederick the Great.[^993] At the same time, the entire work was subordinated to the assumption that cultural progress is identical with the progress of German settlement. From the socio-legal standpoint, the conclusions of M. Beheim-Schwarzbach were supported by Gustav Schmoller, the leading representative of the so-called younger historical school in the field of economics. Without questioning the data on the scale of settlement, he advanced the thesis of its very beneficial influence on the transfer of culture from west to east.[^994] From 1885 onward, minor contributions documenting the presence of Germans in Poland — a presence dating back to the Middle Ages — were published by various authors grouped around the *Historische Gesellschaft für die Provinz Posen*, concentrating mainly on the area of Greater Poland (*Wielkopolska*).[^995] In 1917 there appeared a dissertation by Antonius Pytlak, born in Grzybów in Greater Poland, devoted to German colonization on the state domains of the Kingdom of Poland.[^996] The dissertation was produced under the supervision of Max Sering, the most eminent German specialist in agricultural economics at the time, and it clearly stood out in terms of the quality of its analysis. The author made use of Polish archival materials that no longer exist today, providing a substantive and objective picture of the conditions and progress of the settlement of German peasants directed to the state domains. The source base, reduced during World War II, does not allow anything to be added beyond the findings of A. Pytlak, which are, however, limited exclusively to the sphere of the settlers' economic life. The wartime circumstances meant that the dissertation entered scholarly circulation only in the interwar years, without, however, arousing a more lively response. The opinions of military chaplains active in 1915–1917 on the territory of the General Governorate of Warsaw speak unequivocally of a lack of interest in their own past among the Germans in the Kingdom of Poland.[^997] This situation began to change only in the 1920s.

In Polish historiography, research on settlement has a long and well-established tradition. The works of Karol Potkański, Franciszek Bujak, Kazimierz Zimmermann, Władysław Rusiński, and a number of other scholars working in their circle form the canon in this historical specialty. At the end of the nineteenth century and in the first decade of the twentieth century in Poland, as in other European countries, the problems of medieval settlement enjoyed particular interest above all else. On the other hand, little attention was devoted to migration and settlement processes in the modern period. Only the Frederician colonization directed at the borderlands of Polish territory received an extensive monograph.[^998] The area located at the center, encompassed after 1815 by the borders of the Kingdom of Poland, did not attract the attention of researchers, with the exception of Ignacy Baranowski, who initiated research into Olęder settlement, pointing to its economic rather than ethnic significance.[^999] The first mentions of settlers, or as they were commonly called at the time — colonists, who had arrived from German lands and were scattered across almost the entire Polish territory, appeared in the works of economists, publicists, and political activists of the Duchy of Warsaw and Kingdom of Poland periods.[^1000] The distinct legal situation of agricultural colonists compared to the general Polish peasantry was noted, as was the contribution of foreign craftsmen to economic life. Nevertheless, opinions on colonization and colonists formulated at the beginning of the nineteenth century were general in nature and merely signaled the phenomenon without attempting to describe it in greater detail. Among them stood out an exceptionally apt assessment of the conditions of German migration eastward, made by Hugo Kołłątaj.[^1001] It did not, however, become embedded in collective consciousness, nor did it become an element taken into account in later analyses of the phenomenon. Far more details concerning, among other things, the layout of settlements, their buildings, social relations prevailing in the colonies, and the customs of their inhabitants, we owe to the few descriptions and accounts of ethnographers from the second half of the nineteenth century. These have today the value of source materials.[^1002] It is regrettable that the appeal made at that time by Stanisław Ciszewski for more comprehensive research in the Olęder community went unanswered. Observing the colonists' resistance to assimilation and the passivity of the surrounding Polish population, Ciszewski encouraged the documentation of all elements of the culture of the German peasants.[^1003] The impetus for a closer look at the colonists in the Kingdom of Poland came only with the expropriation action conducted by the German authorities in the Prussian partition territories in 1908–1912. The resonance it aroused in Polish society across the three partitions resulted in the Germans-colonists of the Kingdom of Poland being noticed, as if anew. The political background of this interest produced an abundant crop of publicistic works, over which — as over the few book-length publications — presided the thesis of the German danger threatening the Polish possession of land in the Kingdom. It is worth noting that Russian publicists, reacting to the deteriorating German-Russian relations, spoke of the Germans in the Kingdom of Poland in a similar tone.[^1004] Among the most frequently cited and commented upon were the works of Bolesław Koskowski,[^1005] and especially Stefan Górski[^1006] and Henryk Wiercieński. S. Górski pointed to the very dynamic — indeed twofold — growth of the German population in the Kingdom of Poland in the period 1863–1903. At the beginning of the twentieth century, Germans constituted 5% of the total population, numbering close to half a million persons, of whom 62% lived in the countryside. In the demographic potential of the German colonists, in their economic superiority and organizational skills, he saw a real threat of a formal economic invasion of Germany upon landed property in our country. This catastrophic vision was accompanied by arguments about a deliberate, Germany-directed course of expansion. S. Górski pointed to the zonal, latitudinal layout of the colonist villages, the concentration of settlements along communication lines, the clustering around military fortresses (Modlin, Toruń, Dęblin) or railway junctions (Koluszki, Skierniewice). This was supposed to attest to colonization in accordance with plans for expanding the borders of the German state, of its drive toward the Black Sea. The concentration of German colonist settlements in only selected districts of Lublin gubernia was also confirmed by H. Wiercieński, who in the years preceding World War I was regarded as a specialist in demographic and population questions in the Kingdom of Poland, and especially in the problems of German colonization in the Lublin region. In numerous articles and contributions scattered across various periodicals, he included factual material that remains valuable today, concerning the southeastern territories of the Kingdom.[^1007] The argumentation of S. Górski and H. Wiercieński, maintained in a nationalist and Neo-Slavist spirit, proved surprisingly tenacious, and for a long time set a certain standard in approaches to problems connected with German rural settlement and the attitudes of colonists. One can speak outright of the formation of a peculiar topos in the descriptions of these questions.[^1008] It may be surprising that Adam Stebelski — a historian known for solid, source-based works — succumbed, for example, to the temptation of repeating arguments about the strategically determined location of villages.[^1009] For our purposes, the most essential are, however, statements which on the one hand undermine, if not entirely negate, the validity of all the accusations leveled against the colonists, and on the other hand confirm the almost complete absence of scholarly inquiry into this community before 1914. Writing about it, concern was expressed that due to a lack of appropriate information it was impossible to illuminate [...] thoroughly and present in figures the state of that silent, ant-like, cunningly advancing colonization, constituting a peaceful conquest of the country, carried out under the protection of the laws of a friendly power [i.e. Russia — K.P.W.'s note].[^1010]

Complaint was made that:

no one has yet taken it [colonization — K.P.W.'s note] up seriously, examined it comprehensively, devoted the time and effort to move beyond generalities and reach the actual state of affairs and depict it as it should be depicted. We do not possess statistical data [...], we do not yet have a map that would show us the strength of German colonization and demonstrate its planfulness; we have not sought to examine closely the life of the foreign newcomers living on our land.[^1011]

Similar neglect in research on settlement in the modern period was also noted in Germany.[^1012] It would be difficult to find a more emphatic confirmation of the a priori nature of assessments and the lack of source grounding for opinions about colonization and colonists formulated at the turn of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries, which created around this topic a climate far removed from scholarly impartiality.

The basic stock of studies on the problems that interest us was produced in the years 1920–1944, mainly through the efforts of authors coming from the German minority community in Poland. With numerous, minor, contributory but not infrequently detail-rich works, they responded to their community's interest in its past and historical roots of German presence in Poland (*Deutschtum in Polen*).[^1013] At the same time, they preserved the tradition of their own diaspora national group, sketching a fragment of the broad panorama of German settlement in Europe. In this group, a leading role was played by historians and regional scholars — mostly teachers in gymnasium and German schools in the Second Polish Republic (among others Albert Breyer, Erwin Kiss, Robert Klatt, Wolfgang Kohte, Walter Kuhn, Hermann Textor) — gathered around the journal *Deutsche Wissenschaftliche Zeitschrift für Polen*, edited from 1924 by Alfred Lattermann. The 1920s also saw the debut of Oskar Eugen Kossmann, a geographer and historian (after 1945 he would drop his second name in most publications), and of Otto Heike, a Łódź journalist and autodidact in the field of history. The personnel capacity and scholarly skills of this group were quite sufficient to undertake various works in the field of regional history, in accordance with the motto *jeder Stadt und jedem Dorf eine Heimatgeschichte*.[^1014] Individual topics were treated in several versions, intended for publication in various periodicals addressed to different categories of readers. This created the impression of great research and popularizing activity, while also contributing to growing interest in the problem and broadening knowledge of it.

*Unsere Forschung um die Geschichte des Deutschtums in Kongreßpolen steckt ja erst in den Anfängen*, wrote August Müller in 1929, encouraging the undertaking of archival research, fieldwork, and the search for documents remaining in private hands — including those of the families of former village headmen.[^1015] He appealed for making accurate copies of documents found and publishing them even in the daily press. This appeal did not go unanswered. The newspapers published in Łódź lent their columns willingly: the *Lodzer Volkszeitung*, and especially the more conservative, nationally-oriented *Freie Presse*.[^1016] Already in 1926, Hermann Rauschning published in the widely-read *Deutsche Blätter in Polen* a comprehensive and detailed questionnaire, intended as a methodical guide for all those interested in documenting the folk culture of the German community in Poland.[^1017] Research of this kind was undertaken on a broader scale by Robert Klatt, a long-serving teacher at the German gymnasium in Sompolno. Among his studies, those that recorded folkloric texts deserve particular attention.[^1018] It is interesting that R. Klatt regarded Adolf Eichler as his spiritual patron, calling him *der Altmeister deutschen Heimatforschung unseres Landes*.[^1019] A. Eichler was undoubtedly the leading figure among the German community of the Kingdom of Poland in the first two decades of the twentieth century, a spokesman for the preservation of its far-reaching distinctiveness. Political engagement that was detrimental to Polish interests led in 1920 to his expulsion from Poland. Beyond an extensive publicistic output, Eichler also left a synthetic work *Das Deutschtum in Kongreßpolen*, regarded before 1939 as a complete, panoramic picture of the achievements of the Germans in the former Russian partition.[^1020] Evaluating the results of the rural colonists' farming, he found that only those living in the Vistula valley had agricultural achievements comparable to the state of peasant farming in other Prussian provinces.[^1021]

In terms of breadth of interests, the richness of materials gathered, and the number of publications, Albert Breyer — a teacher at schools in Zgierz and Sompolno — clearly stood out among the scholars of the German past in Poland. His extensive output, based both on archival and fieldwork research conducted in villages inhabited by German peasants, can still today serve as a foundation for deeper research into the history and culture of the German community in Poland before 1945. For Breyer, the existence of German peasant culture in Poland was an undeniable fact. He repeatedly demonstrated that German national elements were most carefully cultivated and survived the longest in precisely this community.

*Wie jene Rodebauern ist Breyer zuerst seinen Weg allein gegangen, vergessen und unbekannt im Mutterland*, wrote E. O. Kossmann in an obituary devoted to Breyer, simultaneously signaling a certain essential characteristic of the output of the German regional scholars in the Second Polish Republic.[^1022] The significance of Breyer's works for knowledge of the German past in central Poland was assessed as highly as the merits of Erich Schmidt with regard to the Poznań region, or of Raimund Friedrich Kaindl with regard to Galicia. However, this was an opinion that did not extend beyond the community in which he worked. The significant output of Breyer, like that of the other German authors of his generation documenting the history and achievements of the Germans in central Poland, was practically unknown outside the borders of Poland. Also telling was the absence of contacts with Polish historians. Representatives of the German community took part in the IV Congress of Polish Historians held in Poznań in 1925, at which directions of research in the field of Polish history were being mapped out, but the subject of the German past in Poland was entirely passed over.[^1023] The German regional historians had no connections with academic circles in Germany, and those circles showed no interest in research on German presence (*Deutschtum*) in the east. This remained the domain of other institutions, such as the *Deutsches Ausland Institut* in Stuttgart, which was among other things the publisher of the aforementioned work by A. Eichler.[^1024] The history of German rural settlement in Poland remained the domain of regional and local history (*Heimatgeschichte, Regionalgeschichte*).[^1025] The works of German authors produced in the 1920s and 1930s, beyond their documentary purposes, were intended to strengthen German national consciousness and served to build bonds among compatriots remaining in the diaspora (*Zusammenwachsen der Volksgruppe*). In the changed political conditions after 1918, it became necessary to justify the thesis of the generational rootedness of Germans in Poland, building national consciousness on the basis of historical consciousness. In numerous publications from the interwar period, nineteenth-century settlement was presented as continuing the tradition of German colonization under German law in the Middle Ages. In extreme cases, this opinion took the form of the assertion that the role of German peasants in the nineteenth century was the same as in the thirteenth century: they fulfilled the role of pioneers promoting the best methods of farming.[^1026]

The settlement of the German population between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula rivers was assessed above all as an activity in the service of culture and the building of a German cultural landscape (*Kulturlandschaft, Kulturboden*) in the east. Reference was indeed made to agitation on the part of the government and landowners to bring German peasants to the country, but mainly in order to negate the accusation, raised from the Polish side, of German aggressiveness, and simultaneously to emphasize the role of the newcomers as economic pioneers. The legal privileges accorded to the settlers and the material assistance given to them were treated as one aspect of the question. No recognition was given to them as a fundamental advantage in the phase of founding villages. The economic stagnation of the majority of colonist villages — already noticeable to observers at the end of the nineteenth century — was also passed over in silence. The German colonist was thus presented not as a farmer, but above all as a German. The way in which he farmed was not linked to economic and social development, but was classified from a national point of view. This made it possible to describe the entire activity of the settlers as *deutsche Kulturarbeit*. In the 1930s, a clear politicization of interest in the history of colonists in Poland became visible. The dominant trend remained *Heimatforschung*, increasingly harnessed within the framework of eastern studies (*Ostforschung*), reduced to *Volksforschung*.[^1027] In 1934, Walter Kuhn, historian and linguist, announced a detailed and multifaceted program of research into German linguistic islands (*Sprachinselforschung*). An extensive conceptual apparatus was also created to serve this highly eclectic field of research.[^1028] Soon, almost all works concerning German settlement in Poland acquired a political, evaluative dimension, until in the second half of the 1930s they took the form of the slogan: *The German as creator of the cultural landscape in the East!* After 1933, the achievements of the Germans in Poland began to be interpreted not as their exclusive merit, but as an overall German *Aufbauleistung*. Research on emigration undertaken in the Third Reich and beyond was meant to serve the building of bridges between the Germans in the old Reich and the Germans scattered abroad — the foreign Germans (*Auslanddeutschtum*). The study of linguistic islands, genealogy, and the history of settlement were to constitute parts of an emerging *Volksgeschichte*.[^1029] Migration to the east was interpreted not through the prism of demographic and economic losses in the homelands of the emigrants, but as supporting and strengthening the German nation in the east.[^1030]

One must agree with the opinion that the German literature of the interwar years compellingly defined the scope and manner of writing about rural settlers in the Kingdom of Poland. Almost all later Polish works were written from a defensive position, repelling the arguments of continuity and civilizational superiority of the colonists.[^1031] The output of Polish historiography in the interwar period in the field of rural settlement research was quantitatively more modest. Beyond valuable monographs by Maria Dobrowolska,[^1032] Zofia Cichocka-Petrażycka,[^1033] and Henryk Lepucki,[^1034] relating to the territories of Galicia and Volhynia, one can point to a series of publications documenting research on Olęder settlement.[^1035] With regard to Greater Poland, Władysław Rusiński was working on this problem, but the publication of his research results was prevented by the outbreak of World War II. The minor materials published by Polish regional historians were of little significance.[^1036] In this situation, the image of German settlement between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula rivers functioning in public reception was shaped almost exclusively by German-language publications. It is therefore not surprising that, in the absence of Polish source-based research, many theses formulated by German scholars were accepted as uncontested. One of these was the conviction that the descendants of the first agricultural settlers were supposed to constitute a natural demographic reservoir for the emerging industry in the Łódź district.[^1037] In the mid-nineteenth century in the cities of the district, and particularly in Łódź, many textile enterprises were said to have been founded by newcomers from the nearby German villages (*von Zuwanderern aus den umliegenden deutschen Dörfern errichtet wurden*).[^1038] This argumentation, replicated in numerous publications, was not without influence on the contemporaneous works of Polish authors.[^1039]

At this point it is appropriate to devote somewhat more attention to the broader conditions accompanying the historiographical revival that was already signaled above, which took hold around the problems of German colonization in the interwar years. It cannot be denied that the intensive, multi-stage settlement movement involving the German population in the territory of central Poland objectively invited its scholarly analysis. However, the approach of Polish and German historians to the same subject of research was fundamentally different. An analysis of the extensive and representative set of German literature on the problems of settlement in the Polish lands, mainly in the territory of the Kingdom of Poland, reveals four fundamental theses contained within it: 1) the conviction of the temporal and territorial continuity of German settlement, 2) the emphasis on its numerical scale and, in its wake, the cultural and civilizational role it played; 3) recognition of all waves of settlement as exclusively German; 4) the denial of the phenomenon of assimilation (acculturation) of the settlers. These were formulations entirely consonant with the views constituting the essence of the ideology of the German East, which in the interwar period was accepted by the overwhelming majority of Germans, and after 1933 became part of official policy.[^1040] The identification

Stadt und ihrer Umgebung, Posen 1939, p. 66; F. Böhm, Litzmannstadt. Geschichte und Entwicklung einer deutschen Industriestadt, Posen 1941, p. 38. See also: E. O. Kossmann, Die Anfänge des Deutschtums im Litzmannstädter Raum, Leipzig 1942, pp. 167–247; O. Heike, 150 Jahre Schwabensiedlungen in Polen 1795–1945, Leverkusen 1979, pp. 40–149.

Heinrich Sybel's view that only the colonization of the east is the proper task of the German nation, alongside the conviction that the eastward movement (Ostbewegung) is a lawful pattern of Germanic and German history, set the dominant historiographical model. With regard to Polish lands, works devoted above all to the location of towns under Magdeburg law and to agrarian colonization were subordinated to it.[^1041] The difference in approach to key issues in the shared Polish-German past — including the question of settlement — manifested itself most starkly at the International Historical Congress in Warsaw in 1933, to which the German delegation brought a freshly prepared volume, Deutschland und Polen. Beiträge zu ihren geschichtlichen Beziehungen, edited by Albert Brackmann. In the German view, the work explained in an impartial manner the Polish-German historical community of fate (Schicksalgemeinschaft).[^1042] The theses contained in the volume were not discussed at the conference, since Polish historians had not had time to familiarise themselves with them. Soon, however, sharp and incisive critical responses by Polish scholars appeared in professional periodicals.[^1043] Not much later — in 1934 — another polemic arose, prompted by a book by Kurt Lück, substantial portions of which were devoted to rural colonization.[^1044] Kazimierz Tymieniecki, in a factual and precise reply concerning the medieval period, demonstrated that K. Lück was trying at all costs to inflate the scale of German colonization, but he did not engage in polemic with the German historian's findings regarding agricultural colonization in the nineteenth century.[^1045] The decision to adopt such a position was determined not only by the research competence of the outstanding Polish medievalist. Every historian attempting to go beyond the basic factography — concerning, for example, the time and circumstances of the founding of individual colonies, or the endowment of settlers with land — faced numerous difficulties. The most serious of these was the dispersal of individual source materials, which forced painstaking and very time-consuming archival searches. The conclusions that could be drawn on that basis could only be partial in character, disproportionate to the effort involved. This explains the lack of greater interest among Polish historians in research on German agricultural colonization in the nineteenth century.[^1046] It should be noted, however, that at the seminar conducted by Stanisław Arnold at the Warsaw School of Economics (Szkoła Główna Handlowa), several diploma theses were prepared on the economic activity of German settlers in the lands of the Kingdom of Poland in the first half of the nineteenth century. Those preserved to this day in the SGH Library deserve to be recalled if only because of the sources used in them, some of which no longer exist.[^1047]

For understanding the differences in approach to the subject of research and the quantitative disproportion between Polish and German publications, the fact of the ideological — or even political — stimulation of German research on settlement was also of significance.[^1048] For Polish historians of the interwar period this problem had only a cognitive significance, whereas for German researchers it was connected in a more or less visible way with the desire to document the presence of representatives of their nation in the east. This dissonance explains to some degree the tenor of the decisive majority of Polish historical works published in the first years after the end of the Second World War, devoted to various aspects of Polish-German relations. Their critical character was often linked with a striving to diminish or relativize German influences on Polish politics, the economy, and culture. Such was the character of the first postwar synthetic panorama of the history of German colonization in Polish lands from the Middle Ages to contemporary times, by Zdzisław Kaczmarczyk.[^1049] Conceived as a riposte to the nationalist current in German writing, it omitted from the German historiographical output those works presenting more balanced assessments. Nor was it free from the repetition of theses not confronted with sources — such as the one speaking of settlers moving from South Prussia to the Black Sea coast.[^1050] A renewed treatment of this topic, presented by Z. Kaczmarczyk in 1974, can be regarded as free from these shortcomings.[^1051] One of the most controversial issues present in both historiographies was the number of German immigrants settled in Polish lands in the nineteenth century. An article by Adolf Różański,[^1052] published posthumously in 1948 and based on research conducted before 1939, constituted an important contribution to the debate on the number of German settlers who arrived in the Kingdom of Poland before 1830. It was a source-documented closing of disputes around a question that had aroused much emotion since G. Schmoller stated that in the years 1818–1830 some 300,000 Germans had flowed into the Kingdom.[^1053] According to the calculations of A. Różański, corrected by Gryzelda Missalowa, in the period 1810–1827 approximately 55,000 immigrants arrived in the Kingdom of Poland from German lands, of whom approximately 35,000 came in the years 1819–1827. More than a quarter of that number were agricultural colonists.

The legacy of the Second World War meant that research into all manifestations of the German presence in Polish lands took entirely different paths in Poland and in Germany. For ideological reasons this subject matter — as indeed the whole question of Germans in the east — was absent from the historiography of the German Democratic Republic, whereas it found continuation in the Federal Republic of Germany, where authors who had already been creatively active in the Second Polish Republic settled. Among them were: Oskar Kossmann, Otto Heike, and Eduard Kneifel. They made use of no new archival resources, a fact that can only be partially explained by the inaccessibility of Polish archives. New texts were based on pre-war publications, occasionally enriched with information from previously unused sources.[^1054] No new methods were applied; no attempts were made at social, cultural-historical analyses, for which the old material to a considerable extent gave licence. It can be said that publications issued in the 1950s were for a long time imprisoned in the tradition of pre-war historiography, repeating conclusions formulated earlier. From an analysis of the content and circumstances of the production of publications speaking of the achievements of German settlers (both rural and industrial) in Poland, it emerges that attempts were made to instrumentalize the social and cultural accomplishments of Germans abroad, using them as a means to serve the integration of resettled persons with the inhabitants of the Federal Republic of Germany. In publications of the 1950s and 1960s an attempt was made to employ the following comparison: just as the ancestors in Poland had created a cultural landscape (Kulturlandschaft) from scratch, so their descendants should grow into the society of Germany.[^1055] In the 1960s and 1970s, in turn, an attempt appeared to interpret German achievements in Polish lands as an accomplishment (Aufbauleistung) of which all Germans could be proud.[^1056] Mention must also be made of a current that might be called the historical-nostalgic. Within its framework there appeared memoirs of lost small homelands, often enriched with previously unknown source accounts and iconographic material.[^1057] It should be noted that the question of German settlement in the east, politically compromised by its close connections with the doctrine of Volksforschung, did not become the subject of research in German academic circles. In 1977 Werner Conze defined the topic of migration as the discovery of a new area in historical research, which seemed to herald a growth of interest in the history of settlement.[^1058] The existence of research institutions specializing in this subject did not, however, result in the appearance of publications presenting findings of research into emigrations to Central and Eastern Europe.[^1059] Researchers were mainly interested in emigration to America.[^1060] All of this contributed to the consolidation of the stereotype of the German colonist in Poland that had already taken shape earlier. On the one hand he was seen as a disseminator of culture; on the other, as a representative of the fifth column and an executor of the Drang nach Osten mission.[^1061]

The difference in approach to the problems of German agricultural settlement by Polish authors after 1945 was connected with a fundamental change in the methodological basis of historical research. One of the key issues with respect to the nineteenth century became the study of the genesis of capitalist relations in the Kingdom of Poland and the associated transformations in the economy and social structure. Therefore, research on the economy of German settlers formed part of research on the history of the Polish village in the period of transition from feudalism to capitalism. The connection between settlement and primary accumulation in agriculture was quickly recognized. The beginning of this process was seen, among other things, in the founding of Olęder settlements at the close of the eighteenth century. The attention of Polish historians was therefore attracted to those clusters of foreign newcomers that had been founded as Olęder settlements. In 1947 a book by Władysław Rusiński appeared, summarizing years of research (as underscored by the recorded publication date) on Olęder settlements in Greater Poland.[^1062] The work provided an exhaustive discussion of the problem, based on thorough analysis of sources. It was at the same time one of the first analyses in Polish historiography of the totality of production in the rural household. Rusiński demonstrated that the Olęder farm developed under better conditions than the farm of serfs under corvée obligations. This was determined by the privileged economic position of the Olęders: personal independence, ownership (usufruct) of land, buildings, and livestock, low rents, minimal labour obligations, freedom in disposing of the fruits of their labour. On the disputed question of the origin of the Olęders, he acknowledged that determining their ethnic affiliation presents great difficulties, since direct source accounts are almost entirely lacking. The subject of Olęder settlement in the central part of Polish lands was also taken up by Jakub Goldberg.[^1063] His dissertation constituted a critical engagement with the work of Eugen Oskar Kossmann from 1942,[^1064] whom he accused of failing to make use of such a basic source as the lustrations of royal estates, as well as of studies concerning the history of the village. On the basis of sources, Goldberg established that in the voivodeships of Łęczyca and Sieradz, Olęder colonization was not a mass process. The number of Olęder villages in the Łęczyca voivodeship did not exceed 60, and in the Sieradz voivodeship there were only 11. Nor was Olęder settlement everywhere the only method of enfranchisement. An important contribution to the analysis of the extent of Olęder colonization was made by the results of the linguistic research of Kwiryna Handke.[^1065] The author, in polemic with the views of A. Breyer, explained the regularity of the transition of the name Holendry to Olendry, consistent with the rules of the Polish language and its dialectal variants.[^1066] On the margins of these reflections there appeared a very interesting observation. The name Holendry was used above all as a distinguishing mark for settlements with a status different from that of neighbouring settlements. In the areas of the Żuławy, where Olęder settlement had made the greatest progress, this name does not occur at all. The thesis of K. Handke is confirmed by an analysis of cartographic materials, particularly interesting in the case of a map of the environs of Gdańsk, made by Daniel Friedrich Sotzmann in 1783. Shown on it are two settlements: Kleine Hollaender and Grosse Hollaender, lying on the right (eastern) bank of the Vistula branch known as the Łacha Szkutnicza (Die Schuiten Lack), now the Kaszubski Canal (within the port of Gdańsk). Drainage ditches draining the surrounding areas and tree plantings (willows?) along the Łacha Szkutnicza leave no doubt as to the activity typical of the Hollanders carried on there.[^1067] The people of the time saw fit to mark their distinctiveness with names indicating the ethnos and legal relations of the two settlements. In central Poland in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, Olęder settlements were perceived similarly as enclaves, and were therefore distinguished by the name holendry (olendry). In the Stanislawian era, the term kolonia began to be used as a synonym for holendry, contrasting it with the nucleated village.

A different way of interpreting the genesis and significance of the terms holendry and olędry was presented by German researchers. Walther Maas, who had been working since 1925 on Olęder settlers across the extensive territories between Silesia and Pomerania, argued for a difference between the designations Holländer and Hauländer. The first of these was to refer to settlements whose inhabitants were able to drain land, while the second, deriving aus dem Aktendeutsch der preußischen Beamten, was to denote settlements established in forests as a result of forest clearing.[^1068] In a publication issued in 1942 and reprinted with minor abridgements in 1968, W. Maas dealt with the characterization of settlements designated as Hauland and Rumunki, maintaining that they were two different forms of settlement. The term Hauland was to designate villages densely inhabited by Dutch, North German, Brandenburgian, or Silesian settlers, established on the basis of a written settlement contract. Rumunki, on the other hand, were small, often single-farmstead (Einzelhöfe) homesteads in which, on the basis of a private, oral agreement, peasants from East Prussia farmed.[^1069] The change of legislation under the influence of French dominance in Europe brought about, in W. Maas's view, the end at the close of the eighteenth century of the process of forming settlements of the Hauland type, whereas Rumunki continued to be established throughout the entire nineteenth century. The results of detailed source-based research prove that the generalizing conclusions of W. Maas were erroneous. The process of clearing forests and establishing settlements in them continued with great intensity throughout the entire pre-enfranchisement period. Rumunki were confined almost exclusively to areas lying east of the Vistula (the voivodeship, later gubernia, of Płock), and owing to their proximity settlers from East Prussia could reach them. In the western part of the Kingdom of Poland the term Rumunki was not used. There one encountered Pustkowia [wastelands]. It should be underscored that W. Maas, like E. O. Kossmann, did not define the features by which a settlement recognized as Olęder should be characterized. As the basis for such a classification, both of the mentioned researchers accepted each time the local practice, the folk tradition (Überlieferung des Vulgus), recording current settlement changes.[^1070]

In 1979 Władysław Rusiński again voiced his views on questions concerning the economy of Olęder settlements.[^1071] This time, in an extensive polemic with the views of W. Maas[^1072] and E. O. Kossmann,[^1073] he formulated the view that the thesis about the greater productivity of the Olęder farm would be justified if it could be demonstrated that under identical conditions and with the same feudal burdens, the Olęder achieved economic results better than the Polish peasant. Rusiński also expressed critical views on the method of both German authors. He charged them with selective use of sources and a very formalistic application of the methods of historical geography, overlooking the social sphere of the settlement process. The economy of the Olęders in the Vistula valley was presented in a new light by Wojciech Marchlewski, who published interesting results of his research in 1988.[^1074] He proposed that the economic model represented by Olęder settlement be examined as a socio-economic system comprising interrelated elements such as: territorial organization, settlement network, construction, technology of drainage works, and economy. By examining the relations between these elements over a long time span, he demonstrated that the basic source of livelihood for the Olęders in the studied area was cattle raising. An important place was occupied by linen weaving, which was the main source of income for specialists resident in every settlement. A comparison of the sizes of areas devoted to cultivation and of livestock numbers with the size of the average Olęder family allows one to conclude that Olęder farms were fully self-sufficient.

Because of the relatively well-preserved and non-dispersed source base, great interest was aroused by the progress of colonization conducted by the Prussian authorities in the new province of South Prussia, created in 1793. This question became the subject of research by Jan Wąsicki. In an article published in 1953, besides describing the process, he demonstrated that the action conducted on a grand scale by the Prussian authorities was not something new in the territories between the Warta, Pilica, and Vistula rivers.[^1075] It constituted rather a natural continuation of the efforts of Polish landowners aiming to settle wastelands and to draw profits from them once they had been cultivated. The Prussian authorities set themselves similar goals, although the local administration did not share the optimism regarding the success of this action. Otto von Voss, serving as minister of the province, pointed to the already settled Olęders, who as good farmers were worthy of government support. The concerns proved justified, since the Württembergers, who were settled in the greatest numbers, farmed incompetently. Moreover, those who had been assigned small plots abandoned them and moved on in search of better living conditions. The decision to continue the colonization action was determined after 1795 by demographic considerations: South Prussia, settled by German colonists, was to become a bridge between Silesia and New East Prussia. According to J. Wąsicki's estimates, in the years 1793–1806 approximately 6,000 colonists were settled in South Prussia. The action was costly and did not fulfil the hopes placed in it of filling the treasury with tax revenues and strengthening the German element. After 1807, the Prussian obligations towards the colonists were taken over by the authorities of the Duchy of Warsaw. These findings were repeated in substantially expanded form by J. Wąsicki in his book devoted to all aspects of Prussian policy in South Prussia.[^1076] In 1959 Tadeusz Mencel referred to J. Wąsicki's reflections in a synthetic sketch characterizing the Greater Polish village in the years 1793–1814.[^1077] He drew attention to peasant migrations in territories devastated by the military operations of 1807. He found that the greatest intensity of wandering occurred among those privileged by their position: colonists, Olęders, and occupancy holders. They frequently sold their livestock for next to nothing and moved to Russia, expecting to find more favourable conditions of settlement there. The remaining colonists bore their burdens collectively, which — with the smaller number of settlers — caused their rapid impoverishment and forced them to abandon their farms.

A characterization of the state of the colonists' economy in the inter-uprising period — that is, after a certain stabilization had been achieved by the group of settlers who arrived in the Kingdom of Poland in the 1830s and 1840s — became the subject of an article published in 1957 by Marian Żychowski.[^1078] Its source basis comprised reports submitted for the years 1842–1864 by civil governors to the Petersburg authorities. The homogeneity of the source material (statistical data) allowed the author to formulate a number of conclusions concerning the dynamics of the progress of colonization in this period and the state of the economy in the colonies. The peak period of German settlement in the Kingdom of Poland was the years 1846–1849. Colonists settled mainly in private estates, on terms very favourable to the landowners who were switching to capitalist farming. Żychowski linked this phenomenon with the mass eviction of Polish peasants that was occurring at the time. Until the end of the 1840s, the number of colonist farms settled on rent grew more rapidly than the number of rent-paying farms of local peasants. Therefore colonists became active on the domestic market earlier and to a greater extent than Polish peasants, and made an essential contribution to deepening the development of the commodity-money economy. This was particularly visible in the western and Lublin gubernias, where at the same time the average land endowment of colonists was higher than that of peasants both under corvée and as rent-payers. Colonists also surpassed their peasant surroundings with regard to the quantity of livestock they possessed. Żychowski's article remains to this day practically the only study that presents a broad characterization of the economy in villages inhabited by German colonists across the entire territory of the Kingdom of Poland. It must be remembered, however, that this is a picture seen from the perspective of local administrative authorities (at county level), almost entirely devoid of details showing the realities of settlers' lives.

In 1957 Ryszard Orłowski discussed the initiative of Andrzej Zamoyski, who in 1784 settled 90 families of German colonists in the estates of the Ordynacja.[^1079] The privileging of settlers both with regard to the size of their land allotments and the manner of its use, and the various forms of assistance with farming, was an imitation of Josephinian solutions. Thanks to these, colonists had better farming conditions than the surrounding peasants until the mid-nineteenth century. The direct impetus for A. Zamoyski was the patent of Joseph II of 1781, encouraging the Polish nobility to receive settlers as agricultural workers. Much suggests, however, that the entailed estate owner was disappointed by the farming practices of the newcomers. This may be evidenced by the fact that on land abandoned by colonists he settled native peasants, contrary to the settlement contracts. Constituting a small enclave, the German colonists quickly grew into the Polish environment and underwent polonization.

The question of attempts at colonization of Western Galicia by the Austrian authorities in the years 1799–1806 was taken up in a source-documented article by Tadeusz Mencel.[^1080] The area in question had already in the years 1781–1786 become the site of the so-called Josephinian colonization, and after its incorporation into the Kingdom of Poland a substantial part of it, lying between the Vistula and the Pilica, attracted the interest of neither the authorities nor the settlers. The action of settling German peasants originating mainly from the Palatinate in the treasury estates of Western Galicia, which practically began in 1802, was merely a minor fragment of the more broadly conceived colonization conducted in the Hungarian Banat occupied by Austria. The results of the Josephinian colonization, disproportionately meagre in relation to the outlays incurred, caused the Vienna authorities to exercise considerably greater caution in committing treasury funds to a subsequent, similar undertaking. Therefore recourse was had above all to Polish peasants, willing to cultivate existing wastelands, even though they had a reputation for laziness.[^1081] The emerging rent-based farms constituted an essential breach in the surrounding environment of corvée villages. Although the imperial decree of 1802 did not grant colonist peasants personal freedom, the possibility of selling the farm and the freedom of movement with exemption from corvée actually conferred personal freedom on this group of peasants. Polish peasants settling on wastelands also availed themselves of it. In general, the colonization action of the years 1799–1806 produced no visible effects; it did not extend beyond the treasury estates, in which it was not possible to settle more than approximately 300 families. Its only lasting result was the cultivation of existing peasant wastelands.

In 1963 there appeared the first of a series of articles by Wiesław Śladkowski devoted to the economy of German colonists in the south-eastern gubernias of the Kingdom of Poland.[^1082] At the centre of the author's interest were above all colonists who settled in the Lublin and Siedlce gubernias in the 1840s and 1850s. The conditions on which they settled were judged by Śladkowski to be worse than those previously granted to Olęders. This was connected with the already typically capitalist treatment of colonization by landowners, who through rents and laudemium fees sought to raise their incomes while limiting their own obligations towards colonists to a minimum. For these reasons colonists were settled on poorer land, often marshy, on forest clearings, or obliged to clear and grub out forest. This is confirmed by the very rapid growth of arable land used by colonists, observed in the years 1842–1852, resulting from the reclamation of wastelands. The quantity of livestock per farm also grew systematically. Possessing more livestock, colonists were able to better manure their arable land. Śladkowski drew attention to the danger lurking in the statistical data illustrating the picture of the economy in the colonies. Available sources indicate that colonists of individual counties differed greatly from one another in terms of their financial status. Particularly poor were the newly arrived, still in the process of establishing themselves. If they constituted a large group, they greatly lowered the average for a given county. Therefore caution is advisable when using statistical materials, particularly calculated averages. Due to the lack of sources it is impossible to compare colonists with rent-paying peasants, but a fairly clearly discernible difference exists between colonist farms and corvée peasant farms. The latter exceeded them only in the larger number of sheep. Colonist farms also stood higher in those areas in which the decisive role was played by women's labour (vegetable growing, dairy farming).

A separate article was devoted by W. Śladkowski to the problem of contacts and mutual interaction between colonists and their Polish surroundings. He based his reflections on a thoroughly exploited body of press material and literature.[^1083] His analysis allowed him to formulate the conclusion that in the contacts of settlers with the environment surrounding them, the essential role was played by processes of an economic and social character, taking place in the sphere of basic material relations. In nationally mixed colonies, in which Polish and German peasants were in direct proximity, contacts were closer, more frequent, and quickly led to polonization. Similarly in dispersed settlement. Fundamental importance in the process of mutual rapprochement was held by the ability to use the Polish language. Polonization also proceeded more rapidly in the milieu of Catholic colonists and in colonies situated close to towns. On the other hand, inhabitants of villages that were compact in area and bound by religious community had only minimal contacts with the Polish surroundings. The hostile attitude of colonists towards Polish national liberation uprisings, recorded in the literature and often outright unfriendly, was determined by economic reasons. The conclusion formulated by Witold Kula with regard to the attitudes of industrial settlers is equally true with regard to German peasants. Both were aware that their entire existence depended on the peaceful coexistence of the Kingdom with the Empire. To demand support for the Polish uprising from them was to demand a willingness to bring about their own material ruin.[^1084]

Nevertheless, W. Śladkowski noted that not infrequently colonists clearly crossed the boundary of the loyalty required of them in relation to the tsarist authorities.

In a subsequent publication devoted to German agricultural colonization in the territory of the Lublin and Siedlce gubernias in the second half of the nineteenth century, W. Śladkowski dealt with five issues: 1) the growth of settlement after the enfranchisement reform, 2) forms of land ownership, 3) property stratification of colonists, 4) agricultural economy, 5) the geographical distribution and purposefulness of colonization.[^1085] The dynamic growth in the number of German agricultural colonies in the described territory was the result of several causes, having nothing to do, however, with the alleged instinctive, almost irresistible drive eastward of the German element. Of fundamental importance was the critical state of large landed property after enfranchisement, urgently seeking sources of income. The price of land, lower by 25–30% in comparison with the western gubernias of the Kingdom of Poland, where the phenomenon of overpopulation in the colonies had just occurred, constituted an important incentive for moving beyond the Vistula. The purely economic premises of this wave of settlement are evidenced by numerous cases of moving to Volhynia, where land prices were even lower, after only a few years of farming, for example in Lubartów county. Among some colonists the desire to enrich themselves — an additional profit after selling land in the western gubernias — cannot be excluded either. As a result of post-enfranchisement migration, in the territory of the Lublin and Siedlce gubernias approximately 250 colonies were established, inhabited by approximately 45,000 settlers, constituting approximately 45% of all colonists in the Kingdom of Poland. In the majority of cases colonists became owners of land. Perpetual leases were common, short-term leases less so. There was a regularity whereby the poorer the colonist, the fewer his own financial resources, the less favourable the conditions of ownership (use) of land he obtained. Property stratification among colonists is difficult to reconstruct due to the lack of sources. In comparison with the local peasantry, colonists possessed a higher percentage of large farms (above 30 morgs) and fewer dwarf ones (below 3 morgs). Despite a certain development of vegetable growing, fruit growing, and livestock raising, the characteristic feature of colonist farms was self-sufficiency. There was a dominant tendency to close oneself off in exclusively agricultural occupations, with the primary aim of buying more land and endowing children with it. Śladkowski confirmed the conservatism of colonists noted by German authors and their stubborn adherence to old, traditional forms of life. As for the geographical distribution of colonies in the Kingdom of Poland, he unequivocally rejected the old opinions of publicists about the political and mainly military purposes of colonization, as finding no confirmation whatsoever in the sources.

W. Śladkowski summarized his many years of research on German agricultural colonization in the south-eastern part of the Kingdom of Poland in the nineteenth century in a book published in 1969.[^1086] In it all the threads signalled in the author's earlier publications were developed. In the studied territory German settlement developed dynamically, albeit in an uneven and irregular rhythm. A particularly violent influx of settlers took place 10–20 years after the enfranchisement reform in the Kingdom of Poland. Whereas the first wave of German settlers arriving in Polish lands was triggered by the character of the Prussian enfranchisement reform unfavourable to peasants, the second — moving within the boundaries of the Kingdom — was a consequence of the development of capitalist relations in agriculture in the western gubernias. Alongside objective factors, an additional moment favouring the development of German colonization was the benevolent attitude towards it on the part of the domestic authorities, first Polish, then Russian. Śladkowski challenged the views of German researchers concerning the economic achievements of colonists, estimated by comparison with the economic achievements of the local population. Until the enfranchisement reform, settlers were privileged rent-payers, possessing larger land allotments, paying lower rents, and their farms had more favourable proportions between different types of agricultural land. A great relief were the years of rent exemption and the absence of the constraints of the three-field system. Therefore they found themselves in a significantly better situation than corvée peasants. It is held that colonists achieved more favourable results visible in the economic balance not so much thanks to superior farming techniques as thanks to the more versatile profile of their farms. Vegetable growing and fruit growing, as well as dairy cattle raising, were fairly widely spread. The use of new tools or cultivation methods was, however, the result of general economic changes and not the result of individual innovative solutions. Even German authors agreed that at the beginning of the twentieth century colonists in the Kingdom of Poland lagged far behind German peasants. In a later period this situation began to change, but technical progress encompassed all categories of peasant farms. The advantage of colonist farms consisted above all in their greater market orientation. W. Śladkowski's book remains the most extensive study of the question of German agricultural colonization in the Kingdom of Poland in Polish historiography to date.

The political changes of 1989–1990 in Central and Eastern Europe confirmed, in an unintended way, how greatly historical scholarship and the dissemination of its results had been harnessed to the mechanisms of historical policy, to presenting a nationally accentuated way of viewing the past. This applies also to the subject matter of German settlement in the lands of central Poland discussed here. The first decade after German reunification can be described as a time of great opening in mutual contacts between Polish and German historians, which bore fruit in the establishment of direct cooperation and also in engagement in joint research ventures. A particular impulse was provided by the opening in 1993 in Warsaw of the German Historical Institute (Deutsches Historisches Institut Warschau), mediating in contacts and scholarly exchange and initiating scholarly discourse in the field of the history of Poland and Polish-German relations examined in a broad European context. Setting aside an analysis of the ideological premises, one must note fundamental changes both in the organization of historical scholarship in unified Germany and in the methodology of history. They can most briefly be described as the triumph of Sozialgeschichte and the decline of traditional Ostforschung.[^1087] This was accompanied by a clear change in research interests. Within the history of the nineteenth century, economic questions, the development of towns, and industry receded to the background, while questions of social transformation and modernization appeared.[^1088] The postulate of interdisciplinary research in the humanities, raised already in the 1970s, found the possibility of realization in historical research inspired more or less clearly by the achievements of Sozialgeschichte. Enormous cognitive value was recognized in the study of societies (social groups, local communities) that were multiply differentiated.[^1089] A kind of fashion arose for searching for roots, reconstructing genealogies, and the history of small homelands. This act of remembrance also became an essential element of Polish historical research. For many decades, the Germanness, or post-Germanness, of the relics of the presence of German colonists preserved in the landscape constituted a barrier excluding them from study and consigning them to oblivion. There thus arose a need to enrich knowledge of one's own past with threads hitherto absent from it. For the progress of research it was also significant that in the 1990s, both in Germany and in Poland, a generation of researchers unburdened in any way by the legacy of the Second World War came to the fore, ready to tackle hitherto sensitive problems in the field of Polish-German neighbourliness sine ira et studio. This brought with it the necessity of evaluating the opinions and conclusions contained in older German works. The Polish-German dispute over the assessment of the historical output of Otto Heike, triggered by the publication of Thomas Fuchs, yielded a number of important observations regarding the conditions in which German historians in the Second Polish Republic worked and created.[^1090] The work of Oskar Kossmann also came to be evaluated. In 1997, through the efforts of the Heimatkreisgemeinschaft aus dem

Lodzerindustriegebiet e. V., two volumes were published comprising the author's output from 1929–1995, scattered across journals and daily press.[^1091] The already elderly author at that time chose not to provide a commentary in which he might have addressed the currency of opinions and conclusions he had once formulated.[^1092] Polish and German researchers went considerably further toward a revaluation of the old German historiographical output — including that devoted to the problem of rural settlement — when they participated in 1998 in a joint conference in Łódź dedicated to the place of central Poland in German eastern research.[^1093] Distancing themselves from the bias of so-called emotional historiography, they identified the shortcomings of that research strand as well as the potential possibilities for supplementing it.[^1094] It was noted with regret that the most recent Polish works concerning Germans in central Poland had gone unnoticed, *weil niemand mehr aktiv über die Deutschen in Zentralpolen arbeitet*.[^1095] Some hopes for developing such research interests were placed in the activity of the Kommission für die Geschichte der Deutschen in Polen, whose membership also included Polish scholars.

### Post-1990 Scholarship and Open Questions

A kind of summary of the state of knowledge about German settlers in the Kingdom of Poland in the 19th century was the conference devoted to this problem, organized in 1996 by the German Historical Institute in Warsaw and the Higher Pedagogical School in Kielce. It gave Polish and German historians an opportunity to exchange views no longer constrained by ideological barriers. The volume of studies that emerged from the meeting simultaneously confirmed that in Poland, from the late 1980s onward, interest in national and religious minorities — including the German and Protestant ones — had been growing.[^1096] This brought with it, among other things, numerous publications concerning Lutherans in the territory of the Kingdom of Poland, authored by Tadeusz Stegner, in which the author went far beyond the findings known from the numerous publications of Eduard Kneiffel, or the syntheses of Stanisław Grelewki and Woldemar Gastpary. Source-rich works devoted to the history of Lutheranism in Mazovia are systematically published by Paweł Fijałkowski. Krzysztof Woźniak also devoted several texts to this subject. Work on Olęder settlement continues as well, being researched by Zbigniew Chodyła (with reference to Greater Poland) and by Wojciech Marchlewski and Jerzy Szałygin (the Vistula valley, Płock region, Łęczyca region). Joint Polish-German scholarly ventures also resulted in the development of research on the problem of acculturation and assimilation, for which the milieu of rural settlers constitutes an interesting area of study.[^1097] The same is true of inquiries into stereotypes in the mutual perception of Poles and Germans.[^1098] Relying almost exclusively on literary sources, Wojciech Wrzesiński wrote about the stereotypical view of colonists.[^1099] In the context of his reflections, it must be noted that when characterizing one stereotype, it is not difficult to employ another, one entrenched over decades. This applies, for instance, to the image of Germans from southern Germany, who in Polish literature are commonly perceived as Catholics, contrasted with the Protestant Prussians.[^1100] Meanwhile, southern Germany encompasses not only Catholic Bavaria, but also Protestant Württemberg, from which the overwhelming majority of peasant settlers in the Kingdom of Poland originated.

The few Polish works devoted specifically to German rural settlers and moving toward a more detailed understanding of their daily life and contacts with their surroundings are based on previously unexploited categories of sources in such research (district-level administration, notarial records).[^1101] For German historians, the presence of German peasants in the lands of central Poland is the legacy of one of the currents of the great migratory movements that unfolded across central Europe in the century from 1750 to 1850.[^1102] The broad panorama of German presence in eastern Europe, published in 1999 and devoted in large part to Polish lands, disappointed in its approach.[^1103] The work — free from evaluative formulations — revealed the methodological difficulties in utilizing literature produced in an entirely different research context of the 1930s.[^1104] The work by Sewerin Gawlitta, published in 2009, can be treated as a summary of the problems of German rural settlement across the entire territory of the Kingdom of Poland in the period 1815–1915.[^1105] In it the author employed a considerable body of archival sources, primarily Polish ones, relating to the south-eastern part of the Kingdom of Poland in the post-enfranchisement period. Gawlitta silently passed over the entire German historiographical legacy produced in the Second Polish Republic. Following the findings of M. Żychowski and W. Śladkowski, he devoted the most attention to the economy of the colonists, adding little — mostly from Polish studies — to questions of social and cultural matters. The title of the work seems to remain within the manner of the old German writings. The invitation (to the Kingdom of Poland) emphasized therein was not, after all, the only instrument facilitating the settlement of German peasants on Polish lands. The spontaneous character of this process, resulting from objective economic premises, is attested by numerous sources. The year 1915 that closes Gawlitta's reflections is, like many other chronological markers, a somewhat conventional date. It is certainly not a milestone in the history of German peasants in the Kingdom of Poland. It has in the background the tragic experience of the first months of that year for many colonists — deportations into the depths of Russia. The term *Ausweisung*, which is difficult to translate as anything other than expulsion or banishment, unnecessarily evokes other connotations (*Vertreibung*) and does not capture the essence of events at that time.[^1106] Without questioning the dramatic nature of the fates that befell approximately 37% of all Germans living in the Kingdom of Poland, it must be noted that the great majority of them returned to their farms and homes after the end of the war. At that point the German community in Poland faced the dilemma of defining its relationship to the newly established Polish state. For many German peasants — Russian subjects accused in 1914–1915 of treason in favour of Germany — this was a dilemma difficult to resolve.

It appears that along with the renewed exploration of issues related to the presence of German peasants on Polish lands, a tendency emerged to view them across a broader chronological scope and in a more extensive geographical perspective.[^1107] The deficits of previous research also became apparent — in many cases impossible to remedy. For instance, not only did scholars fail to investigate the actual Polish spoken by Germans, but the degree of linguistic assimilation was not studied at all.[^1108] The absence of this knowledge cannot be replaced by analyses of literary stylizations. The questions regarding the broadly conceived culture of the settlers, their intellectual horizons, family structure (marriages, fertility, etc.), mobility and the extent of their contacts with their surroundings remain open.[^1109] Under current conditions of conducting historical research, undertaking such painstaking and time-consuming studies seems — despite the existing source base — too difficult to accomplish. Better prospects are offered, in all likelihood, by incorporating the subject of German settlement into the broader current of contemporary research on migratory movements.

F. Kaindl in the work Geschichte der Deutschen… See: O. Balzer, O Niemcach w Polsce, "Kwartalnik Historyczny", vol. 25, 1911, no. 3/4, pp. 429–454. Kurt Lück engaged in conscious forgeries. [^1054]: E. O. Kossmann possessed numerous source extracts made before 1939 in Polish archives. Among them were lists of German settlements and settlers, drawn up by the Voivodeship Commissions in 1835. This was the most extensive — both in terms of geographical scope and detail — a kind of topographical picture of the settlement of the Kingdom of Poland by German newcomers. The originals were irretrievably destroyed in 1944. Fate proved more merciful to the copies, but meine diesbezüglichen Aktennotizen, die Sinn und Zweck der Liste deuten können, sind verloren gegangen, zusammen mit der ersten, noch in Warschau hergestellten Abschrift, wrote Kossmann in 1978, publishing the salvaged materials (O. Kossmann, Die Deutschen in Polen seit der Reformation. Historisch-geographische Skizzen, Marburg/Lahn 1978, pp. 278–279, 367–396). O. Heike, who served as director of the City Archive in the years 1940–1941, also possessed archival materials and copies of documents concerning Germans in Łódź and the Łódź district (B. Sobiczewski, Otto Heike i Archiwum Miejskie w Łodzi w czasach II wojny światowej [in:] Otto Heike. „Niemiecki dziennikarz z Łodzi" jako historyk, ed. B. Kwoka, Herne 2004, pp. 67–80).

[^1055]: This was served by journalism in various periodicals connected with the Hilfskomitee der evangelisch-lutherischen Deutschen aus Polen im Hilfswerk der Evangelischen Kirchen in Deutschland and the Heimatkreisgemeinschaft der Deutschen aus dem Lodzer Industriegebiet and Landsmannschaft Weichsel-Warthe, very frequently evoking memories of the inhabitants of German villages scattered between the Prosna, Pilica, and Vistula rivers.
