In the 1960s it was possible to record examples of dialects used by descendants of German settlers still living in central Poland (O. Heike, Die Sicherung der ostdeutschen Mundarten durch Tonbandaufnahmen, "Jahrbuch für ostdeutsche Volkskunde", Bd 10, 1966/1967, pp. 19–40).

[^1109]: H.-D. Löwe, Deutsche Migration nach Ost- und Südosteuropa im 18. Jahrhundert. Ergebnisse und neue Fragestellungen, [in:] *Migration nach Ost- und Südosteuropa...*, p. 444.

## Conclusion

The intensive influx of German peasants into the lands of central Poland spanned a period of approximately one hundred years, but was characterized by an enormous dynamic of change. Various state structures with differing legal systems and strategies of economic policy created the frameworks within which the economic activity of landowners was very effectively realized. The turn of the 18th and 19th centuries brought with it the beginning of the transformation of feudal society, which was increasingly absorbing solutions typical of the capitalist formation. On the agriculturally dominated Polish lands, the harbinger — and subsequently one of the driving forces — of the new order was the introduction of rent-based farming. It spread together with Olęder settlement. The legal order associated with it formed the basis for organizing rent-paying settlements right up to the enfranchisement of 1864. The settlement contracts, confirming perpetual-rent use of the land, also document the considerable durability of feudal rudiments — from dues in kind and labour rent to the propination compulsion. The progressive role of money rent was more than visible, particularly on the western and northern peripheries of the Kingdom of Poland. By reducing expenditures on the administration of manor farms and achieving greater monetary income from entry fees and rent, landowners began to have access to more considerable cash sums that could be directed toward investment. From the 1830s onward, a new manner of treating labour service — by converting it into rent — slowly began to enter agricultural practice. The settlement of rent-paying colonists was also the cheapest way of bringing wasteland under agricultural cultivation. The changes associated with settlement contributed to the development of a commodity-money economy and introduced proto-capitalist elements into the rural economy. This is unquestionably how one must assess the role played in the economic sphere by German settlers between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula.

The process of German peasants moving eastward, which intensified from the mid-18th century onward, was spontaneous in character. It was triggered by economic factors: the progressing social stratification and pauperization of the peasantry in the overpopulated lands of the German Reich, and the rising price of land. Significant impulses toward emigration were also: the lengthening of military service, motives of a religious nature, and the consequences of natural disasters. The swelling wave of emigration of German peasants from their homelands coincided with the time when landowners in Polish lands were seeking ways to intensify farming and increase profits. The spreading Enlightenment thought directed attention to the significance of the demographic factor for economic development and the growth of state prosperity. That this problem was recognized in the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth is evidenced by the complaints of several leading figures of contemporary political life, formulated in public debate. Observing the benefits that Prussia derived from the settlement of French Huguenots, regret was expressed that Poland had not taken the opportunity to open its borders broadly to French refugees. The recruitment of foreign colonists as a means of modern economic policy was an imitation by the Polish elites of the late 18th and early 19th centuries of both theoretical concepts (mercantilism, cameralism) and the practice prevalent in neighbouring countries: Prussia, Russia, and Austria. In the absolutist monarchies this policy was conducted centrally. In the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth there were several of its implementers: the monarch, the Church, and the nobility — which not infrequently led to rivalry in attracting settlers by offering competitive conditions. A singular novelty was the centrally planned and financially extensive action of populating, after 1793/1795, the new acquisitions of the Hohenzollern monarchy: South Prussia and New East Prussia. In central Poland this consolidated the row (linear) form of village construction, already known in that territory from earlier times but not always consistently applied. The authorities of the Duchy of Warsaw drew on the experiences of Prussian colonization policy, and to a lesser extent on Austrian achievements in Western Galicia — as evidenced by their treatment of the Swabian inhabitants of colonies established in the years 1793–1806. Despite a critical assessment of the results of Prussian colonization, neither in the times of the Duchy nor in the Kingdom of Poland was this method of modernizing the country abandoned. Moreover, the emigration regulations were refined in order to attract wealthy or skilled craftsmen immigrants.

The progress of German settlement in the pre-enfranchisement period cannot be examined without taking into account the creative policy of the government of the Kingdom of Poland, particularly in the autonomous period. Polish historical works have hitherto failed to notice that this policy was consistent with the colonization actions being carried out in Russia, directed toward the vast territories being annexed to the empire. Despite formal cooperation between the Warsaw and St. Petersburg authorities in attracting settlers, conflicts of interest and rivalry frequently arose. For emigrants from the Rhenish lands of the Reich, it was of little significance whether they were resettling to Polish lands or to Russia. The vast spaces in the east were equally unknown, mysterious, and enticing with the possibility of finding there a land flowing with milk and honey — as Württemberg folk tradition had it. The choice of settlement location was determined by a constellation of circumstances: natural conditions, the conditions proposed by the landowner, and finally the physical and financial condition of the immigrants. Over time the role of the geographical factor — most strongly felt in the Vistula valley — diminished in significance. In other areas, the attractiveness of settlement sites was determined by the amount of the entry fee and the level of rent. Numerous examples document the settlement in the Kingdom of Poland of immigrants whose initial intention had been to travel to Russia. Even more numerous sources confirm the undertaking of further journeys eastward after several or a dozen or so years of farming in the Kingdom. The stir that invariably arose in the settler community upon news of new settlement opportunities opening in Russia was telling. Even the most misleading information was given credence and credibility. The motivation for further migration could be either insufficient satisfaction with the results of farming to date, or the hope of finding still more favourable conditions and a rapid improvement in living standards. For this reason, the territories between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula were, in the first four decades of the 19th century, the scene of an exceptionally lively movement of population, among which the most numerous and most dynamic group consisted of newcomers from the lands of the German cultural sphere.

The recruitment and settlement of rent-paying colonists was only one of the instruments serving modernization in agriculture, but the only one that produced long-lasting social and cultural consequences. The newcomers were reluctant to live dispersed among Polish peasants, as was practiced particularly on state estates. They sought the possibility of settling as a community, often among relatives and acquaintances, in order to find support within the framework of a neighbourly and confessional community. The social and cultural dimension of the presence of German farmers between the Prosna and the Pilica and Vistula — once exaggerated in German historiography as culture-creating — is more complex. Through their religious distinctiveness, language, and way of life, German peasants differed from their Polish surroundings, but were treated by those surroundings as a whole — German colonists. The same way of viewing this collective became entrenched in Polish historiography. In reality the milieu of German settlers was strongly differentiated. The newcomers were aware of these differences and consistently endeavoured to cultivate and preserve the specificity of their own culture. The greatest degree of distinctiveness was shown by the *Niederunger* — German colonists in the Vistula valley. The conditions of existence and the mode of farming there influenced the development of specific forms of material culture not found in other concentrations of German peasants in central Poland. For a long time — not infrequently for three generations — differences persisted in the varieties of the German language (dialects) and in customs. Marriages were sought within the known group, most often within the boundaries of the parish. At least until the enfranchisement, German colonists in the Kingdom of Poland preserved a sense of connection with their countries of origin, but given the rarity of direct contacts, this was mainly a sentimental, emotional bond. A similar differentiation — though not as pronounced as in the case of territorial origin — was exhibited by the colonists with respect to confession. Alongside the decisive majority of Lutherans, there were also highly mobile Mennonites, whose mobility was determined mainly by the legislation unfavourable to them, as well as the Moravian Brethren, who propagated Pietist ideas and maintained constant contact with Herrnhut. From the turn of the 1850s and 1860s, Baptism began to gain numerous adherents among the German colonists. The awareness of distinctiveness toward the Polish surroundings rested primarily on the difference of confession and language. Until the 1870s–1880s — and in some localities even longer — the contact of German peasants with the Polish surroundings did not extend beyond economic (market) necessity. Processes of assimilation/acculturation were only in their embryonic stage; mixed marriages — the best catalyst for changes in attitudes — occurred only sporadically. This closing off within the circle of one's own village and parish was also the cause of the ossification of language, customs, and forms of daily life. The economic stagnation within almost self-sufficient households did not allow German peasants to serve as a model for their Polish surroundings. The settlers did not in any way influence the agrotechnical progress of the rural economy, as the authors of German studies of the interwar period had attempted to portray. At the centre of concern — both for the colonists and for those writing about them — remained the matters of religion and education, closely intertwined until 1864. This stemmed from the conviction about the enormous role played by the school in cultivating the mother tongue, treated as the most important component of national consciousness. The pre-enfranchisement period also coincided with the time of the formation and consolidation of the organizational structure of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church in central Poland, finding its support mainly in the rural milieu. Of nearly 100 Lutheran parishes and affiliates existing in the Kingdom of Poland in 1914, 53 were established before the end of the 1840s.[^1110] In the same period the institution of the cantor was formalized. The preserved sources of various provenance, relating to the rural milieu in the Kingdom of Poland before 1864, clearly document the existence of two parallel worlds: that of the German settlers and that of the Polish surroundings.

The distinctiveness — or indeed the foreignness — of the milieu of German settlers meant that for a long time they were not the subject of interest for Polish researchers. As a result, both in scholarly discourse and in ordinary consciousness, there became entrenched an image of the German colonist — or more precisely the colonist-German — as seen through the eyes of German chroniclers, themselves originating in the overwhelming majority from that same milieu. Seeking to preserve the memory of their ancestors' achievements and wishing to consolidate their cultural legacy, they brought about a kind of heroization of the German settlers, often far beyond their actual merits. This suggestive image Polish historians attempted to objectify, but this was not helped by the entanglement of the totality of Polish-German relations in the rules of historical politics — both of the *Ostforschung* current and of Polish propaganda from the years 1945–1990.

The question of the presence of German rural settlers remained one of the contentious problems of Polish-German neighbourliness. A large influence on the contemporary assessment of the settlement process was exerted by the objective progress in the historical and social sciences and the resulting new methodological inspirations. There was an abandonment of the national and evaluative key in the interpretation of processes occurring on ethnic borderlands and resulting from direct contact between different ethnoses. Confessional affiliation and personal nomenclature — once one of the most significant elements of research on German settlement in the lands of central Poland — lost their importance, as did numerical indicators.[^1111] These changes went hand in hand with the marginalization of the influence of ideological (political) factors in the interpretation of historical processes and phenomena, connected with the political transformations in Europe at the end of the 20th century. Depicting settlement processes against the broader background of the socio-economic events of the epoch became the norm. It is fully justified to state that the settlement of German peasants on Polish lands constituted one of the significant currents of migration, the scene of which was Europe between the Rhine and the Volga in the century bounded by the dates 1750–1850. No less dynamic migration processes, connected with the settlement of new lands, took place in the countries of the Habsburg monarchy. The American continent was also becoming an important direction of emigration, to which over 5.5 million Germans relocated in the years 1815–1914 — without it ever being suggested that they did so with a sense of a mission to create culture.[^1112] The view is slowly gaining ground that German settlement on Polish lands was merely part of a far broader migratory wave, undertaken around the mid-18th century and directed toward Russia. Perhaps of fundamental significance were the two manifestos of Catherine II of 4 December 1762 and 22 July 1763 on the free settlement of foreigners in Russia. The Empress intended to take under her protective wings the various Protestant confessions of Western Europe, knowing what benefits had accrued to the countries that had received the Huguenots.

The enfranchisement of peasants in 1864, and formally and definitively the ukase of 9 May 1866, closed the first, pioneering period of the presence of German farmers in the Kingdom of Poland. Consolidated as farmers, the religious bonds were being cemented within the already stabilized structure of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church. The subsequent decades were to bring the search for new places to farm for those among the sons of peasants for whom the family inheritance proved too small. Some of them moved to the south-eastern peripheries of the Kingdom, some to Volhynia and traditionally deeper into Russia, while some began to seek sources of livelihood in the developing cities.

## Summary: German Rural Settlement Between the Prosna, Pilica, and Vistula Rivers from the 1770s to 1866 — the Process and Its Interpretations

The book is the first presentation of the history of German rural settlement in the regions between the rivers Prosna–Pilica and the Vistula since the 1770s until 1866 in the Polish historiography. The inflow of settlers (colonists) from the German cultural area was a spontaneous process, triggered off by economic conditions in their countries of origin. It coincided with Polish landowners' attempts to modernize the agriculture, inspired by the Enlightenment ideas and contemporary economic doctrines. An effective way of such modernization was promoting socage. The author's attention is focused on: 1) the description of peculiar characteristics of the process of German peasantry's settlement in Central Poland, 2) the reconstruction of the particulars of the village life and the relationships between the villagers and their surrounding environment as accessible in source materials, 3) the characterization of the Polish-German controversy over how to assess the course and the importance of the referenced German rural settlement. The study is based on a vast store of Polish and foreign archive sources, including public records (notarial ones), not explored before. An extensive collection of publications made by the German regionalists from the background of the German minority in the Second Polish Republic was analyzed. Previously, those works had been passed over both by German and Polish researchers. The book is enriched with extensive cartographic materials. Nearly all the maps and plans contained in the appendices are published for the very first time. The progress of the settlement process is discussed in chapters I–IV. Chapter V is on the settlers' religious life and education, i.e. the only spheres that became institutionalized all along. They both were ranked very high, which was evidenced by regulations contained in all the known settlement contracts and, later on, legislation of the Duchy of Warsaw and the Kingdom of Poland. Much attention is given to teachers ('Kantor') and their significant part in the preservation of the language and faith in the local communities of the colonists. Chapter VI characterizes the relationships between the German settlers and their Polish milieu, questioning the view of the homogeneity of the German colonists' environment, existing in the Polish historiography. Quite the opposite, the aforesaid environment was strongly diversified, both in terms of territorial and religious backgrounds. German peasants' relationships with their Polish neighbors did not go beyond economic (market) necessities. The processes of assimilation and acculturation were still embryonic. Mixed marriages, acting as the best catalyst for change in attitudes, were contracted occasionally. The last chapter presents a wide variety of judgments of the individual stages in the rural settlement in Central Poland, concentrating on the issues which once were the pivot of the Polish-German historiographic dispute, i.e. the terminology and size of the settlement, the colonists' background, importance and services for the dissemination and elevation of culture in the colonized regions. As proven, the image of the German colonist as a pioneer of progress, widespread in the historiography and among the general public, is far removed from reality. The settlers by no means impacted on the agrotechnological progress of the farmstead. The growing stagnation in the colonists' almost self-sufficient farms prevented them from becoming a model for the Polish neighborhood. Granting freehold to the peasantry in 1864 as well as, formally and finally, the tsar's ukase of 9th May 1866 closed the first – pioneer – period of the settlement and presence of German peasants in the Kingdom of Poland. They became established as farmers, while their religious ties were being strengthened within the stabilized structure of the Lutheran Church of the Augsburg Confession. The next decades witnessed the peasants' sons whose inheritance from their family was too meagre seeking new places to settle in. Some of them headed the south-eastern frontiers of the Kingdom, some went to Volhynia and, traditionally, deep into Russia, and some others began to search after their livelihood in the developing towns.

## Zusammenfassung: Die deutsche bäuerliche Ansiedlung zwischen Prosna, Pilitza und Weichsel ab den 70er Jahren des 18. Jhs. bis 1866. Der Prozess und seine Interpretationen

Das Buch ist die in der polnischen Historiografie erste Präsentation der Geschichte der deutschen bäuerlichen Ansiedlung auf den Gebieten zwischen Prosna, Pilitza und Weichsel ab den 70er Jahren des 18. Jhs. bis 1866. Der Zustrom von Ansiedlern (Kolonisten) aus dem deutschen Kulturkreis war ein lebhafter, durch wirtschaftliche Faktoren in deren Herkunftsländern ausgelöster Prozess. Er fiel mit den von polnischen Landbesitzern unternommenen Versuchen der Landwirtschaftsmodernisierung zusammen, an denen sich Inspirationen durch Ideen aus dem Zeitalter der Aufklärung und durch aktuelle Wirtschaftsdoktrinen deutlich erkennen ließen. Eine erfolgreiche Modernisierungsmethode war damals die Verbreitung der Mietswirtschaft. Die Aufmerksamkeit des Autors konzentriert sich auf: 1) Beschreibung von spezifischen Merkmalen des Prozesses der deutschen bäuerlichen Ansiedlung in Mittelpolen; 2) Wiedergabe von quellenzugänglichen Einzelheiten aus dem Leben innerhalb von Dorfgemeinden und Relationen zwischen deren Bewohnern und ihrer Umgebung; 3) Beschreibung polnisch-deutscher Kontroversen im Bereich Beurteilung von Verlauf und Bedeutung der deutschen bäuerlichen Ansiedlung. Bei der Arbeit wurden umfassende Quellen aus polnischen und ausländischen Archiven benutzt, darunter bisher wenig genutzte Massenquellen (Notarurkunden). Darüber hinaus wurde eine umfangreiche Sammlung von Publikationen deutscher Regionalautoren aus dem Kreis deutscher Minderheit in der Zweiten Polnischen Republik analysiert. Ihre Arbeiten wurden bis jetzt sowohl von deutschen als auch von polnischen Forschern außer Acht gelassen. Eine Bereicherung des Buchs sind die darin enthaltenen umfangreichen kartografischen Materialien. Fast alle Landkarten und Pläne in Buchanhängen werden zum ersten Mal veröffentlicht. Der Verlauf des Ansiedlungsprozesses wurde in den Kapiteln I–IV besprochen. Kapitel V ist dem religiösen Leben und Bildungswesen unter den Ansiedlern gewidmet. Es waren die einzigen Bereiche ihres Lebens, die von Anfang an institutionalisiert wurden. Ihr Rang war sehr hoch und wurde durch die in allen bekannten Siedlungsverträgen enthaltenen Regelungen, und später durch die Gesetzgebung des Herzogtums Warschau und des Königreichs Polen bestätigt. Viel Aufmerksamkeit wurde den Kantoren und deren bedeutenden Rolle bei der Kultivierung von Sprache und Glauben in den Gemeinschaften lokaler Kolonisten gewidmet. Im Kapitel VI wurden die Beziehungen zwischen den deutschen Ansiedlern und deren polnischer Umgebung beschrieben. In Frage gestellt wurde die in der polnischen Historiografie existierende Meinung über die Homogenität des deutschen Kolonistenmilieus, das sich in Wirklichkeit durch große herkunfts- und konfessionsbedingte Unterschiedlichkeit auszeichnete. Die Kontakte der deutschen Bauern zu deren polnischer Umgebung gingen nicht über die wirtschaftliche (marktbedingte) Notwendigkeit hinaus. Assimilierungs-/Akulturierungsprozesse befanden sich erst in der Anfangsphase. Gemischte Ehen, die der beste Katalysator von erfolgender Einstellungsänderungen sind, waren nur sporadisch. Das letzte Kapitel präsentiert die Unterschiedlichkeit von Beurteilungen einzelner Phasen der bäuerlichen Ansiedlung in Mittelpolen, mit Schwerpunkt auf den Aspekten, die einst die Achse des polnisch-deutschen historiografischen Streites bildeten: auf Terminologie und Stärke der Ansiedlung, auf Herkunft, Bedeutung und Verdiensten von Kolonisten bei der Kulturentwicklung in den von der Ansiedlung betroffenen Gebieten. Nachgewiesen wurde die Tatsache, dass das in der Historiografie und öffentlichen Überzeugung weit verbreitete Bild der deutschen Kolonisten als Fortschrittspioniere von der Wirklichkeit abweicht. Die Ansiedler hatten keinerlei Einfluss auf den agrotechnischen Fortschritt in der Landwirtschaft. Die mit der Zeit fortschreitende Stagnierung von beinahe autarken Bauernhöfen der Kolonisten ließ sie nicht zu einem Vorbild für die polnische Umgebung werden. Die Bauernbefreiung von 1864, und formell und endgültig der Zarenerlass vom 9. Mai 1866 schlossen die erste, pionierartige Etappe der Anwesenheit deutscher Bauern im Königsreich Polen. Sie festigten ihre Positionen als Landwirte, religiöse Bindungen im Rahmen einer bereits stabilisierten Struktur der Evangelisch Augsburgschen Kirche wurden zementiert. Weitere Jahrzehnte sollten die Suche nach neuen Agrarwirtschaftsflächen für die Bauernsöhne, deren Familienerben zu klein ausfielen, mit sich bringen. Ein Teil von ihnen begab sich in die südöstlichen Grenzgebiete des Königreichs, ein Teil nach Wolyn und traditionell landeinwärts nach Russland, und ein Teil begann, nach Unterhaltsquellen in den sich entwickelnden Städten zu suchen.

Allergnädigster Herr!

zu resolvieren geruht haben, daß ein Theil des Laznower Forst durchaus urbar
gemacht und einer neuen Kolonisten Niederlassung unter dem Namen.

Groembach

Gewidmet, auch jedem dieser Ansiedler zur festen und unabänderlichen Richtschnur
bey seinen Rechten und Verbindlichkeiten eine in Form eines wahren zweiseitiges
Vertrages abgefaßten, von der unterzeichneten Königl.[ichen] Südpreuß.[ischen]
Kriegs- und Domainen Kammer zu Warschau zu vollziehende, und von dem Hohen
Südpreuß.[ischen] General Finanz Departement zu bestätigende Erbverschreibung
ausgefertigt werden soll; so ist zu dem Ende zwischen der gedachten Königl.[ichen]
Kriegs- und Doaminenkammer an einem, und dem Kolonisten.

Jacob Haaß am anderen Theile, nachstehender Erbzins Kontrakt, auf dem grund des Engagements Protocolls vom 1. Dec[em]br[i]. 1801 geschlossen worden.

§ 1.

Es über läßt die Königl.[iche] Krieges und Doaminenkammer dem Colonisten.

Jacob Haaß

Das zur Kolonie Groembach gehörige mit No. 7 bezeichnete Häusler Etablissement,
welches nach dem reducirten Plan von der Kolonie mit der Straße zwischen den
Zweihüfner und Häusler-Etablissements, mit der Karpiner Forst, und mit den Häusler Etablissements Nro. VI und VIII grenzt, und nach dem Vermessung Register [...]

### Appendix XI
Song of the Württemberg emigrants (1782). (Source: "Zeitschrift der Historischen Gesellschaft für die Provinz Posen", vol. 9, 1894, pp. 421–423).

Aus einem Lied württembergischer
Auswanderer nach „Preussisch Polen"

Jetzund ist es ausgemacht,
daß der Marsch geht nacher Polen;
man hat es herausgebracht,
daß man kein zurück darf holen;
trettet eure Reise an,
In das Polnisch Canaan!

Allhier ist es nimmer gut,
dort in Polen ist es besser;
fasset einen guten Muth,
dort gibt es auch volle Fässer;
bey dem Bier und Branden-Wein
kann man auch vergnüget seyn.

Was hilft euch der edle Wein?
Ihr darf doch sehr wenig trincken,
wollt ihr hier gleich lustig seyn,
müßt ihr an die Schulden dencken,
diese plagen euch alle Tag,
Daß man nimmer leben mag.

Sitz hier einer in der Ehr,
daß er muß ein Amt verwalten,
da kommt er so gleich daher,
thut den Armen übel halten;
spricht der Arme nur ein Wort
heißt es gleich: ins Zucht-Hauß fort!

Nun so lasset uns sein bald
Reissen in das Preussisch Polen,
weil man dorten in dem Wald
kann viel Wax und Honig holen;
Honig in dem Branden-Wein
Das mag auch recht köstlich seyn.

Honig ist recht Zucker süß,
so kann nichts gefunden werden,
drum so hebe auf die Füß,
springe über Stein und Erden,
in das Polnisch Canaan,
wo man Honig gnug trifft an!

Free translation:
**Song of the Württemberg emigrants to "Prussian Poland"**

It is now decided that the march leads to Poland; it has been said that no one will be allowed to turn back; set off on your journey to the Polish Canaan!
Here it is never good; in Poland it is better; be of good cheer, there are also full barrels there; with beer and brandy one can also have a good time.
What use to you is the noble wine? You may drink very little of it; if you wish to be merry here right away, you must think of your debts — they torment you every day, so that you do not want to live.
Someone sits here with dignity, having to hold an office; though he comes from here himself, he treats the poor badly; the poor man says but one word, and the answer is: to prison, away!
Now quickly set off on your way to Prussian Poland, because there one can bring much wax and honey from the forest. Honey in brandy — that too can be exquisite.
Honey is as sweet as sugar, nowhere else can such be found, therefore I lift my feet, I leap over stones and earth to the Polish Canaan, where one can find enough honey!

### Appendix XII
Settlement contract for Johann Georgius Picz [Pietsch], Kietlin, 1802

Source: Private collection of the author.

In the Name of the Most Holy Trinity, Amen

Articles for the forthcoming Contract between the Noble Michał Korabita Ostrowski, Hereditary Lord of the Village of Kietlin, on the one part, and the Honest Joan Georgius Picz, acting on behalf of the Hollanders and others, on the other part, drawn up according to mutual agreement.

1st

The Noble Ostrowski grants in the estate of the village of Kietlin, in perpetuity for the use of the Hollanders undersigned below, [land] from the Kucharski Road to the Łagiewnik boundaries, as shall be marked by lines by a surveyor.

2nd

Whatever trees are found in the said division, the colonists are free to use for their own benefit, except for Thirty Pine trees and all the beams that are already hewn; these pines and beams shall be marked at the survey with the manor's mark, and should any colonist happen to dare to cut one down, he shall be obligated to pay six Polish Groszy for each inch of said pine, subject to free manorial execution.

3rd

The Noble Ostrowski grants freedoms to the contracting colonists for Six Years, that is beginning from the Feast of Saint Wojciech in the present year 1802 until the day of the 24th of April and the Feast of the same in the Year 1808, during which Six Years the said colonists are obligated to clear their fields and erect buildings from their own allotted forest at their own expense, in the straight line designated by the manor; and after the expiration of Six Years, that is from the Feast of Saint Wojciech in the Year 1808, they shall be obligated to pay rents, render dues, and fulfill obligations, according to the following description.

4th

From each Huba, rent of One Hundred and Fifteen Polish Zlotys in two installments — the first at the Feast of Saint Martin, the second at the Feast of Saint Wojciech — to be paid in hard coin with a manor receipt; from each Huba at the Feast of Saint Martin, Four good well-fattened capons, or for each capon one Polish Zloty and fifteen Polish Groszy to be rendered; from each Huba during harvest Twelve Days with scythe or sickle, as the manor shall prefer — Six Days for winter grain, Six Days for spring grain in the month of August to be worked; no fewer than one journey from each Huba with grain, reckoned at Five Warsaw-measure bushels of rye for a load, at a distance of six miles; if at three miles, two such journeys to be made; or should any colonist be unable to make such a journey, he shall pay Twelve Zlotys or two Prussian Thalers in lieu thereof; also from each Huba One Day with one's own draft animals and plough for manorial ploughing, such that with one plough land is ploughed according to the custom of the commune, annually together [...]

[...] The said colonists are obligated in perpetuity.

5th

Four Morgen for the Sołtys Joan Georgius Picz and his successors, one and a half Morgen for a school — the Noble Ostrowski offers to give without any payment, except the current entry fee.

6th

The colonists shall not be free to obtain any liquor anywhere else except from the Noble Ostrowski himself and his succeeding heirs or possessors; for should any colonist transgress this, he shall pay a fine of Eight Polish Zlotys for a quart of spirits obtained elsewhere, and One Zloty for a quart of beer, subject to free manorial execution. The Sołtys shall collect rents and dues from all the colonists himself and then deliver them all together annually to the manor at the specified deadlines above; in case of failure to meet the deadline, the manor shall be free to impose execution, which must be accepted.

7th

The colonists shall obtain firewood and building timber in their own allotted forest; however should they completely cut down their forest, then the Noble Ostrowski and his successors shall not prevent them from taking fallen wood for fuel, if there is any — on this condition however that they shall take it only where the manor's forester designates, and only such fallen wood as proves unfit for building; and for the preservation of order, only one day per week, one load on the day that shall be designated for taking such fallen wood. It shall not be permitted otherwise. Should however the colonists need standing timber for fuel or any other need, they shall purchase it for themselves by agreement in the forests of the Noble Ostrowski.

8th

The colonists shall have free pasturage only in the forests of the Noble Ostrowski, except in the grove where they may not graze; in turn the Noble Ostrowski reserves for himself and for the communes — namely the Kietlin and Kucharski communes — free pasturage in the forest allotted to the colonists; this mutual pasturage is to be without damage to either party in crops and meadows. The keeping of goats by colonists is forbidden under free shooting should any be found in the forest. Likewise the colonists shall not be permitted to keep any sheep. As for cattle, they may keep as many as they please on their Hubas, but on the forests it is not permitted to graze more than 8 head per Huba.

9th

The colonists shall not be permitted to hunt game, except wolves, either in their own allotted forest or in the manorial forests — under free confiscation of firearms and payment of a fine. In turn, the Noble Ostrowski [...]

[...] reserves free hunting in this forest which he allotted to the colonists for himself and his successors, except the period from Saint Wojciech to Saint Bartholomew.

10th

Disputes arising among the colonists shall be adjudicated by the Sołtys with a juror, with free appeal to the manor in matters of greater importance, whose decision they must be satisfied with.

11th

Should any colonist emigrate, the entire commune is obligated to pay his manorial or tavern debt in his place.

12th

The colonists must not take in suspicious persons, nor cause quarrels within neighboring boundaries, for they shall be held responsible for all events arising from such circumstances.

13th

Every colonist shall be free to sell his Huba and buildings; however it shall not be permitted to a nobleman or a Jew, under penalty of invalidity of such purchase and sale, from which a tenth Groszy is to be paid to the manor; therefore such sale must take place with the manor's knowledge and approval.

14th

After the expiration of the years of freedom, all royal and parochial taxes shall be paid by the colonists according to law and custom.

15th

No colonist shall be permitted to keep bees either in an apiary or in hollow trees, under free confiscation thereof by the manor; however, they may keep them near their dwellings, for which they shall annually deliver to the manor one quart of pure skimmed honey for every ten hives of bees.

16th

The pine trees in which hollow trunks are presently found, standing in the forest allotted to the colonists, shall belong to the Noble Ostrowski; no one is permitted to cut them down; however many such pine trees are counted off, the Noble Ostrowski promises to replace them from his own forest.

17th

Should any building burn down, the Noble Ostrowski promises to sell timber from his own forest for money, by agreement.

18th

The colonists shall pay the Noble Ostrowski an entry fee of Seventy-Two Polish Zlotys for each Huba. [...]

[...] 19th

For the survey of the forest allotted to the colonists, to determine how many Hubas are contained therein, the Noble Ostrowski shall bring a sworn surveyor, whose fee the colonists shall pay. After the survey, both parties shall draw up a contract between themselves according to the above articles and submit it for approval by the Most Serene Regency. The payment of costs for such approval shall fall to the colonists. Both parties mutually pledge to abide by all of this. Done in Saniki on the 31st of March in the Year 1802.

20th

The meadows and strips of land that shall be found in the allotted Hubas — the manor exempts these for itself and for the commune for Six Years, during which the colonists shall not be free to harvest or make use of them; after the expiration of Six Years, all of this shall then be handed over to them.

Michał Korabita Ostrowski
mp.

+++ Joan Georgius Picz
+++ Georgi Friderych
+++ Samuel Picz
+++ Andrys Chwic
+++ Joan Libeke

N.B. Each Huba consists of Thirty Morgen. A Morgen contains three hundred square rods, and one rod contains seven and a half ells of Warsaw measure, by which measure these Hubas are allotted.

Michał von Korabita Ostrowski
mp.

## From the Editors

Dr. Krzysztof Paweł Woźniak is a graduate of historical studies (1977) and ethnographic studies (1980) at the University of Łódź. Since 1982 he has been affiliated with the Department of Early Modern Polish History, and after 1989 with the Department of Social and Economic History, currently with the Department of Nineteenth-Century Polish History at the Institute of History, University of Łódź. He completed his doctorate on the basis of the dissertation *Problems of the Łódź Industrial District in the Opinion of German Historiography* (1990). His main area of research interest is the participation of the German population in the social and economic life of the Kingdom of Poland in the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. He also deals with modernization processes in the Kingdom of Poland in the nineteenth century, the history of the Evangelical-Augsburg Church in central Poland, and the history of multicultural Łódź.

His output, comprising over 230 titles, encompasses all forms of historical writing. He is the author or co-author of 7 books and the author of over 30 dissertations published in Polish and German collective works, as well as numerous articles. He has experience as an editor, translator, and publisher of source publications: *Materials on the History of Industry in the Kingdom of Poland. Reports of the Presidents of the Commission of Kalisz Voivodeship from the Years 1823–1830* (with K. Badziaka, 1998); H. A. Geyer, *Z mojego życia. Wspomnienia z lat 1855–1914 / Aus meinem Leben. Erinnerungen aus den Jahren 1855–1914* (2002); B. Kroll, A. Fritsche, *Tak było. Wspomnienia łódzkiego Volksdeutscha* (2010). Author of several entries on economic history in the *Encyklopedia Powszechna PWN* (2001–2005). He was the only Polish author in the prestigious German-Russian volume *„Eine große Zukunft…". Deutsche in Rußlands Wirtschaft*, hrsg. von D. Dahlmann, J. Pietrov [et al.], Berlin–Moscow 2000. From his teaching experience (he has supervised 36 master's theses in history), the book *O pisaniu pracy magisterskiej na studiach humanistycznych* (PWN 1998, 2nd ed. 1999) was born. He cultivates historical journalism with great enthusiasm in periodicals and the regional press, also in the Polish diaspora community ("Kurier Berliński"). He is also the author of the screenplay for the educational film *Wielkie żarcie po polsku. Wiek XIX* (1998).

Scholarship recipient of the Goethe Institut Berlin (1997), Herder Institut Marburg (2000), Universität Konstanz (2001). He has participated in numerous scholarly conferences in Poland and Germany. He is a member of the editorial board of the monthly journal "Tygiel Kultury", a collaborator of the monthly "Ziemia Łódzka", and a member of the Polish Historical Society, the Polish Ethnological Society, the Kommission für die Geschichte der Deutschen in Polen e.V., and a member and co-founder of the Polish Society for Urban Ethnology. For his scholarly and teaching achievements he has been honored with awards from the Rector of the University of Łódź (1989, 1991, 1998, 2003). In 2001 he was honored with the Za zasługi dla miasta Łodzi [For Merit to the City of Łódź] badge for the dissemination of knowledge about the city's past in Poland and abroad.
